Modern Classics

The Story of King Arthur and His Knights

The sword in the anvil, Excalibur, the winning of Guinevere and the founding of the Round Table, then the stories of Merlin, Sir Pelleas and Sir Gawain, retold complete for young readers with all of Pyle's own drawings

by Howard Pyle

1903 2026

  • Download epub
  • Contents

This is an AI modernization of The Story of King Arthur and His Knights into contemporary English. The original is available from Project Gutenberg. Also available as an epub.

Continue reading:

Editor's Introduction

Howard Pyle wrote and drew this book in Wilmington, Delaware, in 1902 and 1903, and Charles Scribner's Sons published it in November 1903. Pyle was born in Wilmington in 1853 and was by then one of the best-known illustrators in America. He drew for Harper's magazines, he had written and illustrated The Merry Adventures of Robin Hood in 1883, and he ran a school for illustrators whose students included N. C. Wyeth, Frank Schoonover and Jessie Willcox Smith. He wrote this book for young readers, and he drew every picture in it: the full-page plates, the decorated panel at the head of each chapter and the small drawings at the ends. It was the first of four Arthur books. The Story of the Champions of the Round Table followed in 1905, then The Story of Sir Launcelot and His Companions in 1907 and The Story of the Grail and the Passing of Arthur in 1910. Pyle died in Florence the next year.

His main source was Sir Thomas Malory's Le Morte d'Arthur of 1485, which this collection also has in full. Pyle follows Malory for the sword in the anvil, Excalibur and the Lady of the Lake, Pellinore, Accolon's fight with Arthur, and the stories of Pelleas and Ettard and of Gawain and the white hart. He changes a good deal too. Arthur winning Guinevere while disguised as a gardener's boy is not in Malory. The damsel who traps Merlin is Malory's Nimue; Pyle calls her Vivien, the name Tennyson gave her in Idylls of the King. Pyle also rearranges the royal family: in his Prologue Morgan le Fay marries King Lot and her sister Margaise marries King Uriens, which is the reverse of Malory. He is not always consistent about this himself, and later chapters give Gawain and Uwain different parents. Those slips are left as he wrote them.

Pyle wrote in an old-fashioned English that nobody had ever actually spoken, full of "thou," "quoth he" and "Lo!" It was his own invention, meant to make the book feel ancient to an American child in 1903. It is not Malory's English either. This edition takes that costume off and keeps everything else: every episode and speech, the jousts and feasts, the colors of every banner, and Pyle's habit of stopping to talk to his reader about courage and courtesy. He meant those talks seriously. Pyle also printed short summaries in the margins beside the paragraphs, and these appear as the italic lines in the text.

Where a character is the same person as in our Malory, the name is spelled the same way: Gawain, Lancelot, Uwain, Pelleas, Morgan le Fay. Pyle's own characters keep his names. His pictures keep their original lettering, so a plate may say "Sir Gawaine" or "Queen Morgana" above a page that says Gawain or Morgan. The plates are reproduced from a scan of a 1909 printing of the Scribner edition, made from the same plates as the first.

On the cover. Howard Pyle's How Arthur Drew Forth ye Sword (1903), one of this book's own plates, which appears again in its place inside. The young Arthur, a squire in a hooded cloak with his face turned down, grips the sword set in the anvil on its block of marble, while bearded onlookers watch from behind. Pyle drew every picture in the book in pen and ink, and this is the moment the whole story starts from.

Foreword

For several years I have thought about and turned over the matters in this book, and now at last, by the grace of God, I have been able to write it, and with such pleasure of spirit that if it gives you even a part of the joy it has given me, I will be very well content with what I have done.

For as I followed this history and came to think about the high nobility of spirit that moved these excellent men to act as they did, I have felt that they set such a perfect example of courage and humility that anyone would do exceedingly well to follow their way of behaving, as far as he is able.

For I believe that King Arthur was the most honorable and gentle knight who ever lived in all the world. And his fellows of the Round Table, who took him as the mirror in which they saw what a knight should be, made up altogether such a company of noble knights that it is hard to imagine their like will ever be seen in this world again. That is why I have taken such extraordinary pleasure in watching how those famous knights behaved whenever circumstances called on them to do their utmost.

So in the year of our Lord nineteen hundred and two I began to write this history of King Arthur and his Knights of the Round Table, and, if I am able, I mean to finish it lovingly at some later time, in another book, to the satisfaction of anyone who may care to read the story.

The Book of King Arthur

Prologue

Long ago there lived a very noble king named Uther Pendragon, and he became High King over all of Britain. Two men helped him greatly to win the high kingship of the realm, and they stood by him in everything he did. The first of these was a very powerful enchanter, who was sometimes a prophet as well, known to men as Merlin the Wise; and he gave Uther Pendragon very good advice. The second was an excellent and famous knight named Ulfius, whom many people thought the greatest leader in war of any man then alive; and he gave Uther Pendragon help and counsel in battle. So, with the help of Merlin and Sir Ulfius, Uther Pendragon was able to overcome all his enemies and become king of the whole realm.

Uther Pendragon takes the Lady Igraine for his wife.

After Uther Pendragon had ruled his kingdom for a number of years, he married a beautiful and gentle lady named Igraine. This noble lady was the widow of Gerlois, the Duke of Tintagil, and by that prince she had two daughters, one named Margaise and the other Morgan le Fay. And Morgan le Fay was a famous sorceress. The Queen brought these daughters with her to the court of Uther Pendragon after she married that mighty king, and there Margaise was married to King Uriens of Gore, and Morgan le Fay was married to King Lot of Orkney.

Of the birth of the young child, and the dangers around him.

Now after a while Uther Pendragon and Queen Igraine had a son, and he was a very beautiful baby, large and strong of bone. And while the child still lay wrapped in his swaddling clothes, in a cradle of gold and deep sea-blue, Merlin came to Uther Pendragon with the spirit of prophecy strong upon him (as it often was), and, speaking in that spirit, he said: "Lord, it has been given to me to see that you will soon fall sick of a fever, and that you may die of a violent sweat that will follow it. Now, if such a grievous thing should happen to us all, this young child, who is truly the hope of this whole realm, will be in great danger of his life. For many enemies will surely rise up and try to seize him for the sake of his inheritance, and either he will be killed or he will be held prisoner, with little hope of ever escaping. So I beg you, Lord, to let Sir Ulfius and me carry the child away at once to some safe place of refuge, where he can be hidden in secret until he grows to manhood and can defend himself against whatever dangers may threaten him."

When Merlin had finished speaking, Uther Pendragon answered him with a very steady face: "Merlin, as far as my death is concerned, when my time comes to die I believe God will give me the grace to meet my end with a cheerful heart. For truly, my lot in that is no different from that of any other man ever born of woman. But as for this young child, if your prophecy is true, then his danger is very great, and it would be well to carry him away to some safe shelter, as you advise. So I ask you to do as you think best in this matter, and to keep in your heart that this child is the most precious thing I shall leave behind me to this land."

King Uther dies, as Merlin foretold.

All this, as I have said, Uther Pendragon spoke with great calm and steadiness of spirit. And Merlin did as he had advised: he and Sir Ulfius carried the child away by night, and no one but the two of them knew where the baby had been taken. And soon afterward Uther Pendragon was seized with the sickness Merlin had foretold, and he died exactly as Merlin had feared he would. So it was a very good thing that the child had been taken to a place of safety.

And after Uther Pendragon had departed this life, things went just as Merlin had feared, for the whole realm fell into great disorder. Every lesser king fought against his neighbor to be high king, and wicked knights and barons prowled the highways as they pleased and cruelly forced helpless travelers to pay them toll. Some travelers they took prisoner and held for ransom, and others they killed because they had no ransom to pay. So it was a very common sight to see a dead man lying by the roadside, if you ventured to make a journey on some business or other. And so, after a while, that whole sorrowful land groaned under the trouble that lay upon it.

The Archbishop of Canterbury takes counsel with Merlin.

Nearly eighteen years passed in this great misery, and then one day the Archbishop of Canterbury sent for Merlin and spoke to him like this: "Merlin, men say that you are the wisest man in all the world. Can you not find some way to heal the troubles of this unhappy realm? Turn your wisdom to this matter, and choose a king who will be a fit high king for us, so that we may live in happiness once more, as we did in the days of Uther Pendragon."

Then Merlin raised his face to the Archbishop and said: "My lord, the spirit of prophecy that sometimes comes upon me moves me now to tell you that I can see this country will soon have a king who will be wiser and greater and more worthy of praise than even Uther Pendragon was. And he will bring order and peace where there is now disorder and war. What is more, I can tell you that this king will be of Uther Pendragon's own royal blood."

To this the Archbishop said: "What you tell me, Merlin, is a wonderfully strange thing. But in this spirit of prophecy can you not foretell when this king is to come? And can you tell us how we shall know him when he appears among us? For there are many lesser kings who would gladly be high king of this land, and many of them think themselves fit to rule over all the others. How then are we to tell the real king from those who may proclaim themselves the rightful king?"

"My lord Archbishop," said Merlin, "if I have your permission to use my magic, I will set up a test, and if any man achieves it, the whole world will know at once that he is the rightful King and High King of this realm." And the Archbishop said to this, "Merlin, I bid you do whatever seems right to you in this matter." And Merlin said, "I will do so."

Merlin prepares a test of kingship.

So Merlin, by his magic, made a huge square block of marble suddenly appear in an open place in front of the cathedral door. And on this block of marble he made an anvil stand, and into the anvil he made a great naked sword be thrust, halfway down its blade. And this sword was the most wonderful that anyone had ever seen, for the blade was of blue steel and extraordinarily bright and gleaming. And the hilt was of gold, chased and carved with marvelous skill and set with a great many precious stones, so that it shone with wonderful brightness in the sunlight. And around the sword these words were written in letters of gold:

Whoever pulls this sword out of the anvil is the rightful King of England, born to the crown.

So a great many people came and gazed at that sword and marveled at it exceedingly, for nothing like it had ever been seen on earth before.

Then, when Merlin had worked this miracle, he told the Archbishop to call together all the chief people of the land at Christmastime, and he told the Archbishop to command that every man should try to draw out the sword, because whoever succeeded in drawing it out of the anvil would be the rightful King of Britain.

So the Archbishop did as Merlin said. And this was the marvel of the marble stone and the anvil, which anyone can easily read about for himself in the book that Robert de Boron wrote a very long time ago, called The Romance of Merlin.

Now when the Lord Archbishop's summons went out, calling all the chief people of the land to try that miracle (for it truly would be a miracle to draw a sword blade out of an anvil of solid iron), the whole realm was thrown at once into a great stir, so that every man asked his neighbor, "Who will draw out that sword, and who will be our King?" Some thought it would be King Lot, and others thought it would be King Uriens of Gore (these two being Uther Pendragon's sons-in-law). Some thought it would be King Leodegrance of Cameliard, and others that it would be King Rience of North Wales. Some thought it would be this king and others that king, for the whole world was divided into parties that thought whatever they liked best.

Then, as Christmas drew near, it soon looked as though the entire world was making its way to London Town. The highways and byways filled up with travelers, kings and lords and knights and ladies and squires and pages and men-at-arms, all heading to the place where the test of the sword and the anvil was to be made. Every inn and castle was so packed with travelers that it was a marvel how so many people could fit inside them, and everywhere along the roads there were tents and pavilions (great tents of silk and canvas) pitched to shelter those who could find no room indoors.

But when the Archbishop saw the crowds that were gathering, he said to Merlin, "Truly, Merlin, it would be a very strange thing if among all these great kings and noble, honorable lords we did not find someone worthy to be King of this realm."

At this Merlin smiled and said, "Do not be surprised, my lord, if among all those who seem so extraordinarily worthy, not one is found worthy; and do not be surprised if, among all those who are unknown, one should rise up who proves himself entirely worthy."

And the Archbishop thought deeply about Merlin's words. And so this story begins.

The Book of King Arthur, Part One: The Winning of Kinghood

Foreword

Here begins the story of the sword, the anvil, and the marble stone, and of how that sword was first won by an unknown youth who until then had no fame at all, either in arms or in rank.

So listen now to what I have written here.

Chapter 1: How Sir Kay Fought in a Great Tournament in London and Broke His Sword, and How Arthur Found Him a New One

Of Sir Ector, the Trustworthy Knight.

Now among the great people who were called to London Town by the Archbishop's summons, as I have told you, there was a certain knight, very honorable and of high rank, named Sir Ector of Bonmaison. He was called the Trustworthy Knight, because he faithfully kept the secrets of those who confided in him, and because he always did for every man, whether of high or low degree, exactly what he had promised to do, without falling short in the least. So this noble and excellent knight was held in great regard by everyone who knew him. For not only was he honorable in his conduct, but he was also of very high rank and great wealth. He owned seven castles in Wales and in the country to the north of it, and also certain rich farmlands with villages belonging to them, and several forests of great size, both in the north country and in the west. This very noble knight had two sons. The elder was Sir Kay, a young knight of great courage and promise, already well known in the tournaments because of several very honorable deeds of arms he had done. The other was a young lad eighteen years old, named Arthur, who at that time was serving, and serving well, as Sir Kay's squire.

Now when Sir Ector of Bonmaison received the Archbishop's summons by messenger, he immediately called these two sons to him and told them to get ready at once to go with him to London Town, and they did so. In the same way he told a great number of his retainers and squires and pages to make themselves ready, and they did so too. And so, with a very fine company of armed men and with great show and ceremony, Sir Ector of Bonmaison made his way to London Town, in obedience to the Archbishop's command.

When he arrived there, he took up his lodging in a certain field where many other noble knights and mighty lords had already settled themselves. There he set up a very handsome pavilion of green silk, and raised his banner bearing the emblem of his house, which was a black gryphon on a field of green.

All over this field stood a great crowd of other pavilions of many different colors, and above each pavilion flew the pennant and the banner of the mighty lord it belonged to. So many pennants and banners were there that in places the sky was almost hidden by the bright colors of the fluttering flags.

Among the great lords who had come there in answer to the Archbishop's summons were many very famous kings and queens and noblemen of high degree. There was King Lot of Orkney, who had married a stepdaughter of Uther Pendragon, and there was King Uriens of Gore, who had married another stepdaughter of that great king. There was King Ban, and King Bors, and King Rience, and King Leodegrance, and many others of the same rank. In all there were no fewer than twelve kings and seven dukes; so that, what with all their courts of lords and ladies and squires and pages waiting on them, the town of London had hardly ever seen the like before that day.

The Archbishop declares a tournament.

Now the Archbishop of Canterbury, bearing in mind how extraordinary an occasion it was that had brought so many kings and dukes and high lords to the adventure of the sword and the anvil, had commanded that a very stately and noble tournament should be proclaimed: a great contest of arms, in which knights fought one another for honor and glory before all the people. He also commanded that this contest should be held in a certain field near the great cathedral, three days before the test of the sword and the anvil was to be made (which, as I told you, was to happen on Christmas Day). To this tournament every knight was invited who was of good enough birth and rank to be fit to take part in it. And so a great many high-born knights applied to be admitted, and in such numbers that three heralds were kept very busy looking into their claims to the right to fight. For these heralds examined the coats of arms and the family records of everyone who applied, with great care and caution.

Sir Kay asks permission to attend the tournament.

Now when Sir Kay heard the news of this tournament, he went to his father, and when he stood before him he said: "Sir, I am your son, and I have inherited from you a very high rank, both in birth and in fortune. And I find that I have a tremendous desire to risk my body in this tournament. So, if I can prove my standing as a knight before this council of heralds, it may bring great honor and credit to you and to our house if I take part in this adventure. And so I ask your permission to do as I wish."

To this Sir Ector replied: "My son, you have my permission to enter this honorable contest. And I hope that God will give you a great deal of strength, and also such grace of spirit that you may win honor for yourself and credit for all of us who are of your blood."

So Sir Kay went away full of joy, and he went at once to the gathering of heralds and laid his claims before them. And after they had duly examined his right to knighthood, they entered his name as one of the knights who would fight, just as he wished; and at this Sir Kay was filled with great contentment and joy of heart.

When his name had been written on the list of those who would fight, Sir Kay chose his young brother Arthur to be his squire, and to carry his spear and his pennant before him onto the field of battle. And Arthur was very glad as well, because of the honor that had come to him and to his brother.

Now, when the day of the tournament came, an enormous crowd of people gathered to watch that noble and courtly contest of arms. For at that time, as I have said, London was extraordinarily full of nobles and knights, so that it was reckoned that no fewer than twenty thousand lords and ladies (besides those twelve kings with their courts and the seven dukes with theirs) were gathered in the stands all around the lists, which were the fenced grounds of the battle, to watch the deeds of those chosen knights. And those noble people sat so close together, and so filled the seats and benches set aside for them, that it looked as though a solid wall of human beings surrounded the meadow where the battle was to be fought. And indeed any knight might well be stirred to do his very best on such a great occasion, with the eyes of so many beautiful ladies and noble lords watching what he did. And so the hearts of all the knights there swelled with eagerness to outdo one another and to tumble their opponents into the dust.

In the center of this wonderful court of lords and ladies stood the box and the throne of the Lord Archbishop himself. Above the throne was a canopy of purple cloth, decorated with silver lilies, and the throne itself was hung all around with purple velvet, embroidered with two designs, turn and turn about: the figure of Saint George in gold, and silver crosses of Saint George circled with golden halos. There the Lord Archbishop sat in great state and splendor, surrounded by a very lofty court of high-ranking churchmen and of honorable knights, so that the whole center of the field glittered with the splendor of gold and silver embroidery, and was made beautiful by every color of rich clothing, and bright with fine armor of excellent workmanship. And indeed, so stately was the whole scene that very few of those who were there had ever seen so noble a preparation for battle as the one they saw that day.

Now, when all that great assembly had taken their places and everything had been made ready in proper order, a herald came and stood before the Archbishop's throne and blew a very strong, loud blast on a trumpet. At that signal the gates of the lists were opened at once, and two parties of knights rode in, one party at the northern end of the battle meadow and the other at the southern end. Then all at once that empty field was aglitter with the bright splendor of sunlight on polished armor and trappings. And so the two parties took up their positions, each at the place that had been assigned to it, the one to the north and the other to the south.

Sir Kay takes part in the lists.

Now the party that Sir Kay had joined was at the north end of the field, and it numbered ninety-three knights. The other party stood at the south end of the field, and it numbered ninety-six. But though Sir Kay's party was three fewer than the other, it was nonetheless the stronger by a good deal, because there were a number of knights of great strength and fame in its company. Indeed, I may mention here that two of those knights later became highly respected companions of the Round Table: Sir Mador de la Porte, and Sir Bedevere, who was the last man to see King Arthur alive on this earth.

So, when everything was prepared according to the rules of the tournament, and when the knights had made themselves ready in every way that was needed, and had set their spears and shields as knights do who are about to enter serious battle, the herald put his trumpet to his lips a second time and blew on it with all his might. Then, having sounded this blast, he waited a while, and then he blew the trumpet again.

And at that blast, each party of knights left its place and rushed against the other in a great uproar, with such noise and fury that the whole earth groaned beneath the hoofs of the war-horses, and trembled and shook as if in an earthquake.

So those two companies met, one against the other, in the middle of the field, and the roar of breaking lances was so terrible that everyone who heard it was astonished and appalled by the sound. Several fair ladies fainted away in terror of the noise, and others shrieked aloud. For not only was there that great uproar, but the air was entirely filled with flying splinters of ash wood.

In that famous charge seventy very noble and honorable knights were thrown from their horses, and many of them were trampled under the horses' hoofs. And so, when the two companies drew back, each to its own place, the ground could be seen to be covered all over with broken pieces of lances and fragments of armor, and many knights could be seen lying sorrowfully in the middle of all that wreckage. Some of these champions tried to get up and could not, while others lay perfectly still, as though they were dead. Squires and pages ran out to them in great numbers, and lifted up the fallen men and carried them away to places of safety. And attendants likewise ran out and gathered up the pieces of armor and the broken spears and carried them off to the barriers, so that after a while the field was entirely clear once more.

Then everyone who was looking down on that meadow cheered loudly, with great joy of heart, for such a noble and glorious contest of arms in friendly trial had hardly ever been seen in all that realm before.

Sir Kay does well in the encounter.

Now let us turn to Sir Kay. For in this charge he had carried himself so well that no knight there had done better than he, and perhaps no one had done as well. Though two opponents had aimed their spears at him at the same time, he had held firm against them both. And he struck one of those two so violently, right in the middle of his defenses, that he lifted the man clean over the back of his horse and flung him down half a spear's length behind it, so that the fallen knight rolled over three times in the dust before he stopped.

And when the knights of Sir Kay's party who were near him saw what he had done, they cheered him loudly and with such enthusiasm that Sir Kay was wonderfully satisfied and pleased at heart.

And indeed it must be said that at that time there was hardly any knight in all the world who was so excellent at deeds of arms as Sir Kay. And though there came knights later of much greater fame and of more glorious deeds (as will be told in this book in good time), yet at that time Sir Kay was reckoned by many to be one of the most wonderfully mighty knights in all that realm, whether riding alone in search of adventure or fighting in battle.

So that round of the contest was run, to the great pleasure and satisfaction of everyone who saw it, and especially of Sir Kay and his friends. And after it was finished, the two parties returned in good order, each to its own place once more.

And when they got there, each knight handed his spear to his squire. For the next charge was to be made with swords, and so all lances and other weapons were to be put aside; such was the rule of that courteous and well-bred contest of arms.

Of the contest with swords.

So when the herald blew his trumpet again, every knight drew his sword, all of them so eager for battle that there was a great splendor of blades flashing in the air at once. And when the herald blew a second time, each party pushed forward into the contest with great nobility of heart and eagerness of spirit, every knight meaning to meet his opponent with all the strength that was in him.

Then at once there began a battle so fierce that if those knights had been bitter enemies of long standing instead of friendly contestants, the blows they dealt each other could not have been more violent, or more astonishing to watch.

And in this round too Sir Kay proved himself so extraordinary a champion that his equal was nowhere to be seen on all that field. For he struck down five knights violently, one after another, before anyone could check his advance.

So, seeing him do such work as this, several knights of the other party tried to get at him so as to meet him as he came on.

Among these was a certain knight named Sir Balamorgineas, who was so huge in body that he rode head and shoulders above every other knight. And he had such extraordinary strength that people believed he could hold his own against three ordinary knights at once. So when this knight saw the work Sir Kay was doing, he called out to him, "Ho! Ho! Sir Knight of the black gryphon, turn this way and do battle with me!"

Sir Kay fights with Sir Balamorgineas.

Now when Sir Kay saw that Sir Balamorgineas meant to come against him like that, very threateningly and set on fighting him, he turned toward his enemy with great cheerfulness of spirit. For at that time Sir Kay was very full of youthful fire, and thought nothing of attacking any enemy who might challenge him to battle.

(So it was at that time. But afterward, when he became Seneschal, the officer who runs the king's household, and when other and mightier knights appeared at the King's court, it happened that he would sometimes avoid a fight with such a knight as Sir Lancelot, or Sir Pelleas, or Sir Marhaus, or Sir Gawain, if he could do so without loss to his honor.)

So, being very full of the spirit of youth, he turned with great vigor of heart, all on fire with the eagerness and fury of battle. And he cried out in a great voice, "Very well, I will do battle with you, and I will cast you down like your fellows!" And with that he struck at Sir Balamorgineas with wonderful fierceness, with all his might. Sir Balamorgineas took the stroke on his helmet and was completely dazed by the fury of it, for he had never felt anything like it before. His head swam so lightly that he had to hold on to the horn of his saddle to keep himself from falling.

Sir Kay breaks his sword.

But it was a great pity for Sir Kay that, with the fierceness of the blow, his sword blade snapped off short at the hilt and flew so high into the air that it seemed to rise above the towers of the cathedral as it went. Yet that is what happened, and so Sir Kay was left without any weapon. And yet people thought that with that stroke he had Sir Balamorgineas entirely at his mercy, and that if he could have struck one more blow with his sword he might easily have overcome him.

But as it was, Sir Balamorgineas soon recovered enough to see that his enemy was now entirely at his mercy. And so, filled beyond measure with rage at the blow he had received, he pressed toward Sir Kay, meaning to strike him down in a violent attack.

At that point it might have gone very badly for Sir Kay, had not three of his companions in arms seen the terrible danger he was in. They pushed in between him and Sir Balamorgineas, meaning to take that knight's attack on themselves and so save Sir Kay from being overthrown. They did this so successfully that Sir Kay was able to push his way out of the crush and escape to the barriers without suffering any more harm at the hands of his enemies.

Sir Kay tells Arthur to get him a sword.

Now when he reached the barrier, his squire, young Arthur, came running to him with a goblet of spiced wine. Sir Kay opened the visor of his helmet to drink, for he was thirsty beyond measure. And his face was all covered with blood and sweat, and he was so parched from the battle that his tongue stuck to the roof of his mouth and he could not speak. But when he had drunk what Arthur gave him, his tongue was loosened, and he shouted at the young man in a loud and violent voice: "Ho! Ho! Brother, get me another sword to fight with, for I am certainly winning a great deal of glory for our house today!" And Arthur said, "Where shall I get you a sword?" And Kay said, "Hurry to our father's pavilion and fetch me another sword from there, for this one I have is broken." And Arthur said, "I will do it as fast as I can." And with that he put his hand on the barrier and vaulted over it into the lane beyond. He ran down the lane as fast as he could go, meaning to do the task his brother had given him; and just as fast he ran on to the pavilion his father had set up in the meadows.

But when he reached Sir Ector's pavilion, he found no one there, for all the attendants had gone off to the tournament. Nor could he find any sword fit for his brother to use, and so he was at a loss to know what to do.

Arthur draws the sword from the anvil.

In this difficulty he thought of the sword that stood thrust into the anvil in front of the cathedral, and it seemed to him that a sword like that would suit his brother's purpose very well. So he said to himself, "I will go there and get that sword if I can, for it will certainly do very well for my brother to finish his battle with." And at that he ran as fast as he could to the cathedral. When he got there he found that no one was standing guard at the block of marble, as there always had been until then, for all the guards had gone off to the contest of arms that was going on. And the anvil and the sword stood where he could reach them. So, with no one there to stop young Arthur, he leaped up onto the block of marble and set his hands on the hilt of the sword. And he bent his body and pulled very strongly on the sword, and it came out of the anvil with wonderful smoothness and ease. And he held the sword in his hand, and it was his.

When he had gotten the sword in this way, he wrapped it in his cloak so that no one might see it (for it shone with an extraordinary brightness and splendor), and he leaped down from the marble block and hurried with it to the field of battle.

Now when Arthur came back into the meadow, he found Sir Kay waiting for him with great impatience. And when Sir Kay saw him, he cried out very fiercely, "Have you got a sword?" And Arthur said, "Yes, I have one here." With that he opened his cloak and showed Sir Kay the sword he had brought.

Now when Sir Kay saw the sword, he knew it at once, and he did not know what to think or what to say. So for a while he stood there, like a man turned to stone, staring at that sword. Then after a while he said, in a very strange voice, "Where did you get that sword?" And Arthur looked at his brother and saw that his face was deeply troubled and had gone as white as wax. And he said, "Brother, what is wrong with you, that you look so strange? I will tell you the whole truth. I could find no sword in our father's pavilion, so I thought of the sword that stood in the anvil on the marble block in front of the cathedral. So I went there and tried to draw it out, and it came out with wonderful ease. And when I had drawn it out, I wrapped it in my cloak and brought it here to you, as you see."

Sir Kay keeps the sword for his own.

Then Sir Kay turned his thoughts inward and said to himself, "Why, my brother Arthur is still hardly more than a child. And besides, he is extremely innocent. So he does not know what he has done, or what the doing of it means. Now, since he has won this weapon, why should I not claim the deed for myself, and so gain the glory that comes with it?" At that he roused himself at once and said to Arthur, "Give me the sword and the cloak," and Arthur did as his brother told him. And when he had done so, Sir Kay said to him, "Tell no one about this, but keep it secret in your own heart. Meanwhile, go to our father where he sits by the lists, and ask him to come at once to the pavilion where we are lodging."

And Arthur did as Sir Kay told him, full of wonder that his brother should seem so troubled in spirit. For he did not know what he had done in drawing that sword out of the anvil, nor what great things were to come of that small deed. For that is how it is in this world: a man may sometimes prove himself worthy of a great trust such as that, and yet, in the humility of his spirit, be entirely unaware that he is worthy of it. And so it was with young Arthur at that time.

Chapter 2: How Arthur Twice Did the Miracle of the Sword in Front of Sir Ector, and How He Learned Who He Really Was

So Arthur hurried to the part of the lists where Sir Ector sat with the people of his household. He stood in front of his father and said, "Sir, my brother Kay has sent me here to ask you to come straight to the pavilion where we have made our lodging. And truly, I think something very extraordinary has happened, for my brother Kay has a look on his face such as I have never seen him wear."

Sir Ector wondered very much what could have made Sir Kay leave the fighting and send for him at such a time, so he got up from where he sat and went with Arthur. They went to the pavilion, and when Sir Ector came in, there stood Sir Kay in the middle of it. Sir Ector saw that his face was as white as wood ash and that his eyes shone with a strange brightness. And Sir Ector said, "My son, what is the matter with you?" Sir Kay answered, "Sir, here is a very wonderful thing." With that he took his father by the hand and led him to the table that stood in the pavilion. On the table lay a cloak, and there was something wrapped inside the cloak. Then Sir Kay opened the cloak, and there lay the sword from the anvil, its hilt and its blade glittering with dazzling splendor.

Sir Ector sees the sword.

Sir Ector knew that sword at once, and where it had come from. He was filled with such astonishment that he did not know what to do. For a while his tongue would not speak at all. Then at last he found his voice and cried out loudly, "What is this I see with my own eyes!"

Sir Kay answered, "Sir, I have the sword that until a little while ago stood fixed in the anvil on the block of marble in front of the great cathedral. So I ask you to tell me what this may mean."

Then Sir Ector said, "How did you come by that sword?"

For a while Sir Kay said nothing. But after a while he said, "Sir, I broke my sword in the fight I was just fighting, and then I found myself this sword in its place."

Sir Ector was completely bewildered and did not know whether to believe what he was hearing. After a while he said, "If it is true that you drew this sword out of the anvil, then it must also be true that you are the rightful King of Britain, for that is what the writing on the sword proclaims. But if you really did draw it out of the anvil, then you will be able just as easily to thrust it back again into the place you took it from."

At this a great trouble came over Sir Kay's spirit, and he cried out in a very loud voice, "Who could do such a thing as that? Who could work so great a miracle as to thrust a sword into solid iron?" Sir Ector answered, "That miracle is no greater than the one you have already worked in drawing it out. For who ever heard of a man who could draw a sword out of a place and yet could not thrust it back where he drew it from?"

Sir Kay did not know what to say to his father, and he was very much afraid that he would not be able to work that miracle. Even so, he comforted himself as well as he could, saying to himself, "If my young brother Arthur could work this miracle, why shouldn't I be able to work one just like it? I am certainly no less worthy than he is. If he drew the sword out so easily, perhaps I shall be able to thrust it back into its place just as easily." And so he took what comfort he could from these thoughts.

Sir Kay tries to put back the sword, but fails.

He wrapped the sword in the cloak again, and when he had done so, he and Sir Ector left the pavilion and made their way to the place in front of the cathedral where the marble block and the anvil stood. Arthur went along with his father and his brother, and they did not forbid him. When they came to the place where the sword had been, Sir Kay climbed up onto the block of marble and looked at the face of the anvil. And the face of the anvil was perfectly smooth, without a scratch or a mark of any kind. Sir Kay said to himself, "What is this my father wants me to do? What man alive could thrust a sword blade into a solid anvil of iron?" But even so, he could not back out of that impossible task, and he was forced to attempt the miracle. So he set the point of the sword against the iron and pushed down on it with all his strength. But it was impossible for him to do it. Though he strove with all his might, pressing the sword against the face of the anvil, he did not pierce the iron by so much as the breadth of a hair.

So after he had tried for a long time, he finally stopped and climbed down from where he stood. And he said to his father, "Sir, no man alive can work that miracle."

Sir Ector answered, "Then how is it possible that you could have drawn the sword out, as you say, and yet cannot put it back again?"

Then young Arthur raised his voice and said, "Father, may I speak?" And Sir Ector said, "Speak, my son." And Arthur said, "I wish I might try my hand with that sword." Sir Ector replied, "By what right would you handle that sword?" And Arthur said, "Because it was I who drew that sword out of the anvil for my brother. And as you say, drawing it out is no harder than thrusting it back again. So I believe I shall be able to set it back into the iron I drew it from."

Then Sir Ector stared at young Arthur so strangely that Arthur did not know why he looked at him that way. So he cried out, "Sir, why are you staring at me so strangely? Are you angry with me?" Sir Ector answered, "Before God, my son, I am not angry with you." Then he said, "If you wish to handle the sword, you certainly may attempt that miracle."

Arthur works the miracle of the sword and the anvil.

So Arthur took the sword from his brother Kay and leaped up onto the marble block. He set the point of the sword on the anvil and pressed down very hard, and the sword slid smoothly into the center of the anvil until it stood halfway deep in it, and there it held fast. And after he had worked that miracle, he drew the sword out again, very quickly and easily, and then thrust it back once more, just as he had done before.

When Sir Ector saw what Arthur had done, he cried out in a tremendously loud voice, "Lord! Lord! What is this miracle I see with my own eyes!" And when Arthur came down from the marble block, Sir Ector knelt down before him and put his hands together, palm to palm.

But when Arthur saw what his father was doing, he cried out like someone in great pain, and he said, "My father! My father! Why are you kneeling to me?"

Sir Ector answered him, "I am not your father. And now it has been made plain that you are certainly of very high birth, and that the blood of kings flows in your veins, or you could never have handled that sword as you have done."

At that Arthur began to weep beyond all measure, and he cried out as if in great anguish of spirit, "Father! Father! What are you saying? I beg you, get up, and do not kneel to me."

So Sir Ector got up from his knees and stood face to face with Arthur, and he said, "Arthur, why are you weeping?" And Arthur said, "Because I am afraid."

All this while Sir Kay had been standing nearby, and he could neither move nor speak, but stood there like someone under a spell. And he said to himself, "What is this? Is my brother a king?"

Then Sir Ector spoke. "Arthur," he said, "the time has come for you to know who you are, for the truth about your life has until now been kept completely hidden from you.

Sir Ector tells Arthur the story of his infancy.

"Now I will confess everything to you, and it is this. Eighteen years ago a certain man came to me, a very wise man and high in the favor of Uther Pendragon, and that man was the enchanter Merlin. Merlin showed me the signet ring of Uther Pendragon, and by the authority of that ring he commanded me to be at a certain place at a particular time that he named. The place he named was the postern gate, the small back gate, of Uther Pendragon's castle, and the time he named was midnight of that very day.

"He told me to tell no one anything about what he had said to me, and so I kept his secret, as he wished me to.

"So I went to that postern gate at midnight as Merlin had commanded. And there Merlin came to me with another man, and the other man was Sir Ulfius, who was the chief knight of Uther Pendragon's household. And I tell you that those two great men stood closer to Uther Pendragon than any other men in all the world.

"Now when those two came to me, I saw that Merlin was carrying something in his arms, wrapped in a scarlet cloak of fine cloth. He opened the folds of the cloak, and there I saw a newborn child wrapped in swaddling clothes. I saw the child by the light of a lantern that Sir Ulfius carried, and I could see that he was very beautiful of face and large of bone. And you were that child.

"Then Merlin gave me my orders. I was to take that child and raise him as my own. He said the child was to be called Arthur, and that no one in all the world was to know anything except that the child was mine. I told Merlin I would do as he wished, and I took the child and carried him away with me. And I announced that the child was my own, and everyone believed me, so that no one ever knew anything but that you were my own son. And the lady who was my wife took that secret with her to Paradise when she died. From then until now, no one in all the world knew anything of this matter except me and those two great men I have named.

"And until now I have never known who your father was. But now I begin to suspect who he was, and that very high and kingly blood flows in your veins. And I have it in my mind that perhaps your father was Uther Pendragon himself. For who but the son of Uther Pendragon could have drawn that sword out of the anvil as you have done?"

When Arthur heard his father say this, he cried out in a very loud and passionate voice, "Woe! Woe! Woe!" saying the word three times. And Sir Ector said, "Arthur, why are you so full of sorrow?" And Arthur said, "Because I have lost my father, and I would rather have my father than be a King!"

Merlin and Sir Ulfius appear to the three.

Now while all this was happening, two men came to that place, very tall, and wonderfully noble and proud in their bearing. And when these two men drew near, Arthur and Sir Ector and Sir Kay saw that one of them was the enchanter Merlin and the other was Sir Ulfius, for those two men were very famous and well known to all the world. And when the two had come up to the three, Merlin spoke and said, "What news?" And Sir Ector answered, "News of a very wonderful kind. For look, Merlin! This is the child you brought to me eighteen years ago, and you can see that he has grown to manhood."

Then Merlin said, "Sir Ector, I know very well who this youth is, for I have kept close watch over him all this time. And I know that in him lies the hope of Britain. What is more, I tell you that this very day, in the surface of an enchanted looking glass, I have seen everything he has done since morning. I know how he drew the sword out of the anvil, and how he thrust it back again; and I know how he drew it out and thrust it back a second time. And I know everything you have been saying to him just now, and I declare that you have told him the very truth. And now the spirit of prophecy is upon me, and I can see into the future. I see that you, Arthur, will become the greatest and most famous King who ever lived in Britain. I see that many knights of extraordinary excellence will gather around you, and that men will tell of their marvelous deeds for as long as this land lasts. I see that through these knights your reign will be full of splendor and glory. And I see that the most marvelous adventure of all, the quest for the Holy Grail, will be achieved by three of the knights of your Court, to your lasting fame, for you will be the King in whose reign that holy cup is won. All these things I foresee. And the time is now at hand when the glory of your House will once again be shown to the world, and all the people of this land will rejoice in you and in your kingship. So, Sir Ector, for the next three days I charge you to guard this young man as the apple of your eye, for in him lies the hope and salvation of this whole realm."

Sir Ector asks a favor of Arthur.

Then Sir Ector raised his voice and cried out to Arthur, "A favor! A favor!" And Arthur said, "Oh, what is this? My father, do you ask a favor of me, when you may have everything in the world that is mine to give? Ask whatever you wish, and it is yours!" Then Sir Ector said, "I beg this of you: that when you are King, your brother Kay may be Seneschal of all this realm." And Arthur said, "It shall be as you ask." And he said, "As for you, it shall be even better for you, for you shall be my father to the end!" And as he said this, he took Sir Ector's head in his hands and kissed him on the forehead and on the cheeks, and so sealed the promise he had given.

But all this while Sir Kay had stood there like a man struck by lightning. He did not know whether to be lifted up to the skies or cast down into the depths, now that his young brother had passed him by and been raised to such an extraordinary height of fortune. And so he stood there like someone with no life or motion left in him.

And let it be said here that Arthur kept every promise he made to his father. For in later times he made Sir Kay his Seneschal, and Sir Ector was a father to him until the day of his death, which came five years after that time.

So I have told you how Arthur's royal birth was first discovered. And now, if you will listen, you shall hear how it was confirmed before all the world.

Chapter 3: How Many Kings and Great Dukes Tried to Draw the Sword from the Anvil and Failed, and How Arthur Tried and Succeeded

So when the morning of Christmas Day had come, many thousands of people of every rank, both high-born and humble, gathered in front of the cathedral to watch the test of that sword.

Now a canopy of embroidered cloth of many colors had been spread above the sword and the anvil, and a platform had been built all around the block of marble. And near that place a throne had been set up for the Archbishop, for the Archbishop was to oversee the test and to see that every part of it was carried out with fairness and great care.

So, when the morning was half gone, the Archbishop himself came with great pomp and ceremony and took his seat on the high throne that had been placed for him. And all his court of clergy and knights gathered around him, so that he made a very proud and splendid show of courtly state.

Now nineteen kings and sixteen dukes had gathered for that test, and each of them was of such noble and lofty rank that he had high hopes he would be proved before the world that day to be the rightful king and High King of all Britain. So after the Archbishop had settled himself on his throne, several of them came and demanded that he put the matter to the test at once. So the Archbishop ordered his herald to sound a trumpet and to call all who had the right to try the sword to come forward to that adventure, and the herald did as the Archbishop ordered.

King Lot of Orkney tries the sword and fails.

And as soon as the herald had sounded his trumpet, the first of those kings appeared to make trial of the sword, and the one who came was King Lot of Orkney and the Isles. With King Lot came eleven knights and five squires, so that he made a very noble show before the eyes of everyone. And when King Lot had arrived at that place, he climbed onto the platform. First he bowed to the Archbishop, and then he laid his hands on the pommel of the sword in the sight of all. He bent his body and pulled on the sword with great strength, but he could not move the blade in the anvil even by the width of a hair, for it stood as fast as the iron in which it was planted. After that first attempt he tried three times more, but still he could not move the blade in the iron at all. Then, having tried four times, he gave up and came down from that place. And he was filled with great anger and indignation that he had not succeeded.

Many others try and fail.

After King Lot came his brother-in-law, King Uriens of Gore, and he too tried the sword in the same way King Lot had done. But he did no better than the other king. After King Uriens came King Fion of Scotland, and after King Fion came King Mark of Cornwall, and after King Mark came King Rience of North Wales, and after King Rience came King Leodegrance of Cameliard. And after him came all those other kings and dukes I have spoken of, and not one of them all was able to move the blade. Some of these high and mighty lords were filled with anger and indignation that they had not succeeded, and others were ashamed that they had failed at that task before the eyes of all who watched them. But whether they were angry or ashamed, it did their cause no good at all.

Now when all the kings and dukes had failed in that adventure, the people who were there were very much astonished, and they said to one another, "How can this be? If all those kings and dukes of such lofty rank have failed at this adventure, who can hope to succeed? Here were all the men most worthy of that high honor, and all of them have tried to draw that sword, and all of them have failed. Who is left to come after them who can hope to succeed?"

The kings and dukes are discontented.

And the kings and dukes talked among themselves in the same way. By and by six of the worthiest of them came forward, namely King Lot, King Uriens, King Pellinore, King Ban, King Rience, and Duke Clarence of Northumberland. They stood before the Archbishop's throne and spoke to him like this: "Sir, all the kings and dukes of this realm have striven before you to draw out that sword, and look: not one of all who have attempted it has succeeded. What are we to understand, then, except that the enchanter Merlin has set up this adventure to bring shame and disgrace on all of us who are here, and on you, who are the head of the Church in this realm? For who in all the world can hope to draw a sword blade out of a bed of solid iron? Surely it is beyond the power of any man. Is it not plain, then, that Merlin has made a mockery of us all? So, rather than have this whole great assembly called here for nothing, we beg you in your wisdom to choose at once, from among the kings gathered here, the one best fitted to be High King of this realm. And when you have chosen him, we promise to obey him in everything he may command. Truly, such a choice will be far more worthwhile than wasting time on this foolish business of trying to draw a sword out of an anvil that no man in all the world can draw."

Then the Archbishop was much troubled in spirit, for he said to himself, "Can it be true that Merlin has deceived me, and has made a mockery of me and of all these kings and lords? Surely this cannot be. For Merlin is exceedingly wise, and he would not make a mockery of the whole realm for the sake of such a sorry joke as this would be. Surely he has some purpose in this that we know nothing of, since we are less wise than he is. So I will be patient a while longer." And so, having reasoned this out within himself, he spoke aloud to those seven great lords. "Sirs," he said, "I still have faith that Merlin has not deceived us, so I ask you to be patient just a little while longer. If, in the time it takes a man to count to five hundred twice over, no one comes forward to perform this task, then at your request I will choose one from among you and proclaim him King and High King of all." For the Archbishop had faith that Merlin was about to declare a king before them all at any moment.

Now let us leave these lords and turn to Arthur and his father and brother.

For Merlin had told those three to stay in their pavilion until he judged the time was right for them to come out. And now that time had come, Merlin and Sir Ulfius went to Sir Ector's pavilion, and Merlin said, "Arthur, get up and come out, for the hour has come for you to perform before the whole world the miracle you performed in private not long ago." So Arthur did as Merlin told him, and he came out of the pavilion with his father and his brother, and he was like someone walking in a dream.

Merlin brings Arthur to the test.

So the five of them went down from there toward the cathedral and the place of the test. And when they came to the crowd gathered there, the people made way for them, marveling greatly and saying to one another, "Who are these with Merlin the Enchanter and Sir Ulfius, and where have they come from?" For the whole world knew Merlin and Sir Ulfius, and the people could tell that something very extraordinary was about to happen. And Arthur was dressed all in flame-colored clothes embroidered with silver thread, so that others in the crowd said, "Surely that young man is very handsome to look at. Now who can he be?"

But Merlin said not a word to anyone. He brought Arthur through the crowd to the place where the Archbishop sat, and the crowd made way for him so that nothing held him back. And when the Archbishop saw Merlin coming with those others, he stood up and said, "Merlin, who are these you bring to us, and what is their business here?" And Merlin said, "My lord, here is one who has come to try that sword." And the Archbishop said, "Which one is he?" And Merlin said, "This is he," and he laid his hand on Arthur.

Merlin proclaims that Arthur is royal.

Then the Archbishop looked at Arthur and saw that the youth had a very handsome face, and his heart went out to Arthur and he loved him a great deal. And the Archbishop said, "Merlin, by what right does this young man come here?" And Merlin answered, "My lord, he comes here by the best right there is in the world. For the young man who stands before you dressed in red is the true son of Uther Pendragon and of his lawful wife, Queen Igraine."

Then the Archbishop cried out in great amazement, and those who stood near and heard what Merlin said were so astonished that they did not know what to think. And the Archbishop said, "Merlin, what is this you are telling me? For who in all the world has ever heard, until now, that Uther Pendragon had a son?"

To this Merlin answered: "No one has ever known of it until now, except a very few. For this is how it happened. When this child was born, the spirit of prophecy was upon me, and I foresaw that Uther Pendragon would die before very long. So I feared that the King's enemies would lay violent hands on the young child for the sake of his inheritance. And so, at the King's command, another man and I took the young child from his mother and gave him to a third, and that man took the royal child and has raised him ever since as his own son. And there are others here who can testify to the truth of these things. For the man who was with me when the young child was taken from his mother was Sir Ulfius, and the man to whom he was entrusted was Sir Ector of Bonmaison. These two witnesses, who are beyond all reproach, will swear to the truth of what I have said, for here they stand before you to confirm it."

And Sir Ulfius and Sir Ector said, "Everything Merlin has spoken is true, and to that we pledge our most faithful and sacred word of honor."

Then the Archbishop said, "Who could doubt the word of such honorable witnesses?" And he looked at Arthur and smiled at him.

Then Arthur said, "Do I have your permission, my lord, to take hold of that sword?" And the Archbishop said, "You have my permission, and may the grace of God go with you in your attempt."

Arthur draws out the sword.

At that Arthur went to the block of marble and laid his hands on the hilt of the sword that was thrust into the anvil. He bent his body and pulled very hard, and the sword came out with great ease and very smoothly. And when he had the sword in his hands, he swung it around his head so that it flashed like lightning. After he had swung it three times around his head, he set its point against the face of the anvil and pressed down on it very hard. And the sword slid very smoothly back into the place where it had stood before; and when it was there, halfway deep, it stood fast. And so Arthur accomplished that marvelous miracle of the sword in the eyes of all the world.

Now when the people gathered there saw this miracle performed before their very faces, they raised their voices all together and shouted so loudly, with such a huge uproar, that it seemed as though the whole earth rocked and trembled with the sound.

And while they were shouting, Arthur took hold of the sword again, drew it out, swung it again, and once more drove it back into the anvil. And when he had done that, he drew it out a third time and did the same thing as before. So everyone who was there saw that miracle performed three times over.

Several of the kings and dukes are angry.

And all the kings and dukes who were there were filled with great amazement. They did not know what to think or say when they saw someone who was little more than a boy perform the task at which the best of them had failed. Some of them, having seen that miracle, were willing to accept Arthur because of it, but others would not accept him. These drew back and stood apart, and as they stood there they said among themselves, "What is this? Who can believe such a thing, that a beardless boy should be set over us all and made King and High King of this great realm to rule us? No! No! We will have none of him for our King." And others said, "Isn't it plain that Merlin and Sir Ulfius are raising up this unknown boy so they can raise themselves up along with him?" So these discontented kings talked among themselves, and the most bitter of them all were King Lot and King Uriens, who were Arthur's brothers by marriage.

Now when the Archbishop saw how discontented these kings and dukes were, he said to them, "What now, sirs! Are you not satisfied?" And they said, "We are not satisfied." And the Archbishop said, "What would you have?" And they said, "We would have some other sort of king for Britain than a beardless boy whom nobody knows, and whose birthright only three men can vouch for." And the Archbishop said, "What of that? Has he not performed the miracle that you yourselves tried and failed to perform?"

But these high and mighty lords would not be satisfied. With angry faces turned away, they left that place, filled with rage and indignation.

Certain kings and dukes accept Arthur.

But others of the kings and dukes came and bowed to Arthur and paid their respects to him, wishing him joy of what he had achieved; and the chief of those who came to him in friendship was King Leodegrance of Cameliard. And the whole multitude acknowledged him and crowded around that place, shouting so that it sounded like thunder.

Now all this while Sir Ector and Sir Kay had stood to one side, and they were heavy with sorrow. For it seemed to them that Arthur had suddenly been raised so far above their station that they could never hope to come near him again. For now he was of royal rank, and they were only ordinary knights. But after a while Arthur saw them standing there with downcast faces, and he went straight to them and took first one and then the other by the hand and kissed each of them on the cheek. At that they were glad again, at being raised up to him like this.

And when Arthur left that place, great crowds of people followed him, so that the streets were completely packed with the press of people. And the multitude kept loudly hailing him as the chosen King of England, and those nearest to him tried to touch the hem of his clothes. So Arthur's heart was lifted up with great joy and gladness, until his soul took wing and flew like a bird into the sky.

So Arthur achieved the adventure of the sword that day and came into his royal birthright. And may God grant His grace to all of you, so that you too may succeed in your undertakings. For any man may be a king in whatever life he is placed in, if only he can draw out the sword of success from the iron of circumstance. So when your own time of testing comes, I do hope it may go with you as it went with Arthur that day, and that you too may win success, to your own entire satisfaction, and to your great glory and perfect happiness. Amen.

Conclusion

Arthur makes several other trials of the sword and succeeds every time.

Now after these things had happened there was a great deal of talk among men, and great confusion and uproar. For while some of the kings and nearly all the common people said, "Look! Here is a king come to us, as it were, straight out of Heaven to bring peace to our troubled land," other kings (and there were more of them) said, "Who is this beardless boy who comes claiming to be High King of Britain? Who ever heard of him before? We will have none of him without further trial and stronger proof." So, for the sake of peace, the Archbishop ordered that another test of the sword should be made at Candlemas, the feast at the start of February. And again, all those who tried to draw out the sword failed, but Arthur drew it out several times, very easily, in the sight of all. After that a third trial was made at Easter, and after that a fourth trial at Pentecost. And at every one of these trials Arthur drew the sword out of the anvil again and again, and no one but he could draw it out.

Arthur is crowned King of Britain.

And after that fourth trial, several of the kings and many of the lesser barons and knights and all of the common people cried out that these were trials enough, and that Arthur had certainly proved himself to be the rightful King. So they demanded that he should be made King in fact, so that he could rule over them. For by now it had come about that wherever Arthur went, great crowds followed him, hailing him as the true son of Uther Pendragon and the rightful High King of Britain. So the Archbishop, seeing how the people loved Arthur and how much they wanted him for their King, ordered that he should be anointed and crowned with royal rank; and so it was done in the great Cathedral. Some say that the Cathedral was St. Paul's, and some say that it was not.

Arthur overcomes his enemies.

But when Arthur had been crowned, all those who were against his kingship withdrew in great anger and at once began to prepare for war against him. But the people were on Arthur's side and joined with him, and so did several kings and many of the lesser barons and knights. And on Merlin's advice, Arthur made friends and allies of a number of other kings, and together they fought two great wars against his enemies and won both of them. In the second war a very famous battle was fought near the Forest of Bedegraine (and so it was called the Battle of Bedegraine), and in that battle Arthur defeated his enemies so completely that they could never again hope to join together in war against him.

Arthur brings his nephews to court.

And from King Lot, his brother-in-law, King Arthur brought two of his sons to court to live there and to serve as hostages for the peace from then on. These two were Gawain and Gaheris, and in time they became very famous and accomplished knights. And from King Uriens, his other brother-in-law, Arthur brought to court his one son, Uwain, to hold as a hostage for the peace; and he too became in time a very famous and accomplished knight. And because of these hostages there was peace between those three royal brothers-in-law for all time afterward. And a certain very famous king and knight named King Pellinore (who was one of his enemies) Arthur drove out of his lands, away from the homes of men and into the forest. And King Rience (who was another of his enemies) he drove into the mountains of North Wales. And other kings who were his enemies he brought under his rule, so that the whole land was more at peace than it had been since the days of Uther Pendragon.

And King Arthur made Sir Kay his Seneschal, as he had promised to do. And he made Sir Ulfius his Chamberlain, and Merlin his Counsellor, and Sir Baudwin of Britain his Constable. And these men were all of such a kind that they greatly added to the glory and fame of his reign and set him securely on his throne.

Now when King Arthur's reign had become firmly established in this way, and the fame of his greatness began to spread through the world, many men of noble soul and generous spirit and high knightly skill (knights who wanted above all things to win glory at arms in the courts of chivalry) saw that great honor and advancement were likely to be won under such a king. And so it came about that from all parts, little by little, there began to gather around King Arthur a court of noble, honorable knights such as men had never seen before that time, and perhaps will never see again.

For even to this day the story of these good knights is known to most of mankind. Yes, the names of many kings and emperors have passed away and been forgotten, but the names of Sir Galahad, and of Sir Lancelot of the Lake, and of Sir Tristram of Lyonesse, and of Sir Percival of Wales, and of Sir Gawain, and of Sir Uwain, and of Sir Bors de Ganis, and of many others of that noble household and worthy brotherhood, are still remembered by men. And so I think it very likely that as long as words are written down, the deeds of these heroes will be remembered.

So in the story that is still to come, I have made it my task to tell whoever reads this book about many of these adventures, and to tell him besides certain things that I do not believe everybody knows. And by and by, when I tell of the founding of the Round Table, I will set down a list of a number of the heroes who gathered at Arthur's court at this time, as the men chosen to found that order of the Round Table, and who for that reason were given the title "The Ancient and Honorable Companions of the Round Table."

For though this whole story is chiefly about King Arthur, the glory of these great and honorable knights was his glory, and his glory was theirs. And so one cannot tell of the glory of King Arthur without also telling of the glory of those noble gentlemen.

The Book of King Arthur, Part Two: The Winning of a Sword

Foreword

Here begins the story of certain adventures of Arthur after he had become King. In it you will hear how, with great knightly courage and skill at arms, he fought a very fierce and bloody battle with a certain Sable Knight, a knight all in black. You will also hear how, because of that battle, he won a certain Sword so famous and glorious that its fame will last as long as our language is spoken. For nothing like that sword was ever seen in all the world before that time, and nothing like it has ever been seen since; and its name was Excalibur.

So, if you care to read this story, I believe it will give you excellent entertainment, and it will without doubt lift your spirit high, because of the remarkable courage those two famous and worthy knights showed when they fought that famous battle together. You will also find great delight in reading here about the wonderful marvels of a certain country of Fairyland into which King Arthur wandered. There he found a Lake of Enchantment, and there he talked with a gentle and beautiful lady of that land, who showed him how to win the famous sword I have just told you about.

For it has given me such pleasure to write these things that at times my heart has swelled as if with pure joy, and so I have great hopes that you may find as much pleasure in them as I have already found. So I ask you to listen to what follows.

Chapter 1: How a Wounded Knight Came to King Arthur's Court, How a Young Knight Tried to Avenge Him and Failed, and How the King Took Up the Challenge Himself

How King Arthur stayed in the Forest of Usk.

Now it happened one pleasant time in the springtime season that King Arthur and his Court were making a royal journey through the part of Britain that lies close to the Forests of the Usk. The weather at that time was extremely warm, and so the King and his Court stopped in the forest, under the trees, in the cool and pleasant shade the place offered. And there the King rested for a while on a couch of rushes spread with scarlet cloth.

The knights who were with the Court at that time were Sir Gawain, and Sir Uwain, and Sir Kay, and Sir Pelleas, and Sir Bedevere, and Sir Caradoc, and Sir Geraint, and Sir Baudwin of Britain, and Sir Constantine of Cornwall, and Sir Brandiles, and Sir Mador de la Porte; and nowhere in the world could you have found a company of knights as noble and as high as these.

Now as the King lay dozing, and as these worthy knights sat talking cheerfully together, there came suddenly a great bustle and stir at the edge of the Court, and soon a very sad and sorrowful sight appeared. For a knight came toward them, badly wounded, held up on his horse by a golden-haired page dressed in white and azure. The knight's clothing and his horse's trappings were also of white and azure, and on his shield he bore the device of a single silver lily on a field of pure azure.

How the wounded knight comes into the forest.

But the knight was in a very pitiful state. His face was as pale as wax and hung down on his chest. His eyes were glazed and saw nothing of what went on around him, and his fine clothing of white and blue was all red with the lifeblood that ran from a great wound in his side. And as they came along, the young page wept and lamented so that it wrung your heart to hear him.

Now as they came near, King Arthur roused himself and cried out, "Alas! What sorrowful sight is this I see? Now hurry, my lords, and bring help to that knight; and you, Sir Kay, go quickly and bring that fair young page here, so that we may hear at once from his own lips what misfortune has happened to his lord."

So several of the knights hurried at the King's command and gave the wounded knight every help they could, and they carried him to King Arthur's own pavilion, which had been pitched a little way off. And when he had been brought there, the King's surgeon attended him at once, although his wounds were of such a kind that he could not hope to live very long.

Meanwhile Sir Kay brought that fair young page before the King where he sat, and the King thought he had hardly ever seen a more beautiful face. And the King said, "Please tell me, Sir Page, who your master is, and how he came to be in such a sad and pitiful state as the one we have just seen."

The page tells the story of the wounded knight.

"That I will, Lord," said the youth. "You must know that my master is called Sir Myles of the White Fountain, and that he comes from the country to the north of here, a good distance away. In that country he is the lord of seven castles and several noble estates, so, as you can see, he is a man of considerable importance. A fortnight ago (moved to it, no doubt, by the high spirits of the springtime), he set out with only me as his squire, for he had a mind to seek adventure as a good knight-errant should. And we had several adventures, and in every one of them my lord was completely successful; for he overcame six knights in different places and sent them all to his castle to bear witness to his valor before his lady.

"At last, this morning, at a certain place a good distance from here, we came upon a fair castle in the forest. It stood in a valley surrounded by open stretches of level lawn, scattered with many flowers of every kind. There we saw three fair damsels tossing a golden ball from one to another. The damsels were dressed all in flame-colored satin, and their hair was the color of gold. And as we came near them they stopped their game, and the one who was the chief of those damsels called out to my lord, asking him where he was going and what his errand was.

"My lord answered her that he was a knight-errant in search of adventure. At this the three damsels laughed, and the one who had spoken first said, 'If you are looking for adventure, Sir Knight, perhaps I can help you to one that will satisfy you to your heart's content.'

"To this my master replied, 'Please, fair damsel, tell me what that adventure may be, so that I can try it at once.'

"Then this lady told my master to take a certain path and to follow it for a league or a little more, about three miles. He would then come, she said, to a stone bridge that crossed a violent stream, and she promised him that there he would find adventure enough to satisfy any man.

"So my master and I went that way, as the damsel had directed us, and after a while we came to the bridge she had spoken of. And there, beyond the bridge, stood a lonely castle with a tall, straight tower, and in front of the castle was a wide and level lawn of well-trimmed grass. Just beyond the bridge stood an apple tree hung all over with a great many shields. And halfway across the bridge hung a single shield, entirely black, and beside it hung a hammer of brass; and beneath the shield these words were written in red letters:

Whoever strikes this shield does so at his peril.

"Now my master, Sir Myles, when he read those words, went straight to that shield, seized the hammer that hung beside it, and struck it such a blow that it rang like thunder.

"At that, as if in answer, the portcullis of the castle was raised, and a knight came riding out at once, armored all in black from head to foot. His clothing and the trappings of his horse, and all its gear besides, were black as well.

The page tells of the Sable Knight.

"Now when that Sable Knight saw my master, he came riding swiftly across the meadow to the far end of the bridge. When he got there he drew rein, saluted my master, and called out, 'Sir Knight, I demand to know why you struck that shield. Now let me tell you this: because of your boldness, I will take your own shield from you and hang it on that apple tree, where you can see all those other shields hanging.' To this my master replied, 'That you will not do unless you can overcome me, knight against knight.' And with that he at once made his shield ready and put himself in position for a charge.

"So my master and this Sable Knight, having made themselves ready for the encounter, soon rode together with all their might and main. They met in the middle of the course, and there my master's spear burst into splinters. But the Sable Knight's spear held, and it pierced through Sir Myles's shield and into his side, so that both he and his horse were thrown violently into the dust. He was so badly wounded that he could not get up again from the ground where he lay.

"Then the Sable Knight took my master's shield and hung it in the branches of the apple tree where the other shields were hanging. And then, without paying any more attention to my master or asking how badly he was hurt, he rode back into his castle, and the portcullis was closed behind him at once.

"So, after he had gone, I got my master onto his horse with great labor and at once took him away from there, not knowing where I could find shelter for him, until I came to this place. And that, my lord King, is the true story of how my master came by the deadly wound he has suffered."

"Ha! By the glory of Paradise!" cried King Arthur. "I count it a great shame that in my kingdom, and so near my Court, strangers should be treated as discourteously as Sir Myles has been treated.
