For it is certainly discourteous to leave a fallen knight on the ground without stopping to ask how badly he is hurt. And it is still more discourteous to take away the shield of a fallen knight who has fought a good fight."

And all the knights of the King's Court cried out in the same way against the discourtesy of that Sable Knight.

Griflet asks a favor.

Then a certain squire who served the King in person, named Griflet, who was much loved by his royal master, came forward and knelt before the King, and cried out in a loud voice, "I ask a favor of you, my lord King! And I beg you to grant it to me!"

Then King Arthur raised his eyes to the youth as he knelt in front of him, and said, "Ask, Griflet, and your favor will be granted."

At that Griflet said, "This is what I ask: I ask that you make me a knight at once, and that you let me go out and try to punish this unkind knight by overthrowing him, and so win back those shields he has hung on that apple tree."

Then King Arthur was very troubled in his spirit, for Griflet was still only a squire and altogether untried in arms. So he said, "Listen, you are still too young to fight so powerful a knight as this black champion must be, who has overthrown so many knights without coming to any harm himself. Please, dear Griflet, think again and ask some other favor."

But young Griflet only cried out all the more, "A favor! A favor! And you have granted it to me!"

At this King Arthur said, "You shall have your favor, though my heart warns me that you will suffer great harm and misfortune from this adventure."

King Arthur makes Griflet a knight.

So that night Griflet kept watch over his armor in a chapel in the forest, and in the morning, after receiving the Sacrament, he was made a knight by King Arthur's own hand, and no knight could have had a greater honor than that. Then King Arthur fastened the golden spurs to Sir Griflet's heels with his own hand.

So Griflet was made a knight. And mounting his charger, he rode straight off on his adventure, full of happiness and singing for pure pleasure.

And it was at this time that Sir Myles died of his wound. For it is often so in this world: death and misfortune come to some, while others laugh and sing with hope and joy, as though such grievous things as sorrow and death could never happen in the world they live in.

Now that afternoon King Arthur sat waiting very anxiously for news of that young knight. But no news came until toward evening, when some of his attendants came hurrying to him, announcing that Sir Griflet was coming back, but without his shield, and in such a state that it looked as though some great misfortune had happened to him. And right after them came Sir Griflet himself, held up on his horse on one side by Sir Constantine and on the other by Sir Brandiles. Sir Griflet's head hung down on his chest, and his fine new armor was all broken and stained with blood and dust. He looked so pitiful that King Arthur's heart tightened with sorrow to see that young knight in so sad a state.

So, at King Arthur's command, they brought Sir Griflet to the Royal Pavilion and laid him down on a soft couch. Then the King's surgeon examined his wounds and found that the head of a spear, and part of its shaft, were still stuck through Sir Griflet's side, so that he was in terrible and grievous pain.

And when King Arthur saw how dangerously hurt Sir Griflet lay, he cried out, "Alas, my dear young knight! What has happened to you, to bring you to such a sorry state as this I see?"

Sir Griflet tells how he was hurt.

Then Sir Griflet, speaking in a very weak voice, told King Arthur how he had fared. He said that he had ridden on through the forest until he found the three beautiful damsels the page of Sir Myles had spoken of. And he said that these damsels had told him how he should go about his adventure. He said that he had found the bridge where the shield and the brass hammer hung, and that he had seen the apple tree hung full of shields there. And he said that he had struck the Sable Knight's shield with the brass hammer, and that the Sable Knight had then come riding out against him. But this knight, he said, did not seem to want to fight him. Instead, he called out to him, very nobly, that he was too young and too untried in arms to fight a seasoned knight, and so he advised Sir Griflet to give up that adventure before it was too late. But in spite of this advice Sir Griflet would not give it up, and declared that he would certainly fight that knight in black. Now at the very first charge Sir Griflet's spear had burst into pieces, but the Sable Knight's spear had held, and it had pierced through Sir Griflet's shield and into his side, giving him the grievous wound he now suffered. And Sir Griflet said that the Sable Knight had then very courteously lifted him back onto his horse (though he had kept Sir Griflet's shield and hung it on the tree with the others hanging there), and had then sent him on his way; and so he had managed to ride back, though in great pain and misery.

King Arthur is very angry.

Then King Arthur was wild with anger and greatly troubled in his mind, for he truly loved Sir Griflet very dearly. So he declared that now he himself would go out to punish that Sable Knight and humble him with his own hand. And though the knights of his Court tried hard to talk him out of that adventure, he still declared that he would bring that proud knight low with his own hand, and that he would undertake the adventure, with God's grace, the very next day.

He was so troubled that he could hardly eat his food that evening for anger, and he would not go to his couch to sleep. Instead, after asking Sir Griflet very carefully where he could find that valley of flowers and those three damsels, he spent the night walking up and down his pavilion, waiting for the day to dawn.

Now as soon as the birds first began to chirp and the east began to brighten with the coming daylight, King Arthur called his two squires. With their help he put on his armor and mounted a milk-white war horse, and soon he set out on the adventure he had made up his mind to undertake.

King Arthur sets out on his adventure.

And truly it is a very pleasant thing to ride out at the dawning of a springtime day. For then the little birds sing their sweetest songs, all joining in one joyful medley, so that you can hardly tell one note from another, so many are the voices in that pretty round of song. Then the growing things of the earth smell sweetest in the freshness of the early day: the fair flowers, the shrubs, and the blossoms on the trees. Then the dew sparkles all over the grass like an unbelievable multitude of jewels of every color. Then the whole world is sweet and clean and new, as though it had just been freshly made for the one who comes out to roam so early in the morning.

So King Arthur's heart swelled with great joy, and he sang an old-fashioned song as he rode through the forest in search of that knightly adventure.

King Arthur comes to the Valley of Delight.

So, at about noon, he came to that part of the forest he had heard about several times before. For suddenly he saw before him a wide and gently sloping valley, with a stream as bright as silver running down it. And the whole valley was strewn with countless fair and fragrant flowers of every kind. In the middle of the valley stood a handsome castle with tall red roofs and many bright windows, so that it seemed to King Arthur a very fine castle indeed. And on a smooth green lawn he saw the three damsels dressed in flame-colored satin that the page of Sir Myles and Sir Griflet had spoken of. They were playing ball with a golden ball, and the hair of each of them was the color of gold. And it seemed to King Arthur, as he came nearer, that they were the most beautiful damsels he had ever seen in all his life.

Now as King Arthur came up to them, the three stopped tossing the ball, and the fairest of all the damsels asked him where he was going and what errand he was bound on.

Then King Arthur answered, "Ha, fair lady! Where should a belted knight ride on such a day as this, and on what business, but in search of adventure, as is right for a knight-errant of proper strength of heart and body?"

The damsels greet King Arthur.

Then the three damsels smiled at the King, for he was very handsome and they liked him very well. "Alas, Sir Knight!" said the one who had spoken before. "Please don't be in such a hurry to take on a dangerous adventure. Stay with us instead for a day, or two, or three, to feast and make merry with us. For good cheer surely does a great deal to gladden the heart, and we would love to enjoy the company of so gallant a knight as you seem to be. That castle is ours, and this whole bright valley is ours, and those who have visited it are pleased, because of its joyfulness, to call it the Valley of Delight. So stay with us a little while, and don't be in such a hurry to go on."

"No," said King Arthur, "I cannot stay with you, fair ladies, for I am bent on an adventure you will know very well when I tell you that I am looking for that Sable Knight, who has overcome so many other knights and taken away their shields. So I ask you, of your kindness, to tell me where I can find him."

"Heaven's grace!" cried the one who spoke for the others. "This is surely a sorry adventure you are seeking, Sir Knight! For already, in these last two days, two knights have tried their strength against that knight, and both have come to great pain and disgrace. Even so, if you will undertake this danger, you shall not go until you have eaten and refreshed yourself." And as she said this, she lifted a little ivory whistle that hung from her neck on a golden chain and blew it very shrilly.

In answer to this summons three fair young pages came out of the castle, dressed all in flame-colored clothes, carrying between them a silver table covered with a white cloth. And after them came five more pages dressed the same way, bringing flagons of white wine and red, dried fruits and sweetmeats, and small loaves of fine white bread.

King Arthur eats and drinks in the Valley of Delight.

Then King Arthur got down from his war horse very gladly, for he was both hungry and thirsty. He sat down at the table with the damsels beside him and ate with great enjoyment, talking pleasantly all the while with those fair ladies, who listened to him with great cheerfulness of spirit. Yet he did not tell them who he was, though they wondered greatly who this noble warrior might be who had come into that place.

So, when he had satisfied his hunger and thirst, King Arthur mounted his horse again, and the three damsels went with him a little way across the valley, he riding on his horse and they walking beside him. After a while he saw a dark pathway leading into the forest on the far side; and when he had come to it, the lady who had spoken to him before said, "That is the way you must take if you mean to go on with this adventure. So farewell, and may good fortune go with you; for truly, you are the most pleasant-spoken knight who has come this way in a long time."

Then King Arthur, after saluting those ladies very courteously, rode away, full of joy over the pleasant adventure he had just passed through.

Now when King Arthur had gone some distance, he came after a while to a certain place where charcoal burners worked at their trade. For here there were many mounds of earth, all smoking from the smoldering logs inside them, while the whole air was filled with the smell of the damped-down fires.

As the King came near this spot, he soon saw that something was going on that was badly wrong. For in the open clearing he saw three sooty fellows with long knives in their hands chasing one old man whose beard was as white as snow. And he saw that this venerable old man, who was richly dressed in black, and whose horse stood a little way off, was running this way and that as though to escape those wicked men, and that he seemed very hard pressed and in great danger of his life.

"By heaven!" said the young King to himself. "Here, surely, is someone in bad need of help." And with that he cried out in a great voice, "Stop, villains! What are you doing?" And at once he set spurs to his horse, lowered his spear into position, and drove down on them with a noise as loud as thunder.

But when the three wicked fellows saw the armed knight thundering down on them like this, they dropped their knives at once and, with loud cries of fear, ran off this way and that until they had escaped into the thickets of the forest, where a man on horseback could not hope to follow them.

King Arthur rescues Merlin from the three villains.

Then, having driven those wicked fellows away, King Arthur rode up to the man he had rescued, meaning to offer him comfort. And as he came near, he saw to his surprise that the old man was the Enchanter Merlin. Yet how Merlin had come there so suddenly, when only a little while before he had been at the King's Court at Caerleon, and what he was doing in that place, the King could not understand at all. So he spoke to the Enchanter like this: "Ha, Merlin! It seems to me that I have saved your life. For surely you would not have escaped from the hands of those wicked men if I had not happened to come this way just now."

"Do you think so, Lord?" said Merlin. "Now let me tell you that although I may have seemed to be in danger, I could have saved myself very easily if I had wanted to. But just as you saw me in this pretended danger, so you may know that a real danger, far greater than this one, lies before you, and there will be no knight-errant to rescue you from it. So I beg you, Lord, to take me with you on this adventure you have set out on, for I tell you that you will certainly suffer great sorrow and pain in it."

"Merlin," said King Arthur, "even if I were facing my death, I would not turn back from this adventure. But as for the advice you give me, it seems to me it would be very wise to take you with me, if such danger lies ahead of me as you say."

And Merlin said, "Yes, it would be very wise for you to do so."

So Merlin mounted his palfrey, and he and King Arthur rode off together from that place in pursuit of the adventure the King had undertaken.

Chapter 2: How King Arthur Fought the Sable Knight and Was Badly Wounded, and How Merlin Carried Him Safely Away

So King Arthur and Merlin rode on together through the forest for a good long while, until they could tell that they must be drawing near to the place where the Sable Knight lived, the knight the King was seeking so eagerly. For the forest, which until then had been a wilderness, very deep and mossy, began to grow thinner and more open, as though some dwelling place of men were close at hand.

And after a little while they saw before them a rushing, violent stream of water that tore through a dark and gloomy glen. They saw, too, that a bridge of stone crossed this stream, and that on the far side of the bridge there lay a smooth and level lawn of green grass, where knights could joust very well. And beyond this lawn they saw a tall and forbidding castle, with smooth walls and a straight tower; and the castle was built upon the rocks in such a way that it seemed to be all one piece with the stone. So they knew that this must be the castle the page and Sir Griflet had told of.

King Arthur comes to the castle of the Sable Knight.

For halfway across the bridge they saw a black shield hanging, and a brass mallet beside it, exactly as the page and Sir Griflet had said. And on the far side of the stream there stood an apple tree, and among its leaves hung a great many shields bearing all sorts of different designs, exactly as those two had reported. Some of those shields were clean and bright, and some were dirty and stained with blood; some were smooth and whole, and some were split, as though by fighting between knight and knight. And every one of those shields had belonged to some knight whom the Sable Knight, who lived in the castle, had overthrown in combat with his own hand.

"By the splendor of Paradise!" said King Arthur. "That must be a truly valiant knight, who by his own strength alone has overthrown and cast down so many other knights. Why, Merlin, there must be a hundred shields hanging in that tree!"

To this Merlin answered, "And you, Lord, may count yourself very lucky if your own shield is not hanging there too before the sun goes down this evening."

"That," said King Arthur, with a very steady face, "will be as God wills. For truly, I want more than ever to try my strength against that knight. Think of it: what special honor would come to me if I were to overcome so valiant a warrior as this Sable Champion seems to be, since he has been victorious over so many other good knights!"

Then, having spoken his mind, King Arthur at once pushed his horse forward and rode onto the bridge, and there he plainly read the challenge written in red letters beneath the shield:

Whoever strikes this shield does so at his peril.

King Arthur challenges the Sable Knight.

When he had read these words, the King seized the brass mallet and struck the shield such a violent blow that the sound of it echoed back from the smooth walls of the castle, and from the rocks it stood on, and from the edges of the forest all around, as though a dozen other shields had been struck in all those different places.

And in answer to that sound, the portcullis of the castle (the great iron grating that guarded its gate) was at once let down, and out rode a knight, very huge of frame and dressed all in black armor. All his clothing, too, and all the trappings of his horse were entirely black, so that he looked very grim and forbidding. And this Sable Knight came across that level meadow of smooth grass at a very stately and dignified pace, neither in haste nor slowly, but with great pride and a haughty bearing, as befitted a champion who, perhaps, had never yet been beaten in battle. So, reaching the head of the bridge, he reined in his horse and saluted King Arthur with great dignity, and very haughtily as well. "Ha, Sir Knight!" he said. "Why, after reading the words written there, did you strike my shield? Now I tell you that for your discourtesy I will soon take your shield away from you, and hang it up on that apple tree, where you see all those other shields hanging. So either hand your shield over to me without any more fuss, or else get ready to defend it with your body, in which case you will certainly suffer great pain and harm."

"Thank you for the choice you give me," said King Arthur. "But as for taking my shield away, I believe that will be as Heaven wills, and not as you will. Know this, you unkind knight: I have come here for no other purpose than to do battle with you, and so to try to win back, with my own body, all those shields that hang there on that apple tree. So make ready at once, so that I can fight with you, perhaps to your great disadvantage."

"That I will," replied the Sable Knight. And with that he turned his horse's head and rode back a certain distance across the level lawn, and took his place wherever seemed best to him. And King Arthur rode out onto the lawn as well, and took his place wherever seemed best to him.

Then each knight set his spear and his shield for the charge, and, being ready for the attack, each shouted to his war horse and drove his spurs deep into its sides.

King Arthur contends with the Sable Knight.

Then those two noble horses rushed forward like lightning, racing across the ground with such tremendous speed that the earth trembled and shook beneath them as if in an earthquake. And the two knights met squarely in the very middle of the field, crashing together like a thunderbolt. So violently did they strike against each other that both spears burst into splinters, right down to the hand guard and the stump of the shaft, and the horses staggered back from the shock. Only the extraordinary skill of their riders kept them from falling in that collision.

But with great spirit each of those two knights lifted up his horse with his own courage, and so finished his course in safety.

And indeed King Arthur was greatly amazed that he had not overthrown his opponent, for at that time, as I have said, he was held to be the very best knight in all of Britain, the one most proven in deeds of arms. So he marveled at the strength and skill of the knight he had charged against, that he had not been overthrown by the force of the blow that had struck his defenses. So when they met again in the middle of the field, King Arthur greeted that knight and spoke to him with great courtesy, saying: "Sir Knight, I do not know who you are, but I give you my knightly word that you are the mightiest knight I have ever met in all my life. Now I ask you to get down from your horse at once, and let the two of us fight this battle on foot and with swords, for it would be a pity to let it end this way."

"Not so," said the Sable Knight. "Not so. Until one of us has been overthrown, I will not fight this battle on foot." And with that he shouted "Ho! Ho!" in a very loud voice, and at once the gateway of the castle opened, and out came running two tall squires dressed all in black, trimmed with crimson. And each of these squires carried in his hand a great spear of ash wood, new and well seasoned, and never yet strained in battle.

So King Arthur chose one of these spears and the Sable Knight took the other, and then each went back to the place from which he had made his first charge.

Then once again each knight rushed his horse to the attack, and once again each struck the other so squarely in the middle of his shield that the spears were splintered, until only the guard and the stump of the shaft were left in each knight's grip.

The knights break lances a second time.

Then, as before, King Arthur would have fought the battle out on foot and with swords, but again the Sable Knight would not have it so. Instead he called aloud to those inside the castle, and at once two more squires came out with fresh new spears of ash wood. So each knight took a spear once more, and, armed with it, each chose his place on that fair, level lawn of grass.

King Arthur is overthrown.

And now, for the third time, having made themselves ready, those two excellent knights hurled themselves together in a furious charge. And now, as twice before, King Arthur struck the Sable Knight so squarely in the center of his shield that the spear he held burst into splinters. But this time the Sable Knight's spear did not break that way, but held; and so violent was the blow he struck on King Arthur's shield that it pierced right through the center of it. Then, under that great and powerful blow, the girths of the King's saddle burst apart, and both he and his horse were thrown violently backward. And King Arthur would have been hurled to the ground if he had not leaped from his saddle with extraordinary skill and knightly quickness. So, though his horse was overthrown, he himself kept his footing and did not fall into the dust. Even so, the blow he had received was so violent that for a little while he was completely stunned, and everything whirled around before his eyes.

But when his sight came back to him, he was filled with an anger so fierce that it seemed to him as though all the blood in his heart had rushed into his brain, so that he saw nothing before his eyes but red, the color of blood. And when this too had passed, he saw the Sable Knight sitting on his horse not far away. At once King Arthur ran to him, caught the bridle rein of his horse, and cried out to the Sable Knight with great fury, "Come down, you black knight, and fight me on foot and with your sword!"

"That I will not do," said the Sable Knight, "for look, I have overthrown you. So hand over your shield to me, so that I may hang it on that apple tree, and go on your way as others have done before you."

"That I will not!" cried King Arthur in a great passion. "I will neither yield nor go away from here until one of us has completely conquered the other." And with that he shoved the Sable Knight's horse backward by the bridle rein so hard that the knight was forced to leap from his saddle to save himself from being thrown to the ground.

The knights fight on foot with swords.

And now each knight was as furious as the other. So each drew his sword and set his shield, and then they rushed together like two wild bulls in battle. They thrust, they struck, they circled, they parried, they struck again and again, and the sound of their blows, crashing and clashing one upon the other, filled the whole space around them with a tremendous uproar. Nobody could ever fully imagine the fury of that fight. For the blows that each gave the other were so violent that whole pieces of armor were hacked from their bodies, and many deep and grievous wounds were given and received, so that the armor of each of them was all stained red with the blood that flowed down over it.

At last King Arthur, growing almost wild with fury, struck so fierce a blow that no armor could have withstood it if it had landed squarely. But with that stroke it happened that his sword broke off at the hilt, and the blade flew up into the air in three pieces. Yet the stroke was so wonderfully fierce that the Sable Knight groaned and staggered and ran around in a circle, as though he had gone blind and did not know where to turn his steps.

But soon he recovered himself, and seeing King Arthur standing close by, and not knowing that his enemy now had no sword to defend himself with, he threw aside his shield, took his own sword in both hands, and struck with it so terrible a stroke that he split through King Arthur's shield and through his helmet and right down to the bone of his skull.

King Arthur is badly wounded.

Then King Arthur thought that he had received his death wound, for his brain swam like water, his thighs shook terribly, and he sank down to his knees, while the blood and sweat, mixed together in the darkness of his helmet, ran down into his eyes in a lather and blinded him. And seeing him so grievously hurt, the Sable Knight called on him very fiercely to yield and to surrender his shield, since he was now too badly wounded to fight any more.

But King Arthur would not yield. Instead, catching the other by his sword belt, he pulled himself up to his feet. Then, having somewhat recovered from his daze, he seized the other knight in both arms, and, putting his knee behind the Sable Knight's thigh, he threw him backward onto the ground so violently that the sound of his fall was astounding to hear. And with that fall the Sable Knight was knocked senseless for a while. Then King Arthur at once unlaced the Sable Knight's helmet and saw his face, and he knew him in spite of the blood that was still running down his own face in great streams. He knew that this knight was King Pellinore, who has been named before in this history, and who had twice made war against King Arthur. (It has already been told how King Arthur had driven that other king away from the homes of men and into the forests, so that now he lived in this poor, gloomy castle, from which he made war on all the knights who came to that place.)

Now when King Arthur saw who it was he had been fighting, he cried out aloud, "Ha! Pellinore, is it you, then? Now yield to me, for you are entirely at my mercy." And with that he drew his misericord (the thin dagger a knight carried to give the finishing stroke) and set its point at King Pellinore's throat.

But by now King Pellinore had largely recovered from his fall, and, seeing that blood was pouring down out of his enemy's helmet, he knew that the other must have been very badly wounded by the blow he had just received. So he caught King Arthur's wrist in his hand and turned the point of the dagger away from his own throat, so that it no longer threatened him with any great danger.

And indeed, what with his terrible wound and the loss of blood, King Arthur had now grown so sick and faint that it seemed to him he was near death. And so, without very much trouble, King Pellinore suddenly heaved himself up from the ground and overthrew his enemy, so that now King Arthur lay beneath his knees.

King Pellinore moves to kill King Arthur.

And by this time King Pellinore was wild with the fury of the hard battle he had fought. He was so enraged that his eyes were all bloodshot, like those of a wild boar, and a froth, like the foam on a champing boar's jaws, stood in the beard around his lips. So he wrenched the dagger out of his enemy's hand, and at once began to unlace his helmet, meaning to kill him where he lay. But at that moment Merlin came hurrying up, crying out, "Stop! Stop, Sir Pellinore! What are you about to do? Hold back your wicked hand! For the man who lies beneath you is none other than Arthur, King of all this realm!"

At this King Pellinore was astonished beyond measure. For a little while he was silent, and then he cried out in a very loud voice, "Is that so, old man? Then truly your words have doomed this man to death. For no one in all this world has ever suffered such harm and such wrongs as I have suffered at his hands. He has taken from me my power, and my kingship, and my honors, and my lands, and has left me only this gloomy, dismal castle in the forest to live in. So, since he is now in my power, he will die right here, if for no other reason than this: if I let him go free now, he will certainly take his revenge once he has recovered from all the harm he has suffered at my hands."

Merlin lays a spell upon King Pellinore.

Then Merlin said, "Not so! He will not die at your hands, for I myself will save him." And with that he raised his staff and struck King Pellinore across the shoulders. At once King Pellinore fell down and lay on the ground on his face, like a man who had suddenly died.

At this, King Arthur raised himself on his elbow and saw his enemy lying there as though dead, and he cried out, "Ha, Merlin! What is this you have done? I am very sorry for it, for I can see that by your magic arts you have killed one of the best knights in all the world."

"Not so, my lord King!" said Merlin. "For truly, I tell you that you are far nearer to your death than he is. He is only asleep and will soon wake up; but you are in such a state that it would take only a very little to make you die."

And indeed King Arthur was terribly sick, sick to the very heart, from the grievous wound he had received, so that it was only with great difficulty that Merlin could help him up onto his horse. When he had done this, and had hung the King's shield on the horn of his saddle, Merlin at once led the wounded man away across the bridge, and, leading the horse by the bridle, took him off into the forest.

Now I must tell you that in that part of the forest there lived a certain hermit who was so holy that the wild birds of the woods would come and rest on his hand while he read his prayer book; and he was so saintly in his gentleness that the wild does would come right up to the door of his hermitage and stand there while he milked them for his own refreshment. And this hermit lived so far away from the homes of men that when he rang the bell for morning prayers or for evening prayers, there was hardly ever anyone to hear the sound of it except the wild creatures that lived nearby. Yet even so, royal people and others of high rank would sometimes come to this remote and lonely place, as though on a pilgrimage, because of the hermit's great holiness.

Merlin brings King Arthur to the cell of a lonely hermit.

So Merlin brought King Arthur to this holy refuge, and when they had reached it, he and the hermit lifted the wounded man down from his saddle, the hermit speaking many words of pity and sorrow, and together they carried him into the holy man's cell. There they laid him on a bed of moss, unlaced his armor, examined his wounds, bathed them with pure water, and dressed his hurts, for that hermit was a very skillful healer. So for all that day and part of the next, King Arthur lay on the hermit's bed like a man about to die. He saw everything around him as though through a thin layer of water, and his breath hung on his lips and fluttered there, and he could not even lift his head from the bed because of the weakness that lay upon him.

The Lady Guinevere consults with the priest.

Now on the afternoon of the second day there arose a great noise and commotion in that part of the forest. For it happened that the Lady Guinevere of Cameliard had come on a pilgrimage to that holy man, together with her whole court of ladies and knights, because the fame of his saintliness had reached even the place where she lived. That lady had a favorite page who was very sick with a fever, and she hoped that the holy man might give her some charm or amulet by whose power the boy might perhaps be cured. So she had come to that place with her entire court, until all that part of the forest was made bright with fine clothes, and its silence was made merry with the sound of talk and laughter, the singing of songs, the chatter of many voices, and the neighing of horses. And the Lady Guinevere rode in the midst of her damsels and her court, and her beauty outshone the beauty of her damsels as the splendor of the morning star outshines all the lesser stars around it. For then and ever afterward she was held by all the courts of chivalry to be the most beautiful lady in the world.

Now when the Lady Guinevere came to that place, she saw King Arthur's milk-white war horse standing and cropping the green grass of the open glade near the hermitage. And she saw Merlin, too, standing beside the door of the cell. So she asked him whose noble war horse that was, grazing on the grass in that lonely place, and who it was that lay inside the cell. And Merlin answered her, "Lady, the one who lies inside is a knight, very badly wounded, so that he is sick almost to death!"

"Heaven have pity!" cried the Lady Guinevere. "What a sad thing this is that you tell me! Now I beg you to take me to that knight at once, so that I may see him. For I have in my court a very skillful physician, who is well practiced at curing the kinds of wounds that knights receive in battle."

The Lady Guinevere bids her physician heal King Arthur.

So Merlin brought the lady into the cell, and there she saw King Arthur where he lay stretched out on the bed. And she did not know who he was. Yet it seemed to her that never in all her life had she seen so noble-looking a knight as the one who lay so badly wounded in that lonely place. And King Arthur lifted his eyes to where she stood beside his bed of pain, surrounded by her maidens, and in the great weakness that lay upon him he did not know whether the one he saw was a mortal lady, or whether she was not rather some tall, straight angel who had come down from one of the lordly courts of Paradise to visit him in his pain and distress. And the Lady Guinevere was filled with great pity at seeing King Arthur in so sorrowful a state. So she called to her the skillful physician who traveled with her court, and she told him to bring a certain alabaster box of very precious balm. And she commanded him to examine that knight's wounds and to anoint them with the balm, so that he might be healed of his hurts as quickly as possible.

So that wise and skillful physician did as the Lady Guinevere commanded, and at once King Arthur felt all his aches ease away, and a great contentment of spirit came over him. And when the lady and her court had gone, he found his heart greatly lifted, and three days later he was completely healed, and as well and strong and vigorous as he had ever been in all his life.

And this was the first time that King Arthur ever saw that beautiful lady, the Lady Guinevere of Cameliard, and from that time on he never forgot her; she was almost always present in his thoughts. And so, when he had recovered, he said to himself: "I will forget that I am a king, and I will cherish the thought of this lady, and I will serve her faithfully, as a good knight may serve the lady he has chosen."

And so he did, as you will hear later in this book.

Chapter 3: How King Arthur Found a Noble Sword in a Most Wonderful Way, and How He Fought Again with It and Won

King Arthur longs to renew his battle.

Now, as soon as King Arthur had been healed by that extraordinary balsam of the grievous wounds he had received in his battle with King Pellinore, he found himself seized by a fierce desire to meet his enemy again, to try the outcome of battle with him once more, and so win back the honor he had lost in that fight. On the morning of the fourth day, being entirely cured, and having eaten his breakfast, he went for a refreshing walk along the edge of the forest, listening all the while to the cheerful sound of the wood-birds singing their morning songs with all their might and main. Merlin walked beside him, and King Arthur told Merlin what was on his mind: that he meant to meet King Pellinore once more in knightly combat. And he said, "Merlin, it troubles me very sorely to have come off so badly in my last encounter with King Pellinore. Truly, he is the very best knight I have ever met in all the world. Even so, things might have gone differently for me if I had not broken my sword and so left myself altogether defenseless. However that may be, I have made up my mind to try this adventure once more, and I will do so as soon as I possibly can."

To this Merlin replied, "You are certainly a very brave man to have so much appetite for battle, considering how close you came to your death not even four days ago. Yet how can you hope to undertake this adventure without proper preparation? For look: you have no sword, and you have no spear, and you do not even have your misericord to fight with. How then can you hope to try this adventure?"

And King Arthur said, "That I do not know. Nevertheless, I will look for some weapon as soon as I can. For even if I have no better weapon than an oak club, I would still try this battle again with so poor a tool as that."

Merlin tells King Arthur of Excalibur.

"Ha! Lord," said Merlin, "I can see that you are entirely set on renewing this quarrel. So I will not try to hold you back from it, but will do everything in my power to help you get what you want. Now, to that end, I must tell you that in one part of this forest (which is, indeed, a very strange place) there is a certain woodland, sometimes called Arroy and at other times called the Forest of Adventure. It has that name because no knight ever enters it without some adventure happening to him. And close to Arroy is a land of enchantment, which has been seen several times. That is a very wonderful land, for in it there is a wide and great lake, which is also enchanted. And in the middle of that lake, for some time now, there has been seen what looks like a woman's arm, exceedingly beautiful and clothed in white samite, which is a rich silk. And the hand of this arm holds a sword of such surpassing excellence and beauty that no eye has ever seen its like. The name of this sword is Excalibur, and it was given that name by those who have seen it because of its marvelous brightness and beauty. For it has happened that several knights have already seen that sword and have tried to win it for their own, but until now no one has been able to touch it, and many have lost their lives in that adventure. For whenever any man comes near it, either he sinks into the lake, or else the arm vanishes entirely, or else it is drawn down beneath the lake. And so no man has ever been able to take possession of that sword. Now, I am able to lead you to that Lake of Enchantment, and there you may see Excalibur with your own eyes. Then, when you have seen him, perhaps you will want to win him; and if you are able to do that, you will have a sword very well fitted for battle."

"Merlin," said the King, "this is a very strange thing you are telling me. Now I want beyond all measure to try to win this sword for my own, and so I beg you to lead me with all speed to this enchanted lake you tell me of." And Merlin said, "I will do so."

So that morning King Arthur and Merlin took their leave of that holy hermit (the King kneeling in the grass to receive his blessing), and, departing from that place, they went into the deeper forest once more, making their way toward that part of it which was known as Arroy.

Merlin and King Arthur follow a white doe.

After a while they came to Arroy, and it was about noon. And when they had entered those woodlands they came to a certain little clearing, and in that clearing they saw a white doe with a golden collar around her neck. King Arthur said, "Look, Merlin, there is a wonderful sight." And Merlin said, "Let us follow that doe." At that the doe turned, and they followed it. By and by, as they followed it, they came to an opening in the trees where there was a little lawn of sweet, soft grass. Here they saw a bower, a little shelter of leaves and branches, and in front of the bower was a table spread with a fine snow-white cloth and set with refreshments: white bread, wine, and meats of several kinds. At the door of this bower there stood a page, dressed all in green, whose hair was as black as ebony, and whose eyes were as black as jet and exceedingly bright. When this page saw King Arthur and Merlin, he greeted them and welcomed the King very pleasantly, saying, "Ha! King Arthur, you are welcome to this place. Now please get down from your horse and refresh yourself before you go any farther."

At this King Arthur wondered whether there might be some enchantment here meant to do him harm, for he was astonished that a page in the deep forest should know him so well. But Merlin told him to be of good cheer, and said, "Truly, Lord, you may freely share in this meal, which, I may tell you, was prepared especially for you. What is more, you may take it as a sign that this adventure will turn out very happily."

King Arthur is refreshed in a mysterious way.

So King Arthur sat down at the table with a glad and easy heart (for he was hungry), and that page and another just like him waited on him, serving him all the food on silver plates and all the wine in golden goblets, just as he was used to being served in his own court, except that these things were far more skillfully made and fashioned, and far more beautiful, than the tableware of the King's court.

Then, after he had eaten his fill, and had washed his hands in a silver basin that the first page offered him, and had dried his hands on a fine linen napkin that the other page brought him, and after Merlin had also refreshed himself, they went on their way, greatly rejoicing at this pleasant adventure, which, it seemed to the King, could only mean that his undertaking would turn out very well.

Now, about the middle of the afternoon, King Arthur and Merlin came suddenly out of the forest onto a fair and level plain, covered all over with so many flowers that no one could imagine how many there were or how beautiful.

King Arthur comes to a strange land.

This was a very wonderful land, for all the air seemed to be made of gold, it was so bright and so strangely radiant. Here and there upon that plain stood various trees all in blossom, and the fragrance of the blossoms was so sweet that the King had never smelled anything like it. In the branches of those trees were a multitude of birds of many colors, and the melody of their singing filled the heart of anyone who heard it with delight. And in the middle of the plain was a lake of water as bright as silver, and all around the edges of the lake grew incredible numbers of lilies and daffodils. Yet, although this place was so exceedingly beautiful, there was nowhere in it a single sign of human life of any kind; it seemed as lonely as the empty sky on a summer day. So, because of all the marvelous beauty of the place, and its strangeness, and its utter loneliness, King Arthur understood that he must have come into a land of powerful enchantment, where perhaps there lived a fairy of very high rank. And so a kind of fear wrapped itself around his spirit as he pushed his great milk-white war-horse through that long, beautiful grass, all sprinkled with flowers, and he did not know what strange things were about to happen to him.

King Arthur sees Excalibur.

King Arthur meets the Lady of the Lake.

When he had come to the edge of the lake, he saw there the miracle that Merlin had told him of before. For there, in the middle of the stretch of water, was what looked like a fair and beautiful arm, like a woman's, clothed all in white samite. The arm was circled with several bracelets of worked gold, and the hand held a sword of marvelous workmanship high in the air above the surface of the water. Neither the arm nor the sword moved so much as a hair's breadth, but they stood as still as a carved statue upon the surface of the lake. And the sun of that strange land shone down upon the hilt of the sword, which was of pure gold set with jewels of many kinds, so that the hilt of the sword and the bracelets around the arm glittered in the middle of the lake like some single star of surpassing splendor. King Arthur sat on his war-horse and gazed from a distance at the arm and the sword, and he marveled greatly at them. Yet he did not know how he could reach that sword, for the lake was wonderfully wide and deep, and so he did not know how he might get to it to make it his own. And as he sat turning this over in his mind, he suddenly became aware of a strange lady who was coming toward him through the tall flowers that bloomed along the edge of the lake. When he saw her coming toward him, he quickly got down from his war-horse and went forward to meet her, with the bridle-rein over his arm. And when he had come near her, he saw that she was extraordinarily beautiful: her face was as clear as wax, and her eyes were perfectly black, and they were as bright and glistening as two jewels set in ivory. He saw that her hair was like silk, and as black as anything could be, and so long that it reached the ground as she walked. The lady was dressed all in green, except that a fine cord of crimson and gold was woven into the braids of her hair. Around her neck hung a very beautiful necklace of several strands of opals and emeralds, set in skillfully worked gold, and around her wrists were bracelets of the same kind, of opals and emeralds set in gold. So when King Arthur saw how wonderful she looked, like an ivory statue of surpassing beauty dressed all in green, he immediately knelt before her in the midst of all those flowers, and said, "Lady, I can see for certain that you are no mortal maiden, but one of the fairy folk. And I can see that this place, because of its extraordinary beauty, can be nothing other than some part of Fairyland into which I have come."

And the Lady answered, "King Arthur, you speak the truth, for I am indeed of Fairyland. What is more, I may tell you that my name is Nimue, and that I am the chief of those Ladies of the Lake you may have heard people speak of. You should also know that what you see over there as a wide lake is, in truth, a plain like this one, all covered with flowers. And you should know too that in the middle of that plain there stands a castle of white marble and of ultramarine, the deep blue of lapis, brightened with gold. But so that mortal eyes may not see our dwelling place, my sisters and I have caused this appearance of a lake to spread over that castle, so that it is entirely hidden from sight. And no mortal man may cross that lake except in one way; any other way, he will certainly die in it."

"Lady," said King Arthur, "what you tell me fills me with very great wonder. And indeed, I am afraid that in coming here I have done wrong by intruding upon the solitude of your dwelling place."

"No, not so, King Arthur," said the Lady of the Lake, "for in truth you are very welcome here. What is more, I may tell you that I feel a greater friendship for you and for the noble knights of your court than you could easily know. But I ask you, of your courtesy, to tell me what it is that brings you to our land."

"Lady," said the King, "I will tell you the whole truth. Not long ago I fought a battle with a certain sable knight, in which I was sorely and grievously wounded, and in which I burst my spear and snapped my sword and lost even my misericord, so that I had not a single thing left to me by way of a weapon. In this extremity Merlin, here, told me of Excalibur, and of how he is held up forever by an arm in the middle of this magical lake. So I came here, and I find it just as he said. Now, Lady, if it is possible, I would gladly win that excellent sword, so that with it I might fight my battle through to the very end."

"Ha! my lord King," said the Lady of the Lake, "that sword is not an easy thing to win, and what is more, I may tell you that several knights have lost their lives trying to do what you have in mind. For, in truth, no man may win that sword over there unless he is without fear and without reproach."

"Alas, Lady!" said King Arthur, "that is a sad saying indeed for me. For though I may not be lacking in knightly courage, yet in truth there are many things for which I reproach myself. Nevertheless, I would gladly attempt this thing, even if it puts me in great danger. So I beg you, tell me how I may best undertake this adventure."

The Lady of the Lake summons a boat.

"King Arthur," said the Lady of the Lake, "I will do as I say, and help you in what you wish in this matter." At that she lifted a single emerald that hung by a small gold chain at her belt, and the emerald was skillfully carved into the shape of a whistle. She set the whistle to her lips and blew on it very shrilly. And at once there appeared upon the water, a great way off, something that shone very brightly. It came toward them at great speed, and as it drew near, they saw that it was a boat made all of carved brass. The prow of the boat was carved into the head of a beautiful woman, and on either side were wings like the wings of a swan. And the boat moved upon the water like a swan, very swiftly, so that long lines like silver threads stretched far away behind it across the face of the water, which otherwise was as smooth as glass. When the brass boat had reached the bank, it rested there and moved no more.

Then the Lady of the Lake told King Arthur to get into the boat, and so he got in. And the moment he had done so, the boat moved away from the bank as swiftly as it had come. Merlin and the Lady of the Lake stood at the edge of the water and gazed after King Arthur and the brass boat.

And King Arthur saw that the boat floated swiftly across the lake to the place where the arm held up the sword, and that the arm and the sword did not move, but stayed where they were.

King Arthur wins Excalibur.

Then King Arthur reached out and took the sword in his hand, and at once the arm sank out of sight beneath the water. And King Arthur held the sword, and its scabbard, and its belt in his hand, and they were his own.

Then truly his heart swelled with joy as if it would burst in his chest, for Excalibur was a hundred times more beautiful than he had thought possible. And so his heart was close to breaking with pure joy at having won that magic sword.

Then the brass boat carried him very quickly back to the land again, and he stepped ashore where the Lady of the Lake and Merlin stood. And when he stood on the shore, he thanked the Lady beyond measure for all she had done to help him in his great undertaking, and she answered him with cheerful and pleasant words.

Then King Arthur bowed to the lady, as was fitting, and when he had mounted his war-horse, and Merlin had mounted his palfrey, his gentle riding horse, they rode away from there about their business. And all the while the King's heart was still swelling with pure delight at having for his own that beautiful sword, the most beautiful and the most famous sword in all the world.

That night King Arthur and Merlin stayed with the holy hermit at the forest sanctuary. And when the next morning came (the King having bathed in the ice-cold forest spring, and being wonderfully refreshed by it), they set out, thanking that saintly man for the shelter he had given them.

Then, about noon, they reached the valley of the Sable Knight, and there everything was arranged exactly as it had been when King Arthur was there before: that gloomy castle, the lawn of smooth grass, the apple tree hung all over with shields, and the bridge on which hung that single black shield.

"Now, Merlin," said King Arthur, "this time I strictly forbid you to interfere in this quarrel. And you are not, on pain of my displeasure, to use any of your magic arts on my behalf. So listen to what I say, and heed it with all possible care."

King Arthur challenges King Pellinore to battle again.

At once the King rode out onto the bridge, and, seizing the brass hammer, he struck the sable shield with all his might and main. Immediately the portcullis of the castle was let down, as I told you before, and in the same way as that other time the Sable Knight rode out, already armed and equipped for the encounter. He came to the head of the bridge, and there King Arthur spoke to him: "Sir Pellinore, we now know each other entirely well, and each of us judges that he has a quarrel with the other. You judge that I, for reasons of my own that seemed right to me, have taken away your royal rank and have driven you into this lonely forest. I judge that you have set yourself up here to do injury and insult to the knights and lords and other people of this kingdom of mine. So, since I am here as a knight-errant, I challenge you to fight me, man to man, until one of us has conquered the other."

King Pellinore bowed his head in agreement to this speech, and then he wheeled his horse, rode off a little distance, and took his place where he had stood before. And King Arthur also rode off a little distance and took up his position where he had stood before. At the same time there came out of the castle one of those tall pages dressed all in black, patched with crimson, and he gave King Arthur a good, stout spear of ash wood, well seasoned and never yet used in battle. And when the two knights were ready, they shouted and drove their horses together, and each struck the other so squarely in the middle of his defenses that the spears shattered in their hands, bursting into small splinters just as they had done before.

Then each of the two knights at once leapt from his horse with great skill and grace, and each drew his sword. And they fell to fighting so furiously and so violently that two wild bulls on the mountains could not have fought a more desperate battle.

King Arthur overcomes King Pellinore.

But now, with Excalibur to help him in the battle, King Arthur soon overcame his enemy. For he gave him several wounds and yet received none himself; he did not shed a single drop of blood in that whole fight, though in a little while his enemy's armor was all stained with crimson. And at last King Arthur struck so mighty a blow that King Pellinore was entirely stunned by it, so that his sword and his shield dropped from their guard, his legs trembled beneath him, and he sank to his knees on the ground. Then he called on King Arthur to have mercy, saying, "Spare my life, and I will yield myself to you."

King Arthur demands two of the sons of King Pellinore as hostages.

And King Arthur said, "I will spare you, and I will do more than that. For now that you have yielded yourself to me, I will give you back your power and your rank. I bear you no ill will, Pellinore. Nevertheless, I cannot allow any rebels against my power in this realm. For, as God is my judge, I declare that I keep only the good of the people of my kingdom in my sight. And so whoever is against me is also against them, and whoever is against them is also against me. But as a pledge of your good faith toward me in the future, I shall require you to send me your two eldest sons, Sir Aglovale and Sir Lamorak, as hostages for your good will. Your young son, Durnore, you may keep with you for your comfort."

So those two young knights came to the court of King Arthur, and they became very famous knights, and in time they were made Companions of the Round Table, in great honor.

And King Arthur and King Pellinore went together into King Pellinore's castle, and there King Pellinore's wounds were dressed and he was made comfortable. That night King Arthur stayed in King Pellinore's castle, and when the next morning came, he and Merlin returned to the King's court, which was waiting for him in the forest at the place where he had set it up.

How King Arthur rode through the forest with great joy and delight.

Now King Arthur took very great pleasure as he and Merlin rode back together through that forest, for it was the leafiest time of all the year, when the woodlands dress themselves in their best clothes of clear, bright green. Every shady hollow and little wooded valley was full of the perfume of the thickets, and in every tangled depth the little bird sang with all his might and main, as though he would burst his little throat with the melody of his song. The ground beneath the horses' feet was so soft with fragrant moss that the ear could not hear any sound of hoofbeats on the earth. And the bright yellow sunlight came down through the leaves, so that the whole ground was scattered with a great multitude of trembling circles, like pure yellow gold. And every now and then that sunlight would fall upon the armed knight as he rode, so that every little while his armor seemed to catch fire with a great glory, shining like a sudden bright star among the dark shadows of the woodland.

So it was that King Arthur took great joy in that forest land, for he was without ache or pain of any kind, and his heart was lifted very high by the wonderful success of the adventure he had undertaken. For in that adventure he had not only turned a very bitter enemy into a friend who would be of great use and comfort to him, but he had also won for himself a sword whose like the world had never seen before. And whenever he thought of that wonderfully splendid sword that now hung at his side, and whenever he remembered that Fairyland into which he had wandered, and what had happened to him there, his heart would grow so full of pure joy that he hardly knew how to contain himself, because of the great delight that filled his whole being.

And indeed, I know of no greater good I could wish for you in all your life than that you might enjoy the kind of happiness that comes to someone when he has done his very best and has succeeded completely in what he set out to do. For then the whole world seems to be filled with a bright, shining light, and the body seems to grow so light that the feet are lifted from all heaviness and touch the earth very lightly, because of the lightness of the spirit within. And that is why, if I had it in my power to give you the very best the world has to give, I would wish that you might win your battle as King Arthur won his battle that time, and that you might ride homeward in such triumph and joy as filled him that day, and that the sunlight might shine around you as it shone around him, and that the breezes might blow and all the little birds might sing with all their might and main as they sang for him, and that your heart, too, might sing its song of rejoicing in the pleasantness of the world in which you live.

Merlin tells King Arthur of the virtues of Excalibur's sheath.

Now, as they rode together through the forest like this, Merlin said to the King, "Lord, which would you rather have, Excalibur, or the sheath that holds him?" To which King Arthur replied, "I would rather have Excalibur than his sheath ten thousand times over." "In that you are wrong, my Lord," said Merlin, "for let me tell you that though Excalibur is of so fine a temper that he can cut in two either a feather or a bar of iron, yet his sheath is of such a kind that whoever wears it can suffer no wound in battle, nor can he lose a single drop of blood. As proof of this, you may remember that in your recent battle with King Pellinore you suffered no wound, nor did you lose any blood."

Then King Arthur turned a face of great displeasure upon his companion and said, "Now, Merlin, I declare that you have taken from me all the glory of the battle I have just fought. For what credit can there be to any knight who fights his enemy by means of enchantment such as you tell me of? Indeed, I have a mind to take this glorious sword back to that magic lake and throw it in, where it belongs. For I believe that a knight should fight by his own strength, and not by magic."

"My Lord," said Merlin, "you are certainly entirely right in what you hold. But you must bear in mind that you are not like an ordinary knight-errant. You are a King, and your life does not belong to you, but to your people. So you have no right to put it in danger, but should do everything in your power to preserve it. And that is why you should keep that sword, so that it may safeguard your life."

Then King Arthur thought over that saying for a long while in silence, and when he spoke, these were his words: "Merlin, you are right in what you say. For the sake of my people, I will keep both Excalibur, to fight for them, and his sheath as well, to preserve my life for their sake. Nevertheless, I will never use him again except in serious battle." And King Arthur held to that promise, so that from then on he never fought in sport except with the lance and on horseback.

King Arthur kept Excalibur as the greatest treasure of all his possessions. For he said to himself, "Such a sword as this is fit for a king above other kings and a lord above other lords. Now, since God has seen fit to place this sword in my keeping in so marvelous a way as happened, He must mean that I am to be His servant to do unusual things. And so I will treasure this noble weapon not so much for its excellent worth as because it shall be to me a sign of those great things that God, in His mercy, has plainly ordained for me to do in His service."

So King Arthur had a strong chest made for Excalibur, a coffer bound around with many bands of wrought iron, studded all over with great iron nails, and locked with three great padlocks. In this strongbox he kept Excalibur lying on a cushion of crimson silk and wrapped in swathings of fine linen, and very few people ever saw the sword in its glory, except when it shone like a sudden flame in the uproar of battle.

For when the time came for King Arthur to defend his realm or his people from their enemies, he would take out the sword and fasten it at his side. And when he did so, he was like a hero of God armed with a blade of shining lightning. Yes, at such times Excalibur shone with so terrible a brightness that the very sight of it would shake the spirit of every wrongdoer with such great fear that he would, in a way, suffer the pangs of death before the edge of the blade had ever touched his flesh.

So King Arthur treasured Excalibur, and the sword stayed with him all his life, so that the name of Arthur and the name of Excalibur are one. And so I believe that this sword is the most famous of any sword that was ever seen or heard of in all the courts of chivalry.

As for the sheath of the blade, King Arthur lost that through the treachery of one who should by rights have been his dearest friend (as you will hear before long), and in the end the loss of that miraculous sheath brought him a very great deal of pain and sorrow.

All that, too, you will read about, God willing, in due time.

So ends the story of the winning of Excalibur. And may God give to you in your life His Truth to help you, like a shining sword, to overcome your enemies; and may He give you Faith (for Faith holds Truth as a scabbard holds its sword), and may that Faith heal all your wounds of sorrow as the sheath of Excalibur healed all the wounds of him who wore that excellent weapon. For with Truth and Faith belted upon you, you will be as well able to fight all your battles as was that noble hero of old whom men called King Arthur.

The Book of King Arthur, Part Three: The Winning of a Queen

Foreword

So, now that I have told you how King Arthur won that most excellent sword, Excalibur, to be his weapon of defense, I will soon tell you about several other noble and knightly adventures by which he won a most beautiful and gentle lady to be his Queen.

For though all the world knows very well the fame of that perfectly gracious lady, the Lady Guinevere, I do not think that the whole story of the adventures by which King Arthur won her favor has ever yet been told.

And since what I am about to tell contains not only the story of that affair, but also the account of a certain enchanted disguise that King Arthur put on for his own purposes, as well as several adventures of very knightly daring that he undertook, I have great hope that whoever reads what I have written will find it both a pleasant and an entertaining history.

Chapter 1: How King Arthur Went to Tintagalon with Four of His Court, and How He Disguised Himself for a Secret Purpose

How King Arthur held a feast at Caerleon on Usk.

Now one day King Arthur proclaimed a high feast, which was held at Caerleon on Usk. Many noble guests were invited, and an extremely splendid Court gathered at the King's castle. For at that feast there sat seven kings and five queens in royal state, and there were high lords and beautiful ladies of rank, sixty-seven of them in all; and there was a great crowd of those famous knights of the King's Court who were counted the most renowned in arms in all of Christendom. And of all this great gathering of kings, lords, and knights, not one man looked sideways at his neighbor with suspicion, but all were united in good fellowship. So when the young King looked around him and saw such peace and friendship among all these noble lords, where before there had been quarreling and ill will, he said to himself, "Truly, it is wonderful how this reign of mine has knit men together in kindness and good fellowship!" And because of thoughts like these, his spirit took wing like a bird and sang within him.

Now while the King sat feasting like this, suddenly there came a herald, a messenger from the west country. And the herald came and stood before the King and said, "Greetings to you, King Arthur!"

Then the King said, "Speak, and tell me what your message is."

A herald-messenger comes from the west country.

To this the herald replied: "I come from King Leodegrance of Cameliard, who is in great trouble. For this is how things stand. His enemy and yours, King Rience of North Wales (the one who once, in scorn of you, commanded you to send him your beard to trim his cloak), is making several demands of my master, King Leodegrance, and King Leodegrance is entirely unwilling to grant them. And King Rience of North Wales threatens to bring war into Cameliard because King Leodegrance does not grant those demands at once. Now King Leodegrance no longer has such a host of knights and armed men around him as he once had to defend his kingdom against attack. For since you in your majesty have brought peace to this realm, and have brought low the power of all the kings beneath you, those knights who once made the Court of King Leodegrance so famous have gone elsewhere to look for better chances to show their great courage and skill at arms than his peaceful Court can offer. And so my master, King Leodegrance, begs help from you, who are his King and High King."

King Arthur, and all the Court that feasted with him, listened to what the herald said in complete silence. And the King's face, which a moment before had been open and cheerful, grew clouded and dark with anger. "Ha!" he cried. "This is no good news, truly, that you have brought here to our feast. Now I will give your master, King Leodegrance, whatever help I can in this hour of need, and I will give it quickly. But tell me, sir herald, what things is it that King Rience demands of your master?"

"That I will tell you, Lord," said the herald. "First, King Rience demands from my master a great part of those lands of Cameliard that lie along the borders of North Wales. Second, he demands that the Lady Guinevere, the King's daughter, be given in marriage to Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, who is a kinsman of King Rience. And that Duke, though he is a mighty warrior, is so evil to look at, and so violent of temper, that I believe there is no one like him for ugliness or for madness of mood in all the world."

King Arthur is very angry at the message the herald brings.

Now when King Arthur heard what the messenger said, he was seized at once with an extraordinary fit of anger. His eyes seemed, as it were, to shoot out sparks of pure light; his face flamed like fire; and he ground his teeth together like the stones of a hand mill grinding grain. Then he rose at once from the chair where he sat and went out of that place, and everyone who saw his anger shuddered at it and turned their eyes away from his face.

Then King Arthur went by himself into an inner room of the castle, and there he walked up and down for a long while, and in all that time no one of his household dared come near him. And the reason for the King's fury was this. Ever since he had lain wounded and sick almost to death in the forest, he had remembered how the Lady Guinevere had suddenly appeared before him like some tall, straight, shining angel come down to him out of Paradise, all full of pity, and extremely beautiful. So at the thought of that wicked, mad Duke Mordaunt of North Umber demanding to marry her, he was seized with a rage so violent that it shook his spirit like a mighty wind.

So for a long while he walked up and down in his fury, as I have said, and no one dared come near him, but everyone stood far off, watching him from a distance.

Then, after a while, he gave orders that Merlin and Sir Ulfius and Sir Kay should come to him where he was. And when they had come, he talked with them for a good while. He told Merlin to make ready to go on a journey with him, and he told Sir Ulfius and Sir Kay to gather a large army of chosen knights and armed men, and to bring that army straight into the country around the royal castle of Tintagalon, which stands close to the borders of North Wales and of Cameliard.

So Sir Ulfius and Sir Kay set about doing as King Arthur commanded, and Merlin also set about doing as he commanded. And the next day King Arthur and Merlin set out for Tintagalon across the forest country of Usk, together with certain famous knights of the King's Court who had proved themselves the best at arms of all those around him: namely, Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain (who were the King's nephews), and Sir Pelleas, and Sir Geraint, the son of Erbin.

How King Arthur came to Tintagalon.

They traveled all that day and part of the next, and they met with no adventure or misadventure of any kind. And so at last they came to that large and noble castle called Tintagalon, which guards the country bordering on Cameliard and North Wales. Here King Arthur was welcomed with great rejoicing, for wherever the King went the people loved him very dearly. So the folk of Tintagalon were very glad when he came to them.

Now on the morning after King Arthur came to Tintagalon (the summer night having been very warm), he and Merlin were glad to get up early and go out to enjoy the dewy freshness of the early day. So in the cool of the morning they walked together in the garden, which was a very pleasant place, beneath the shadow of a tall, straight tower. And all around were many trees giving good shade, where the little birds sang sweetly in the cheerfulness of the summer weather.

And here King Arthur opened his heart very freely to Merlin, and he said: "Merlin, I truly believe that the Lady Guinevere is the most beautiful lady in all the world. And so my heart always seems to be completely filled with love for her, so much so that I think of her all day long (whether I am eating, or drinking, or walking, or sitting still, or going about my business), and I dream of her many times at night as well. And it has been this way with me, Merlin, ever since a month ago, when I lay sick in that hermit's cell in the forest, and she came and stood beside me like a shining angel out of Paradise. So I am not willing that any man other than I should have her for his wife.

King Arthur opens his heart to Merlin.

"Now I know very well that you are wonderfully skilled in those arts of magic that can change a man's appearance so that even those who know him best cannot recognize him. So I very much want you to disguise me in such a way that I can go into Cameliard without anyone knowing me, and live there in such a way that I can see the Lady Guinevere every day. For I tell you truly, I greatly wish to look upon her in such a way that she will not know in the least that I am watching her. I would also like to see for myself how great the dangers are that surround King Leodegrance, for the King is my very good friend."

"My Lord King," said Merlin, "it shall be as you wish, and this very morning I will disguise you so well that no one in all the world will be able to tell who you are."

So that morning, a little before the early hour of prayer, Merlin came to the King where he was and gave him a little cap. And the cap was of such a kind that when the King put it on his head, he took on in an instant the appearance of a rough country fellow from the farmlands. Then the King ordered a jerkin of coarse wool to be brought to him, and with this he covered his royal and knightly clothes, and with it he hid the golden collar and the jewel hanging from it, which he always wore around his neck. Then, putting the cap on his head, he at once took on the look of that peasant farmhand.

King Arthur leaves Tintagalon in disguise.

And so, completely disguised, he left Tintagalon without anyone knowing him, and made his way on foot to the town of Cameliard.

Now toward the slanting of the day he drew near that place, and there in front of him he saw a large and important town of many handsome houses with red walls and shining windows. The houses of the town all sat on a high, steep hill, one above another, and the town itself was surrounded by a great wall, high and strong. A great castle guarded the town, and the castle had very many towers and roofs. And all around the tower there were many lovely gardens and lawns and meadows, and several orchards and groves of trees with thick and pleasant shade. Now at that time of day the sky behind the tower was, as it were, one single flame of fire, so that the towers and the battlements of the castle, and its roofs and chimneys, stood out completely black against the brightness of the light. And great flocks of pigeons circled the towers of the castle, flying on and on against that fiery sky. And because King Arthur was weary from walking all day, it seemed to him that he had hardly ever in all his life seen so lovely and pleasant a place as that fine castle with its gardens and lawns and groves of trees.

King Arthur comes to Cameliard.

So King Arthur came to the castle of Cameliard in the guise of a poor peasant from the countryside, and no one in all the world knew who he was.

When he had reached the castle, he asked for the head gardener. And when he got to speak with the gardener, he begged to be taken on as a servant in that part of the garden that belonged to the Lady Guinevere's dwelling. The gardener looked him over and saw that he was tall and strong and well built, so he liked him very much and took him into his service, just as he wished.

And that is how King Arthur of Britain became a gardener's boy at Cameliard.

King Arthur lives as gardener's boy at the castle.

Now the King was very glad to be in that garden, for in this pleasant summer season the Lady Guinevere came every day to walk with her damsels among the flowers, and King Arthur, disguised as a peasant gardener's boy, saw her a great many times when she came there.

So King Arthur stayed in that place for more than a week, and he did not care at all that in all that time he enjoyed none of the splendor of a king, but was only a gardener's boy in the castle garden of Cameliard.

Now it happened one day, when the weather was very warm, that one of the damsels who waited on the Lady Guinevere got up very early in the morning, while the air was still cool and fresh. Leaving the Lady Guinevere still asleep, this damsel, whose name was Mellicene of the White Hand, went into the anteroom, and opening its window, looked out into the rose garden that lay next to the Lady Guinevere's private rooms.

Now in that place there stood a carved marble figure of a youth holding a marble pitcher in his arms, and a fountain of water, as clear as crystal, flowed out of the pitcher into a marble basin. The figure, and the fountain, and the marble basin into which the fountain flowed all lay beneath the shade of a linden tree. And all around grew a thick tangle of roses, so that the place was completely hidden except from those windows of the castle that were above it.

The damsel sees a knight at the fountain.

And so, as the damsel looked down toward it out of the window, she saw a very wonderful sight. For there was a strange knight, kneeling beside the fountain and bathing his face and his chest in its crystal water. The damsel saw that the sunlight fell down through the leaves of the linden tree and lay on that strange knight. And she saw that his hair and his beard were the color of red gold, shining wonderfully in the brightness of the morning. And she saw that his brow and his throat and his chest were as white as alabaster. And she saw that around his neck and shoulders there hung a golden collar of marvelous beauty, so that when the sunlight shone on it, it flashed like pure lightning.

So, seeing this strange sight, which was like a vision, the damsel Mellicene stood for a long while, quite spellbound with wonder and delight. She did not know whether what she saw was a dream or not, nor whether the one who sat there was a spirit or a man of flesh and blood.

Then, by and by, coming partly to herself after her astonishment, she drew back softly from the window, turned around, and ran swiftly down the turret stairs, and so came out into that lovely, blooming garden at the foot of the tower. She ran through the garden as fast and as quietly as she could, and so came down a pathway to the marble fountain and the linden trees and the rosebushes around it, where she had just now seen that strange knight bathing in the crystal water.

The damsel finds only the gardener's boy.

But King Arthur had heard the damsel coming, and had quickly put the cap back on his head. So when the damsel Mellicene got there, she found no one by the fountain but the gardener's boy. She demanded of him, "Who are you, fellow? And why are you sitting here by the fountain?"

And he answered her, "I am the gardener's lad who came here a little while ago to take service in this place."
