"Then tell me, fellow," she said, "and tell me truly. Who was that young knight who was here beside the fountain just now, and where has he gone?" "Lady," he said, "there has been no one at this fountain today but me."

"No, fellow," she cried, "you are deceiving me, for I assure you that I saw with my own eyes, just now, a strange young knight sitting here bathing himself in the water of this fountain." And the gardener's boy said, "Lady, what I have told you is the very truth, for indeed no one has been here this morning but me. So if you think you saw anyone else, you are certainly mistaken."

At this the damsel fixed her gaze on him in great bewilderment. She wondered at it very much as well, for she could not altogether disbelieve him. And yet she could not entirely believe him either, because her eyes had seen what they had seen, and she knew she had not been mistaken. So she did not know what to think, and because she was so puzzled, she felt very annoyed with that gardener's boy. "Truly," she said, "if you are deceiving me, I will certainly make you suffer a great deal of pain, for I will have you whipped with cords." With that she turned and went away from that place, wondering greatly at that strange thing and what it could all mean.

That morning she told the Lady Guinevere everything she had seen, but the Lady Guinevere only laughed at her and teased her, telling her that she had been asleep and dreaming when she saw that vision. And indeed, the damsel herself had begun to think this must be so. All the same, after that she looked out of her window every morning, though for a long while she saw nothing, for King Arthur did not come back to that place for some time.

And so, by and by, there came another morning when she looked out of the window, and there sat that strange knight by the fountain once more, just as he had sat before. And he bathed his face and his chest in the water as he had done before. And he looked as handsome and as noble as he had looked before, and his hair and his young beard shone like gold in the sun as they had shone before. And this time she saw that his golden collar lay on the edge of the fountain beside him, and it sparkled with great splendor in the sunlight while he bathed his chest. Then, after the damsel had watched him for a good while, she ran as fast as she could to the room where the Lady Guinevere still lay, and she cried out loudly, "Lady! Lady! Wake up and come with me! For that same young knight I saw before is bathing himself at the fountain under the linden tree right now!"

Then the Lady Guinevere, wondering greatly, got up very quickly, and dressing herself in all haste, went with the damsel to the window that looked out on that part of the garden.

The Lady Guinevere sees the knight at the fountain.

And there she herself saw the young knight as he washed himself at the fountain. She saw that his hair and his beard shone like gold in the sunlight. She saw that his undershirt was of purple linen threaded with gold. And she saw that beside him lay that skillfully made collar of gold, set with many jewels of different colors, and the collar shone with great splendor where it lay on the marble rim of the fountain.

For a while she gazed, utterly astonished. Then she told the damsel Mellicene to come with her, and with that she turned and went down the turret stairs and out into the garden, just as her damsel had done before. Then, as that damsel had done, she hurried as fast as she could down the pathway toward the fountain.

But when she got there, she found no young knight, but only the gardener's boy, exactly as had happened to the damsel Mellicene before. For King Arthur had heard her coming, and had at once put that enchanted cap on his head. Then the Lady Guinevere wondered greatly to find only the gardener's boy there, and she did not know what to think of so strange a thing. So she demanded of him, just as Mellicene had done, where the young knight had gone whom she had seen there at the fountain just a moment ago. And the gardener's lad answered her as before: "Lady! There has been no one at this place at any time this morning but me."

Now when King Arthur had put on his cap as the Lady came, he had, in his great haste, forgotten his golden collar, and Guinevere saw it lying there, shining very brightly, beside the edge of the fountain. "What is this?" she said. "Would you dare to make a fool of me? Now tell me, you fellow, do gardeners' boys in the land you come from wear golden collars around their necks, like that collar lying over there beside the fountain? Now, if I had you soundly whipped, it would be no more than you deserve. But take that trinket over there and give it to the one it rightly belongs to, and tell him from me that it ill becomes a true belted knight to hide himself away in a lady's private garden." Then she turned with the damsel Mellicene and left that place and went back to her rooms.

And yet, all that day, as she sat over her embroidery, she never stopped marveling and wondering how it could be that the strange young knight had vanished so suddenly and left only the poor gardener's boy in his place. And for a long time she could not solve that strange riddle.

Then, all of a sudden, at the time when the heat of the day was sloping toward the cooler part of the afternoon, she sat up because of a thought that had come to her in an instant. So she called the damsel Mellicene to her, and told her to go and tell the gardener's lad to bring her a basket of fresh roses at once to decorate her tower room.

The gardener's boy wears his cap before the Lady Guinevere.

So Mellicene went and did as she was told, and after a good while the gardener's lad came carrying a great basket of roses. And he was wearing his cap on his head. And when all the damsels waiting on the Lady Guinevere saw that he wore his cap in her presence, they cried out against him, and Mellicene of the White Hand demanded of him, "What! What is this, Sir Lout? Do you know so little of what is owed to a king's daughter that you wear your cap even in the presence of the Lady Guinevere? Now I order you to take that cap off your head this instant."

And King Arthur answered her, "Lady, I cannot take off my cap."

The Lady Guinevere said, "And why can't you take off your cap, you surly fellow?"

"Lady," he said, "I cannot take off my cap because I have an ugly spot on my head."

"Then wear your cap," said the Lady Guinevere. "Only bring the roses to me."

The Lady Guinevere discovers the knight of the fountain.

So at her command he brought the roses to her. But when he had come near the lady, she suddenly snatched at the cap and plucked it off his head. And in that instant he was transformed, for instead of the gardener's boy there stood before the Lady Guinevere and her damsel the figure of a noble young knight with hair and beard like threads of gold. Then he let his basket of roses fall, so that the flowers were scattered all over the floor, and he stood and looked at everyone there. And some of the damsels waiting on the Lady Guinevere shrieked, and others stood still in pure amazement and did not know how to believe what their eyes were seeing. But not one of those ladies knew that the man she was looking at was King Arthur. Still, the Lady Guinevere remembered that this was the knight she had found so badly wounded, lying in the hermit's cell in the forest.

Then she laughed and tossed him back his cap. "Take your cap," she said, "and go on your way, you gardener's boy with the ugly spot on his head." She said this because she wanted to tease him.

But King Arthur did not answer her. Instead, very soberly, he put his cap back on his head at once. And so, taking on his humble disguise once more, he turned and left that place, leaving the roses scattered all over the floor just as they had fallen.

And after that, whenever the Lady Guinevere came upon the gardener's lad in the garden, she would say to her damsel, in a voice loud enough for him to hear, "Look! There is the gardener's lad who has an ugly spot on his head, so that he always has to wear his cap to hide it."

So she spoke openly, teasing him. But privately she told her damsels to say nothing about these things, and to keep everything that had happened to themselves.

Chapter 2: How King Rience Came to Cameliard, and How King Arthur Fought the Duke of North Umber

King Rience and Duke Mordaunt come to Cameliard.

Now one day about this time a messenger came to the Court of King Leodegrance with news that King Rience of North Wales and Duke Mordaunt of North Umber were on their way there, and that they were bringing with them a very noble and very large Court of knights and lords. King Leodegrance was much troubled in spirit at this news. He did not know what such a visit might mean, and yet he greatly feared that it meant trouble for him. So on the day that King Rience and the Duke of North Umber appeared before the castle, King Leodegrance went out to greet them, and the three of them met in the meadows that lie beneath the castle walls of Cameliard.

There King Leodegrance welcomed them in a fitting manner, and asked them to come into the castle with him so that he might entertain them as their rank deserved.

The King and the Duke send a challenge to King Leodegrance.

But King Rience did not trouble to give any pleasant answer to this courtesy of King Leodegrance's. "No," he said, "we will not go with you into your castle, King Leodegrance, until we learn whether you are our friend or our enemy. For right now, truly, we are not such good friends of yours that we care to sit down at your table and eat your salt. Nor can we be anything but your enemies until you have first satisfied our demands: that is, that you give me the lands I have demanded of you, and that you give my cousin, Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, the Lady Guinevere to be his wife. In these matters it is in your power to make us either your friends or your enemies. So we will stay here, outside your castle, for five days. In that time you may frame your answer, and so we shall know whether we are to be friends or enemies."

"And in the meantime," said Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, "I hold myself ready to fight for my right to the hand of the Lady Guinevere against any knight of your Court who cares to deny my just claim to her. And if there is no knight in all your Court who can stand against me in a bout of arms, then you yourself can hardly hope to defend yourself against the great army of knights that King Rience has gathered to bring against you, if you refuse us what we ask."

Then King Leodegrance was terribly downcast, for he feared those proud lords and did not know what to say in answer to them. So he turned and walked back into his castle, weighed down with great anxiety and sorrow. And King Rience and Duke Mordaunt and their Court of lords and knights pitched their pavilions in the meadows facing the castle, until the whole plain was covered with them. There they made their lodging with great rejoicing, with the sound of feasting and singing and merrymaking, for it was a very noble Court that King Rience had gathered around him.

Duke Mordaunt rides before the castle.

When the next morning came, Duke Mordaunt of North Umber rode out clad all in armor of proven strength. He rode up and down the field before the castle and shouted a great challenge to those within, daring any knight to come out and meet him in knightly combat. "Ho!" he cried. "What now, you Knights of Cameliard! Is there no one who will come out to meet me? How can you hope to stand against the Knights of North Wales, if you are afraid to meet one single knight from North Umber?" So he jeered at them in his pride, and no one dared to come out of Cameliard against him. For the Duke of North Umber was one of the most famous knights of his day, of enormous strength and great success in arms, and now, in these times of peace, there was no one in King Leodegrance's Court who was anywhere near able to face a warrior of such proven skill and valor. So no one took up the challenge that the Duke of North Umber gave to the Court of Cameliard. Meanwhile many people gathered on the walls of Cameliard and gazed down at that proud and haughty duke, all decked out in his splendid armor, and they were all grieved and ashamed that there was no one in that peaceful town to go out against him. And all the lords and knights of King Rience's Court came and stood in front of the King's pavilion, and laughed and clapped their hands and cheered Duke Mordaunt as he rode up and down before them. And the merrier they grew, the more shamefaced the people of Cameliard became. "Ho! Ho!" cried that proud Duke. "What now! Will no one come out to meet me? How then can you of Cameliard hope to face the King of North Wales and all his knightly army, when I am only one man of it?" And the people of Cameliard, gathered on the walls, listened to him in shame and sorrow.

Now all this while King Arthur was digging in the garden. Even so, he knew very well everything that was happening, and how the Duke of North Umber was riding so proudly up and down before the castle walls. And suddenly it came to him that he could not bear it any longer. So he laid aside his spade and slipped out secretly by a back way, and went up into the town.

Now there was in Cameliard a very rich merchant named Ralph of Cardiff, and the fame of his wealth and his high position had reached even King Arthur's ears at Caerleon. So it was to his house that King Arthur made his way.

King Arthur seeks armor to do battle.

And while he was in a narrow lane not far from the merchant's house, he took off his magic cap of disguise and took on something of his noble appearance once more, for now he meant to let those who looked at him see that he was a knight. So when he stood before the rich merchant in his private room, and the merchant looked up into his face, the merchant did not know what to think at seeing so noble a lord dressed all in frieze, the coarse woolen cloth of working people. For though King Arthur was a stranger to the good man, so that he did not know his face, the merchant could tell that he was no ordinary knight, but surely someone of high rank and authority, even though he was dressed in frieze.

Then King Arthur opened the front of his jerkin and showed the merchant the gold collar that hung around his neck. He also showed him that under the rough frieze coat he wore an undergarment of fine purple silk embroidered with gold. And then he showed the good man his own signet ring, and when the merchant saw it, he knew it for the ring of the King of Britain. So, seeing these signs of high and lordly authority, the merchant rose and stood before the King and took off his cap.

"Sir Merchant," said the King, "you should know that I am a stranger knight here in disguise. Even so, I can tell you that I am a very good friend to King Leodegrance and wish him very well. You surely know how the Duke of North Umber rides up and down continually before the King's castle, and challenges anyone inside to come out and fight him for the Lady Guinevere. Now I have a mind to try that combat myself, and I very much hope that I will succeed in upholding the honor of Cameliard and in bringing shame on its enemies.

"Sir Merchant, I know very well that you have several suits of noble armor in your treasury, for their fame has reached my ears even though I live a good distance from here. So I want you to equip me as well as you are able, so that I can go at once and try a bout of arms with that Duke of North Umber. And I pledge you my knightly word that you shall be fully paid for the best suit of armor you can let me have, and that within a very little while."

"My Lord," said Master Ralph, "I can see that you are no ordinary wandering knight, but someone of very high rank; so it is a great pleasure to carry out all your wishes. But even if you were other than you are, I would be entirely willing to equip you with armor, since you have a mind to ride out against that duke over there."

At this he rang a little silver bell that stood near him, and at its sound several attendants appeared at once. Into their hands he gave the King, telling them to do him extraordinary honor. So some of those attendants prepared a bath of warm water for the King, perfumed with ambergris and very pleasant to the body. And after he had bathed in it and been dried with soft linen towels, other attendants led him to a hall all hung with tapestries and embroideries, and there a noble feast had been spread ready for his refreshment. Here the lordly merchant himself waited on the King, serving him with meats of several kinds, all very delicate, and with fine white bread as well. And he poured him wines from many countries, some as red as rubies and others as yellow as gold. Indeed, the King had hardly ever enjoyed a better feast than the one that the merchant Ralph of Cardiff had spread for him.

King Arthur is armed by Ralph of Cardiff.

And after he had refreshed himself fully with eating, six pages came, richly dressed in fine azure silk, and they took the King to a very grand room and there dressed him in a suit of Spanish armor, most skillfully made and all inlaid with gold. Armor like it could hardly be found anywhere in the land. The jupon, the cloth tunic worn over the armor, and all the other trappings of the armor were of satin as white as milk. And the shield was white, with no coat of arms or emblem of any sort upon it. Then these attendants led the King into the courtyard, and there stood a noble war-horse, as white as milk. All the trappings of the horse were of milk-white cloth, with no emblem or ornament of any kind, and the bridle and the reins were studded all over with knobs of silver.

Then, after the attendants had helped King Arthur mount this horse, the lordly merchant came forward and gave him many words of good cheer, and the King said farewell to him and rode away, all shining in white and glittering in his fine armor, so that he looked like the full moon at harvest time.

And as he rode down the stony streets of the town, the people turned and gazed after him, for he made a very noble sight as he passed along the narrow way between the houses.

So King Arthur made his way to the postern gate of the castle, and when he reached it he dismounted and tied up his horse. Then he went straight into the garden, and finding an attendant there, he asked to speak with the Lady Guinevere at once. The attendant, amazed at his lordly bearing, went and delivered the message. And after a while the Lady Guinevere came, wondering greatly, and walked along a gallery with several of her damsels until she stood above the place where King Arthur was. And when King Arthur looked up and saw her above him, he loved her with all his heart. And he said to her: "Lady, I have a great wish to do you whatever honor I can. For I am going out now to fight that Duke of North Umber who rides up and down before this castle. And I hope, and truly believe, that I shall bring about his downfall. So I beg of you some token, such as a lady may give a knight to wear when he rides out to do her honor."

The Lady Guinevere accepts King Arthur as her champion.

Then the Lady Guinevere said: "Truly, Sir Knight, I wish I knew who you are. Yet, though I do not know, I am entirely willing to take you for my champion, as you offer. So, as for the token you speak of, if you will tell me what it is you want, I will gladly give it to you."

"If that is so, Lady," said King Arthur, "I would very much like to have the necklace you wear around your throat. For it seems to me that if I had it tied around my arm, I would find my courage greatly increased by it."

"Indeed, Sir Knight," said the Lady, "what you ask of me you shall certainly have." And as she spoke, she took from her long, smooth neck the necklace of pearls she was wearing, and dropped it down to King Arthur where he stood.

And King Arthur took the necklace and tied it around his arm, and thanked her warmly for it. Then he saluted the Lady Guinevere with very knightly grace, and she saluted him in return. And then he went straight away from that place, swelling with joy that the Lady Guinevere had shown him such favor.

Now word had gone around Cameliard that a knight was going out to fight the Duke of North Umber. So great crowds gathered on the walls, and King Leodegrance and the Lady Guinevere and the whole Court of the King came to the part of the castle walls that overlooked the meadow where the Duke of North Umber held the field. So huge a crowd was soon gathered that any knight might be encouraged to do his very best before such a multitude as looked down on that field.

Then suddenly the portcullis of the castle was raised and the drawbridge let down, and the White Champion rode out to the combat he had undertaken. And as he rode across that narrow bridge, the hoofs of his war-horse struck the planks with a noise like thunder. And when he came out into the sunlight, his armor suddenly blazed like lightning, and when the people saw him they shouted aloud.

Then, when the Duke of North Umber saw a knight all clad in white, he rode straight to him and spoke to him with words of knightly greeting. "Sir," he said, "I see that you wear no crest on your helmet and have no emblem of any sort on your shield, so I do not know who you are. Even so, I believe that you are a knight of good rank and proven courage, or else you would not have come here like this."

"Truly, Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "I am of a rank equal to your own. And as for my courage, I believe it has been proven in as many combats as yours has."

"Sir Knight," said the Duke of North Umber, "you speak with a very large spirit. Even so, you had better say whatever prayers you can, for I am about to throw you down from your saddle so hard that you will never get up again. That is how I have served better men than you can ever hope to be."

To this King Arthur answered very calmly: "That will be as Heaven wills, Sir Knight, and not as you will."

King Arthur overthrows Duke Mordaunt.

So each knight saluted the other and rode to his appointed place, and there each set his spear and his shield and made himself ready for the charge. Then a silence fell over everyone, so deep that a man could hear his own heart beating in the stillness. For a little while each knight sat like a statue made of iron. Then, suddenly, each shouted to his war-horse and drove his spurs into its flanks and launched himself forward from his place. And so they met in the middle of the course with a noise like a violent clap of thunder. And the spear of the Duke of North Umber burst into splinters right down to the handle. But King Arthur's spear did not break; it held, so that the Duke was hurled out of his saddle like a windmill, whirling through the air and striking the earth so hard that the ground shuddered beneath him. Indeed, he rolled over and over a full three times before he stopped falling.

Then all the people on the wall shouted with all their might, so that the noise of it was altogether astonishing; for they had hardly dared to hope that their champion would prove so extraordinarily strong and skillful.

Meanwhile, the men of King Rience's Court ran at once to the Duke of North Umber where he lay on the ground, and they quickly unlaced his helmet to give him air. At first they thought he was dead, and then they thought he was likely to die, for he lay without any life or motion at all. Nor did he come out of the faint in which he lay for a full two hours and more.

Now while the attendants were busy around Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, King Arthur sat on his horse very quietly, watching everything they did. Then, seeing that his enemy was not dead, he turned around and rode away from that place.

He did not go back into Cameliard at that time. For he judged that he was not yet entirely finished with these enemies to the peace of his realm, and so he meant not to return the horse and the armor to the merchant just yet, but to keep them a while for another occasion.

So he remembered how, on his way to Cameliard, he had passed through an arm of the forest where some woodcutters were at work felling trees. And, remembering that place, he thought he would go there and leave his horse and armor in the care of those simple country folk until he needed them again. So now he rode away into the countryside, leaving behind him the town and the castle and all the noise of shouting and rejoicing. And he did not once so much as turn his head to look back at the place where he had so violently overthrown his enemy.

And now you will soon hear of some pleasant and very joyous adventures that happened to him before he had accomplished all his purposes. For when a man is a king among men, as King Arthur was, he is of so calm and even a temper that neither victory nor defeat can make him either puffed up in his own opinion or so troubled in spirit that he is cast down into despair. So if you want to become like King Arthur, you will take all your triumphs as he took this victory. You will not be turned aside from your final purpose by the great applause that many people may give you, but you will first finish the work you have set yourself to do before you let yourself sit down at ease and enjoy the fruits of your victory.

Yes, he who is a true king of men will not say to himself, "Look! I am worthy to be crowned with laurels." Instead he will say to himself, "What more is there that I can do to make the world better because of my efforts?"

Chapter 3: How King Arthur Met Four Knights, and What Came of It

Now the day was wonderfully sweet and pleasant to someone as strong of body and as light of heart as good King Arthur. For the bright clouds sailed smoothly across the blue sky in enormous heaps of vapor, and the wind blew across the long grass of the meadowlands and across the fields of growing wheat, so that a multitude of waves rolled over the hills and valleys as if over a whole sea of green. One moment the whole earth would be darkened by the wide shadows of those clouds, and the next, everything would suddenly burst out again into a wonderful radiance of sunlight. The little birds sang merrily in the hedgerows and the leafy thickets as though they would burst their tiny throats with singing, and the rooster crowed, loud and lusty, from the farmyard. Everything was so bright and lovely that the young King, with the visor of his helmet raised to let in the cool of the gentle breeze, would sometimes sing out very joyfully as he went along. So King Arthur traveled in that gay and tender summer season, when the earth was young, in the days of long ago.

Now you must remember that when King Arthur came from Caerleon to the castle of Tintagalon, he brought four young knights with him to keep him company. These were the knights: there was Sir Gawain, the son of King Lot and Queen Margaise; and there was Sir Uwain, the son of King Uriens and Queen Morgan le Fay (and these two were the King's nephews, by his half sisters); and there was Sir Pelleas; and there was Sir Geraint, the son of Erbin. These were the four noble young knights who had come with King Arthur from Camelot to Tintagalon.

King Arthur comes upon a knight entertaining the ladies in green.

Now it happened, as King Arthur rode along so merrily in the summertime, that he came to a certain part of the road where he saw before him a tall and handsome tower standing on a green hillock right beside the road. And there on the balcony of that tower stood three lovely damsels, dressed all in green taffeta. And on the high road in front of the castle there was a knight in very fine armor. The knight sat on a noble war-horse, and in his hands he held a lute, and he played on the lute and sang in a voice of extraordinary sweetness. While he sang, those three ladies in green taffeta listened to him with very cheerful faces. And whenever the knight stopped singing, the three ladies would clap their hands with great applause and beg him to sing to them again; and he would do so very readily.

King Arthur saw all this, and it seemed to him a very pleasant sight, so that he was greatly delighted by it.

And as he drew near, he saw that the knight who sat on his horse there, playing the lute and singing to it, was none other than Sir Geraint, the son of Erbin. For that knight wore the figure of a gryphon on his crest, and the emblem on his shield was two gryphons rearing up and facing each other on a blue field, and King Arthur knew that this was the crest and the emblem of Sir Geraint. And when the King saw who the knight was who sat there singing, he laughed to himself, and at once closed his visor and made himself ready for whatever encounter might happen. So he drew near to where the knight sang and the ladies listened.

Now when Sir Geraint saw King Arthur coming, he stopped singing and hung his lute behind him across his shoulder. Then, looking up at the three lovely ladies above him, he said: "Ladies, you have been pleased to listen to the songs I have attempted entirely in your honor. Now, also in your honor, I will perform a deed of knightly prowess, which I very much hope will bring you great glory. For if you will be pleased to lend me the encouragement that your great beauty can so easily give, you will see me, I have no doubt, completely overthrow that knight over there, and all to your great credit and renown."

"Sir Knight," said the lady who spoke for the others, "you are truly a lord of noble bearing and very pleasing manners, and so we wish you great success in this undertaking. And we believe that you will succeed in what you are attempting."

At this Sir Geraint thanked the three damsels warmly for their words, and then he closed the visor of his helmet. And so, setting his spear and his shield, and saluting the three ladies very humbly, he rode out to meet King Arthur, who now sat a little way off, waiting very quietly and soberly for him to be ready.

Now Sir Geraint did not know King Arthur, because he wore no crest on his helmet and no emblem on his shield. So, as he saluted him, he spoke to him like this: "Ha! Sir, I do not know who you are, since you bear neither crest nor emblem. Even so, I have a mind to do you what honor I can by riding a tilt against you on behalf of those three damsels you see up there on that balcony. For I declare, and I am ready to prove it with my own knightly body, that those ladies are lovelier than your lady, whoever she may be."

"Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "I will gladly ride a course against you in honor of my lady. For I may tell you that she is a princess, and many people hold her to be the most beautiful lady in all the world. But I will fight you on one condition only, and the condition is this: that whoever is overthrown shall give himself as servant to the other for seven days, and in that time he shall do whatever is asked of him."

"I accept your challenge, Sir Unknown Knight," said Sir Geraint, "and when I have overthrown you, I will hand you over to those lovely ladies up there to be their servant for seven days. And I tell you that a great many knights would certainly think that both a pleasant and an honorable task."

"And if I should happen to overthrow you," said King Arthur, "I will send you to serve my lady for the same length of time, and that will be an even pleasanter and more honorable task than the one you have in mind for me."

King Arthur overthrows Sir Geraint.

So each knight saluted the other, and then each took up a position that would bring the encounter right below where the three lovely damsels looked down from the balcony. Then each knight set his spear and his shield, and when he was ready for the charge, each sat for a little while fully prepared. Then each shouted to his war-horse and drove his spurs into its flank and launched forward at wonderful speed to the attack. So they met in the very middle of the course with such fury that the noise of it was dreadful to hear. Each knight struck the other in the very center of his guard. And the spear of Sir Geraint burst into small pieces, right down to the handle; but King Arthur's spear held, and Sir Geraint was flung backward so violently that both he and his horse were thrown down into the dust with a din like a monstrous roar of thunder.

When Sir Geraint got back on his feet, he was so dazed for a while that he did not know where he stood, for he had never been thrown like that in all his life before. Then, quickly coming to himself again, he drew his sword at once and called on King Arthur, very hotly, to get down out of his saddle and fight him on foot.

"No, not so, Sir Knight," said King Arthur. "I will not fight you that way. Besides, you must not forget that you promised to give yourself to me as my servant for seven days. For I have certainly beaten you fairly in this encounter, and now you are pledged to be my servant."

Then Sir Geraint did not know what to say, for he was utterly ashamed and vexed at being overthrown. Even so, he saw that he must keep his knightly word and do what he had pledged himself to do. So he put up his sword again, though very unwillingly. "Sir Knight," he said, "I admit that I have been beaten in this encounter, and so I now yield myself to your commands, as I gave my word."

King Arthur sends Sir Geraint to the Lady Guinevere.

"Then here are my commands," said King Arthur. "My command is that you go at once to the Lady Guinevere at Cameliard, and that you tell her you have been overthrown by the knight to whom she gave her necklace as a token. And I also want you to obey her in everything she may command you to do, for the next seven days."

"Sir Knight," said Sir Geraint, "what you tell me to do, I will do just as you command."

Then he mounted his horse and went his way. And King Arthur went his way. And the three ladies who stood on the balcony of the castle were very glad that they had seen so noble a contest of arms as the one they had looked down on.

Now after King Arthur had traveled on for two or three leagues or more, he came to a stretch of moorland where there were many ditches of water, and where the heron and the marsh hen took shelter in the reeds. Here and there stood several windmills, their sails all turning slowly in the sunlight before a wind that blew across the flat plains of mud. And at this place there was a long, straight causeway, with two long rows of pollard willows, one on either side. Now when he had come near the middle of this causeway, King Arthur saw two knights sitting on their horses in the shade of a great windmill that stood on one side of the road. A huge shadow of the sails swept again and again across the road as the wheel of the mill turned slowly before the wind. And all around the mill, and everywhere about, there were great numbers of swallows darting this way and that, like bees around a hive in midsummer. And King Arthur saw that those two knights, as they sat in the shadow of the mill, were eating a great loaf of rye bread, fresh baked, with a crisp crust; and they were eating good white cheese, which the miller, all white with flour dust, was serving them. But when the two knights saw King Arthur, they stopped eating their bread and cheese at once and quickly closed their helmets. As for the miller, when he saw them getting ready like this, he hurried back into the mill and shut the door, and then went and looked out of a window right above where the knights were standing.

King Arthur comes upon two knights at the windmill.

But King Arthur was very merry to himself when he saw that those two knights were Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain. He knew that the one was Sir Gawain because the crest of his helmet was a leopard rearing up, and because he bore on his shield the emblem of a leopard rearing up on a red field. And he knew that the other was Sir Uwain, because he bore a unicorn on his crest, and because the emblem on his shield was a lady holding a naked sword in her hand, on a field of gold. So, while he was still some distance off, King Arthur closed his helmet so that those two young knights would not know who he was.

So when he had come close to the two knights, Sir Gawain rode forward a little way to meet him. "Sir Knight," he said, "you should know that this is truly dangerous ground you have ventured onto. For there is no side path from here across the marsh, and you cannot go forward without riding a tilt against me."

"Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "I am very willing to ride a tilt against you. Even so, I will only fight you on one condition, and it is this: that whoever is overthrown shall serve the other in everything for seven full days."

"I accept your challenge, Sir Knight," said Sir Gawain. For he said to himself, "Surely so very strong and skillful a knight as I am will easily bring about the overthrow of this unknown knight."

King Arthur overthrows Sir Gawain.

So each knight at once took his appointed place. And when each had set his spear and his shield, and prepared himself fully in every way, and rested for a little while, each suddenly shouted to his horse and drove his spurs into its flanks, and so they rushed to the encounter. Each knight struck the other in the middle of his guard, and the spear of Sir Gawain burst into fragments. But King Arthur's spear held, so that Sir Gawain was lifted clean out of his saddle and over the hindquarters of his horse. And indeed he fell into the dust with tremendous force. Nor could he get up at once after that fall, but lay all dazed for a little while. And when he did get up, he saw that the white knight who had overthrown him was sitting on his horse close beside him.

Then King Arthur spoke and said: "Sir Knight, I have overthrown you completely, and so now you must serve me according to your knightly word."

Then Sir Uwain, who sat on his horse nearby, spoke up. "Not so, Sir Knight," he said. "Not so, not until you have dealt with me. For I demand that you joust with me at once. And if I overthrow you, I will claim from you that you release my cousin from the service he has pledged himself to. But if you overthrow me, then I will serve you, just as he has pledged himself to serve you."

"Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "I accept your challenge with all my heart!"

King Arthur overthrows Sir Uwain.

So each knight took his appointed place and made himself ready for the encounter. Then they shouted and drove together, rushing at each other like two rams on a hillside. And the spear of Sir Uwain also shattered into pieces. But King Arthur's spear held, so that the girths of Sir Uwain's saddle burst apart, and both saddle and knight were swept off the horse's back with such violence that a falling tower could not have made a greater noise than Sir Uwain made when he struck the dust of that causeway.

King Arthur sends the two knights to the Lady Guinevere.

Then Sir Uwain got to his feet and stared at him, utterly amazed. King Arthur came up to him and spoke to him like this: "Ha, Sir Knight, it seems to me that you have been fairly beaten today. And so, by your promises, both you and that other knight there must carry out all my commands for seven full days to come. Now this is the command I lay on you both: that you go at once to the Lady Guinevere at Cameliard and bring her greetings from her knight. And you shall tell her that her knight, the one to whom she gave her necklace, has sent you, who are the sons of kings, to obey her. And whatever she commands you to do in the seven days to come, you shall carry out to the smallest grain."

"Sir Knight," said Sir Gawain, "we will do as you command, since we have pledged ourselves to it. But when these seven days have passed, I vow that I will seek you out and fight this combat out to the very end. For any knight may happen to be unhorsed, as I have been; yet I believe I may have better success against you if I fight you with every ounce of my strength."

"Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "it shall be just as you wish. Yet I truly believe that when these seven days have passed you will not have so great a desire to fight me as you have now."

When he had said this, King Arthur saluted the two knights and they saluted him. Then he turned his horse and went his way. And whenever he thought of how those two good knights had fallen before his charge, and of how astonished and ashamed they had been at their overthrow, he laughed aloud for pure fun, and told himself that he had never in all his life had so joyous an adventure as this.

So when Sir Uwain had mended the girths of his saddle, he and Sir Gawain mounted their horses and set off toward Cameliard in very low spirits.

Then the miller came out of the mill once more, delighted to have watched such a wonderfully knightly encounter from so safe a place.

And so King Arthur rode on, well content in his mind, until the afternoon began to slant toward evening; and by that time he had come near the arm of the forest that he had in mind as the right place to leave his horse and his armor.

Now as he drew near this edge of the forest, he saw before him by the roadside a gnarled and stunted oak tree. And he saw that a shield hung on the oak tree, and that beneath the shield these words were written in fine large letters:

Whoever strikes this shield does so at the peril of his body.

Then King Arthur was filled with a great spirit, and, lifting his spear, he struck that shield so that it rang like thunder.

King Arthur strikes the shield of the White Knight.

At once King Arthur heard a voice come out of the forest, crying, "Who has dared to strike my shield?" And straightaway a knight of large build came riding out of the forest on a white horse, like the one King Arthur himself was riding. And the trappings of the horse and the knight were all white, like those of King Arthur and his horse. The knight wore on his helmet, for his crest, a swan with outspread wings, and on his shield he bore three swans on a silver field. And by the crest and the arms on the shield, King Arthur knew that this knight was Sir Pelleas, who had come with him from Camelot to Tintagalon.

So when Sir Pelleas had come near to where King Arthur waited for him, he reined in his horse and spoke to him in a very stern voice. "Ho! Ho! Sir Knight," he said. "Why did you dare to strike my shield? Truly, that blow will bring you great danger and grief. Now get ready to defend yourself at once for what you have done."

"Wait! Wait, Sir Knight," said King Arthur. "It shall be as you wish, and I will fight you. Yet I will not try this adventure until you have agreed that the knight who is beaten in the encounter shall serve the other in whatever way that other may wish, for one week from today."

"Sir Knight," said Sir Pelleas, "I accept that risk. So now I bid you get ready at once for the encounter."

King Arthur overthrows Sir Pelleas.

Then each knight took his place and set his spear and shield. And when they were ready, they launched at each other at once with the force of two stones hurled from a catapult. So they met in the middle of the course, and once again King Arthur was entirely successful in his charge. For the spear of Sir Pelleas burst to pieces, and King Arthur's spear held; and Sir Pelleas was hurled out of his saddle with tremendous force, landing more than half a spear's length behind the hindquarters of his horse. Nor did he fully recover from that fall for a long time, so that King Arthur had to wait beside him for quite a while before he was able to lift himself up from the ground where he lay.

King Arthur sends Sir Pelleas to the Lady Guinevere.

"Ha! Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "it certainly has not gone well with you today. For you have been completely overthrown, and now you must at once make good your pledge to serve me for the next seven days. So this is my command to you: that you go at once to Cameliard and greet the Lady Guinevere from me, telling her that her knight, the one to whom she gave her necklace, has been victorious in battle against you. And I also command that you obey her for seven days in whatever she may command you to do."

"Sir Knight," said Sir Pelleas, "it shall be just as you order. Yet I wish I knew who you are, for I declare that I have never in all my life been overthrown as you have overthrown me. And indeed I think there are very few men in the world who could treat me the way you have treated me."

"Sir Knight," said King Arthur, "someday you shall know who I am. But for now I am bound to complete secrecy."

At that he saluted Sir Pelleas, and turned and rode into the forest and was gone.

And Sir Pelleas mounted his horse and made his way to Cameliard, very downcast and troubled in spirit, and yet wondering greatly who that knight could be who had treated him the way he had been treated.

The Lady Guinevere is pleased with her champion.

So that day there came to Cameliard, first Sir Geraint, and then Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain, and last of all Sir Pelleas. And when these four saw one another, they were all so ashamed that none of them dared look another in the face. And when they came before the Lady Guinevere and told her their situation, and told her how the knight who wore her necklace had overthrown them all and sent them there to serve her for a week, and when she considered how great and famous those four knights were in deeds of chivalry, she was filled with great pride that her knight had proven himself so mighty in the feats of arms he had undertaken. But she wondered greatly who that champion could be, and turned the question over and over in her mind. For it was a thing completely unheard of that one knight, in one day, with a single spear, should have overthrown five such well-proven and famous knights as Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, Sir Geraint, Sir Gawain, Sir Uwain, and Sir Pelleas. So she rejoiced greatly that she had given her necklace to so worthy a knight, and she was lifted up with extraordinary pleasure at the thought of the honor he had brought her.

King Arthur takes up his disguise again.

Now after King Arthur had entered the forest, he came after a while to the place where the woodcutters we spoke of earlier were at their work. He stayed with them that night, and when the next morning came, he left his horse and armor in their care, charging them to guard those things with every care and promising that they would be wonderfully rewarded for it. Then he set off from that place, meaning to return to Cameliard. And he was dressed in the frieze jerkin he had worn ever since he left Tintagalon.

And when he reached the edge of the forest, he put his cap of disguise on his head and so took on his humble appearance once more. So, with all his knightliness completely hidden, he went back to Cameliard to be the gardener's boy, just as he had been before.

Chapter 4: How the Four Knights Served the Lady Guinevere

The gardener scolds his boy.

Now when King Arthur came back to Cameliard once more (which was in the afternoon of the second day), he found the gardener waiting for him, and the gardener was filled with fury. He had brought a long birch rod with him, meaning to punish his boy with it as soon as he came back to the garden. So when he saw King Arthur he said: "You good-for-nothing! Why did you leave your work to go gadding about?" And King Arthur laughed and said: "Do not touch me." At this the gardener grew so angry that he caught the King by the collar of his jerkin, meaning to beat him, and said: "Do you laugh at me, you rascal, and make fun of me? Now I'll give you a good beating for the wrong you've done."

Then, when King Arthur felt that man's hand laid on him, and when he heard the words the gardener spoke in his anger, his royal spirit swelled up within him, and he cried out: "Ha, wretch! Would you dare to lay your hands upon my sacred person?" And as he said this, he seized the gardener by the wrists, took the rod straight out of his hand, and struck him with it across the shoulders. And when that poor fellow felt himself caught in the powerful grip of the angry King, and felt the rod across his shoulders, he at once set up a great outcry, although the blow did not hurt him in the least. "Now get out of here!" said King Arthur, "and trouble me no more, or I will treat you in a way you will not like at all." With that he let the poor man go; and the gardener was so dazed with terror that the earth and the sky both swam before his eyes. For King Arthur's eyes had flashed at him like lightning, and those two hands had held his wrists with wonderful strength. So, when the King let him go, he took himself off as fast as he could, trembling all over and sweating with fear.

The gardener complains to the Lady Guinevere.

So he went straight to the Lady Guinevere and complained to her of the way he had been treated. "Lady," he said, weeping at the memory of his fright, "my boy goes off for a day or more, I don't know where; and when I try to whip him for leaving his work, he takes the rod right out of my hand and beats me with it. So now, I beg you, deal with him as he deserves, and have some strong men drive him out of this place with sticks."

Then the Lady Guinevere laughed. "Let him be!" she said. "Don't meddle with him any more; for indeed he seems to be a very saucy fellow. As for you, pay no attention to his coming or his going, and perhaps I will deal with him myself in whatever way is fitting."

So the gardener went his way, greatly wondering that the Lady Guinevere should be so mild with that unruly boy. And the Lady Guinevere went her way, very merry. For she had begun to think that there must truly be some excellent reason why, whenever the White Champion who did such wonderful deeds came to Cameliard, the gardener's boy should be gone; and why, whenever that same Champion went away, the gardener's boy should come back again. So she suspected a great many things, and was wonderfully merry and cheerful in her spirits.

The Lady Guinevere mocks the gardener's boy.

Now that same afternoon the Lady Guinevere happened to walk in the garden with her damsels, and with her walked those four noble knights whom her White Champion had sent to her: that is, Sir Gawain, Sir Uwain, Sir Geraint, and Sir Pelleas. And the gardener's lad was digging in the garden; and as they passed by the place where he was, the Lady Guinevere laughed out loud and cried: "Look! Look, Sirs and Ladies! There is a very saucy fellow for a gardener's lad, for he always wears his cap, even when he stands in the presence of lords and ladies."

Then Sir Gawain spoke up and said: "Is that so? Then I will go to that rascal this minute and take his hat off for him, and in a way he will like so little that I don't believe he will ever offend by wearing it in our presence again."

At this the Lady Guinevere laughed a great deal. "Let him be!" she said. "Let him be, Sir Gawain! It would not become a knight as noble as you to have anything to do with that saucy fellow. Besides, he assures us all that he has an ugly spot on his head, so let him wear his cap, in God's mercy."

The Duke of North Umber issues a second challenge.

So the Lady Guinevere, though she suspected a very great deal, was still pleased to make fun of the one she suspected.

Now by that day Duke Mordaunt of North Umber had entirely recovered from the painful hurts he had suffered when the White Champion overthrew him. So, when the next morning came, he appeared before the castle again as he had appeared before, clad from head to foot in armor. But this time two heralds rode before him, and when the Duke and the two heralds had come to that part of the meadows that lay just in front of the castle of Cameliard, the heralds blew their trumpets very loudly. At the sound of the trumpets many people came and gathered on the walls; and King Leodegrance came too, and took his stand on a lesser tower that looked down on the plain where the Duke of North Umber and the two heralds were. Then the Duke of North Umber lifted his eyes and saw King Leodegrance standing above him on the top of that tower, and he cried out in a loud voice: "Ho there, King Leodegrance! Do not think that because I was thrown from my horse by a piece of bad luck in a contest of arms, you are therefore rid of me. All the same, I now make you this fair offer. Tomorrow I shall appear before this castle with six knights as my companions. Now, if you have any seven knights who are able to stand against me and my companions in a contest of arms, whether with spears or with swords, on horseback or on foot, then I will bind myself to give up every claim whatever to the hand of the Lady Guinevere. But if you cannot provide such champions to fight successfully against me and my companions, then I will not only lay claim to the Lady Guinevere, but I will also seize, and hold as my own, three certain castles of yours that stand on the borders of North Umber. And I will likewise seize, and hold as my own, all the lands and fields that belong to those same castles. What is more, this challenge of mine will hold only until sunset tomorrow; after that it will be null and void. So, King Leodegrance, you had best see to it at once that you find such champions as can defend you against these demands."

King Leodegrance is downcast.

At this the two heralds blew their trumpets once more, and Duke Mordaunt of North Umber turned his horse around and rode away from that place. Then King Leodegrance also went his way, very sorrowful and downcast in his spirits. For he said to himself: "Is it at all likely that another champion will come to me, like that wonderful White Champion who came two days ago, I don't know from where, to defend me against my enemies? And as for that same White Champion: just as I do not know where he came from, so I do not know where he has gone. How then shall I know where to look for him, to beg his help again in this time of my great need?" So he went his way, very sorrowful, not knowing what to do to defend himself. And being so deeply troubled in his spirit, he went straight to his own room and shut himself in; and he would see no one and speak to no one, but gave himself up entirely to sorrow and despair.

The Lady Guinevere begs the four knights for help.

Now in this time of need the Lady Guinevere thought of those four knights who had been pledged to serve her for seven days. So she went to them where they were and spoke to them like this: "Sirs, you were sent here pledged to serve me for seven days. Now I command you to take up this challenge of Duke Mordaunt's at my bidding, and I very much wish you to go out tomorrow to meet this Duke of North Umber and his companions in battle. For you are tremendously mighty knights, and I believe you can easily defend us against our enemies."

But Sir Gawain said, "No, Lady; no! For though we are pledged to your service, we are not pledged to the service of King Leodegrance, your father. Nor do we have any quarrel of any kind with this Duke of North Umber, or with his six companions. For we are knights of King Arthur's Court, and we may not, except at his command, take up any foreign quarrel in the service of another king."

Then the Lady Guinevere was very angry, and she said with great heat: "Either you are a wonderfully faithful lord to your King, Sir Gawain, or else you are afraid to meet this Duke of North Umber and his companions."

And at this speech of the Lady Guinevere's, Sir Gawain was very angry too, and he answered: "If you were a knight and not a lady, Dame Guinevere, you would think three or four times before you found the courage to say those words to me." With that he got up and left the place, his face all burning with anger. And the Lady Guinevere also left that place and went to her bower, her own private rooms, where she wept a great deal, both from sorrow and from anger.

King Arthur sends the gardener on an errand.

Now all this while King Arthur had known very well everything that happened; so by and by he got up and went out and found the gardener. And he took the gardener firmly by the collar of his coat and held him where he stood. And he said to him: "You there! I have an order to give you, and you will carry out that order to the letter. If you do not, a great deal of pain may come to you." And as he spoke, he thrust his hand into the front of his jerkin and drew out the necklace of pearls that the Lady Guinevere had taken from her own neck and given him. And he went on to the gardener: "You will take this necklace to the Lady Guinevere, and you will say this to her: that she is to send me at once bread and meat and wine and sweets from her own table. And you will tell her that I wish her to call those four knights, that is, Sir Gawain, Sir Uwain, Sir Geraint, and Sir Pelleas, and that she is to order those four to come and serve me with those things for my refreshment. And you are to tell her that she must lay her commands on those knights that they are to go on serving me in whatever I may command, and that from now on they are to be my servants and not hers. These are the orders I lay on you: that you are to say these things to the Lady Guinevere."

Now when the gardener heard these words he was so astonished that he did not know what to think, for he believed the gardener's lad had gone completely mad. So he raised his voice and cried out loud, "What's this? What are you saying? Truly, if I did such a thing as you tell me to do, either it would cost me my life or else it would cost you yours. For who would dare to say such words to the Lady Guinevere?"

But King Arthur said: "All the same, you will certainly do as I command you, fellow. For if you disobey in a single point, I promise you it will go very badly with you. For I have it in my power to make you suffer as you have never suffered before."

At this the gardener said, "I will go." For he said to himself, "If I do as this fellow tells me, then the Lady Guinevere will have him punished severely, and so I will be revenged on him for what he did to me yesterday. Besides, it annoys me terribly to have a lad working in the garden who behaves the way this fellow does. So," he said, "I will go." So he took the necklace of pearls that King Arthur gave him, and he went off, and after a while he found the Lady Guinevere where she was. And when he had found her, he spoke to her like this:

"Lady, my garden boy has certainly gone completely mad. For, with threats of some great harm he would do me if I did not carry out his errand, he has sent me to offer you a very grievous insult. He has sent me with this string of big beads to give to you; and he tells me to tell you that you are to send him bread and meat and sweets and wine, such as you have at your own table; and he tells me to tell you that these things are to be served to him by the four noble knights who came here the day before yesterday. And he says you are to command those same knights to obey him in whatever he may command, because from now on they are to be his servants and not yours. And truly, Lady, he would not listen to a word I said to him against it, but he threatened me with terrible harm if I did not come here and give you this message."

Now when the Lady Guinevere heard what the gardener said, and when she saw the necklace she had given to the White Champion, and when she knew that the White Champion and the gardener's boy were indeed one and the same, she was lifted up with such joy that she did not know whether to laugh or to cry for pure happiness. So she rose and took the necklace of pearls, and she told the gardener to come with her. Then she went on until she found those four knights, and when she had found them she spoke to them like this:

The Lady Guinevere commands the four knights to serve the gardener's boy.

"My lords, a little while ago, when I commanded you to take up my quarrel with Duke Mordaunt of North Umber for my sake, you would not do it. And you, my lord Gawain, spoke such angry words as it is not fitting for one who serves to speak to his mistress, much less for a knight to speak to the daughter of a king. So I have decided that you shall do a certain thing as a penance; and if you refuse to do it, I will know very well that you do not mean to keep the word you pledged to my knight when he overthrew all four of you in fair combat. Now this is my command: that you take certain food prepared for my table, meats and white bread and sweets and wine, and that you carry that food to my gardener's boy, whose cap, Sir Gawain, you threatened so bravely to take away from him this very morning. And you four are to serve the food to him as though he were a royal knight. And when you have served him, you are to obey him in whatever he may order. I lay this on you as a penalty because you did not take up my quarrel as true knights should; for from now on you are to be servants to that gardener's boy and not to me. So now you are to go to the buttery of the castle, the storeroom where the food and drink are kept, and you are to tell the sewer, the officer in charge of serving the table, to give you meats such as are served at my own table. And you are to serve the food on silver plates, and the wine in silver cups and goblets. And you are to wait on that gardener's boy as though he were a great lord of the highest fame and renown."

So spoke the Lady Guinevere; and when she had finished speaking, she turned and left those four knights, and took the gardener with her. And he was so astonished at what he had heard that he did not know whether he had gone mad or the Lady Guinevere had gone mad. And the Lady Guinevere told the gardener to go to the gardener's boy and tell him that everything would be done just as he had commanded. And so the gardener did as he was told.

The four knights are angry.

Now let us turn to those four knights whom the Lady Guinevere had left behind. For they were bewildered and dumbfounded at the strange commands she had laid on them. And when they had recovered from their amazement, they were burning with such fierce indignation that for a time they could not tell whether what they saw with their eyes was the light of day or utter darkness. Not one of them could look another in the face, so overcome were they with shame at the insult that had been put on them. Then Sir Gawain spoke up, and his voice shook so with his great anger that he could hardly hold it steady enough to get his words out. "Sirs," he said, "don't you see how this lady has deliberately put a great insult on us because we would not do what she told us to do this morning, and because we would not take up her quarrel against the Duke of North Umber? Well, we will indeed serve this gardener's boy just as she has ordered. We will serve him with meat and drink as she has commanded, and we will do him service as she has told us to. But mark this: we are no longer her servants, but his servants, and so we may serve him in whatever way we choose. So, when we have carried out her commands and have served him with meat and drink, and when we have obeyed all the orders he gives us, then I swear that I, with my own hand, will kill that gardener's boy. And when I have killed him, I will put his head in a bag, and I will send that bag to the Lady Guinevere by the lowest messenger I can find for the purpose. And so this proud lady will receive an insult as great as the insult she has put on us." And they all said that what Sir Gawain had planned should be done exactly as he had said.

The four knights serve the gardener's boy.

So those four lords went to the sewer of the castle, and they asked for the best of the food that was to be served to the Lady Guinevere: meats and bread and sweets and wine. Then they took silver plates and platters and put the food on them; and they took silver cups and silver goblets and poured the wine into them; and they went out with these things. And when they had come around to the back of the castle, near the stables, they found the gardener's boy, and they told him to sit down and eat and drink. And they waited on him as though he had been some great lord. And not one of those four knights knew who he was, or that he was the great King whose servants, in truth, they were. For he wore his cap of disguise on his head, and so they took him for nothing but a poor peasant fellow.

Now when Sir Uwain saw that he still kept his cap on in front of them, he spoke to him with great indignation, saying: "Ha, you villain! Would you wear your cap even in the presence of great princes and lords like us?"

To this Sir Gawain said, "Let it be; it doesn't matter." And then he said very bitterly to the gardener's boy: "Eat well, fellow! For you will hardly eat another meal on this earth."

To this the gardener's boy answered: "Sir Knight, perhaps that will be for another will than yours to decide. For maybe I shall eat many other meals besides this one. And maybe you will serve at them as you are serving me now." And those four lords were astonished beyond measure that he should speak to them so calmly and without any sign of fear.

Then, after he had eaten, the gardener's boy said to those knights, "There, Sirs, I have had enough and I am finished; and now I have other commands for you to carry out. My next command is that you get ready at once to ride out with me, and for that purpose you are to put on full armor. And you, Sir Gawain, are to go to the head stable-keeper of this castle and order him to make ready the Lady Guinevere's palfrey, so that I can ride out on it at once. And when you are all armed, and when everything has been arranged as I command, you are to bring that palfrey to the postern gate of the castle, and there I will meet you and ride out with you."

And Sir Gawain said: "It shall be done in every way just as you command. But when we ride out of this castle, it will be a sorry journey for you."

And the gardener's boy said: "I do not think so, Sir Gawain."

Then those four went away and did as the gardener's boy had commanded. And when they had made themselves ready in full armor, and had got the Lady Guinevere's palfrey, they went to the postern gate, and there the gardener's boy met them. And when he saw that they stayed sitting on their horses and did not stir when he came, he said: "Ha, Sirs! Is this how you treat the one you have been ordered to serve? Now I order you, Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain, to get down and hold my stirrup for me; and I order you, Sir Geraint and Sir Pelleas, to get down and hold my palfrey for me while I mount."

Then those four noble knights did as they were commanded. And Sir Gawain said: "You may command as you please, and I advise you to make the most of it while you can; for you will have only a little while longer to enjoy the great honor that has fallen on you. For that honor which has fallen on you, mark my words, will soon crush you to death."

And the gardener's boy said: "Not so; I believe I shall not die just yet." And again those four lords were greatly astonished at how calm he was.

And so they rode out from that place; and the gardener's boy would not allow them to ride either in front of him or beside him, but commanded them to ride behind him, since they were still his servants.

So they rode as he had ordered them for a good long way. Then, after they had gone forward a great distance, they drew near to a gloomy and dismal woodland that lay entirely outside the country around Cameliard. And when they had come close to this woodland, Sir Gawain rode a little forward, and he said: "Sir Gardener's Boy, do you see that woodland over there? Now, when we come into it you will die at once, and by a sword that has never yet been touched by any but noble or knightly blood."

And King Arthur turned around in his saddle and said: "Ha! Sir Gawain! Would you ride forward like this, when I have ordered you to ride behind me?"

King Arthur reveals himself to the four knights.

And as he spoke he took the cap off his head, and suddenly they all saw that it was King Arthur who rode with them.

Then a great silence of pure astonishment fell on them all, and each man sat as though he had been turned into a statue of stone. And it was King Arthur who spoke first. He said: "Ha! What now, Sir Knights? Have you no words of greeting for me? Truly, you have served me with a very bad grace this day. What is more, you have threatened to kill me; and now, when I speak to you, you say nothing in reply."

Then those four knights at once cried out aloud; and they leaped down from their horses and knelt in the dust of the road. And when King Arthur saw them kneeling there, he laughed with great joy of spirit, and he told them to mount their horses again, for time was passing and there was much to do.

So they mounted their horses and rode away, and as they went along the King told them everything that had happened to him, so that they were greatly amazed, and gave high praise to the knightly way he had carried himself through all those excellent adventures. And they rejoiced greatly that they had a king to rule over them who had so high and knightly a spirit.

So they rode to that edge of the forest where King Arthur had left his horse and his armor.

Chapter 5: How King Arthur Defeated King Leodegrance's Enemies, and How His Royalty Was Made Known

The Duke of North Umber and his six companions appear before the castle.

Now, when the next day came, the Duke of North Umber and six knights who were his companions appeared on the field in front of the castle of Cameliard, just as he had declared that they would. And those seven champions appeared in very great state. In front of them rode seven heralds with trumpets and tabards, and behind them rode seven squires, each squire carrying the spear, the shield, the crest, and the banner of the knight who was his lord and master. The seven heralds blew their trumpets so tremendously loud that the sound reached the farthest corners of Cameliard, and people came running from everywhere. And while the heralds blew their trumpets, the seven squires shouted and waved the spears and the banners. So those seven knights rode in such proud state that those who watched them had hardly ever seen so splendid a show of chivalry.

So they paraded up and down that field three times, along its entire length. Meanwhile a great crowd of people, called there by the blowing of the heralds' trumpets, stood on the walls and gazed down at that noble spectacle. And all the Court of King Rience came out and stood on the plain in front of the King's pavilion, and they shouted and cheered for the Duke of North Umber and his six companions.

Meanwhile King Leodegrance of Cameliard was so cast down with trouble and shame that he did not wish to show his face, but hid himself away from his whole Court. Nor would he allow anyone to come into his presence at that time.

The Lady Guinevere cheers her father.

Nevertheless, the Lady Guinevere went to him with several of her damsels. And when the King would not let her in, she spoke to him through the door and gave him words of good cheer. "My lord King and father," she said, "I beg you to look up and take heart. For I promise you that there is one who has our cause in his hands, and that one is truly a very glorious champion. He will surely come by and by, before this day is over, and when he comes he will certainly overthrow our enemies."

But King Leodegrance did not open the door. He said: "My daughter, you say these things to comfort me. For in this time of trouble there is no help for me except God alone, and His good strong help and grace." And she said: "No, what I say is the truth. And the help that God will send you, He will certainly send through a worthy champion who at this very moment has our cause in his hand."

So spoke the Lady Guinevere. And although King Leodegrance did not come out, he was greatly comforted by what she had said to him.

Five knights-defender appear at the field.

So the whole morning went by, and part of the afternoon, and still no one appeared to take up the challenge the seven knights had declared. But while the sun was still three or four hours high, a cloud of dust suddenly appeared a great way off. And out of that cloud of dust there soon appeared five knights, riding toward the castle at great speed. When they came near the walls, the people saw that the one who rode in front of all the others was that same White Champion who had overthrown the Duke of North Umber before. They saw, too, that the four knights riding with the White Champion were very famous knights, of great prowess and glory in arms. For one was Sir Gawain, and another was Sir Uwain, and another was Sir Geraint, and another was Sir Pelleas. The people of the castle and the town knew those four knights, because they had stayed at Cameliard for two days; and they were so very renowned that folk crowded in from far and near to look at them whenever they went out walking.

So when the people on the walls saw who those knights were, and when they recognized the White Champion who had already brought them such great honor, they shouted aloud a second time, with a voice even mightier than the one they had shouted with the first time.

Now King Leodegrance heard the people shouting, and all at once hope woke up inside him. So he came out at once, as fast as he could, to see what was happening, and there he saw those five noble champions about to ride onto the field below the castle walls.

The Lady Guinevere heard the shouting too, and she came out also. And there was the White Champion, and those four other knights with him! When she saw that White Knight and his four companions-in-arms, her heart was ready to burst inside her for pure joy and gladness, so that she wept with the strength of her feeling, and laughed while she wept. She waved her handkerchief to those five noble lords and kissed her hand to them, and the five knights saluted her as they rode past her and onto the field.

Now when the Duke of North Umber learned that these five knights had come against him and his companions to take up his challenge, he came out of his pavilion at once and mounted his horse. And his companions came out and mounted their horses, and he and they rode out to meet those who had come against them.

The Duke of North Umber refuses the combat.

When the Duke of North Umber had come near enough, he saw that the leader of those five knights was the White Champion who had overthrown him before. So he said to the White Champion: "Sir Knight, I have already lowered myself once to deal with you, though you are a complete stranger to me and to everyone else here. Without asking anything about your rank, I ran a course with you, and by the luck of arms you overthrew me. Now this quarrel is more serious than that one. So my companions-in-arms and I will not run a course with you and your companions, nor will we fight with you at all, until I first know the rank of the man I am to fight. So I order you to declare yourself right now: who you are, and what your rank is."

Then Sir Gawain opened the visor of his helmet and said: "Sir Knight, look at my face, and know that I am Gawain, the son of King Lot. So you can see that my rank and station are even higher than your own. Now I declare to you that the White Knight over there is of such high rank that it is he who lowers himself when he fights with you, and not you who lower yourself to him."

"Ho, Sir Gawain!" said the Duke of North Umber. "What you say is a very strange thing, for there are truly few people in this world so exalted that they lower themselves in dealing with me. Even so, since you vouch for him, I cannot dispute what you say. But there is still another reason why we cannot fight with you. For look: we are seven tried and famous knights, and you are only five. So consider how unequal our forces are, and what great danger you are in by taking on so perilous an encounter."

Then Sir Gawain smiled very grimly at that Duke of North Umber. "Many thanks for your pity, Sir Duke," he said, "and for the tender concern you show for our safety. But even so, you may leave that matter to us, with your mind completely at ease. For I consider that the danger you seven are in is fully equal to ours. What's more, if you were anything but a belted knight, a plain man might suppose that you were more worried about your own safety in this affair than about ours."

At these words the face of the Duke of North Umber turned red all over, for he knew that in truth he had no great wish for this battle, and so he was ashamed at what Sir Gawain had said to him. Then each knight closed his helmet, and they all turned their horses. One party rode to one end of the field, and the other party rode to the other end, and there each took its stand in the place assigned to it.

They arranged themselves like this: King Arthur was in the middle, with two knights on either side of him; and the Duke of North Umber was in the middle, with three knights on either side of him. When they had lined up in this way, they set their spears and their shields and made themselves completely ready for the charge. Then King Arthur and Duke Mordaunt each gave a great shout, and the one party hurled itself against the other with such violence that the ground shook and thundered under the horses' hoofs, and the clouds of dust rose up toward the sky.

And so they met in the middle of the field with a crash so dreadful and violent that you could have heard it more than a mile away.

When the one party had passed the other, and the dust of the collision had risen, three of the seven had been overthrown, and not one of the five had lost his seat.

King Arthur overthrows the Duke of North Umber.

One of those who had been overthrown was Duke Mordaunt of North Umber. And he never got up again from the ground where he lay. For King Arthur had aimed his spear at the very center of his defenses, and the spear had held. Its point had pierced the Duke of North Umber's shield, and pierced his body armor; and the stroke was so violent that the Duke of North Umber had been lifted clean out of his saddle and hurled a full spear's length behind the tail of his horse. So that wicked man died. For as King Arthur rode past him, his evil soul left his body with a thin little noise like the squeaking of a bat, and the world was well rid of him.

Now when King Arthur turned around at the end of the course and saw that only four knights were left on their horses, out of all the seven he and his companions had charged against, he raised his spear, reined in his horse, and spoke to his knights like this: "Sirs, I am weary of all this fuss and quarreling, and I do not care to fight any more today. So go at once and do battle with those knights. As for me, I will wait here and watch your adventure."

"Lord," they said, "we will do our best, as you command."

So those four good knights did as he commanded, and they rode out at once against the other four, greatly encouraged that their King was watching what they did. And King Arthur sat with the butt of his spear resting on his foot and looked out over the field with great contentment of spirit and a steady face.

As for the four companions of the Duke of North Umber's party who were left, they did not come out to this second encounter with nearly as much eagerness as they had the first time.
