For now they knew very well how great the prowess of those other knights was, and they saw that their enemies were coming at them for this second encounter very fiercely, with great valor and eagerness. So their hearts melted away inside them with doubt and worry about how this second encounter would end.

Nevertheless, they made themselves ready with what courage they could, and came out as they were bound to do.

Then Sir Gawain rode straight at the foremost knight, a very well-known champion named Sir Dinador of Montcalm. And when he had come close enough to him, he raised himself up in his stirrups and struck Sir Dinador so fierce a blow that he split that knight's shield in two, and split his helmet, and a piece of the blade of his sword broke off and stuck fast in it.

The knights-challenger flee before the knights-defender.

When Sir Dinador felt that blow, his brains swam like water, and he had to catch hold of the horn of his saddle to keep himself from falling off. Then a great terror fell on him all at once, so that he wrenched his horse's rein to one side. And so he fled away from that place, with the terror of death hanging over him like a black cloud of smoke. And when his companions saw the stroke Sir Gawain had dealt, and saw Sir Dinador flee away from him, they too wrenched their reins to one side and fled away as fast as they could, filled with utter terror of their enemies. And Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain and Sir Geraint and Sir Pelleas chased them as they fled. They chased them straight through the Court of King Rience, so that the knights and nobles of that Court scattered this way and that like chaff before them. They chased those fleeing knights in among the pavilions of King Rience's Court, and no one stopped them. And when they had chased those knights clean away, they came back to the place where King Arthur still kept his post, steadily waiting for them.

Now when the people of Cameliard saw their enemies overthrown, and saw how those enemies fled away before the faces of their champions, they shouted with all their might and cheered and cheered. Nor did they stop their loud shouting when those four knights came back from chasing their enemies and rejoined the White Champion. And they cheered still more when those five knights rode across the drawbridge, and through the gateway of the town, and into the town itself.

So ended that great contest of arms, which was one of the most famous in all the history of chivalry of King Arthur's Court.

King Arthur returns his armor to the merchant.

Now when King Arthur had done what he had set out to do, and had come back into the town, he went to the merchant from whom he had gotten the armor he wore, and he gave that armor back to him. And he said, "Tomorrow, Sir Merchant, I will send you two bags of gold as rent for the armor you let me have."

To this the merchant said: "Lord, there is no need for you to repay me for that armor, for you have done Cameliard great honor by your prowess."

But King Arthur said: "Enough, Sir Merchant. You must not forbid what I say. So take what I will send you."

Then he went on his way, and, after setting his cap of disguise on his head, he came back into the Lady Guinevere's gardens once more.

Now when the next morning came, the people of Cameliard looked out, and saw that King Rience had gone clean away from in front of the castle. For that night he had taken down his pavilions, withdrawn his Court, and left the place where he and his people had camped for the past five days. He had taken the body of the Duke of North Umber with him, carrying it away on a litter surrounded by many lighted candles and borne along with strange and solemn ceremony. And when the people of Cameliard saw that he was gone, they were overjoyed, and made merry, and shouted and sang and laughed. For they did not know how deeply enraged King Rience was against them. His hatred of King Leodegrance before had been only a small flame compared with the anger that now possessed him.

That morning the Lady Guinevere walked into her garden, and Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain walked with her. And there she saw the gardener's boy again.

At that she laughed aloud, and said to those two knights, "Sirs, look! There is the gardener's boy, who wears his cap all the time because he has an ugly place on his head."

Then those two knights, who knew who that gardener's boy was, were terribly embarrassed at her words, and did not know what to say or where to look. And Sir Gawain spoke aside to Sir Uwain and said: "Heaven help us, that lady has no idea what kind of man that gardener's boy is. If she did, she would be more careful with her words."

The Lady Guinevere heard Sir Gawain speak, but she did not catch his words. So she turned to Sir Gawain and said: "Sir Gawain, perhaps it offends you that the gardener's boy should wear his cap in front of us, and maybe you will go and take it off his head, as you offered to do two or three days ago."

And Sir Gawain said: "Hush, Lady! You do not know what you are saying. That gardener's boy could more easily take my head off my shoulders than I could take his cap off his head."

At this the Lady Guinevere laughed openly. But in her heart she secretly turned that saying over and over, and wondered greatly what Sir Gawain meant by it.

King Rience threatens King Leodegrance.

Now about noon that day a herald came from King Rience of North Wales, and he appeared boldly before King Leodegrance where the King sat in his hall with a number of his people around him. And the herald said: "My lord King, my master, King Rience of North Wales, is greatly displeased with you. For you set certain knights upon Duke Mordaunt of North Umber, and those knights have killed that excellent nobleman, who was a close kinsman of King Rience. What is more, you have made no reply to the demands my master, King Rience, has made concerning the handing over to him of certain lands and castles on the border of North Wales. And so my master is offended with you beyond measure. My master, King Rience, therefore commands me to put two conditions before you, and these are the conditions: first, that you immediately hand over to him the White Knight who killed the Duke of North Umber; and second, that you promise at once that the lands in question will be handed over to King Rience without delay."

Then King Leodegrance rose from where he sat and spoke to the herald with great dignity. "Sir Herald," he said, "the demands King Rience makes on me pass all bounds of insolence. The death the Duke of North Umber suffered, he suffered because of his own pride and folly. Nor would I hand the White Knight over to your master, even if I were able to. As for the lands your master demands of me, you may tell King Rience that I will not hand over to him so much as a single blade of grass, or a single grain of corn that grows there."

And the herald said: "If that is your answer, King Leodegrance, then I am ordered to tell you that my master, King Rience of North Wales, will soon come here with a great army and take from you by force the things you will not give him in peace." And having said this, he left that place and went on his way.

King Leodegrance talks with the Lady Guinevere.

Now after the herald had gone, King Leodegrance went into his private room, and when he got there he sent secretly for the Lady Guinevere. So the Lady Guinevere came to him there. And King Leodegrance said to her: "My daughter, it has happened that a knight dressed all in white, and bearing no crest or sign of any kind, has twice come to our rescue and overthrown our enemies. Now everyone says that this knight is your own particular champion, and I hear that he wore your necklace as a favor when he first rode out against the Duke of North Umber. So I ask you, daughter, tell me who that White Champion is, and where he can be found."

At this the Lady Guinevere was overwhelmed with confusion, and she looked away from her father's face and said: "Truly, my Lord, I do not know who that knight may be."

Then King Leodegrance spoke very seriously to the Lady Guinevere. He took her by the hand and said: "My daughter, you have now come to the age when you must think of being married to a man who will cherish you as he should and protect you from your enemies. For I am growing old fast, and I cannot hope to defend you forever from the dangers that surround people of our rank. Besides, since King Arthur (who is a very great King indeed) has brought peace to this realm, all that noble court of chivalry that once gathered around me has scattered to other places, where greater adventures can be found than in my peaceful kingdom. So (as all the world has seen this past week) I now have not one single knight I can depend on to defend us in times of danger like these that now overshadow us. Now, my daughter, it seems to me that you could not hope to find anyone who could keep you as safe as this White Knight, for he truly seems to be a champion of extraordinary prowess and strength. So it would be well if you found your heart leaning toward him, as his seems to lean toward you."

Then the Lady Guinevere turned rosy red, as if with fire, all the way down to her throat. And she laughed, though the tears overflowed her eyes and ran down her cheeks. So she wept, and yet laughed as she wept. And she said to King Leodegrance: "My Lord and father, if I give my heart to anyone in the way you speak of, I will give it only to the poor gardener's boy who digs in my garden."

At these words King Leodegrance's face twisted up with violent anger, and he cried out: "Ha, Lady! Would you make a mockery and a joke of what I say?"

Then the Lady Guinevere said: "Indeed, my Lord, I am not joking and I am not mocking. And I tell you truly that this same gardener's boy knows more about the White Champion than anybody else in the whole world." Then King Leodegrance said: "What is this you are telling me?" And the Lady Guinevere said: "Send for that gardener's boy, and you will find out." And King Leodegrance said: "Truly, there is more in this than I can understand just now."

So he called the chief of his pages, whose name was Dorisand, and said to him: "Go, Dorisand, and bring the gardener's boy here from the Lady Guinevere's garden."

So Dorisand the page went as King Leodegrance commanded, and in a little while he came back, bringing the gardener's boy with him. And Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain and Sir Pelleas and Sir Geraint came with them. Those four lords stood across the room by the door where they had come in, but the gardener's boy came and stood beside the table where King Leodegrance sat. The King raised his eyes and looked at the gardener's boy, and he said: "Ha! Would you wear your cap in our presence?"

Then the gardener's boy said: "I cannot take off my cap."

But the Lady Guinevere, who stood beside King Leodegrance's chair, spoke up and said: "I beg you, Sir, to take off your cap to my father."

At that the gardener's boy said: "At your bidding I will take it off."

King Arthur reveals himself to King Leodegrance.

So he took the cap off his head, and King Leodegrance looked at his face and knew him. And when he saw who it was standing before him, he cried out aloud in pure amazement. "My Lord and my King!" he said. "What is this?" Then he rose from where he sat, and went and knelt on the ground before King Arthur. He put the palms of his hands together and placed his hands between the hands of King Arthur, and King Arthur took the hands of King Leodegrance in his own. And King Leodegrance said: "My Lord! My Lord! Is it you, then, who has done all these wonderful things?"

Then King Arthur said: "Yes. Such as those things were, I have done them." And he bent down and kissed King Leodegrance on the cheek, and lifted him to his feet, and gave him words of good cheer.

Now the Lady Guinevere, when she saw these things happening, was astonished beyond measure. And all at once she understood everything with amazing clearness. A great fear fell on her, so that she trembled all over and said to herself: "What things have I said to this great King! How I have mocked him and made a joke of him in front of everyone around me!" And at the thought of it she pressed her hand to her side to still the wild beating of her heart. So, while King Arthur and King Leodegrance exchanged royal greetings and compliments, she slipped away and went and stood across the room by the window, near the corner of the wall.

Then, after a while, King Arthur raised his eyes and saw her standing there far off. So he went straight to her, took her by the hand, and said: "Lady, how are you?"

And she said: "Lord, I am afraid of your greatness." And he said: "No, Lady. Rather it is I who am afraid of you. For your kind regard is dearer to me than anything else in all the world. Otherwise I would not have served for these twelve days as a gardener's boy in your garden, all for the sake of your good will." And she said: "You have my good will, Lord." And he said: "Do I have your good will in great measure?" And she said: "Yes, you have it in great measure."

King Arthur is betrothed to the Lady Guinevere.

Then he bent his head and kissed her in front of everyone who was there, and so their promise to marry was pledged.

Then King Leodegrance was filled with such great joy that he did not know how to contain himself.

Now, after these things, there followed a war with King Rience of North Wales. For Sir Kay and Sir Ulfius had gathered a great army, as King Arthur had told them to do, so that when King Rience came against Cameliard he was completely routed, his army was scattered, and he himself was chased, an outcast, into his mountains.

Then there was great rejoicing in Cameliard. For after his victory King Arthur stayed there for a while with a wonderfully splendid Court of noble lords and beautiful ladies. And there was feasting and jousting and many famous contests of arms, the like of which those parts had never seen before. And King Arthur and the Lady Guinevere were completely happy together.

Now one day, while King Arthur sat at a feast with King Leodegrance, and the two of them were brimming over with good cheer, King Leodegrance said to King Arthur: "My Lord, what shall I offer you as a dowry with my daughter, when you take her away from me to be your Queen?"

Then King Arthur turned to Merlin, who stood near him, and said: "Ha, Merlin! What shall I ask of my friend for that dowry?"

Merlin said to him: "My lord King, your friend King Leodegrance has one thing which, if he gives it to you, will greatly increase the glory and renown of your reign, so that its fame will never be forgotten."

And King Arthur said: "I bid you, Merlin, tell me what that thing is."

So Merlin said: "My lord King, I will tell you a story:

Merlin tells of the Round Table.

"In the days of your father, Uther Pendragon, I had a certain table made for him in the shape of a ring, and so men called it the Round Table. At this table there were seats for fifty men, and these seats were meant for the fifty knights who were the most worthy knights in all the world. The seats were of such a kind that whenever a worthy knight appeared, his name appeared in letters of gold on the seat that belonged to him; and when that knight died, his name would suddenly vanish from the seat he had once sat in.

"Now forty-nine of these seats were all alike (except only one that was set aside for the King himself, which was raised above the other seats and was skillfully carved and inlaid with ivory and gold), and the one seat was different from all the others, and it was called the Seat Perilous. For this seat was unlike the others both in how it was made and in what it meant. It was all skillfully set with gold and silver in strange designs, and it was covered with a canopy of satin embroidered with gold and silver, and it was altogether wonderfully magnificent to look at. And no name ever appeared on this seat, for only one knight in all the world could hope to sit in it and be safe. If any other knight dared to sit in it, either he would die a sudden and violent death within three days, or else some great misfortune would fall on him. That is why the seat was called the Seat Perilous.

"Now in the days of King Uther Pendragon, thirty-seven knights sat at the Round Table. And when King Uther Pendragon died, he gave the Round Table to his friend, King Leodegrance of Cameliard.

"And at the beginning of King Leodegrance's reign, twenty-four knights sat at the Round Table.

"But times have changed since then, and the glory of King Leodegrance's reign has grown pale beside the glory of your reign, so that his noble Court of knights has left him altogether. And so now not one name remains on all the fifty seats around the Round Table, except only the name of King Leodegrance. So now the Round Table lies under its pavilion, entirely unused.

"Yet if King Leodegrance will give you that Round Table, my lord King, as a dowry with the Lady Guinevere, it will lend your reign its greatest glory. For in your day every seat at that Table will be filled, even the Seat Perilous, and the fame of the knights who sit at it will never be forgotten."

"Ha!" said King Arthur. "That would indeed be a dowry worthy of any king to have with his queen."

King Leodegrance gives the Round Table to King Arthur.

"Then," said King Leodegrance, "you shall have that dowry with my daughter. And if it brings you great glory, then your glory will be my glory, and your renown will be my renown. For if my glory fades and your glory grows, why, is my child not your wife?"

And King Arthur said: "You speak well and wisely."

So King Arthur became the master of that famous Round Table. And the Round Table was set up at Camelot (which some people now call Winchester). And by and by there gathered around it such a company of knights as the world had never seen before that time, and will never see again.

That was the story of the beginning of the Round Table in King Arthur's reign.

Chapter 6: How King Arthur Was Married in Royal State, and How the Round Table Was Founded

And now the early fall of the year had come: that pleasant season when the meadows and the open uplands were still green with the summer that had only just passed; when the sky, too, was like a summer sky, wonderfully blue and full of great floating clouds; when a bird might sing here and another there, a short song in memory of springtime; when all the air was mild with warmth, and yet the leaves everywhere were turning brown and red and gold, so that when the sun shone through them it was as though a cloth of gold, embroidered with brown and crimson and green, hung over your head. At this season of the year it is a wonderful pleasure to be out in the fields among the nut trees with hawk and hound, or to travel far and wide through the yellow world, whether on horseback or on foot.

Now this was the time of year that had been set for the marriage of King Arthur and the Lady Guinevere at Camelot, and in that place there was wonderful pomp and glory and splendor. All the world was astir and bubbling over with joy, for everyone was very glad that King Arthur was going to have a Queen.

How the town of Camelot was decorated.

To prepare for that great occasion, the town of Camelot was decked out most magnificently. The stony street along which the Lady Guinevere must come to the King's royal castle was strewn thick with freshly cut rushes, smoothly laid. In many places it was spread besides with carpets of fine pattern, fit to lay on the floor of some handsome hall. All the houses along the way were hung with fine woven hangings worked with threads of azure and crimson, and everywhere flags and banners floated in the warm and gentle breeze against the blue sky. So all the world seemed alive with bright colors, and when you looked down that street, it was as though you saw a winding path of the greatest beauty and gaiety stretched out before you.

And so the King's wedding day came, bright and clear and wonderfully radiant.

King Arthur sat in his hall, surrounded by his Court, waiting for news that the Lady Guinevere was on her way. And about the middle of the morning a messenger came riding in haste on a milk-white horse. The clothes of that messenger and the trappings of his horse were all of cloth of gold embroidered with scarlet and white, and the messenger's tabard was set with many jewels of different kinds, so that he glittered from far off as he rode, a sight of rare splendor.

This herald came straight into the castle where the King sat waiting, and he said: "Arise, my lord King, for the Lady Guinevere and her Court are drawing near to this place."

At that the King rose at once with great joy, and immediately he rode out with his Court of Knights in great state. And as he went down that marvelously decorated street, all the people cheered aloud as he passed, so that he smiled and bowed his head from side to side; for that day he was happy beyond words and loved his people with a wonderful warmth.

So he rode on to the town gate, and out through it, and on into the country beyond, where the broad, well-trodden highway ran winding down beside the shining river between the willows and the osiers.

Of the Court of the Lady Guinevere.

And then King Arthur and those with him caught sight of the Princess's Court, appearing in the distance, and they rejoiced greatly and hurried forward with all speed. And as they came near, the sun fell on robes of silk and cloth of gold, and on golden chains and the jewels that hung from them, so that all that noble company around the Lady Guinevere's litter flashed and sparkled with dazzling brightness.

For seventeen of the noblest knights of the King's Court, in full armor, whom he had sent as an escort to the lady, rode in great splendor around the litter in which the Princess lay. The frame of that litter was of richly gilded wood, and its curtains and cushions were of crimson silk embroidered with threads of gold. And behind the litter, in gay and joyful array and shining with many colors, rode the Court of the Princess: her ladies-in-waiting, gentlemen, ladies, pages, and attendants.

So the King's party and the Lady Guinevere's drew closer and closer, until they met and mingled with each other.

King Arthur greets the Lady Guinevere.

Then King Arthur at once got down from his noble horse, and, clothed in all his royalty, he went on foot to the Lady Guinevere's litter, while Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain held his horse's bridle. One of her pages drew back the silken curtains of the litter, and King Leodegrance gave her his hand, and she stepped down at once, wrapped, as it were, in the most wonderful beauty. King Leodegrance led her to King Arthur, and King Arthur came to her and placed one hand beneath her chin and the other on her head, and bent his face and kissed her on her smooth cheek, which was warm and fragrant and as soft as velvet, and without any blemish at all. And when he had kissed her cheek, everyone there raised their voices in a great shout, crying out for joy that those two noble souls had met at last.

That was how King Arthur welcomed the Lady Guinevere and King Leodegrance, her father, on the highway below the walls of the town of Camelot, about a mile and a half from that place. And no one who was there ever forgot that meeting, for it was full of wonderful grace and noble courtliness.

Then King Arthur and his Court of knights and nobles brought King Leodegrance and the Lady Guinevere with great ceremony to Camelot and to the royal castle, where rooms were given to everyone, so that the whole place was alive with joy and beauty.

King Arthur and the Lady Guinevere are married.

And when high noon came, the whole Court went in great state and ceremony to the cathedral, and there, surrounded by wonderful magnificence, those two noble souls were married by the Archbishop.

And all the bells rang out joyfully, and all the people standing outside the cathedral cheered loudly, and then the King and the Queen came out, all shining, like the sun for splendor and like the moon for beauty.

Of the feast at the King's castle.

In the castle a great noonday feast was spread, and four hundred and eighty-six lordly and noble people sat down to it: kings, knights, and nobles, with queens and ladies in magnificent dress. And near the King and the Queen sat King Leodegrance and Merlin, and Sir Ulfius, and Sir Ector the trustworthy, and Sir Gawain, and Sir Uwain, and Sir Kay, and King Ban, and King Pellinore, and many other famous and high-born people, so that no one had ever seen such magnificent courtliness as was seen at that famous wedding feast of King Arthur and Queen Guinevere.

And that day was famous in the history of chivalry for another reason, too, for in the afternoon the famous Round Table was founded, and that Round Table was the very flower and the greatest glory of King Arthur's reign.

For about the middle of the afternoon the King and Queen, with Merlin going before them and all that splendid Court of kings, lords, nobles, and knights in full array following after, went in procession to the place where Merlin, partly by magic and partly by skill, had had a most wonderful pavilion built over the Round Table where it stood.

Of the pavilion of the Round Table.

And when the King and the Queen and the Court had gone inside, they were amazed at the beauty of that pavilion, for it seemed to them a great open space, like some marvelous Fairyland. The walls were all richly gilded and painted with wonderful figures of saints and angels, clothed in ultramarine and crimson, and all those saints and angels were shown playing on different musical instruments that seemed to be made of gold. Overhead, the roof of the pavilion was made to look like the sky, all of cerulean blue sprinkled with stars. And in the middle of that painted sky was an image, as it were, of the sun in his glory. And underfoot was a pavement all of marble, set in squares of black and white, and blue and red, and many other colors.

In the middle of the pavilion was a Round Table, with seats around it for exactly fifty people. At each of the fifty places was a golden chalice filled with fragrant wine, and at each place was a golden plate holding a small loaf of fine white bread. And when the King and his Court entered the pavilion, music suddenly began to play with a wonderful sweetness.

Then Merlin came and took King Arthur by the hand and led him away from Queen Guinevere. And he said to the King, "Look! This is the Round Table."

And King Arthur said, "Merlin, what I see is wonderful beyond telling."

King Arthur is seated at the Round Table.

After that Merlin showed the King the various marvels of the Round Table. First he pointed to a high seat, wonderfully made of precious woods and gilded, so that it was very beautiful, and he said, "Look, lord King: that seat is called the Seat Royal, and it is yours to sit in." And as Merlin spoke, letters of gold suddenly appeared on the back of that seat, and the letters of gold spelled out the name,

Arthur, King.

And Merlin said, "Lord, that seat may well be called the center seat of the Round Table, for truly you are the very center of all that is most worthy in true knighthood. And so that seat shall be called the center of all the other seats."

Then Merlin pointed to the seat that stood opposite the Seat Royal, and that seat too was wonderful to look at, as has been told before in this history. And Merlin said to the King: "My lord King, that seat is called the Seat Perilous, for only one man in all this world shall sit in it, and that man is not yet born on the earth. And if any other man dares to sit in it, he will suffer either death or some sudden and terrible misfortune for his boldness. That is why that seat is called the Seat Perilous."

"Merlin," said the King, "everything you tell me is so marvelous that it is beyond understanding. Now I beg you to hurry and find at once enough knights to fill this Round Table, so that my glory may be wholly complete."

Then Merlin smiled at the King, though not cheerfully, and said, "Lord, why are you in such a hurry? Know this: when every seat of this Round Table is filled, then your glory will be fully achieved, and from that moment your day will begin to decline. For when any man has reached the crown of his glory, then his work is done, and God breaks him, as a man might break a chalice from which a drink so perfect has been drunk that no lesser wine may ever be allowed to stain it. So when your work is done and ended, God will shatter the chalice of your life."

Then the King looked very steadily into Merlin's face and said, "Old man, what you say is always full of wonder, for you speak words of wisdom. Even so, since I am in God's hands, I do wish for my glory and for His good will to be accomplished, even though He should break me entirely when I have served His purposes."

"Lord," said Merlin, "you speak like a worthy king, and with a very great and noble heart. Even so, I cannot fill the Round Table for you at this time. For although you have gathered about you the very noblest Court of Chivalry in all of Christendom, there are only thirty-two knights here present who may be thought worthy to sit at the Round Table."

"Then, Merlin," said King Arthur, "I ask you to choose those thirty-two for me at once."

"I will do so, lord King," said Merlin.

Merlin chooses the knights of the Round Table.

Then Merlin looked around, and he saw King Pellinore standing a little way off. Merlin went to him and took him by the hand. "Look, my lord King," he said. "Here is the knight who, next to you yourself, is at this time the most worthy in all the world to sit at this Round Table. For he is very gentle in his bearing toward the poor and needy, and at the same time he is so terribly strong and skillful that I do not know whether you or he is more to be feared when knight meets knight in combat."

Then Merlin led King Pellinore forward, and on the high seat that stood at the left hand of the Seat Royal there suddenly appeared the name,

Pellinore.

The name was blazoned in letters of gold that shone with an extraordinary luster. And when King Pellinore took his seat, everyone standing around gave him a great and loud cheer that went on for a long time.

Then, after Merlin had chosen King Arthur and King Pellinore in this way, he chose from King Arthur's Court the following knights, thirty-two in all. These were the knights of great renown in chivalry who first founded the Round Table, and for that reason they were named "The Ancient and Honorable Companions of the Round Table."

To begin with, there were Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain, who were the King's nephews, and they sat near him on his right hand. There was Sir Ulfius, who held his seat for only four years and eight months, until the time of his death; after that Sir Gaheris, who was squire to his brother Sir Gawain, held that seat. And there was Sir Kay the Seneschal, who was the King's foster brother; and there was Sir Baudwin of Britain, who held his seat for only three years and two months, until his death, and after that Sir Agravain held that seat. And there were Sir Pelleas and Sir Geraint and Sir Constantine, son of Sir Caderes the Seneschal of Cornwall (the same Constantine who was king after King Arthur). And there were Sir Caradoc, and Sir Sagramore, called the Desirous, and Sir Dinadan, and Sir Dodinas, called the Savage, and Sir Bruin, called the Black, and Sir Meliot of Logres, and Sir Aglovale and Sir Durnore and Sir Lamorak (and these three young knights were sons of King Pellinore). And there were Sir Griflet and Sir Ladinas and Sir Brandiles and Sir Persavant of Ironside, and Sir Dinas of Cornwall, and Sir Brian of Listinoise, and Sir Palomides and Sir Degraine, and Sir Epinogres, the son of the King of North Umberland and brother of the enchantress Vivien, and Sir Lamiel of Cardiff, and Sir Lucan the Butler, and Sir Bedevere his brother (the same who carried King Arthur to the fairy ship when he lay so grievously wounded, near to death, after the last battle he ever fought). These thirty-two knights were the Ancient Companions of the Round Table. Others were added to them, until there were forty-nine in all, and then Sir Galahad was added, and with him the Round Table was made wholly complete.

Now as each of these knights was chosen, at the very moment Merlin took him by the hand, that knight's name suddenly appeared in letters of gold, very bright and shining, on the seat that belonged to him.

But when all had been chosen, King Arthur saw that the seat at the right hand of the Seat Royal had not been filled, and that it bore no name. And he said to Merlin: "Merlin, how is this? The seat at my right hand has not been filled, and it bears no name."

And Merlin said: "Lord, there will be a name on it in a very little while, and he who sits in it will be the greatest knight in all the world, until the knight comes who will fill the Seat Perilous. For he who is coming will surpass all other men in beauty and in strength and in knightly grace."

And King Arthur said: "I wish he were with us now." And Merlin said: "He is coming soon."

So the Round Table was founded with great pomp and great ceremony of state. First the Archbishop of Canterbury blessed each and every seat, moving from place to place surrounded by his holy attendants. The choir among them sang most musically in harmony, while others swung censers from which rose a very fragrant smoke of frankincense, filling that whole pavilion with a scent of heavenly blessedness.

And when the Archbishop had blessed every one of those seats, each chosen knight took his own place at the Round Table, and his squire came and stood behind him, holding above the knight's head the little banner with his coat of arms upon the spear point. And everyone standing around that place, knights and ladies alike, raised their voices in a loud cheer.

Of the ceremony of installation of the Round Table.

Then all the knights stood up, and each knight held up before him the cross of his sword hilt, and each knight repeated, word for word, what King Arthur spoke. And this was the covenant of their Knighthood of the Round Table: That they would be gentle to the weak; that they would be brave against the strong; that they would be terrible to the wicked and the evildoer; that they would defend the helpless who called on them for help; that all women should be sacred to them; that they would stand ready to defend one another whenever such defense was needed; that they would be merciful to all men; that they would be gentle in deed, true in friendship, and faithful in love. This was their covenant, and every knight swore to it on the cross of his sword, and as a sign of it he kissed the hilt. And at that, everyone standing around once more gave a loud cheer.

Then all the knights of the Round Table sat down, and each knight broke bread from the golden plate and drank wine from the golden chalice that stood before him, giving thanks to God for what he ate and drank.

And that was how King Arthur was married to Queen Guinevere, and how the Round Table was founded.

Conclusion

So ends this Book of King Arthur, which I have told with such joy of spirit that, now that I close this first part of my work, it is a very great pleasure to look forward to writing a second part, which follows right after.

In that part I shall tell the history of several very noble worthies who belonged to the Court of the King, and it seems to me a good thing to spend time with the history of such noble and honorable knights and gentlemen. For truly, anyone might well enjoy reading such a history, and hearing those worthies speak, and seeing how they behaved in times of trial and trouble. For their example will surely help all of us to behave in the same way when we find ourselves in the same case.

The Book of Three Worthies

Foreword

Here begins the Second Book of the History of King Arthur. It is called The Book of Three Worthies, because it tells of three very excellent and honorable lords of the Court of King Arthur.

Of these three, the first is Merlin the Wise; the second is Sir Pelleas, who was called the Gentle Knight; and the third is Sir Gawain, the son of King Lot of Orkney and the Isles.

So now, very soon, follows the story of the passing of Merlin the Wise. In it you will see how the very wisdom that Merlin had in such great measure was the cause of his own undoing. And so I hope that you yourselves will take that story to heart, and that you will see to it that the gifts of mind or of person which God gives you are never so misused, by you or by others, that they become the means of bringing about your own downfall.

For it will not excuse you in any way that, as you journey on through your life, you will find many men who, like Merlin, have been given by the grace of God very great gifts of talent, which they might very easily use for the great good of mankind, but which they so misuse that they bring all the greater ruin upon themselves and all the greater harm upon other men. For if you should prove so weak or so wicked as to misuse your talents in that way, to the harm of others and of yourself, it will not make your fault any the less that others have done greater evil than you.

So let this story of Merlin be a warning to you, I beg you all. For though I do not believe that Merlin ever meant his powers of magic to do harm to others, yet, because of his folly, they did as much harm as if he himself had planned to do evil with them. Yes; it is hard to tell whether the wickedness or the follies of men do the greater harm in the world. So take good care to guard yourself, not only against sin, but against folly and weakness as well.

Prologue

One day King Arthur sat in the Royal Hall of Camelot with the Queen, and all of his Court and all of her Court were there with them. And there was great joy and merriment in that place.

A messenger comes to the King at Camelot.

While they sat there, an armed knight suddenly came into the Hall. His armor was all covered with blood and dust, and he had a great many wounds upon his body. Everyone there was astonished and frightened at the sight of that knight, for the look of him promised no good news for King Arthur. The knight-messenger came to where the King was, so weak from his many wounds that he was nearly fainting. And he brought those who were there the news that five kings, enemies of King Arthur, had suddenly come into that land and were burning and plundering the country on every side.

And the knight-messenger said that these five kings were the King of Denmark, the King of Ireland, the King of Soleyse, the King of the Vale, and the King of Longtinaise. They had brought a great army with them and were laying waste the land all around, so that the whole realm was in bitter hardship and sorrow because of the ruin they made.

At this news King Arthur struck his palms together with great force and cried out, "Alas! Who would be a king! Will the time never come when these wars and troubles will end, and we shall have perfect peace in this land?" With that he rose up in great agitation and went out of that place, and everyone who was there was deeply troubled.

So King Arthur at once sent messengers to the two friendly kings who were nearest to him, that is, to King Pellinore and to King Uriens of Gore, and he asked them to come to his aid without losing any time. Meanwhile he himself gathered together a large army, meaning to go out and meet his enemies without delay.

King Arthur asks for the help of King Uriens and King Pellinore.

So he went out, and on the third day he came with his army to the forest of Tintagalon. There he stopped, meaning to rest for a little while until King Pellinore and King Uriens should join him.

But the five kings, his enemies, had news that King Arthur was in that place. At once they made a forced march through North Wales, meaning to strike him before those other two kings could come to his aid. So they came by night to where King Arthur was, and they fell upon him so unexpectedly that his army was in great danger of being put to flight by that attack.

But King Arthur drew his army together by his own courage and greatness of heart, and so they defended themselves with great spirit until King Pellinore appeared with his army and joined in the battle.

King Arthur is victorious.

So in the end King Arthur won a great victory over his enemies, for they were put to flight and scattered in every direction. And through that war, and because these five kings submitted to him, King Arthur won back all of the realm that had once been his father's, and more besides.

Eight knights of the Round Table are killed in battle.

Now in that war eight of the knights of the Round Table lost their lives, and King Arthur mourned their loss with great sorrow; for these were the first knights of the Round Table to lose their lives in battle in his defense.

While King Arthur was grieving very bitterly for these eight knights, Merlin came to him and said, "Do not be downcast, lord. For look! You still have many excellent knights around you, and you certainly cannot have very much trouble in filling the eight places that death has left empty. Now if you follow my advice, you will choose some very worthy adviser from among the knights-companion of your Round Table, and you will consult with him in this matter (for the counsel of two is better than the counsel of one), and between you, you can fill the places that the war has left empty."

This advice seemed very good to King Arthur, so he did as Merlin advised. That very morning he called King Pellinore into his private chamber and laid the matter before him, and the two of them talked it over together. In that consultation King Pellinore gave King Arthur this advice: that four old and worthy knights should be chosen to fill four of those empty seats, and four young and eager knights should be chosen to fill the other four, and in that way all eight seats would be filled.

King Arthur and King Pellinore choose eight old and worthy knights for the Round Table.

Now that advice seemed good to King Arthur, and so he said, "Let it be done that way." So first the two of them chose the four old knights, and these were they: King Uriens of Gore, and King Lac, and Sir Hervise de Reuel, and Sir Galliar of Rouge. And from the younger knights of the Court they chose Sir Marvaise of Leisle, and Sir Lionel, the son of King Ban of Benwick, and Sir Cadar of Cornwall. So there was still one place left to be filled.

They choose three young knights for the Round Table.

Now it was a very hard thing to decide who should fill that place, for there were at that time two very honorable young knights at the Court. One of these was Sir Baudemagus, a young knight, the brother of Sir Uwain and the son of King Uriens of Gore and Queen Morgan le Fay (and that lady was King Arthur's half-sister, as has been told before). The other young knight was Sir Tor, who, though he had only lately come to the Court, had already carried out several very famous adventures. Sir Tor was a son of King Pellinore (though not by his Queen), and King Pellinore loved him a very great deal.

Then King Pellinore said to King Arthur, "Lord, there are certainly only two knights in all your Court to choose from to fill this eighth seat at the Round Table. One of these is your sister's son, Sir Baudemagus, and the other is my son, Sir Tor. Now I cannot advise you in this matter, so you, Lord, must choose one or the other of these young knights to fill that place. But this much I may say: it will please me very greatly if your favor should fall on Sir Baudemagus. For then all the world will believe that I have been above reproach in my dealings in this affair, whereas if Sir Tor is chosen, all men will say that I favored my own son."

King Arthur chooses Sir Tor for the Round Table.

Then King Arthur thought about this matter for a long while, and at last he spoke and said, "Sir, I have weighed this whole affair, and it is my belief that Sir Tor is the better knight of the two. For he has carried out several very excellent adventures, while Sir Baudemagus, though he is a worthy knight, has not yet shown any very great achievement in the fields of knighthood. So, in God's name, let Sir Tor be seated as a companion of the Round Table."

Then King Pellinore said, "So be it," and with that they both rose and went out of that place.

And at that very moment, wonderful to tell, the names of the eight worthies they had chosen appeared, each upon the back of the seat at the Round Table that belonged to him. And so the decision of those two knights was confirmed in that way in the sight of all the world.

Queen Morgan le Fay is offended with King Arthur.

Now when word of all this reached the ears of Queen Morgan le Fay, she was greatly offended that Sir Baudemagus, her son, had been passed over and another chosen in his place. So she cried out against King Arthur in the hearing of several people, saying: "Ha! What is this? Are blood and kinship worth nothing in the eyes of this King, that he passes over so worthy a knight as his own nephew and chooses in his place one who was not born in wedlock? My husband's house has suffered many grievous wrongs at the hands of King Arthur. Why, he has taken away our royal power and made us all little better than captives in his own Court! That in itself is as great an insult as if we were his bitter enemies instead of his near kin. But what he has now done to my son, in passing him over like this, is a greater insult than that other."

And Queen Morgan le Fay spoke like this not only to King Uriens, who was her husband, but to Sir Uwain and to Sir Baudemagus, who were her sons. But King Uriens of Gore rebuked her for her words, for he had grown to love King Arthur very much because of the high nobility of his nature. And Sir Uwain rebuked her too, saying that he would listen to nothing evil said of King Arthur. For not only did he love King Arthur better than anyone else in all the world, but the King was at once the mirror of all knighthood and the very fountainhead of honor.

So these two spoke. But Sir Baudemagus listened to what his mother, Queen Morgan, said, for he was very angry with King Arthur because the King had passed him over. So he left the Court without asking King Arthur's leave and went riding as a knight-errant in search of adventure, and at this King Arthur was very sorry.

Now, as I have said, Queen Morgan le Fay spoke of her indignation to several other people of the Court, so that word of it came at last to the ears of King Arthur and grieved him a very great deal. So when Queen Morgan came to him one day and begged his leave to depart from the Court, he spoke to her with great sadness of spirit, saying, "My sister, I am very sorry that you are not pleased with what I have done in this matter, for God knows that I have tried to do the best I could. And though I would very much rather that Sir Baudemagus were a fellow of the Round Table, yet it was my most honest belief that, for several reasons, Sir Tor had the better right to a seat at that Table. Now if I had chosen otherwise than by my true judgment, what virtue would the Round Table have, when I should have shown favor to a man because he is my kinsman?"

Then Queen Morgan le Fay said with great heat, "Sir, all that you say only adds to the insult our house has suffered at your hands. For now you not only deny my son that seat, but you belittle him by comparing him unfavorably with this low-born knight you have chosen. Now the only pleasure I can have in talking with you is to beg you to let me go away from this place."

Then King Arthur, speaking with great dignity, said, "Lady, it shall be as you wish, and you shall go wherever it pleases you. For God forbid that I should stand in the way of your wishes. What is more, I shall see to it that you do not leave this place without such a Court for company as may well befit one who is the wife of one king and the sister of another."

Queen Morgan le Fay leaves the Court of King Arthur.

And he did just as he said he would, for he sent Queen Morgan le Fay away from his Court with great honor and in high state. But the more patient King Arthur was with her and the more favor he showed her, the angrier Queen Morgan le Fay grew with him and the more she hated him.

So she made her way to a broad mouth of a river where it met the sea. There she sent away those whom the King had sent with her, and she took ship with her own Court in several vessels and sailed away to that enchanted island called Avalon, which was her home.

This island of Avalon was a very strange and wonderful land, such as was not to be seen anywhere else in all the world. For it was like a Paradise for beauty. It was covered all over with gardens of every kind of flower, mingled with groves of lovely trees, some bearing fruit and others all in bloom with blossoms. And besides these there were many terraces of lawn, and smooth slopes of grass lying all around the edges of the island and looking out over the sea from tall white walls of pure marble. And in the midst of these gardens and orchards and groves and lawns and terraces there stood a multitude of castles and towers, built up one above another, some as white as snow and others very gay with many colors.

And the greatest marvel of that wonderful island was this: in the midst of all those castles and towers stood a single tower built entirely of lodestone, the magnet-stone. And in that lay the great mystery of the place.

For the island floated on the surface of the water, and that tower of lodestone had such power that Avalon would float from place to place at the will of Queen Morgan le Fay. So sometimes it would be here and sometimes it would be there, just as that royal lady willed it.

Nor were there very many people who had seen that island. For at times it would be covered all over with a silvery mist of enchantment, so that no eyes could see it unless they were fairy eyes. But sometimes it had been seen, like a vision of Paradise. And then whoever saw it would hear happy voices sounding from its lawns and groves, very thin and clear because of the great distance (for no one ever came near Avalon unless Queen Morgan le Fay allowed it), and he would hear music so sweet that his very soul might grow faint with it. Then Avalon would suddenly vanish in a most marvelous way, and whoever had seen it would know that he was not likely ever to see it again.

Such was the island of Avalon. If you would like to read more about it, you will find a great deal in a certain book written in French and called "Ogier le Danois," which is to say, Ogier the Dane.

Queen Morgan le Fay loved this island a very great deal. And many people say that King Arthur is still alive in that place, lying there very peacefully and quietly while he waits for the appointed time when he will return to the world to set right all that is wrong in it. That is why I have told you about it here, with all these particulars.

The Book of Three Worthies, Part One: The Story of Merlin

Foreword

Here follows a full account of how Merlin was enchanted by a certain damsel named Vivien, and of everything that had to do with it.

Here, too, you will hear how King Arthur was betrayed by his own sister, and how he would certainly have been killed if it had not been for the help of that same enchantress, Vivien, who brought about Merlin's downfall.

And you will also hear how the sheath of Excalibur was lost at that time.

Chapter 1: How Queen Morgan le Fay Plotted Against King Arthur, and How She Sent a Damsel to Charm the Enchanter Merlin

Now Morgan le Fay was a very skillful enchantress, and she had so mastered magic that by her powerful spells she could work her will on all things, whether living or dead. For Merlin himself had been her teacher in times past, and he had taught her his arts while she was still a young damsel at the court of Uther Pendragon. So it was that, next to Merlin, she was at that time the most powerful enchanter in all the world. Even so, she lacked Merlin's knowledge of things to come and his gift of prophecy. Those were things he could not pass on to anyone, and so she had never learned them from him.

Queen Morgan plots evil against King Arthur.

Now, after Queen Morgan le Fay had come to the Island of Avalon, as you have already heard, she brooded a great deal over the insult she believed King Arthur had done to her house. And the more she brooded on it, the bigger it grew in her mind, until at last it seemed to her that she could take no pleasure in life unless she could punish King Arthur for what he had done. Yes; she would have been glad to see him lying dead at her feet, so great was the anger she felt against him.

But Queen Morgan knew very well that she could never do the King, her brother, any harm so long as Merlin was there to guard him. For Merlin would surely foresee any danger that threatened the King, and he would undo it. And so she knew that if she wanted to destroy the King, she must first destroy Merlin.

Now at the court of Queen Morgan le Fay there was a certain damsel of such marvelous and bewitching beauty that her equal could hardly be found anywhere in the world. This damsel was fifteen years old and of royal blood, for she was the daughter of the King of Northumberland, and her name was Vivien. And this damsel, Vivien, was wise and crafty beyond all measure for someone so young. What was more, she had no heart at all, and she was cold and cruel to anyone who stood in the way of what she wanted. Because she was so clever and so wise, Queen Morgan liked her, and taught her many of the things of magic and sorcery that she knew. But for all that Queen Morgan did for her, this maiden felt no love at all for her mistress, for she was entirely without a heart.

Queen Morgan talks with the Damsel Vivien.

One day this damsel and Queen Morgan le Fay were sitting together in a garden on that magic island of Avalon. The garden lay on a very high terrace overlooking the sea. The day was very fair, and the sea was so wonderfully blue that it seemed as though the blue sky had melted into the water and the water into the sky. As Vivien and the Queen sat in this beautiful place, the Queen said to the damsel, "Vivien, what would you rather have than anything else in the world?" And Vivien answered, "Lady, I would rather have wisdom like yours than anything else."

At that Queen Morgan laughed and said, "You can be as wise as I am, and wiser too, if you will do as I tell you. For I know a way by which you can gain wisdom."

"How can I gain that wisdom, Lady?" said Vivien.

Then Queen Morgan le Fay said, "Listen and I will tell you. You must know that Merlin, whom you have seen several times at the court of King Arthur, is master of all the wisdom that anyone can possibly have in this world. Everything I know of magic Merlin taught me, and he knows many things that he did not teach me, but kept back from me. Merlin taught me when I was a young damsel at the court of my mother's husband, because I was beautiful in his eyes. For Merlin loves beauty more than anything else in the world, and so he taught me many things of magic and was very patient with me.

"But Merlin has one gift that belongs to him alone, and that he cannot pass on to anyone else, for it is born in him. That gift is the gift of seeing into the future and the power to prophesy what he sees.

"Yet though he can foresee the fate of others, he is blind to his own. He confessed as much to me several times: that he could not tell what was going to happen in his own life when it concerned himself alone.

"Now you, Vivien, are far more beautiful than I was at your age, and so I believe you will easily win Merlin's affection. And if I give you, besides, a certain charm that I own, I can make Merlin love you so much that he will teach you a great deal more of his wisdom than he ever taught me when I was his pupil.

"But you must understand, Vivien, that in winning this gift of knowledge from Merlin you will put yourself in great danger. For by and by, when the charm of your beauty has faded for him, he may well regret having given you his wisdom. And then there will be great danger that he may lay some spell on you to take your powers away. For it would be impossible for both you and he to live in the same world, each of you knowing so much of the craft of magic."

Vivien listened to all this very closely, and when Queen Morgan had finished, the damsel said, "Dear Lady, everything you tell me is very wonderful, and I am filled with a fierce desire to gain that kind of knowledge of magic. So if you will help me in this, so that I can charm Merlin's wisdom out of him, I will be in your debt for as long as I live. And as for any danger that may come to me in this business, I am entirely willing to take it on myself. For I have great hopes that I can protect myself from Merlin so that no harm will come to me. When I have drawn out of him all the knowledge I can get, I will use that same knowledge to cast such a spell on him that he will never be able to harm me or anyone else again. In this I will play my wits against his wisdom and my beauty against his skill, and I believe I will win that game."

At that Queen Morgan burst out laughing beyond all measure, and when she had stopped laughing, she cried, "Ha, Vivien! You are truly more crafty than anyone I ever heard of, and I believe you are as wicked as you are crafty. For who ever heard of a child fifteen years old speaking words like the ones you have just spoken? Or who could ever suppose that so young a girl could dream of bringing about the downfall of the wisest magician who ever lived?"

Queen Morgan gives Vivien two enchanted rings.

Then Queen Morgan le Fay set to her lips a small whistle of ivory and gold and blew a very shrill note on it, and in answer a young page of her court came running. Queen Morgan ordered him to bring her a certain casket of alabaster, skillfully carved, decorated with gold, and set with several precious stones. And Queen Morgan opened the box and took out of it two rings of pure yellow gold, beautifully made and set. The one ring held a clear white stone of extraordinary brilliance, and the other a stone as red as blood. Then Queen Morgan said, "Vivien, look at these two rings! Each of them holds a spell of wonderful power. For if you wear the ring with the white stone, then whoever wears the ring with the red stone will love you with such a passion that you can do with him whatever you please. So take these rings and go to King Arthur's court, and use them however your cleverness may plan."

So Vivien took the two rings and thanked Queen Morgan le Fay for them beyond all measure.

Vivien appears before King Arthur at the Feast of Pentecost.

Now King Arthur took great pleasure in holding a great feast every Pentecost, when his court was gathered around him with much merriment and rejoicing. At such times it delighted him to have some excellent entertainment to amuse himself and his court, and so it nearly always happened that something took place that gave the King great entertainment. So the Feast of Pentecost came, and King Arthur sat at table with a great many noble and lordly people and several kings and queens. And as they all sat at that feast, their spirits high with merriment and good cheer, there suddenly came into the hall a very beautiful young damsel, and with her a dwarf, wonderfully misshapen and with a very hideous face. The maiden was dressed all in flame-colored satin, very rich, with beautiful embroidery of gold and of silver. Her hair, which was red like gold, was coiled up in a net of gold. Her eyes were as black as coals, and extraordinarily bright and glittering. And around her throat she wore a necklace of gold of three strands, so that with all that gold and those bright clothes she shone with wonderful splendor as she came into the hall. The dwarf who came with her was likewise dressed all in flame color, and in his hands he carried a cushion of flame-colored silk with tassels of gold, and on the cushion he carried a ring of great beauty set with a red stone.

So when King Arthur saw this beautiful maiden, he supposed that here was some excellent entertainment, and he rejoiced at it a very great deal.

But when he looked closely at the damsel, it seemed to him that he knew her face, and so he said to her, "Damsel, who are you?" "Sir," she said, "I am the daughter of the King of Northumberland, and my name is Vivien." And with that King Arthur was satisfied.

Then King Arthur said to her, "Lady, what is that you have on the cushion there, and why have you honored us by coming here?" And Vivien answered, "Lord, I have here a very fine entertainment to give you pleasure at this Feast of Pentecost. For here is a ring of such a kind that only the wisest and most worthy of all the men here may wear it." And King Arthur said, "Let us see the ring."

So Vivien took the ring from the cushion the dwarf held, and she came and brought it to King Arthur, and the King took the ring in his own hand. And he saw that the ring was extraordinarily beautiful, so he said, "Maiden, do I have your permission to try this ring on my finger?" And Vivien said, "Yes, lord."

King Arthur tries on the ring.

So King Arthur tried to put the ring on his finger; but the ring shrank in size so that it would not go past the first joint. And so King Arthur said, "It seems that I am not worthy to wear this ring."

Merlin wins the ring.

Then the damsel Vivien said, "Do I have my lord's permission to offer this ring to others of his court?" And King Arthur said, "Let the others try the ring." So Vivien took the ring to the different people of the court, both lords and ladies, but not one of them could wear it. Then, last of all, Vivien came to the place where Merlin sat, and she knelt on the ground before him and offered him the ring. And because this concerned Merlin himself, he could not see into the future to know that harm was meant for him. Even so, he looked sourly at the damsel and said, "Child, what is this silly trick you are offering me?" "Sir," said Vivien, "I beg you to try this ring on your finger." Then Merlin looked at the damsel more closely, and he saw that she was very beautiful, and his heart softened toward her a great deal. So he spoke to her more gently and said, "Why should I take the ring?" And she answered, "Because I believe that you are the wisest and the most worthy of any man in this whole place, and so the ring should belong to you." Then Merlin smiled, and took the ring and put it on his finger; and it fitted his finger exactly. At that Vivien cried out, "Look! The ring has fitted his finger, and he is the wisest and the most worthy!" And Merlin was greatly pleased that the ring the beautiful damsel had given him had fitted his finger like that.

Then, after a while, he would have taken the ring off again, but to his astonishment he could not, for the ring had grown onto his finger as though it were part of its flesh and bone. At this Merlin became deeply troubled in spirit and very anxious, for he did not understand what the magic of the ring might mean. So he said, "Lady, where did this ring come from?" And Vivien said, "Sir, you know all things. Do you not know, then, that this ring was sent here by Morgan le Fay?" Then once again Merlin was filled with doubt, and he said, "I hope there may be no evil in this ring." And Vivien smiled at him and said, "What evil could there be in it?"

By this time the great magic that was in the ring had begun to work on Merlin's spirit, and he gazed at Vivien very steadily, and suddenly he took great delight in her beauty. Then the magic of the ring took complete hold of him, and a wonderful passion seized his heart all at once and wrung it so that it was pierced as if with a violent agony.

And Vivien saw what was passing in Merlin's mind, and she laughed and turned away. Several others who were there also noticed the very strange way in which Merlin gazed at her, and they said among themselves, "Surely Merlin is bewitched by the beauty of that young damsel."

The ring works its charm on Merlin the Wise.

So from that time on, the enchantment of Morgan le Fay's ring worked so strongly on Merlin's spirit that he could in no way free himself from Vivien's witchery. From that day forward, wherever she went, he could be found not far away. If she was in the garden, he would be there; if she was in the Hall, he would be there too; and if she went hawking, he would be on horseback as well. All the court noticed these things, and many of them made merry over it and joked about it. But Vivien hated Merlin with all her might, for she saw that everyone was laughing at Merlin's folly, and he wearied her with his attentions. Still, she hid her dislike from him and behaved toward him in every way as though she felt a great friendship for him.

Now it happened one day that Vivien was sitting in the garden in wonderfully pleasant summer weather, and Merlin came into the garden and saw Vivien where she sat. But when Vivien saw Merlin coming, she suddenly felt such a dislike for him that she could not bear to be near him just then, so she got up in haste, meaning to escape from him. But Merlin hurried and caught up with her, and he said to her, "Child, do you hate me, then?" And Vivien said, "Sir, I do not hate you." But Merlin said, "In very truth, I believe you do hate me." And Vivien was silent.

Then after a little Merlin said, "I wish I knew what I could do for you so that you would stop hating me, for I find that I have a wonderful love for you." At this Vivien looked at Merlin very strangely, and after a while she said, "Sir, if only you would share your wisdom and your skill with me, then I believe I could love you a very great deal. For look, I am only a young child in knowledge, and you are so old and so wise that I am afraid of you. If you would teach me your wisdom so that I might be your equal, then perhaps I might come to feel for you the way you would have me feel."

At this Merlin looked very steadily at Vivien and said, "Damsel, you are surely not the foolish child you claim to be, for I see that your eyes are very bright with a cleverness beyond your years. Now, I fear that if I were to teach you the wisdom you long to have, it would be either your undoing or else mine."

Then Vivien cried out in a very loud and piercing voice, "Merlin, if you love me, teach me your wisdom and the secrets of your magic, and then I will love you more than anyone else in all the world!"

But Merlin sighed very deeply, for his heart was full of foreboding. Then after a while he said, "Vivien, you shall have your way, and I will teach you all the things of wisdom and magic that you want to know."

At this Vivien was filled with such a fierce agony of joy that she did not dare let Merlin look into her face, for fear he would read what was written there. So she cast down her eyes and turned her face away from him. Then after a little while she said, "Master, when will you teach me that wisdom?"

Merlin answered, "I will not teach you today, or tomorrow, or in this place. For I can only teach you these things in a solitude where nothing will disturb your studies. But tomorrow you must tell King Arthur that you have to go back to your father's kingdom. Then we will set out together, along with your attendants; and when we have come to some secluded place, I will build a dwelling there by means of my magic, and we will stay in it until I have taught you wisdom."

Then Vivien was filled with joy, and she caught Merlin's hand in hers and kissed it with great passion.

Merlin and Vivien leave the court of the King.

So the next day Vivien begged King Arthur to give her permission to go back to her father's court, and on the third day she and Merlin, and a number of attendants who served the damsel, left the court of King Arthur and set out as though they were on their way to the Kingdom of Northumberland.

But when they had gone some little distance from the court of the King, they turned to the east and made their way toward a certain valley that Merlin knew of. It was such a fair and pleasant place that it was sometimes called the Valley of Delight and sometimes the Valley of Joyousness.

Chapter 2: How Merlin Traveled with Vivien to the Valley of Joyousness and Built Her a Castle There, How He Taught Her the Secrets of Magic, and How She Used Them to Bring Him Down

Merlin and Vivien come to an enchanted forest.

So Merlin and Vivien and those who were with them traveled east for three days, until, toward the end of the third day, they came to the edge of a very dark and gloomy forest. There before them they saw trees so thickly woven together that no eye could see any of the sky through the thickness of the leaves. And they saw that the branches and roots of the trees looked like serpents all twisted together. So Vivien said, "Sir, this is a very gloomy woodland." "Yes," said Merlin, "so it seems. Even so, inside this forest lies the place that some call the Valley of Joyousness and others the Valley of Delight, because of its great beauty. And there are several paths running through this forest by which a man may reach that valley, whether on horseback or on foot."

And after a while they found that it was as Merlin said, for by and by they came upon one of those paths, and they entered it and went deep into the forest. And inside that dismal woodland it was so dark that it seemed as though night had fallen, although it was bright daylight outside its borders, so that many of the party were very much afraid. But Merlin kept cheering them on, and so they went forward on their way. And by and by they came out of that place at last into the open again, and they were greatly relieved and took much comfort.

Merlin and Vivien come to the Valley of Joyousness.

By this time evening had come, very peaceful and still, and wonderfully beautiful. In the center of the valley lay a small lake, so smooth and as clear as crystal that it looked like an oval shield of pure silver laid down on the ground. And all around the edge of the lake were level meadows covered with an unbelievable multitude of flowers of every color and kind, very beautiful to see.

When Vivien saw this place she cried out to Merlin, "Master, this is truly a very joyous valley, for I do not believe the blessed meadows of Paradise are more beautiful than this." And Merlin said, "Very well; let us go down into it." So they went down, and as they went, night fell quickly, and the round moon rose into the sky. And it was hard to tell whether the valley was more beautiful by day or more beautiful when the moon shone down on it like that.

So at last they all came to the shore of the lake, and they saw that there was neither house nor castle in that place.

At this Merlin's followers grumbled among themselves, saying, "This enchanter has brought us here, but how is he going to provide us with a resting place to shelter us from the changes of bad weather? For the beauty of this spot alone cannot shelter us from rain and storm." And Merlin overheard their grumbling, and he said, "Peace! Do not trouble yourselves about that, for I will very soon provide you with a good resting place." Then he said to them, "Stand a little way off until I show you what I will do." So they drew back a little, as he commanded, and he and Vivien stayed where they were. And Vivien said, "Master, what will you do?" And Merlin said, "Wait a little and you will see."

With that he began a certain very powerful spell, so that the earth began to tremble and shake, and something like a great cloud of red dust rose into the air. In this dust various shapes and forms began to appear, and these shapes and forms rose very high into the air, until by and by those who were watching saw that a great building was taking shape in the middle of the cloud of red dust.

Merlin builds a castle by means of his magic.

Then, after a while, everything grew quiet and the dust slowly faded from the air, and there before them stood a marvelous castle such as no one there had ever seen before, even in a dream. For its walls were of ultramarine and vermilion, decorated and adorned with figures of gold, so that the castle shone in the moonlight like a pure vision of great glory.

Vivien saw everything Merlin had done, and she went to him and knelt on the ground before him and took his hand and pressed it to her lips. And as she knelt there, she said, "Master, this is surely the most wonderful thing in the world. Will you teach me such magic that I can build a castle like this one out of the elements?" And Merlin said, "Yes; all this I will teach you, and more besides. For I will teach you not only how to create a building like this out of invisible things, but also how, with a single touch of your wand, to scatter that castle instantly into the air, just as a child with a stroke of a straw can burst a beautiful shining bubble, which one instant is there and the next instant is not. And I will teach you more than that, for I will teach you how to change and transform one thing so that it looks like something else; and I will teach you spells and charms such as you have never heard of before."

Then Vivien cried out, "Master, you are the most wonderful man in all the world!" And Merlin looked at Vivien, and her face was very beautiful in the moonlight, and he loved her a very great deal. So he smiled at her and said, "Vivien, do you still hate me?" And she said, "No, master."

But she did not speak the truth, for in her heart she was evil, and Merlin's heart was good, and what is evil will always hate what is good. So, though Vivien hungered for the knowledge of the black arts, and though she spoke so lovingly with her lips, yet in her spirit she both feared and hated Merlin because of his wisdom. For she knew very well that, if it were not for the enchantment of the ring he wore, Merlin would no longer love her as he did. And so she said in her heart, "If Merlin teaches me all his wisdom, then the world cannot hold both him and me."

Merlin teaches magic to Vivien.

Merlin stayed with Vivien in that place for a year and a little more, and in that time he taught her all the magic he was able to pass on. And at the end of that time he said to her, "Vivien, I have now taught you so much that I believe there is no one in all the world who knows more than you do of the things of magic you have studied in this time. For not only do you have such powers of sorcery that you can make the invisible elements take shape at your will, and not only can you change one thing at your will so that it looks like something altogether different, but you have such powerful magic in your possession that you can tangle any living soul in its meshes, unless that person has some very good charm to defend himself against your wiles. Even I myself have not very much more power than what I have given you."

So said Merlin, and Vivien was filled with great joy. And she said in her heart, "Now, Merlin, if I have the good fortune to tangle you in my spells, you will never see the world again."

Vivien prepares a feast for Merlin.

When the next day came, Vivien had a very splendid feast prepared for herself and Merlin. And by means of the knowledge Merlin had taught her, she made a certain very powerful sleeping potion that had no taste at all. She herself mixed this potion into a certain fine wine, and she poured the wine into a golden chalice of extraordinary beauty.

So when the feast was over, and while she and Merlin sat together, Vivien said, "Master, I have a mind to do you a great honor." And Merlin said, "What is it?" "You will see," said Vivien. With that she clapped her hands, and at once a young page came to where they were, carrying that chalice of wine in his hand, and he gave it to Vivien. Then Vivien took the chalice and went to where Merlin sat, and knelt down before him and said, "Sir, I beg you to take this chalice and drink the wine that is in it. For just as that wine is very noble and very precious, so your wisdom is very noble and very precious; and just as the wine is held in a chalice of priceless worth, so your wisdom is held in a life that has been beyond all value to the world." And with that she put her lips to the chalice and kissed the wine that was in it.

Merlin suspected no evil, but took the chalice and drank the wine very cheerfully.

Merlin is overcome by the wine that Vivien gives him.

After that, in a little while, the fumes of that powerful drink began to rise into Merlin's brain, and it was as though a cloud came down over his sight. And when this came upon him, he knew at once that he was betrayed, and he cried out three times, in a voice very bitter and full of agony, "Woe! Woe! Woe!" And then he cried out, "I am betrayed!" And with that he struggled to rise from where he sat, but he could not.

All this while Vivien sat with her chin in her hands and watched him very steadily, smiling at him strangely. So before long Merlin stopped struggling and sank into a sleep so deep that it was almost as if he had died. And when that had happened, Vivien got up and leaned over him and laid a very powerful spell on him. She stretched out her forefinger and wove an enchantment all around him, so that it was as if he were wrapped all about in a silver web of enchantment. And when she had finished, Merlin could move neither hand nor foot, nor even so much as a fingertip, but was exactly like some great insect that a clever and beautiful spider had caught in a net of fine, strong web.

Vivien bewitches Merlin.

When the next morning came, Merlin woke from his sleep and saw Vivien sitting across from him, watching him very closely. They were in the same room in which he had fallen asleep. And when Vivien saw that Merlin was awake, she laughed and said, "Merlin, how are you?" And Merlin groaned with great passion, saying, "Vivien, you have betrayed me."

At this Vivien laughed again, very shrilly and piercingly, and she said, "Look, Merlin, you are completely in my power, for you are wound all about in the very enchantments that you yourself taught me. Why, you cannot move a single hair without my will. And when I leave you, the world will see you no more, and all your wisdom will be my wisdom, and all your power will be my power, and there will be no one else in the whole world who has the wisdom I have."

Then Merlin groaned so deeply that it seemed his heart would burst. And he said, "Vivien, you have brought me to such shame that even if I were freed from this spell I could not bear for any man ever to see my face again. For I do not grieve so much for my ruin as I grieve at the folly that has turned my own wisdom against me to destroy me. So I forgive you everything you have done to betray me; yet there is one thing only that I beg of you."

And Vivien said, "Does it concern you?" And Merlin said, "No, it concerns someone else." Then Vivien said, "What is it?"

Merlin makes one request of Vivien.

Then Merlin said, "It is this. My gift of foresight has come back to me, and I see that King Arthur is at this moment in great danger of his life. So I beg you, Vivien, to go at once to where he is in danger, and to use your powers of sorcery to save him.
