By doing this one good deed, you may perhaps lessen the sin of what you have done in betraying me."

Now at that time Vivien was not so wholly bad as she later became, for she still felt some small pity for Merlin and some small respect for King Arthur. So now she laughed and said, "Very well, I will do what you ask in this matter. Where shall I go to save that King?"

Merlin answered, "Go into the West Country, to the castle of a certain knight named Sir Damas le Noir, and when you get there you will see at once how you can help the good King." At this Vivien said, "I will do this thing for you, for it is the last favor anyone will ever do for you in this world."

With that Vivien clapped her hands and called many of her attendants. And when they had come in, she showed Merlin to them and said, "See how I have bewitched him! Go! See for yourselves! Feel his hands and his face, and see if there is any life in him." And they went to Merlin and felt him, his hands and arms and face, and they even plucked at his beard, and Merlin could not move in any way, but could only groan in great misery. So they all laughed and made merry over his woeful state.

Vivien places Merlin beneath the stone.

Then by means of her magic Vivien made a great chest of stone appear in that place. And she commanded those who were there to lift Merlin up and lay him in it, and they did as she commanded. Then by means of her magic she had a huge slab of stone, such as ten men could hardly lift, placed on top of that chest, and Merlin lay beneath that stone like a man who was dead.

Then Vivien made the magic castle vanish in an instant, and it happened just as she willed. Then she made a mist rise up over that place, and the mist was of such a kind that no one could make his way into it or break through it, and no human eye could see what was inside. And when she had done all this, she went away from that valley with all her attendants, rejoicing greatly that she had triumphed over Merlin.

Even so, she did not forget her promise, but went to the castle of Sir Damas le Noir; and after a while you will hear everything that happened there.

That was the passing of Merlin. And may God grant that you never so misuse the wisdom He gives you that it is turned against you to your undoing. For there can be no greater bitterness in the world than this: that a man should be betrayed by someone to whom he himself has given the power to betray him.

And now let us turn to King Arthur, to learn how things went with him after Merlin had been betrayed to his undoing like this.

Chapter 3: How Queen Morgan le Fay Came Back to Camelot Meaning to Harm King Arthur, and How the King and Others Went Hunting, and What Came of It

Now, after Merlin had left the Court with Vivien in the way I have already told you, Queen Morgan le Fay came back to Camelot. There she came to King Arthur and knelt before him, bowing her face, with a look of great humility. And she said, "Brother, I have thought a great deal about the things that have happened, and I see that I did very wrong to speak against you as I did, and to rebel against your royalty as I did. So I beg you to forgive me my evil words and my evil thoughts against you."

Queen Morgan le Fay and King Arthur are reconciled.

Then King Arthur was very much moved. He came to Queen Morgan and took her by the hand and lifted her to her feet, and he kissed her brow and her eyes, saying, "My sister, I bear you no ill will. There is nothing but love for you in my heart." And so Queen Morgan le Fay stayed at the Court just as she had before, for King Arthur believed that the two of them were reconciled.

King Arthur shows Excalibur to Queen Morgan.

Now one day Queen Morgan and the King fell into a friendly talk about Excalibur, and Queen Morgan le Fay said she very much wished to see that noble weapon more closely than she had yet been able to, and King Arthur said that he would show it to her sometime. So the next day he said, "Sister, come with me and I will show you Excalibur." With that he took Queen Morgan by the hand and led her into another room, where there stood a strong wooden chest bound with bands of iron. Then the King opened the chest, and there Queen Morgan le Fay saw Excalibur, lying in his sheath. And King Arthur said to her, "Lady, take this sword and look at it as much as you please." So Queen Morgan took Excalibur into her hands and lifted him out of the chest. And she drew the sword from the sheath, and the blade flashed like lightning. Then she said, "Sir, this is a very beautiful sword, and I wish I might take it away with me and keep it for a little while, so that I could enjoy it to the full."

Now King Arthur wished to show the Queen great courtesy at this time when they had made their peace, so he said to her, "Take it, and be its keeper for as long as you like." So Queen Morgan took Excalibur and his sheath and carried them away with her to her lodging, and she hid the sword in the bed where she slept.

Then Queen Morgan sent for goldsmiths, eight of them, and for armorers, eight of them, and for skilful jewelers, eight of them, and she said to them, "Make me a sword exactly like this sword that I have here, in every single detail." And then she showed them Excalibur in his sheath. So these goldsmiths and armorers and jewel-cutters worked with great diligence, and in two weeks they had made a sword so exactly like Excalibur that no eye could have told the one from the other. And Queen Morgan le Fay kept both swords by her until her plans should be carried out.

Then one day it happened that King Arthur proclaimed a hunt, and he and all his Court were to take part in it.

Queen Morgan le Fay gives a horse to King Arthur.

Now the day before the hunt, Queen Morgan le Fay came to King Arthur and said, "Brother, I have here for you a very beautiful and noble horse, which I mean to give you as a gift of love." With that she called aloud, and two grooms came leading a horse as black as jet, all decked with trappings and harness of silver. And the horse was so extraordinarily beautiful that neither King Arthur nor anyone with him had ever seen its equal. So a wonderful delight filled the King at the sight of that horse, and he said, "Sister, this is the noblest gift anyone has given me for a long time." "Ha, brother," said Queen Morgan, "so the horse pleases you?" "Yes," said King Arthur, "it pleases me more than any horse I have ever seen." "Then," said Queen Morgan, "think of it as a gift of reconciliation between you and me. And as a sign of that reconciliation, I beg you to ride that horse on the hunt tomorrow." And King Arthur said, "I will do so."

So the next day he rode out to the hunt on that horse, as he had said he would.

King Arthur rides hunting.

Now some time after noon it happened that the hounds started a hart of extraordinary size, and the King and all his Court followed the chase with great eagerness. But King Arthur's horse soon outran all the other horses except one, which belonged to a very honorable and worthy knight of the Court named Sir Accolon of Gaul. So Sir Accolon and the King rode at a great pace through the forest, and they were so eager in the chase that they did not notice where they were riding. At last they overtook the hart and found it trapped in a very thick and tangled part of the forest, and there King Arthur killed the stag, and so the chase was ended.

King Arthur and Sir Accolon of Gaul are lost in the forest.

After this the King and Sir Accolon would have gone back the way they had come, but soon they found that they were lost in the maze of the woods and did not know where they were. For they had followed the chase so far that they were in a completely strange country. So they wandered this way and that for a long time, until evening, and by then they were worn down with hunger and weariness. Then King Arthur said to Sir Accolon, "Sir, it seems to me that we shall have nowhere to rest tonight except under a tree in this forest."

To this Sir Accolon answered, "Lord, if you will take my advice, you will let our horses find their own way through this wilderness. Perhaps, by the instinct such creatures have, they will bring us to some place where people live."

This advice seemed very good to King Arthur, so he did as Sir Accolon advised: he let his bridle rein go loose and allowed his horse to go wherever it liked. So King Arthur's horse went along a certain path, and Sir Accolon followed after the King. And they went a good long way like this, while the night came down upon them in the forest.

But before it was entirely dark they came out of the forest into an open place, where they saw before them a very wide estuary, like an inlet of the sea. In front of them lay a beach of sand, very smooth and white, and the two of them went down to that beach and stood on the shore. And they did not know what to do, for there was no house or dwelling in sight in any direction.

King Arthur and Sir Accolon see a wonderful ship.

Now while they stood there in doubt, they suddenly saw a ship a very great distance away. And as the ship came nearer they saw that it was very strange and wonderful to look at. It was painted in many different colors, very gay and brilliant, and its sails were all of silk cloth, woven in many colors and embroidered with figures like the figures of a tapestry. And King Arthur was very greatly amazed at the sight of that ship.

As they stood there watching it, they saw that it was drawing nearer and nearer to the place where they were, and after a little while it ran itself aground on the sandy shore not very far away from them.

Then King Arthur said to Sir Accolon, "Sir, let us go down to the water's edge, where we can look into this ship. For I have never seen anything like it in all my life, and I have a notion that it may be a fairy ship."

So the two of them went to where the ship lay, and they stood on the shore and looked down into it. At first they thought there was no one aboard, for it seemed to be entirely deserted. But as they stood there marveling at how wonderful the ship was and at the way it had come there, they suddenly saw the curtains that hung before a cabin at the far end of the ship part, and out of that place came twelve very beautiful damsels. Each of them was dressed in a rich gown of scarlet satin, very bright and shining, and each wore a circlet of gold around her head, and each had many bracelets of gold upon her arms. These damsels came forward to where the two knights were, and they said, "Welcome, King Arthur!" And they said, "Welcome, Sir Accolon!"

King Arthur was very much astonished that they should know him, and he said, "Fair ladies, how is this? You seem to know me very well, but I do not know you. Who are you, that you know me and my companion and call us by our names?"

The chief of those damsels answered, "Sir, we are part fairy, and we know all about you. We know that you have been following a very long chase, and we know that you are weary and hungry and thirsty. So we beg you to come aboard this ship and rest and refresh yourselves with food and drink."

Now this seemed to King Arthur a very fine adventure, so he said to Sir Accolon, "Sir, I have a great mind to go aboard this ship and follow this adventure to the end." And Sir Accolon said, "Lord, if you go, I will go too."

King Arthur and Sir Accolon enter the ship of damsels.

So the ladies let down a gangplank from the ship, and King Arthur and Sir Accolon drove their horses up the gangplank and aboard. And the ship sailed away the way it had come, very swiftly. By now it was early night, and the moon hung very round and full in the sky, like a disk of pure shining silver.

Then the twelve damsels helped King Arthur and Sir Accolon to dismount. Some of them took the horses away, and others led the two knights into a fair room at the end of the ship. And in this room King Arthur saw that a table had been set as though for their entertainment, spread with a linen cloth and laid with many kinds of savory meats, with small loaves of white bread, and with several different sorts of excellent wine. At the sight King Arthur and Sir Accolon were greatly cheered, for they were very hungry indeed.

So they sat down at the table at once and ate and drank with great heartiness. And while they did so, some of the damsels served them their food, and others kept them in pleasant conversation, and others played music on lutes and citterns to entertain them. So they feasted and were very merry.

But after a while a very great drowsiness began to come over King Arthur, though he thought it had come upon him from the weariness of the chase. So soon he said, "Fair damsels, you have refreshed us a great deal, and this has been a very pleasant adventure. But now I wish you had a place for us to sleep."

The chief of the damsels replied, "Lord, this ship has been made ready for your refreshment, so everything you could need has been prepared for you in full."

With that, some of the twelve damsels led King Arthur to a bedchamber that had been prepared for him, and others led Sir Accolon to another chamber prepared for him. And King Arthur marveled at the beauty of his room, for he thought he had never seen a more splendidly furnished bedchamber than the one he had just entered. So King Arthur lay down in great comfort of body, and he fell at once into a deep and gentle sleep, without any dream or disturbance of any kind.

King Arthur finds himself in a dreadful prison.

Now when King Arthur woke from that sleep, he was astonished beyond all measure, so that he did not know whether he was still asleep and dreaming or whether he was awake. For he was lying on a pallet of straw in a very dark and dismal room, all of stone. And he saw that this room was a dungeon, and all around him he heard the sound of many voices complaining in misery. Then King Arthur said to himself, "Where is the ship I was on last night, and what has become of those ladies I spoke with?"

At that he looked around him, and he saw that he really was in a dungeon, and that there were many knights in a very sad state all around him. So he understood that they too were captives, and that it was they who had made that sound of miserable lamenting which he had heard as he woke.

Then King Arthur roused himself from where he lay, and he saw that all the knights who were prisoners there were strangers to him: he did not know them and they did not know him. And there were twenty-two of these knights held prisoner in that place.

Then King Arthur said, "Sirs, who are you, and where am I now?" And the chief of the knights who were prisoners answered, "Sir, like you, we are prisoners in a dungeon of this castle. And the castle belongs to a certain knight named Sir Damas, who is called le Noir, which means the Black."

Then King Arthur marveled greatly at what had happened to him, and he said, "Sirs, a very strange thing has happened to me. For last night I fell asleep in a very wonderful ship that I believe was a fairy ship, and a knight was with me as my companion. And now this morning I wake up alone in this dungeon, and I do not know how I came here."

"Sir," said the knight who was speaking for the others, "last night you were brought here by two men dressed in black, and they laid you down on that pallet without waking you. So it is very plain to me that you are in the same plight as we are, and that you are a prisoner of this Sir Damas le Noir."

Then King Arthur said, "Tell me, who is this Sir Damas? For I declare I have never heard of him before." "I will tell you," said the captive knight, and he did so, like this:

"I believe," he said, "that this Sir Damas is the falsest knight alive, for he is full of treachery and lies, and in his heart he is a complete coward. Yet he is a man of very great wealth and very powerful in these parts.

The prisoner knight tells King Arthur about Sir Damas.

"Now there are two brothers. Sir Damas is one, and the other is named Sir Ontzlake, and Sir Damas is the elder and Sir Ontzlake the younger. When the father of these two knights died, he left them equal shares of his inheritance. But now it has come about that Sir Damas holds nearly all those estates, and Sir Ontzlake has only one castle, which he now holds by force of arms and by his own courage. For though Sir Damas is a coward in his heart, he has more craft and cunning than any man I ever heard of. And so it has turned out that of their father's inheritance Sir Damas has everything, and Sir Ontzlake has nothing but that one castle and the lands that go with it.

"Now you would think Sir Damas would be satisfied with all this. Yet he is not satisfied. He covets that one castle and that small estate of his brother's so much that he can hardly take any pleasure in life for wanting it. Yet he does not know how to get that estate away from his brother. For Sir Ontzlake is a very excellent knight, and the only way Sir Damas could lay hands on his land would be to fight his brother man to man in a contest of arms, and that he is afraid to try.

"So for a long time Sir Damas has been searching for a knight who will take up his cause for him and fight Sir Ontzlake on his behalf. And so every knight he can capture he brings to this castle, and he gives each one a choice: either to take up his cause against his brother, or else to stay in this place as his prisoner, with no ransom ever accepted. He has captured all of us like that, and he has demanded of each of us that we fight for him. But not one of us will take up the cause of a knight as wicked as Sir Damas, and so we all remain his prisoners."

"Well," said King Arthur, "this is a very strange business. But I think that if Sir Damas asks me, I will take up his cause, for I would rather do that than stay a prisoner here all my life. But if I take on this battle and win it, then afterward I will settle with Sir Damas himself, in a way that I do not believe will be very much to his liking."

A damsel comes to King Arthur.

King Arthur agrees to fight for Sir Damas.

Now a little while after this, the door of the prison was opened by the porter, and in came a very fair young damsel. She came to King Arthur and said to him, "How are you faring?" "I cannot tell," said King Arthur, "but it seems to me I am in a very sorry plight in this place." "Sir," said the damsel, "it grieves me to see so noble-looking a knight in so sad a case. But if you will undertake to fight with your own body for the cause of the lord of this castle against his enemy, then you shall have leave to go wherever you please." King Arthur answered her, "Lady, this is a very hard choice, that either I must fight a battle I do not care about or else stay a prisoner here, with no ransom, for all my days. But I would rather fight than live here all my life, and so I will undertake that adventure as you wish me to. But if I fight for the lord of this castle, and if by the grace of Heaven I win that battle, then all these, my companions in prison, must go free with me."

To this the damsel said, "Very well, so be it, for that will satisfy the master of this castle."

Then King Arthur looked more closely at the maiden, and he said, "Damsel, it seems to me that I know your face, for I think I have seen you somewhere before this." "No, sir," she said, "that can hardly be, for I am the daughter of the lord of this castle."

But in this she lied. For she was one of Morgan le Fay's damsels, and she was one of those who had lured King Arthur into the ship the night before, and it was she who had brought him to that castle and handed him over to Sir Damas. And she had done all these things at the command of Queen Morgan le Fay.

Then King Arthur said, "But if I fight this battle, you must carry a message for me to the Court of King Arthur, and that message must be delivered to Queen Morgan le Fay, into her own hands. Then, when that is done, I will fight this battle for the cause of Sir Damas." And the damsel said, "It shall be done."

Queen Morgan sends a false sword to King Arthur.

So King Arthur wrote a sealed letter to Queen Morgan le Fay, asking her to send him his sword Excalibur, and he sent that message to her. And when Queen Morgan received the letter she laughed and said, "Very well. He shall have a sword that will please his eye just as well as Excalibur." And with that she sent him the other sword, the one she had had made exactly like Excalibur.

Then Sir Damas sent word to his brother, Sir Ontzlake, that he now had a champion to fight on his behalf and win back all that part of their inheritance which Sir Ontzlake still kept from him.

Now when Sir Ontzlake received this message he was thrown into great distress of mind. For a little while before, he had been very badly wounded in a tournament, where a spear had been driven through both his thighs, so that he was now lying in bed with that wound and had no power to get up. So he did not know what to do, for he could not fight on his own behalf, and he had no one to fight for him.

Chapter 4: What Happened to Sir Accolon, and How King Arthur Fought a Duel with Swords and Nearly Lost His Life

Here follows the account of what happened to Sir Accolon on the morning after he went aboard that magic ship with King Arthur, as I have told you.

Sir Accolon finds himself beside a fountain.

Now when Sir Accolon woke from that same sleep, it was with him just as it had been with King Arthur: at first he did not know whether he was still asleep and dreaming or whether he was awake. For he found himself lying beside a marble basin of clear water that gushed up very high from a silver pipe. And he saw that not far from this fountain stood a large pavilion of many-colored silk, at the edge of a fair green meadow.

Sir Accolon was utterly astonished to find himself in this place, when he had fallen asleep on board that ship, and he was afraid that all this was the work of some very evil spell. So he crossed himself and said, "God save King Arthur from all harm, for it seems to me that those damsels on that ship have worked some magic on us to separate us from each other." And as he said this, he got up from where he lay, meaning to look further into the matter.

Now, as he made some noise in stirring himself, a very ugly dwarf came out of the pavilion and greeted him with every courtesy and with great respect. Then Sir Accolon said to the dwarf, "You there, who are you?" And the dwarf answered, "Sir, I belong to the lady of that pavilion over there, and she has sent me to bid you welcome to this place and to invite you in to share a meal with her." "Ha!" said Sir Accolon, "and how did I come here?" "Sir," said the dwarf, "I do not know. But when we looked out this morning we saw you lying here beside the fountain."

Then Sir Accolon marveled greatly at what had happened to him, and after a while he said, "Who is your lady?" And the dwarf replied, "She is named the Lady Gomyne of the Fair Hair, and she will be more than glad of your company in her pavilion."

Sir Accolon enters the pavilion of the Lady Gomyne.

At this Sir Accolon rose, and when he had washed himself at the fountain and so refreshed himself, he went with the dwarf to the lady's pavilion. And when he came there he saw that in the middle of the pavilion stood a table of silver, spread with a fair white cloth and covered with very excellent food for a man to break his fast with.

The moment Sir Accolon came into the pavilion, the curtains on its far side were parted, and a very beautiful lady came in from an inner room and welcomed Sir Accolon to that place. And Sir Accolon said to her, "Lady, I think you are very kind to invite me into your pavilion like this." "No, sir," said the lady, "it takes no great effort to be kind to a knight as worthy as you." Then she said to Sir Accolon, "Sir, will you sit here at the table with me and have your breakfast?"

Sir Accolon was very glad of this, for he was hungry. And the lady's beauty pleased him a great deal, so that it gave him great joy to be in her company.

So the two of them sat at the table in a very cheerful and pleasant mood, and the dwarf waited on them.

Now after Sir Accolon and the Lady of the Pavilion had eaten their breakfast, she spoke to him like this: "Sir Knight, you seem to be a very strong and worthy lord, and one well used to feats of arms and to proving his courage in battle."

To this Sir Accolon replied, "Lady, it is not fitting for me to speak of my own worth. But this much I may freely say: I have taken part in a number of fights, as a belted and spurred knight may do, and I believe both my friends and my enemies have had reason to say that I have always done my duty to the best of my power."

Then the damsel said, "I believe you are a very brave and worthy knight. And being such a knight, you might be of service to a good and worthy knight who sorely needs the kind of service one knight can give another."

Sir Accolon said, "What is that service?" And the damsel replied, "I will tell you. Not far from this place there lives a certain knight named Sir Ontzlake, who has an elder brother named Sir Damas. This Sir Damas has treated Sir Ontzlake very badly in many ways and has robbed him of nearly all his inheritance, so that only a little is left to Sir Ontzlake of all the great possessions that once were his father's. But Sir Damas begrudges Sir Ontzlake even that small holding, so that Sir Ontzlake has to keep what he has by whatever force of arms he can manage himself. Now Sir Damas has found himself a champion, a man of great strength and courage, and through this champion Sir Damas challenges Sir Ontzlake's right to keep even that small part of the lands that once were his father's. So if Sir Ontzlake wants to keep what is his, he must soon fight a battle for it.

"Now this is a very sad case for Sir Ontzlake. For a short time ago he was wounded by a spear at a tournament and pierced through both his thighs, and so he cannot now sit on his horse and defend his rights against attack. So it seems to me that a knight could have no better chance to show his prowess than in defending a cause as sad as this one."

So the lady spoke, and Sir Accolon listened with great attention to everything she said. And when she had finished he said, "Lady, I would indeed be very willing to defend Sir Ontzlake's right. But I have no armor, and I have no weapons to fight with."

Then the damsel smiled very kindly at Sir Accolon and said to him, "Sir, Sir Ontzlake can easily fit you out with armor that will be entirely to your liking. And as for weapons, I have in this pavilion a sword that has only one other like it in all the world."

At this she rose and went back into the curtained room she had come from, and soon she came back carrying something wrapped in a scarlet cloth. And she opened the cloth before Sir Accolon's eyes, and what she had there was King Arthur's sword Excalibur, in his sheath. Then the damsel said, "This sword shall be yours if you will take up this quarrel on behalf of Sir Ontzlake."

The Lady Gomyne shows Excalibur to Sir Accolon.

Sir Accolon agrees to fight for Sir Ontzlake.

Now when Sir Accolon saw that sword he did not know what to think, and he said to himself, "Surely either this is Excalibur or else it is his twin brother." With that he drew the blade out of its sheath, and it shone with extraordinary splendor. Then Sir Accolon said, "I do not know what to think, for pure wonder. This sword is the very image of another sword that I know of." When he said this the damsel smiled at him again, and she said, "I have heard it said that there is another sword in the world like this one."

Then Sir Accolon said, "Lady, to win this sword for myself I would be willing to fight in any battle whatever." And the damsel replied, "Then if you will fight this battle for Sir Ontzlake, you may keep that sword for your own." And at that Sir Accolon was glad beyond all measure.

So it came about that, through the tricks of Queen Morgan le Fay, King Arthur was brought, without knowing it, to fight a battle against a knight he loved very much, and that knight had Excalibur to use against his own master. For all these things had happened through the cunning of Morgan le Fay.

Now a fair field was made ready for the battle, in a place that suited both Sir Damas and Sir Ontzlake. They came there on the day appointed, each with his champion and his attendants, Sir Ontzlake being carried there in a litter because of the bad wound in his thighs. A great many other people came as well to watch the combat, for the news of it had spread far and wide all around that place. So when everything was ready, the two knights who were to fight on that field were brought inside the barriers, each fully armed and each mounted on a very good horse.

Now King Arthur was dressed all in Sir Damas's armor, and Sir Accolon was dressed in armor that belonged to Sir Ontzlake. And each one's head was covered by his helmet, so that neither of the two knew the other.

Then the herald came forward and announced that the battle was about to begin, and each knight at once made himself ready for the charge. Then the word to charge was given, and the two rushed out, each from his own end of the field, with such speed and fury that it was wonderful to see. They met in the middle of the course with a roar like thunder, and each knight's spear burst into small pieces, right down to the stump he held in his hand. At that each knight leaped from his horse with great skill and quickness and let it run loose in the field. And each threw aside the stump of his spear and drew his sword, and they came at each other with all the fury of battle.

Vivien comes to the field of battle.

It was at this time that Vivien came to that place, at Merlin's bidding. And she brought with her a train of followers so splendid and so beautiful that a great many people looked at her with great pleasure. So Vivien and her followers took their stand at the barriers, where they could see everything that happened. And Vivien watched the two knights, and she could not tell which one was King Arthur and which was his enemy. So she said, "Well, I will do as Merlin wished me to do. But I must watch this battle for a while before I can tell which one is King Arthur, for it would be a pity to cast my spells on the wrong knight."

King Arthur is badly wounded by Excalibur.

So the two knights came together in battle on foot. First they thrust at each other, and then they both struck at the same moment. And King Arthur's sword did not bite into Sir Accolon's armor, but Sir Accolon's sword bit very deep into King Arthur's armor and wounded him so badly that the blood ran down inside his armor in great streams. After that they struck very often and very powerfully, and as it was at first, so it was afterward: Sir Accolon's sword always bit into King Arthur's armor, and King Arthur's sword did not bite into his enemy's armor at all. So in a little while King Arthur's armor was stained red all over with the blood that flowed from a great many wounds, while Sir Accolon did not bleed at all, because of the sheath of Excalibur that he wore at his side. And King Arthur's blood ran down onto the ground, so that all the grass around was soaked with it. So when King Arthur saw how the ground was wet with his own blood, while his enemy did not bleed at all, he began to fear that he would die in that battle, and he said to himself, "How is this? Has the power gone out of Excalibur and his sheath? If it were not so, I would think that the sword that cuts me so badly is Excalibur, and that this sword is not Excalibur."

Then a great despair of death came over him, and he ran at Sir Accolon and struck him so hard a blow on the helmet that Sir Accolon nearly fell to the ground.

But with that blow King Arthur's sword snapped off short at the crosspiece of the hilt and fell into the grass among the blood, and the pommel and the crosspiece were all that King Arthur held in his hand.

Now that blow made Sir Accolon wild with rage, and he ran at King Arthur meaning to strike him some terrible blow. But when he saw that King Arthur had no weapon, he stopped his attack and said, "Sir Knight, I see that you have no weapon and that you have lost a great deal of blood. So I demand that you yield to me as a beaten knight."

Then King Arthur was again greatly afraid that his death was near. Yet because he was a king, it was not possible for him to yield to any knight. So he said, "No, Sir Knight, I may not yield to you, for I would rather die with honor than yield without honor. For though I lack a weapon, there are particular reasons why I may not lack honor. So you may kill me as I stand here without a weapon, and that will be your shame and not mine."

"Well," said Sir Accolon, "as for the shame, I will not spare you unless you yield to me." And King Arthur said, "I will not yield." Then Sir Accolon said, "Then stand away from me, so that I may strike you." And when King Arthur had done as Sir Accolon told him, Sir Accolon struck him such a dreadful blow that the King fell to his knees. Then Sir Accolon raised Excalibur to strike King Arthur again, and at that all the people who were there cried out to him to spare so honorable a knight. But Sir Accolon would not spare him.

Vivien puts a spell on Sir Accolon.

Then Vivien said to herself, "Surely that must be King Arthur who is so near to death. And I vow that it would be a great pity for him to die after he has fought so fiercely." So when Sir Accolon raised his sword the second time to strike his enemy, Vivien struck her hands together with great force and at the same moment cast a spell of such power that it seemed to Sir Accolon, that very instant, as though he had been dealt a very heavy blow on the arm. For with that spell his arm went numb all the way from his fingertips to the hollow of his armpit, and at that Excalibur fell out of Sir Accolon's hands into the grass.

Then King Arthur looked at the sword, and he saw that it was Excalibur, and in that moment he knew that he had been betrayed. So he cried out three times in a very loud voice, "Treason! Treason! Treason!" And with that he set his knee on the blade, and before Sir Accolon could stop him he had seized it in his hands.

King Arthur overcomes Sir Accolon.

Then it seemed to King Arthur that a great power had come into him because of that sword. So he rose from his knees and ran at Sir Accolon and struck him so hard that the blade cut through his armor to the depth of half a hand's breadth. And he struck him again and again, and Sir Accolon cried out in a loud voice and fell down on his hands and knees. Then King Arthur ran to him and caught hold of the sheath of Excalibur and tore it away from Sir Accolon and flung it aside, and at once Sir Accolon's wounds burst out bleeding heavily. Then King Arthur seized Sir Accolon's helmet and wrenched it off his head, meaning to kill him.

Now because King Arthur was blinded by his own blood, he did not recognize Sir Accolon, and he said, "Sir Knight, who are you, who have betrayed me?" And Sir Accolon said, "I have not betrayed you. I am Sir Accolon of Gaul, and I am a knight in good standing at King Arthur's Court."

But when King Arthur heard this he cried out aloud and said, "How is this? Do you know who I am?" And Sir Accolon said, "No, I do not know you." Then King Arthur said, "I am King Arthur, your master." And at that he took off his helmet, and Sir Accolon knew him.

And when Sir Accolon saw King Arthur, he fainted away and lay like a dead man on the ground. And King Arthur said, "Take him away."

Then, when the people who were there understood who King Arthur was, they burst over the barriers and ran toward him with a great cry of pity. And King Arthur would have left that place, but at that moment he too fainted away from the great loss of blood that had come from him. And all those who stood around were filled with sorrow, thinking that he was dying, and they lamented without end.

Then Vivien came out into the field and said, "Let me have him, for I believe I shall be able to cure his wounds." So she commanded that two litters should be brought, and she placed King Arthur in one litter and Sir Accolon in the other, and she carried them both away to a priory of nuns, a convent that was not far from that place.

Vivien heals King Arthur.

When Vivien had come there, she examined King Arthur's wounds and bathed them with a very precious balm, so that they began to heal at once. As for Sir Accolon, she would have nothing to do with his wounds, but let one of her attendants bathe him and dress his hurts.

Now when the next morning came, King Arthur had recovered so much that he was able to get up, though he was very weak and sick nearly to death. So he rose from his couch, and he would not let anyone stop him, and he wrapped a cloak around himself and went to the place where Sir Accolon lay. When he came there he questioned Sir Accolon very closely, and Sir Accolon told him everything that had happened to him after he left the ship, and how the strange damsel had given him a sword to fight with. So when King Arthur had heard all that Sir Accolon had to tell him, he said, "Sir, I think you are not to blame in this matter. But I very much fear that there is treachery here, meant to bring about my ruin."

Then he went out from that place, and he found Vivien and said to her, "Damsel, I beg you to dress that knight's wounds with the same balm that you used to dress mine." "Lord," said Vivien, "I cannot do that, for I have no more of that balm." But what she said was false, for she did have more of that balm. She simply did not choose to use it on Sir Accolon.

Sir Accolon dies of his wounds.

So that afternoon Sir Accolon died of the wounds he had received in his battle with King Arthur.

And that same day King Arthur summoned Sir Damas and Sir Ontzlake to come before him. They came and stood before him, but they were so filled with terror of his majesty that they did not have the strength to stand, and they fell down on their knees to him.

King Arthur deals with Sir Damas and Sir Ontzlake.

Then King Arthur said, "I will pardon you, for you did not know what you were doing. But you, Sir Damas, are, I believe, a very false and treacherous knight. So I shall take from you all your possessions except the one single castle that your brother had, and that I shall give to you. But all your other possessions I shall give to Sir Ontzlake. And I further command that you shall never again have the right to ride any horse but a palfrey, for you are not worthy to ride a war horse as a true knight has the right to do. And I command you to set free at once all those knights who were my companions in captivity, and to pay them for all the harm you have done them, as a court of chivalry shall decide."

With that he dismissed the two knights, and they were very glad that he had dealt with them so mercifully.

Conclusion

Queen Morgan comes to King Arthur.

Now shortly after that combat between King Arthur and Sir Accolon, the news of it was brought to Queen Morgan le Fay. And the next day after that she heard that Sir Accolon was dead, and she could not understand how her plans could have gone so wrong. Then she was uncertain how much King Arthur might know of her treachery, so she said to herself, "I will go and see my brother the King, and if he knows of my treason, I will beg him to pardon my wrongdoing." So, after inquiring carefully where King Arthur lay, she gathered together her court of knights and squires and set out for that place.

She came there on the fifth day after the battle, and when she arrived she asked those who were attending the King how he was faring. They answered her, "He is asleep, and he must not be disturbed." To this Queen Morgan le Fay replied, "No matter. I am not to be kept out, for I must see him and speak with him at once." And they did not dare to stop her, because she was the King's sister.

Queen Morgan steals the sheath of Excalibur.

So Queen Morgan went into the room where the King lay, and he did not wake at her coming. Then Queen Morgan was filled with hatred and a great desire for revenge, and she said to herself, "I will take Excalibur and his sheath and carry them away with me to Avalon, and my brother shall never see them again." So she went very softly to where King Arthur lay, and she looked at him as he slept, and she saw that he had Excalibur beside him and was holding the hilt of the sword in his hand as he slept. Then Queen Morgan said, "Alas for this! For if I try to take Excalibur away from him, perhaps he will wake and kill me for my treason." Then she looked and saw where the sheath of Excalibur lay at the foot of the couch. So she took the sheath of Excalibur very softly and wrapped it up in her cloak and went out of the room, and King Arthur did not wake at her going.

So Queen Morgan came out of the King's chamber and said to those who were attending him, "Do not wake the King, for he is sleeping very soundly." With that she mounted her horse and rode away from that place.

Now after a good while King Arthur woke, and he looked for the sheath of Excalibur, but he saw that it was gone. So he said at once, "Who has been here?" Those attending him answered, "Queen Morgan le Fay has been here. She came in and saw you, and went her way without waking you." Then King Arthur's heart misgave him, and he said, "I fear that she has dealt treacherously with me from the beginning to the end of these adventures."

At that he got up and summoned all his knights and squires and mounted his horse to pursue Queen Morgan, although he was still very sick and faint from his deep wounds and his loss of blood.

Now, just as the King was about to set out, Vivien came to him where he was, and she said, "Lord, take me with you. For if you do not, you will never recover the sheath of Excalibur, and you will never catch up with Queen Morgan le Fay." And King Arthur said, "Come with me, damsel, in God's name." So Vivien went with him in pursuit of Queen Morgan.

Now after a while, as she fled, Queen Morgan le Fay looked behind her, and she saw that Vivien was riding with King Arthur's party. At that her heart failed her, and she said, "I fear that I am now completely ruined. For I helped that damsel to gain so much knowledge of magic that I shall have no spells to save myself from her counter-spells. But at any rate, King Arthur shall never have the sheath of Excalibur again to help him in his hour of need."

Queen Morgan throws the sheath of Excalibur into the lake.

Now at that time they were passing along the shore of a lake of considerable size. So Queen Morgan le Fay took the sheath of Excalibur in both her hands and swung it by its belt above her head, and she threw it far out into the water.

And then a very strange miracle happened. For suddenly a woman's arm rose out of the water, and it was clothed in white. And it was adorned with many bracelets. And the hand of that arm caught the sheath of Excalibur and drew it down under the water, and no one ever saw that sheath again.

So the sheath of Excalibur was lost, and that was a grievous thing for King Arthur in later days, as you may one day read.

Queen Morgan works her magic.

Now after Queen Morgan le Fay had thrown the sheath of Excalibur into the lake, she went on a little farther, to a very lonely place where a great many rocks and stones lay scattered about on the ground. And when she had come to that place she worked very powerful spells of magic that Merlin had taught her. And by means of those spells she turned herself and all her court and all their horses into large round stones of many different sizes.

Then, a little while later, King Arthur came to that place with his knights and squires. And his heart was very heavy, for he had seen from a great distance how Queen Morgan le Fay had thrown the sheath of Excalibur into the lake.

Now when the King and his Court had come to that spot, the damsel Vivien called out to him to stop, and she said to him, "Lord, do you see all those great round stones?" "Yes," said the King, "I see them." Then Vivien said, "Those stones are Queen Morgan le Fay and the court that was with her. For this magic she has worked, to change herself and them into stones, is a certain thing that Merlin taught her. Now I myself know that magic, and I also know how to undo it whenever I wish. So, if you will promise to punish that wicked woman at once for all her treason by taking her life, then I will bring her back to her true shape, so that you may have her in your power."

King Arthur rebukes Vivien.

Then King Arthur looked at Vivien with great displeasure, and he said, "Damsel, you have a cruel heart! You yourself have suffered no harm at the hands of Queen Morgan. Why, then, would you have me kill her? If it were not for all you have done for me, I would be very much offended with you. As for her, I forgive her all of this, and I shall forgive her again and again and yet again if she sins against me. For her mother was my mother, and the blood that flows in her veins and in mine comes from the same fountainhead, and so I will do no evil thing against her. Let us go back the way we came."

Then Vivien looked at King Arthur very bitterly, and she laughed with great scorn and said, "You are both a fool and a feeble-minded dotard." And with that she vanished from the sight of everyone.

And after that, because King Arthur had rebuked her for her wickedness in front of others, she hated him even more than Morgan le Fay had hated him.

Some time after that, King Arthur heard how Merlin had been tricked by Vivien, and he grieved very bitterly that Merlin was lost to the world in that way.

So ends the story of the passing of Merlin.

The Book of Three Worthies, Part Two: The Story of Sir Pelleas

Foreword

Here follows the story of Sir Pelleas, whom many people called the Gentle Knight.

For Sir Pelleas was the kind of man of whom it was said that all women loved him and came to no harm by it, and that all men loved him and were much the better for it.

And so, in the end, when he won for his beloved that beautiful Lady of the Lake, who was one of the very highest damsels of Fairyland, and when he went to live as lord and master in that wonderful dwelling place which no mortal but he and Sir Lancelot of the Lake had ever seen, then all men rejoiced at his great good fortune. And yet the whole Court of King Arthur grieved that he had gone so far away from them, never to return again.

So I believe you will take pleasure in reading the history of Sir Pelleas that is written here for your instruction and delight.

Chapter 1: How Queen Guinevere Went Maying, and How Sir Pelleas Took On a Quest for Her

Queen Guinevere goes Maying.

Now it happened one pleasant day in the springtime that Queen Guinevere went Maying, out into the fields to welcome the spring, with a fine company of the knights and ladies of her Court. Among those knights were Sir Pelleas, and Sir Geraint, and Sir Dinadan, and Sir Aglovale, and Sir Agravain, and Sir Constantine of Cornwall, and various others, so that hardly such another Court was to be found in all the world, then or before or since.

The day was wonderfully pleasant. The sunlight was all yellow, like gold, and the breeze was soft and gentle. The little birds sang with very great joy, and everywhere there bloomed so many flowers of every kind that the meadows were all carpeted with their tender green. So it seemed to Queen Guinevere that it was very good to be out in the fields and under the open sky at such a season.

A damsel comes to the May party.

Now as the Queen and her Court walked joyfully among the blossoms, one of the damsels who waited on the Lady Guinevere suddenly cried out, "Look! Look! Who is that coming over there?" At that Queen Guinevere raised her eyes, and she saw a damsel riding across the meadows on a milk-white palfrey, with three pages dressed in sky-blue. The damsel too was dressed entirely in blue. She wore a finely made chain of gold around her neck and a band of gold around her forehead, and her hair, which was as yellow as gold, was wrapped all about with ribbons of blue embroidered with gold. And one of the pages who followed the damsel carried a square frame, not very large, which was wrapped and covered with a curtain of crimson satin.

When the Queen saw that fine company approaching, she told one of the knights who attended her to go out and meet the damsel. And the knight who went out at her command was Sir Pelleas.

Sir Pelleas talks with the damsel.

So when Sir Pelleas met the damsel and her three pages, he spoke to her like this: "Fair damsel, I am commanded by the lady over there to greet you and to ask you the favor of your name and your errand."

"Sir Knight," said the damsel, "I can see from your face and your bearing that you are some lord of very high rank and great nobility, so I will gladly tell you that my name is Parcenet. I am a damsel belonging to the Court of a certain very great lady who lives a long way from here, and who is called the Lady Ettard of Grantmesnle. I have come here hoping to be brought before Queen Guinevere. So if you can tell me where I may find that noble lady, I shall certainly be very much in your debt."

"Ha, Lady!" said Sir Pelleas. "You will not have very far to go to find Queen Guinevere, for there she walks, over there, surrounded by her Court of lords and ladies." Then the damsel said, "Please, take me to her."

So Sir Pelleas brought Parcenet to the Queen, and Queen Guinevere received her very graciously, saying, "Damsel, what is it you want of us?"

The damsel tells Queen Guinevere about the Lady Ettard.

"Lady," said the damsel, "I will tell you that very readily. The Lady Ettard, my mistress, is thought by everyone in the country where she lives to be the most beautiful lady in the world. Now, lately, such reports have come to us of your great beauty that the Lady Ettard has seen fit to send me here to find out with my own eyes whether what is said of you is really true. And indeed, Lady, now that I stand before you, I must say that you are the fairest lady my eyes have ever seen, unless it is the Lady Ettard herself."

At that Queen Guinevere laughed with great merriment. And she said, "It seems to me a very funny business that you should have traveled so far for so small a matter." Then she said, "Tell me, damsel, what is it that your page carries so carefully wrapped up in that curtain of crimson satin?"

"Lady," said the damsel, "it is a true and perfect likeness of the Lady Ettard, my mistress."

Then Queen Guinevere said, "Show it to me."

The damsel shows the Lady Ettard's picture to the Queen and her Court.

At this the page who carried the picture got down from his palfrey, and, coming to Queen Guinevere, he knelt on one knee and uncovered the picture so that the Queen and her Court could look at it. Then they all saw that the picture was very skillfully painted on a panel of ivory, framed in gold and set with many jewels of different colors. And they saw that it was the picture of a lady of such extraordinary beauty that everyone who looked at it marveled. "Why, damsel!" said Queen Guinevere, "your lady is indeed blessed with wonderful beauty. If she truly looks like that picture, then I believe her equal in loveliness is not to be found anywhere in the world."

At this Sir Pelleas spoke up and said, "Not so, Lady. For I declare, and I am ready to defend my words at the risk of my life, that you yourself are far more beautiful than that picture."

"Well, well, Sir Knight!" said the damsel Parcenet. "It is a good thing you say so this far away from Grantmesnle. For at that place there is a certain knight named Sir Engamore of Malverat, who is a very strong knight indeed, and who upholds the opposite of what you say, in favor of the Lady Ettard, against all comers who dare to fight him."

Sir Pelleas takes on the adventure in honor of the Queen.

Then Sir Pelleas knelt down before Queen Guinevere and put his palms together. "Lady," he said, "I beg you of your grace to honor me so far as to accept me as your true knight in this matter. For I would gladly attempt an adventure on your behalf, if I have your permission to do so. So, if you give me leave, I will go out at once to meet this knight the damsel speaks of, and I very much hope that when I find him I shall overthrow him, to the increase of your glory and honor."

Then Queen Guinevere laughed again out of pure merriment. "Sir," she said, "it pleases me beyond measure that you should take so small a quarrel as this on yourself for my sake. For if you take up so small a quarrel, how much more readily would you take up a serious quarrel of mine! So I accept you very joyfully as my champion in this affair. Now go at once and arm yourself in whatever way is fitting for this adventure."

"Lady," said Sir Pelleas, "if I have your leave, I will go into this affair dressed just as I am. For I hope to win armor and equipment for myself along the way, and in that case this adventure will be still more to your credit than it would otherwise be."

The Queen was very much pleased at this, that her knight should undertake so serious an adventure dressed only in his holiday clothes, and so she said, "Let it be as you wish." Then she told her page, Florian, to go and fetch the best horse he could find for Sir Pelleas. And Florian, running with all speed, soon came back with a noble steed, so black that I believe there was not a single white hair on him.

Then Sir Pelleas said farewell to Queen Guinevere and her merry May Court, and they bade him farewell with great cheering. And then he mounted his horse and rode away with the damsel Parcenet and the three pages dressed in blue.

When they had gone some distance, the damsel Parcenet said, "Sir, I do not know your name or your rank, or who you are."

To this Sir Pelleas said, "Damsel, my name is Pelleas, and I am a knight of King Arthur's Round Table."

Sir Pelleas and Parcenet talk together.

At that Parcenet was very much astonished, for Sir Pelleas was thought by many to be the best knight-at-arms alive, except only King Arthur and King Pellinore. So she cried out, "Sir, it will certainly be a very great honor for Sir Engamore to fight with so famous a knight as you." To this Sir Pelleas said, "Damsel, I think there are several knights of King Arthur's Round Table who are better knights than I am." But Parcenet said, "I cannot believe that is so."

Then after a while Parcenet said to Sir Pelleas, "Sir, how will you get yourself armor to fight Sir Engamore?" "Maiden," said Sir Pelleas, "I do not know just now where I shall find armor. But before the time comes for me to fight Sir Engamore, I have faith that I shall find armor fit for my purpose. For you must understand that it is not always the armor a man wears on his body that brings him success. More often it is the spirit that lifts him up to his undertakings."

Then Parcenet said, "Sir Pelleas, I do not believe it often happens that a lady has so good a knight as you to fight on her behalf." To which Sir Pelleas said very cheerfully, "Damsel, when your time comes, I hope you will have a much better knight than I am to serve you." "Sir," said Parcenet, "nothing like that is likely to happen to me." At this Sir Pelleas laughed with a very light heart. Then Parcenet said, "Heigh-ho! I wish I had a good knight to serve me."

To this Sir Pelleas answered very gravely, "Maiden, the first one I catch I will give to you for your very own. Now, would you like him fair or dark, short or tall? For if you would rather have him short and fair, I will let the tall dark one go; but if you would rather have him tall and dark, I will let the other sort go."

Then Parcenet looked very steadily at Sir Pelleas, and she said, "I would like him about as tall as you are, with the same color of hair and eyes, and with a straight nose like yours, and with a good wit such as you have."

"Alas!" said Sir Pelleas. "I wish you had told me this before we came so far from Camelot, for I could easily have found you such a knight there. They have them in such plenty at that place that they keep them in wicker cages and sell them two for a penny." At this Parcenet laughed very cheerfully and said, "Then Camelot must be a very wonderful place, Sir Pelleas."

So, talking merrily, they traveled on their way with great joy and contentment, taking much pleasure in the springtime and in the pleasant meadows they rode through, without a care of any sort, their hearts full of cheerfulness and goodwill.

That night they stayed at a very quaint and pleasant inn that stood at the edge of the Forest of Usk. And the next morning they set out early, in the freshness of the new day, leaving that place and riding into the shadows of the forest.

Sir Pelleas and Parcenet come to the Forest of Adventure.

Now, after they had traveled a good distance in that forest, the damsel Parcenet said to Sir Pelleas, "Sir, do you know what part of the woods this is?" "No," said Sir Pelleas. "Well," said Parcenet, "this part of the woodland is sometimes called Arroy, and sometimes the Forest of Adventure. For I must tell you it is a very wonderful place, full of magic of many kinds. It is said that no knight may enter this forest without some adventure happening to him."

"Damsel," said Sir Pelleas, "what you tell me is very good news. For if we should happen on some adventure here, perhaps I may be able to get armor suitable for my purpose."

So they went straight into the Forest of Adventure, and they traveled through it for a long way, marveling greatly at the look of the place they had come into. For the forest was very dark and silent and wonderfully strange, and altogether different from any other place they had ever seen. So it seemed to them that it would not be at all surprising if some extraordinary adventure should happen to them.

They find an old woman beside the stream.

After they had traveled like this for a good while, they suddenly came out of the thicker part of the woods into a wide open clearing. And there in front of them they saw a violent stream of water, which rushed along very wildly with a great roar of many noises. And they saw that beside the stream there was a thorn tree, and under the thorn tree a bank of green moss, and on the bank of moss there sat an aged woman who looked very miserable. For that old woman was extraordinarily withered with age. Her eyes were all red, as though they watered with endless weeping, many bristles grew on her cheeks and chin, and her face was covered with so many wrinkles that there was no part of it free of them.

When the old woman saw Sir Pelleas and Parcenet and the three pages coming toward the place where she sat, she called out in a loud voice, "Sir, will you not carry me over this water on your horse? For you see, I am very old and feeble, and I cannot cross this river by myself."

Then Parcenet scolded the old woman, saying, "Hush, be quiet! Who are you, to ask this noble knight to do you such a service as that?"

Sir Pelleas carries the old woman across the water.

But Sir Pelleas was not pleased with Parcenet, and he said, "Damsel, you do not speak rightly in this matter. What becomes a true knight is to give help to anyone at all who needs it. For King Arthur is the perfect mirror of knighthood, and he has taught his knights to help all who ask them for help, whatever their rank may be." With these words Sir Pelleas got down from his horse and lifted the old woman up onto the saddle. Then he mounted again himself and rode straight into the ford of the river, and so brought the old woman safely across the torrent to the other side. And Parcenet followed him, marveling greatly at his knightliness, and the three pages followed her.

Of the wonderful Lady of the Lake.

When they had reached the other side of the water, Sir Pelleas got down, meaning to help the old woman off the horse. But she did not wait for his help. Instead she leaped down very lightly at once from where she sat. And suddenly Sir Pelleas saw that she whom he had taken for an aged and withered old crone was really a very strange and wonderful lady of extraordinary beauty. And, marveling greatly, he saw that she was dressed in clothing of a kind that neither he nor anyone else there had ever seen before. Because of the way she looked he knew that she was no ordinary mortal, but surely a creature of enchantment. For he saw that her face was wonderfully clear and as white as ivory, and that her eyes were very black and extraordinarily bright, like two jewels set in ivory. He saw that she was dressed all in green from head to foot, and that her hair was long and perfectly black, as soft and glossy as fine silk. And he saw that she wore around her neck a collar of opals and emeralds set in gold, and that around her wrists were bracelets of finely worked gold set with opals and emeralds. And from all these things he knew that she must be one of the fairy folk.

(For this was how the Lady Nimue of the Lake looked; this was how she had appeared to King Arthur, and this was how she appeared now to Sir Pelleas and those who were with him.)

So, seeing the wonderful magic of that lady, Sir Pelleas knelt down before her and put his hands together, palm to palm. But the Lady of the Lake said, "Sir, why do you kneel to me?" "Lady," said Sir Pelleas, "because you are so wonderfully strange and beautiful." "Sir," said the Lady of the Lake, "you have done me a very good service, and you are certainly a most excellent knight. So rise, and kneel no longer!" So Sir Pelleas got up from his knees and stood before her, and he said, "Lady, who are you?" And she answered, "I am one who feels a very great kindness toward King Arthur and all his knights. My name is Nimue, and I am the chief of those Ladies of the Lake you may have heard tell of. I took on the form of a wretched old woman to test your knightliness, and, as you see, I have not found you lacking in true service." Then Sir Pelleas said, "Lady, you have certainly done me a great favor in this." At that the Lady of the Lake smiled at Sir Pelleas very kindly, and she said, "Sir, I have a mind to do you a greater favor than that."

The Lady of the Lake gives Sir Pelleas the collar of gold and jewels.

And with these words she at once took from around her neck that collar of opals and emeralds and gold, and hung it around the shoulders of Sir Pelleas, so that it lay on his breast in a wonderful glory of shimmering colors.

"Keep this," she said, "for it has very powerful magic."

And with that she vanished instantly from the sight of everyone there, leaving them astonished and amazed beyond measure at what had happened.

And Sir Pelleas was like a man in a dream, for he did not know whether what he had seen was a vision, or whether he had seen it with his waking eyes. So he mounted his horse in complete silence, as though he did not know what he was doing, and in complete silence he led the way from that place. Nor did any of the others speak just then. Only after they had gone a good distance did Parcenet say, in a frightened voice, "Sir, that was a very wonderful thing that happened to us." And Sir Pelleas said, "Yes, maiden."

Now the necklace that the Lady of the Lake had hung around Sir Pelleas's neck had this power: whoever wore it was loved by everyone who looked at him. For the collar was enchanted with that particular power. But Sir Pelleas knew nothing at all about this, and so he simply took joy in the rare beauty of the jewel the Lady of the Lake had given him.

Chapter 2: How Sir Pelleas Defeated a Red Knight Named Sir Adresack and Freed Twenty-Two Prisoners from His Castle

Now, after that wonderful happening, they traveled on without stopping for a long while. They did not pause anywhere until, about an hour after the first hours of the morning, they came to a part of the forest where charcoal-burners were at work at their trade. Here Sir Pelleas ordered them to draw rein and rest for a while, and so they dismounted to rest and refresh themselves, just as he had ordered.

A sorrowful lady and a squire come into the forest.

Now as they sat there refreshing themselves with food and drink, there came suddenly out of the forest a sound of great weeping and loud crying, and almost at once a lady in a pitiful state rode out of the thickets into that open place, mounted on a spotted palfrey. Behind her rode a young squire dressed in the colors green and white and seated on a sorrel horse. He too seemed to be full of great sorrow, for he was all in disorder and very downcast in his face. The lady's face was all swollen and red with weeping, and her hair hung down on her shoulders with neither net nor band to hold it in place, and her clothes were badly torn by the brambles and much stained from traveling through the forest. And the young squire who rode behind her came with his head drooping and his clothes in the same sad disorder, his cloak dragging behind him, fastened to his shoulder by only a single lace.

Now when Sir Pelleas saw the lady and the squire in such a sorry state, he got up at once from where he sat, went straight to the lady, took her horse by the bridle, and stopped it where it was. The lady looked at him, but she did not see him, for she was altogether blinded by her grief and distress. Then Sir Pelleas said to her, "Lady, what is wrong, that you are grieving so terribly?" And she answered him, "Sir, it does not matter, for you cannot help me." "How do you know that?" said Sir Pelleas. "I fully intend to help you, if I can possibly do so."

Then the lady looked more closely at Sir Pelleas, and she made him out as though through a mist of sorrow. She saw that he was not dressed in armor, but only in holiday clothes of fine crimson cloth. At that she began weeping all over again, and very bitterly, for she thought that here was someone who could give her no help in her trouble. So she said, "Sir, you mean kindly, but how can you hope to help me, when you have neither weapons nor armor to aid you in taking up such a quarrel?" But Sir Pelleas said, "Lady, I cannot tell how I may help you until you tell me what your sorrow is. Yet I have good hope that I may serve you once I know what it is that has troubled your mind so." Then, still holding her horse by the bridle, he led the lady forward to the place where Parcenet still sat beside the cloth spread with the food they had been eating. When he had come there, he very gently persuaded the lady to get down from her horse. Then, just as gently, he made her sit down on the grass and eat some of the food. When she had done so, and had drunk some of the wine, she found herself greatly refreshed, and she began to take heart again. Then, seeing her so far recovered, Sir Pelleas asked her once more what her trouble was, and begged her to open her heart to him.

So, encouraged by his cheerful words, she told Sir Pelleas about the trouble that had brought her to such a state.

The sorrowful lady tells her story.

"Sir Knight," she said, "the place where I live is a long way from here. I came from there this morning with a very good knight named Sir Brandemere, who is my husband. We have been married only a little more than four weeks, so that until this morning our happiness was still entirely new to us. Now this morning Sir Brandemere wanted to take me out hunting at the break of day, and so we went out with a little hunting hound that my knight was wonderfully fond of. When we came to a certain place in the forest, a doe suddenly sprang up in front of us, and the hound chased after it at once, barking and baying furiously. Then my lord and I and this squire followed after it, with great high spirits and delight in the chase. Now when we had followed the doe and the hound for a long distance (the hound chasing the doe with a great passion of eagerness), we came to a place where we saw ahead of us a rushing stream of water, crossed by a long, narrow bridge. And we saw that on the other side of the stream there stood a strong castle with seven towers, and that the castle was built up on the rocks in such a way that the rocks and the castle looked altogether like one single rock.

"Now, as we came near that bridge, all of a sudden the portcullis of the castle was raised and the drawbridge was let down very suddenly and with a great noise, and out of the castle at once rode a knight dressed entirely in red. All the trappings and gear of his horse were red as well, and the spear he carried in his hand was of ash wood painted red. He came out in a very terrible way, and rode forward until in a moment he stood at the far end of that narrow bridge. Then he shouted aloud to Sir Brandemere, my husband, 'Where do you think you are going, Sir Knight?' And Sir Brandemere answered him, 'Sir, I want to cross this bridge, for my hound, which I love dearly, has crossed here chasing a doe.' Then that Red Knight cried out in a loud voice, 'Sir Knight, you set foot on this bridge only at your peril. For this bridge belongs to me, and whoever wants to cross it must first overthrow me, or else he may not cross.'

"Now my husband, Sir Brandemere, was dressed at that time only in light clothing, such as a man might wear for hunting or for hawking, except that on his head he wore a light steel cap wrapped with a scarf I had given him. Even so, he was so great of heart that he would not let any such challenge from that Red Knight go unanswered. So, telling me and this squire (whose name is Ponteferet) to stay on the near side of the bridge, he drew his sword and rode forward to the middle of the bridge, meaning to force his way across if he could. And when that Red Knight, who was dressed in full armor, saw what my husband meant to do, he threw aside his spear, drew his sword, and rode forward to meet my knight. So they met in the middle of the bridge, and when they met, that Red Knight raised himself in his stirrups and struck my husband, Sir Brandemere, on the crown of his steel cap with his sword. And I saw the blade of the Red Knight's sword cut through Sir Brandemere's cap and deep into his skull, so that the blood ran down over the knight's face in a great flood. Then Sir Brandemere fell straight down from his horse and lay there as though he were dead.

"Having overthrown him in this way, that Red Knight got down from his horse and lifted Sir Brandemere back onto the horse he had fallen from, so that he lay across the saddle. Then, taking both horses by the bridles, the Red Knight led them straight back across the bridge and into his castle. As soon as he had gone into the castle, the portcullis was closed behind him at once and the drawbridge was raised. He paid no attention at all either to me or to Ponteferet the squire, but went away leaving us without one word of comfort. And now I do not know whether my husband, Sir Brandemere, is alive or dead, or what has happened to him."

And as the lady spoke these words, the tears poured down her face again.

Sir Pelleas takes up an adventure to help the sorrowful lady.

Then Sir Pelleas was deeply moved with pity, and he said, "Lady, your case is truly a very sorrowful one, and I am greatly grieved for you. Indeed, I would gladly help you as far as it lies in my power. So, if you will lead me to this bridge and to that grim castle you speak of, I make you my vow that I will try my very best to find out where your good knight is, and what has happened to him."

"Sir," said the lady, "I am very grateful to you for your goodwill. Yet you cannot hope to succeed if you venture to take on so dangerous an adventure as that without either weapons or armor to defend yourself. For think how cruelly that Red Knight treated my husband, Sir Brandemere, with no regard at all for his lack of weapons or armor. So it is not likely that he will treat you any more courteously." And Parcenet too raised her voice loudly in support of the lady's words, begging Sir Pelleas not to be so unwise as to do what he had in mind. And Ponteferet the squire called out to Sir Pelleas in the same way, telling him he should not do this thing, or that at the very least he should arm himself before he set out on this adventure.

But to everything they said Sir Pelleas answered, "Do not hold me back from what I mean to do. For I tell you all that I have more than once taken on adventures even more dangerous than this, and yet I have escaped without any great harm to myself." And he would not listen to anything the lady or the damsel might say, but got up from that place and helped the lady and the damsel onto their palfreys. Then he mounted his own horse, and when the squire and the pages had mounted theirs, the whole party set off from that place at once.

They come to the castle of the Red Knight.

So they traveled a long way through the forest, with Ponteferet the squire telling them which way to go so as to bring them at last to the castle of the Red Knight. At last they came to a more open place in that wilderness, where a steep, bare hill rose in front of them. When they had climbed to the top of that hill, they saw below them a river, very wild and rushing. They also saw that the river was spanned by a bridge, very straight and narrow, and that on the far side of the bridge and the river there stood a very strong castle with seven tall towers. What is more, the castle and its towers were built up on the rocks, very lofty and high, so that it was hard to tell where the rocks ended and the walls began, and the towers and the walls looked altogether like one rock of stone.

Then Ponteferet the squire pointed with his finger and said, "Sir Knight, that is the castle of the Red Knight over there, and he carried Sir Brandemere into it after he had wounded him so terribly." Then Sir Pelleas said to the lady, "Lady, I will go at once and ask after your husband's welfare."

With that he set spurs to his horse and rode down the hill toward the bridge very boldly. And when he had come nearer to the bridge, suddenly the portcullis of the castle was raised and the drawbridge was let down with a great noise and clatter, and at once out of the castle rode a knight dressed all in red armor and red gear, and this knight came forward at great speed toward the head of the bridge. When Sir Pelleas saw him coming on so threateningly, he said to those who had followed him down the hill, "Stay where you are, and I will go forward and speak with this knight, and ask him about the injury he has done to Sir Brandemere." At this Ponteferet the squire said to him, "Wait, Sir Knight, you will be hurt." But Sir Pelleas said, "No, I will not be hurt."

So he went out onto the bridge very boldly, and when the Red Knight saw him coming, he said, "Ha! Who are you, who dare to come onto my bridge like this?"

Sir Pelleas answered him, "It does not matter who I am. But you are to know, you discourteous knight, that I have come to ask you where you have put that good knight Sir Brandemere, and to ask you why you treated him so cruelly a little while ago."

At this the Red Knight grew very full of rage. "Ha! Ha!" he cried fiercely, "you will soon find that out, to your great sorrow! For as I have treated him, so will I quickly treat you, and in a little while I will bring you to him. Then you may ask him whatever you please. But since you are unarmed and without armor, I would not want to do you any bodily harm. So I demand that you surrender yourself to me at once. Otherwise it will be very much to your pain and sorrow, if you force me to use violence to make you surrender."

Then Sir Pelleas said, "What! What! Would you really attack a knight who is entirely without weapons or armor, as I am?" And the Red Knight said, "Certainly I will, if you do not yield yourself to me at once."

"Then," said Pelleas, "you are not fit to be dealt with as a proven knight should be dealt with. So, if I fight you, your overthrow must be of a kind that would shame any belted knight who wears golden spurs."

Sir Pelleas overthrows the Red Knight with a great stone.

With that he looked around him for some weapon to suit his purpose, and he saw that a certain huge stone lay loose on the top of the bridge's wall. This stone was so big that five men of ordinary strength could hardly have lifted it. But Sir Pelleas lifted it out of its place with great ease, and, raising it in both hands, he ran quickly toward the Red Knight and flung the rock at him with tremendous force. The stone struck the Red Knight in the middle of his shield and drove the shield back against his chest with great violence. And the force of the blow drove the knight backward out of his saddle, so that he fell from his horse to the ground with a terrible crash and lay on the bridge as though he were altogether dead.

And when the people inside the castle who were looking out saw that blow, and when they saw the Red Knight overthrown, they raised their voices in loud wailing, so that the outcry was terrible to hear.

But Sir Pelleas ran as fast as he could to the fallen knight and set his knee on his chest. He unlaced the knight's helmet and lifted it off. And he saw that the knight's face was strong and handsome, and that he was not altogether dead.

So when Sir Pelleas saw that the Red Knight was not dead, and saw that he was about to get his breath back after the blow he had taken, he drew that knight's misericord from its sheath and set the point at his throat. So when the Red Knight came to from his swoon, he saw death before him, in the face of Sir Pelleas and in the point of the dagger.

When the Red Knight saw how near death was, he begged Sir Pelleas for mercy, saying, "Spare my life!" And Sir Pelleas said, "Who are you?" And the knight said, "My name is Sir Adresack, and men call me Adresack of the Seven Towers." Then Sir Pelleas said to him, "What have you done with Sir Brandemere, and how is that good knight faring?" And the Red Knight answered, "He is not so badly wounded as you think."

Now when Sir Brandemere's lady heard these words, she was filled with such joy that she clapped her hands together, crying out loud in thanksgiving.

But Sir Pelleas said, "Now tell me, Sir Adresack, do you have other prisoners at your castle besides that knight, Sir Brandemere?" Sir Adresack answered, "Sir Knight, I will tell you truly. There are twenty-one other prisoners in my castle besides him: that is, eighteen knights and squires of rank, and three ladies. For I have guarded this bridge for a long time, and everyone who has tried to cross it I have taken prisoner and held for ransom. And in that way I have gathered great wealth and won great estates."

Sir Pelleas lays his commands on the Red Knight.

Then Sir Pelleas said, "You are truly a wicked and discourteous knight, to treat travelers who come your way like this, and I would do well to kill you where you lie. But since you have begged me for mercy, I will grant it to you, though only with great shame to your knighthood. What is more, if I spare your life, there are several things you must do. First, you must go to Queen Guinevere at Camelot, and there you must tell her that the knight who left her unarmed has taken your armor from you and has armed himself with it to defend her honor. Second, you must confess your wrongs to King Arthur, just as you have confessed them to me, and you must beg his pardon for them, asking him in his mercy to spare your life. These are the things you must do."
