To this Sir Adresack said, "Very well. I promise to do these things, if you will spare my life."

Sir Pelleas puts on the armor of Sir Adresack.

Then Sir Pelleas let him get up, and he came and stood before Sir Pelleas. And Sir Pelleas called Ponteferet the squire to him and said, "Take this knight's armor off his body and put it on mine, as you know how to do." And Ponteferet did as Sir Pelleas told him. He unarmed Sir Adresack and dressed Sir Pelleas in Sir Adresack's armor, while Sir Adresack stood ashamed before them all. Then Sir Pelleas said to him, "Now take me into your castle, so that I may free the prisoners you so wickedly hold there." And Sir Adresack said, "It shall be done as you command."

So they all went together to the castle and into it, and it was an exceedingly stately place. There they saw a great many servants and attendants, and these came at Sir Adresack's command and bowed down before Sir Pelleas. Then Sir Pelleas told Sir Adresack to send for the keeper of the dungeon, and Sir Adresack did so. And Sir Pelleas ordered the keeper to lead them to the dungeon, and the keeper bowed down before him in obedience.

Now when they came to that dungeon, they saw that it was a very lofty place and exceedingly strong. And there they found Sir Brandemere and the others Sir Adresack had spoken of.

Sir Pelleas frees the prisoners.

And when that sorrowful lady saw Sir Brandemere, she ran to him with loud cries of joy, and threw her arms around him and wept over him. And he held her and wept, and forgot his wound entirely in the joy of seeing her again.

In the various rooms of that part of the castle there were, in all, eighteen knights and squires and three ladies, besides Sir Brandemere. Among those knights were two from King Arthur's court: Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte. And when these two saw that it was Sir Pelleas who had set them free, they came to him and embraced him with great joy and kissed him on both cheeks.

And all those who had been set free rejoiced greatly, and gave Sir Pelleas such praise and acclaim that he was well pleased with it.

Then, when Sir Pelleas saw all the prisoners who had been in that dungeon, he grew very angry with Sir Adresack, and he turned to him and said, "Go, Sir Knight, and do the penance I have laid on you. For I am very much displeased with you, and I am afraid I may come to regret the mercy I have shown you."

At that Sir Adresack turned away and left that place at once. He called his squire to him, took him along, and rode off to Camelot to do the penance he had promised Sir Pelleas to do.

After he had gone, Sir Pelleas and the prisoners he had freed went through the different parts of the castle. And there they found thirteen chests of gold and silver money and four caskets of jewels, very fine and very brilliant. All this treasure had been paid as ransom by prisoners who had been held there by force in times past.

Sir Pelleas ordered all those chests and caskets to be opened, and when the people there looked inside them, all their hearts leapt with joy at the sight of that great treasure.

Sir Pelleas divides the treasures of Sir Adresack among the prisoners.

Then Sir Pelleas ordered all that treasure of gold and silver to be divided into nineteen equal parts. When it had been divided, he said, "Now let each of you who has been held prisoner in this place take one part of this treasure for his own, to repay him for the sorrows he has suffered." What is more, to each of the ladies who had been held prisoner there he gave a casket of jewels, saying to her, "Take this casket of jewels to repay you for the sorrow you have suffered." And to Sir Brandemere's lady he gave a casket of the jewels for what she had suffered.

But when the people there saw that Sir Pelleas had kept no part of that great treasure for himself, they all cried out to him, "Sir Knight! Sir Knight! What is this? Look, you have set aside no part of this treasure for yourself."

Then Sir Pelleas answered, "You are right, I have not. For there is no need for me to take any of this gold and silver, or any of these jewels, for myself. You see, you have suffered much at the hands of Sir Adresack, and so you ought to be repaid for it. But I have suffered nothing at his hands, and so I need no such repayment."

Then they were all amazed at his generosity, and praised him highly for his greatness of heart. And all those knights swore to be faithful to him until death.

They stay at the castle of Sir Brandemere.

Then, when all these things were done, Sir Brandemere begged everyone there to come with him to his castle, so that they might refresh themselves with a time of merriment and good cheer. They all said they would go with him, and they did go. And at Sir Brandemere's castle there was great rejoicing, with feasting and jousting, for three days.

And everyone there loved Sir Pelleas with an astonishing love, because of that collar of emeralds and opals and gold. Yet no one knew of the magic power of that collar, and Sir Pelleas did not know of it either.

So Sir Pelleas stayed at that place for three days. And when the fourth day came, he got up early in the morning and ordered his horse to be saddled, and the palfrey of the damsel Parcenet, and the horses of their pages.

Then, when everyone there saw that he meant to leave, they begged him not to go. But Sir Pelleas said, "Do not hold me back, for I must go."

Then those two knights of Arthur's court, Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte, came to him and begged him to let them go with him on that adventure. At first Sir Pelleas refused them, but they begged him all the more, so that at last he was glad to say, "You shall go with me."

So he left that place with his company, and all those who stayed behind were very sorry that he had gone away.

Chapter 3: How Sir Pelleas Fought Sir Engamore, the Knight of the Green Sleeves, and What Happened to the Lady Ettard

Now Sir Pelleas and his party, and the damsel Parcenet and hers, traveled on until some time in the afternoon they came to the farthest edge of the forest. There the woodlands ended altogether, and many fields and meadows lay spread out beneath the sky, with farms and small crofts and orchards of trees all in bloom with tender leaves and sweet-smelling blossoms.

And Sir Pelleas said, "This is truly a very beautiful land we have come into." At that the damsel Parcenet was very well pleased, and she said, "Sir, I am very glad that what you see pleases you, for all this country belongs to the Lady Ettard, and it is my home. What is more, from the top of that hill over there you can see the castle of Grantmesnle, which lies in the valley below." Then Sir Pelleas said, "Let us hurry! For I am wonderfully eager to see that place."

They reach Grantmesnle.

So they set spurs to their horses and rode up that hill at an easy gallop. And when they had reached the top of it, there below them lay the Castle of Grantmesnle, as plain to see as if it were held in the palm of a hand. Sir Pelleas saw that it was an exceedingly beautiful castle, built entirely of red stone on the outside, with many buildings of red brick inside the wall. Behind the walls lay a little town, and from where they stood they could see its streets, and the people coming and going about their business. So Sir Pelleas, seeing how fine that castle was, said, "Truly, maiden, that is a very fair estate over there."

"Yes," said Parcenet, "those of us who live there think it a very excellent estate."

Then Sir Pelleas said to Parcenet, "Maiden, that grove of young trees near the castle looks like a very pleasant spot. So my companions in arms and I will make our camp there. There, too, we will have three pavilions set up to shelter us by day and by night. In the meantime, I beg you to go to the lady your mistress and tell her that a knight has come to this place who, although he does not know her, holds that the Lady Guinevere of Camelot is the most beautiful lady in all the world. And I beg you to tell the lady that I am here to maintain that claim against all comers at the risk of my body. So if the lady has any champion who will fight on her behalf, I will meet him in that field over there tomorrow at midday, a little before I eat my midday meal. For at that time I mean to ride into that field and parade up and down there until my friends call me in to my dinner; and I will take my stand in that place in honor of the Lady Guinevere of Camelot."

"Sir Pelleas," said the damsel, "I will do just as you ask of me. And though I cannot wish that you will be the victor in that fight, yet truly I am sorry to leave you. For you are both a very brave and a very courteous knight, and I find that I feel a great friendship for you."

Then Sir Pelleas laughed and said, "Parcenet, you are bent on giving me praise far beyond what I deserve." And Parcenet said, "No, sir, for you deserve everything I can say to your credit."

Then the two of them took leave of one another with great goodwill and much kindness, and the maiden and the three pages went one way, while Sir Pelleas and his two companions and the attendants they had brought with them went into the grove of young trees, as Sir Pelleas had ordered.

Sir Pelleas and his fellow knights make their camp in a grove of trees.

There they set up three pavilions in the shade of the trees: one pavilion of fine white cloth, the second of green cloth, and the third of scarlet cloth. And over each pavilion they set a banner bearing the emblem of the knight to whom that pavilion belonged. Above the white pavilion was the emblem of Sir Pelleas, which was three swans with spread wings on a silver field. Above the red pavilion, which was Sir Brandiles's, was a red banner bearing his emblem, which was a hand in armor gripping a hammer. Above the green pavilion, which was Sir Mador de la Porte's, was a green banner bearing his emblem, which was a carrion crow holding a white lily in one claw and a sword in the other.

Sir Pelleas challenges Sir Engamore.

So when the next day came, and midday was near, Sir Pelleas rode out into that field in front of the castle as he had promised to do. He was dressed from head to foot in the red armor he had taken from the body of Sir Adresack, so that in that armor he looked very terrible. He rode up and down before the castle walls for a long while, crying in a loud voice, "Hear me! Hear me! Here stands a knight of King Arthur's court and of his Round Table, who declares, and is ready to prove it with his body, that the Lady Guinevere, King Arthur's Queen of Camelot, is the most beautiful lady in all the world, not excepting anyone at all. So if any knight holds otherwise, let him come out at once and defend his opinion with his body."

Now after Sir Pelleas had appeared in that meadow, a great stir arose inside the castle, and many people came up onto the walls and gazed down at Sir Pelleas as he paraded up and down that field. After some time had passed, the drawbridge of the castle was let down, and out rode a knight who was very huge of frame and exceedingly proud in his bearing. This knight was dressed from head to foot in green armor, and on each arm he wore a green sleeve, which was why he was sometimes called the Knight of the Green Sleeves.

So that Green Knight rode toward Sir Pelleas, and Sir Pelleas rode toward the Green Knight, and when they came together they saluted each other with a great deal of civility and knightly courtesy. Then the Green Knight said to Sir Pelleas, "Sir Knight, will you grant me the great favor of knowing your name?"

And Sir Pelleas answered, "That I will. I am Sir Pelleas, a knight of King Arthur's court and of his Round Table."

Then the Green Knight said, "Ha, Sir Pelleas, it is a great honor for me to deal with so famous a knight, for who is there in the courts of chivalry who has not heard of you? Now, if I have the good fortune to overthrow you, then all your honor will become my honor. And in return for your courtesy in telling me your name, I give you my name and title, which is Sir Engamore of Malverat, also known as the Knight of the Green Sleeves. And I may tell you further that I am the champion of the Lady Ettard of Grantmesnle, and that I have defended her claim to be the most beautiful lady without equal for eleven months, against all comers. If I defend it successfully for one month longer, then I am to win her hand and become lord of all this fair estate. So I am ready to do the very utmost in my power in her honor."

Then Sir Pelleas said, "Sir Knight, I thank you for your words of greeting, and I too will do my utmost in this encounter." At that the two knights saluted each other with their lances, and each rode to his appointed place.

Sir Pelleas overthrows Sir Engamore.

Now a great crowd of people had come down to the lower walls of the castle and of the town to watch the contest of arms that was about to take place, so it would be hard to imagine a worthier occasion for knights to meet in a glorious contest of friendly jousting. So each knight made himself ready in every way, and set his spear and lance with great care and caution. When everything had been made ready for that encounter, a herald, who had come out from the castle into the field, gave the signal for the charge. At once, in an instant, each knight drove his spurs into his horse, and they rushed at each other with such terrible speed that the ground shook and trembled under the pounding of their horses' hooves. They met exactly in the center of the field, each knight striking the other in the middle of his shield with a violence that was very terrible to see. And the spear of Sir Engamore burst into as many as thirty pieces, but the spear of Sir Pelleas held, so that the Green Knight was hurled out of his saddle so violently that he struck the ground more than a spear's length behind his horse's tail.

Now when the people standing on the walls saw how completely the Green Knight had been overthrown in that encounter, they raised a great outcry, for there was no other knight like Sir Engamore in all that country. And the Lady Ettard cried out loudest of all, for Sir Engamore was very dear to her, and when she saw him flung down so violently on the ground, she thought that perhaps he had been killed.

Then three squires ran to Sir Engamore, lifted him up, and unlaced his helmet to give him air. And they saw that he was not dead, but only in a deep swoon. After a while he opened his eyes, and at that Sir Pelleas was very glad, for it would have grieved him to have killed that knight. Now when Sir Engamore came back to his senses, he demanded very fiercely that he be allowed to go on with the fight against Sir Pelleas on foot and with swords. But Sir Pelleas would not have it. "No, Sir Engamore," he said, "I will not fight you over so serious a quarrel as that, for I have no such ill will against you." And at this refusal Sir Engamore burst into tears out of pure vexation and shame at being so completely overthrown.

Then Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte came up and praised Sir Pelleas highly for the excellent way he had borne himself in the encounter, and at the same time they spoke kindly to Sir Engamore and tried to comfort him for the misfortune that had happened to him. But Sir Engamore took very little comfort from their words.

Now while they were all standing there together, the Lady Ettard came out from the castle with a very gay and handsome company of squires and ladies, and they came across the meadow toward where Sir Pelleas and the others stood.

When Sir Pelleas saw that lady coming, he drew his misericord and cut the laces of his helmet, and took the helmet off his head, and went forward bareheaded to meet her.

Sir Pelleas greets the Lady Ettard courteously.

But when he came near her, he saw that she was many times more beautiful than the picture of her painted on the ivory panel he had seen before, and his heart went out to her with a very great strength of liking. So he knelt down on the grass before her, and put his hands together palm to palm, and said, "Lady, I very humbly beg your forgiveness for having fought against your claim like this. For if I had not undertaken that fight for my Queen, I would rather have been your champion than the champion of any lady I have ever seen."

Now at that time Sir Pelleas wore around his neck the collar of emeralds and opals and gold that the Lady of the Lake had given him. So when the Lady Ettard looked at him, that necklace drew her heart to him with a very powerful enchantment. She smiled at Sir Pelleas very warmly, gave him her hand, and made him rise from where he knelt. And she said to him, "Sir Knight, you are a very famous warrior, for I do not suppose there is anyone who knows anything of chivalry who has not heard of the fame of Sir Pelleas, the Gentle Knight. So although my champion, Sir Engamore of Malverat, has overthrown all comers until now, he need not feel very much ashamed to have been overthrown by so tremendously strong a knight."

Sir Pelleas and his fellow knights go to the castle of Grantmesnle.

Sir Pelleas was very glad of the kind words the Lady Ettard spoke to him, and he presented Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte to her. The Lady Ettard spoke very graciously to these knights as well, moved to it by the extraordinary liking she felt for Sir Pelleas. She begged those knights to come into the castle and refresh themselves with good cheer, and the knights said they would do so very soon. So each knight went back to his pavilion, and there each dressed himself in fine clothes and ornaments of gold and silver, in such a way that he was noble company for any court. Then those three knights went to the castle of Grantmesnle, and when they arrived there, everyone was astonished at how noble they looked.

But Sir Engamore, who had by now recovered from his fall, was very downcast, for he said to himself, "Who am I, beside these noble lords?" So he stood off to one side, very low in heart and heavy in spirit.

Then the Lady Ettard set out a very fine feast, and Sir Pelleas and Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte were very glad of it. She seated Sir Pelleas at her right hand and Sir Engamore at her left. And Sir Engamore was even more downcast, for until now he had always sat at the Lady Ettard's right hand.

Now because Sir Pelleas wore that wonderful collar the Lady of the Lake had given him, the Lady Ettard could not keep her liking from him. So after they had refreshed themselves and had gone out into the castle's pleasure garden to walk in the warm sunshine, the lady wanted Sir Pelleas beside her all the time. And when it was time for those visiting knights to leave the castle, she begged Sir Pelleas to stay a while longer. Sir Pelleas was very glad to do so, for he was pleased beyond measure with the graciousness and the beauty of the Lady Ettard.

So after a while Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte went back to their pavilions, and Sir Pelleas stayed on in the castle of Grantmesnle for a while longer.

Sir Pelleas and the Lady Ettard feast together.

That night the Lady Ettard had a supper prepared for herself and Sir Pelleas, and at that supper only she and Sir Pelleas sat at the table, while the damsel Parcenet waited on the lady. While they ate, some young pages and squires played very sweetly on harps, and some maidens who attended the lady's court sang so sweetly that it made the listener's heart swell to hear them. And Sir Pelleas was so enchanted by the sweetness of the music and by the beauty of the Lady Ettard that he did not know whether he was really on earth or in Paradise. So, out of his great happiness, he said to the Lady Ettard, "Lady, I wish I could do something for you, to show you how high is the regard and the honor in which I hold you."

Now all the while Sir Pelleas sat beside her, the Lady Ettard had kept her eyes on that wonderful collar of gold and emeralds and opals that hung around his neck, and she wanted that collar very badly. So now she said to Sir Pelleas, "Sir Knight, you can indeed do me a great favor, if you have a mind to." "What favor can I do you, Lady?" said Sir Pelleas. "Sir," said the Lady Ettard, "you can give me that collar that hangs around your neck."

At this Sir Pelleas's face fell, and he said, "Lady, I cannot do that, for that collar came to me in such an extraordinary way that I cannot part with it."

Then the Lady Ettard said, "Why can you not part with it, Sir Pelleas?"

So Sir Pelleas told her all about that extraordinary adventure with the Lady of the Lake, and how that fairy lady had given him the collar.

At this the Lady Ettard was greatly astonished, and she said, "Sir Pelleas, that is a very wonderful story. Even so, though you cannot give me that collar, you can at least let me wear it for a little while. For truly I am charmed by the beauty of that collar beyond anything I can tell, and so I beg you to let me wear it for a little."

Sir Pelleas lets the Lady Ettard wear the collar.

Then Sir Pelleas could refuse her no longer, and he said, "Lady, you shall have it to wear for a while." So he took the collar from around his neck and hung it around the neck of the Lady Ettard.

Then, after a little while, the magic power of that jewel left Sir Pelleas and passed into the Lady Ettard, and the Lady Ettard looked at Sir Pelleas with altogether different eyes from those she had looked at him with before. She said to herself, "Ha! What was wrong with me, that I was so enchanted with that knight, to the disgrace of my champion who has served me so faithfully? Has this knight not done me a grievous insult? Did he not come here for no other reason than that? Did he not overthrow my own true knight in scorn of me? Then what was wrong with me, that I showed him such favor as I have shown him?" But although she thought all this, she gave no sign of it to Sir Pelleas, but seemed to laugh and talk very cheerfully. All the same, she began at once to turn over in her mind some way to take revenge on Sir Pelleas. For she said to herself, "Look! Is he not my enemy, and is my enemy not now in my power? So why should I not take full revenge on him for everything he has done to us of Grantmesnle?"

The Lady Ettard lays plans against Sir Pelleas.

So after a while she made an excuse, got up, and left Sir Pelleas. She took Parcenet aside and said to the damsel Parcenet, "Go and bring me a powerful sleeping draught, right away." Then Parcenet said, "Lady, what are you going to do?" And the Lady Ettard said, "Never mind." And Parcenet said, "Would you give a sleeping draught to that noble knight?" And the lady said, "I would." Then Parcenet said, "Lady, that would surely be a wicked thing to do to someone who sits in peace at your table and eats your salt as your guest." To which the Lady Ettard said, "Don't you trouble yourself about that, girl, but go at once and do as I tell you."

Then Parcenet saw that it would not be wise for her to disobey the lady. So she went at once and did as she was told. She brought the sleeping draught to the lady in a cup of pure wine, and the Lady Ettard took the cup and said to Sir Pelleas, "Take this cup of wine, Sir Knight, and drink it to me, as deeply as the goodwill you bear me." Now Parcenet stood behind her lady's chair, and when Sir Pelleas took the cup, she frowned and shook her head at him. But Sir Pelleas did not see it, for he was intoxicated with the beauty of the Lady Ettard, and with the enchantment of the collar of emeralds and opals and gold that she now wore. So he said to her, "Lady, even if there were poison in that cup, I would still drink the wine in it at your command."

At that the Lady Ettard burst out laughing beyond all measure, and she said, "Sir Knight, there is no poison in that cup."

So Sir Pelleas took the cup and drank the wine, and he said, "Lady, what is this? The wine is bitter." And the Lady Ettard answered, "Sir, that cannot be."

Sir Pelleas sleeps.

Then in a little while Sir Pelleas's head grew extremely heavy, as though it were made of lead, so that he bowed his head down on the table where he sat. All the while the Lady Ettard sat watching him very strangely, and after a while she said, "Sir Knight, are you asleep?" Sir Pelleas made no answer, for the fumes of the sleeping draught had risen into his brain, and he slept.

Then the Lady Ettard got up laughing, and she clapped her hands and called her attendants. And she said to them, "Take this knight away and carry him into an inner room. When you have brought him there, strip him of his fine clothes and his ornaments, so that nothing is left on him but his undergarments. When you have done that, lay him on a cot and carry him out of the castle into the meadow below the walls where he overthrew Sir Engamore, so that when morning comes he will be a mockery and a joke to everyone who sees him. In this way we will humiliate him in the very field where he overthrew Sir Engamore, and his humiliation will be greater than Sir Engamore's has been."

Now when the damsel Parcenet heard this, she was deeply distressed, so that she went off by herself and wept for Sir Pelleas. But the others took Sir Pelleas and did to him just as the Lady Ettard had commanded.

When the next morning came, Sir Pelleas woke with the sun shining in his face. He had no idea at all where he was, for his brain was still fogged by the sleeping draught he had taken. So he said to himself, "Am I dreaming, or am I awake? For surely the last thing I remember is that I sat at supper with the Lady Ettard, and yet here I am in an open field, with the sun shining down on me."

So he raised himself on his elbow, and he saw that he was lying under the castle walls, near the postern gate. And above him, on the top of the wall, was a great crowd of people, and when they saw that he was awake, they laughed at him and mocked him. The Lady Ettard too gazed down at him from a window, and he saw that she was laughing at him and making merry. And then he saw that he lay there dressed only in his linen undershirt, and that his feet were bare, as though he were ready for bed at night. So he sat on the cot, saying to himself, "Surely this must be some shameful dream that is weighing on me." And he could not shake off his bewilderment at all.

The damsel Parcenet brings help to Sir Pelleas.

Now, as he sat there, the postern gate suddenly opened, and the damsel Parcenet came out of it. Her face was all wet with tears, and she carried in her hand a flame-colored cloak. She ran straight to Sir Pelleas and said, "You good and gentle knight, take this and wrap yourself in it."

At this Sir Pelleas knew that this was no dream, but a shameful truth, and he was seized with such an agony of shame that he began to tremble, and his teeth chattered as though he had a fever. Then he said to Parcenet, "Maiden, I thank you." And he could find no more words to say. So he took the cloak and wrapped himself in it.

Now when the people on the walls saw what Parcenet had done, they hooted at her and abused her with many hard words. So the maiden ran back into the castle, but Sir Pelleas got up and made his way toward his pavilion, wrapped in that cloak. And as he went, he staggered and tottered like a drunken man, for a great burden of shame lay upon him, almost more than he could carry.

Sir Pelleas grieves greatly over his shame.

So when Sir Pelleas reached his pavilion, he went in and threw himself face down on his couch and lay there without saying a word. After a while Sir Brandiles and Mador de la Porte heard of the plight Sir Pelleas had fallen into, and they hurried to where he lay and grieved greatly over him. They were also extremely angry at the shame that had been put upon him, and they said, "We will get help from Camelot, and we will break open that castle and bring the Lady Ettard here to beg your pardon for this insult. This we will do even if we have to drag her here by the hair of her head."

But Sir Pelleas did not lift his head; he only groaned and said, "Let it be, Sirs, for under no circumstances shall you do that, since she is a woman. As things are, I would defend her honor even if I died defending it. For I do not know whether I am bewitched or what it is that is wrong with me, but I love her with a very great passion, and I cannot tear my heart away from her."

At this Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte were greatly astonished, and they said to each other, "Surely that lady has laid some powerful spell on him."

Then after a while Sir Pelleas told them to go away and leave him, and they did so, though not very willingly.

So Sir Pelleas lay there all that day until the afternoon came. Then he roused himself and told his squire to bring him his armor. When Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte heard of this, they went to where he was and said, "Sir, what do you mean to do?" Sir Pelleas said, "I am going to try to make my way into the Lady Ettard's presence." Then they said, "What madness is this?" "I do not know," said Sir Pelleas, "but it seems to me that if I do not see the Lady Ettard and talk with her, I shall surely die of longing to see her." And they said, "Surely this is madness." And he answered, "I do not know whether it is madness, or whether I am caught in some enchantment."

So the squire brought Sir Pelleas his armor as he had ordered, and he dressed Sir Pelleas in it so that he was fully armed from head to foot. Then at once Sir Pelleas mounted his horse and rode out toward the castle of Grantmesnle.

Now when the Lady Ettard saw Sir Pelleas parading up and down the meadow below the castle again, she called six of her best knights to her and said to them, "Look, Sirs, there is that knight who brought so much shame on us yesterday. Now I order you to go out against him and punish him as he deserves."

So those six knights went and armed themselves, and when they had done so, they rode straight out against Sir Pelleas.

Sir Pelleas overcomes six knights.

Now when Sir Pelleas saw them coming, his heart overflowed with fury, and he shouted in a great voice and charged at them. For a while they stood up to him, but he could not be withstood, for he fought with a fury beyond anything, so that soon they broke away before him and fled. He chased them furiously all around that field and struck four of them down from their horses. Then, when only two of those knights were left, Sir Pelleas suddenly stopped fighting and called out to those two knights, "Sirs, I surrender myself to you."

Sir Pelleas yields himself prisoner.

At that those two knights were greatly astonished, for they were completely filled with fear of his strength, and they did not know why he should yield to them. All the same, they came and took hold of him and led him toward the castle. At this Sir Pelleas said to himself, "Now they will bring me to the Lady Ettard, and I shall speak with her." For it was for this reason that he had let himself be taken by those two knights.

But it was not to happen as Sir Pelleas wished. For when they had brought him close under the castle, the Lady Ettard called down to them from a window in the wall. She said, "What are you doing with that knight?" They said, "We are bringing him to you, Lady." At this she cried out very fiercely, "Do not bring him to me! Take him and tie his hands behind his back and tie his feet under his horse's belly, and send him back to his companions."

Then Sir Pelleas lifted his eyes to that window and cried out in a great passion of despair, "Lady, it was to you I surrendered, and not to these unworthy knights."

But the Lady Ettard cried out all the more fiercely, "Drive him away, for I hate the sight of him."

The Lady Ettard puts Sir Pelleas to shame.

So those two knights did as the Lady Ettard said: they took Sir Pelleas and bound him hand and foot upon his horse. And when they had done that, they let his horse carry him back to his companions in that state.

Now when Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte saw Sir Pelleas coming back to them with his hands tied behind his back and his feet tied under his horse's belly, they were utterly filled with grief and despair. So they loosed the cords from his hands and feet, and they cried out to Sir Pelleas, "Sir Knight, Sir Knight, are you not ashamed to put up with such disgrace as this?" And Sir Pelleas shook and trembled as though with a fever, and he cried out in great despair, "I do not care what happens to me!" They said, "Not to yourself, Sir Knight, but think what shame you bring on King Arthur and his Round Table!" At this Sir Pelleas cried aloud in a great and terrible voice, "I do not care about them, either."

All this happened because of the powerful enchantment of the collar of emeralds and opals and gold that Sir Pelleas had given to the Lady Ettard, and that she wore all the time. For it was beyond the power of any man to resist the enchantment of that collar. And so it was that Sir Pelleas was bewitched and brought to that great depth of shame.

Chapter 4: How Queen Guinevere Quarreled with Sir Gawain, and How Sir Gawain Left King Arthur's Court for a While

Now, just as much as Queen Guinevere thought highly of Sir Pelleas, she disliked Sir Gawain. For although many people said that Sir Gawain had a silver tongue, and although at times he could talk in such a way as to win others over to whatever he wanted, he was still of a proud temper, and very stern and haughty. So he would not always put up with it when the Lady Guinevere ordered him about as she ordered the other knights of that Court. What is more, she could never forget how Sir Gawain had refused her that time at Cameliard, when she begged him and his companions for help in her time of trouble, nor how discourteous his words to her had been then. So there was no great liking between these two proud souls, for Queen Guinevere held to her own way and Sir Gawain held to his own way, whatever happened.

Sir Gawain and others sit beneath the Queen's window.

Now it happened once that Sir Gawain and Sir Griflet and Sir Constantine of Cornwall were sitting and talking with five ladies of the Queen's Court in a garden of trimmed and interwoven hedges that lay below the Lady Guinevere's tower, and they were having a very pleasant time together. Sometimes they would talk and make merry with jokes and tales, and at other times one or another of them would take up a lute they had brought with them, and play on it, and sing.

Now while these lords and ladies sat enjoying their pleasant talk and singing like this, Queen Guinevere was sitting at a window that overlooked the garden. It was not very high above the ground, so she could overhear everything they said. But the lords and ladies had no idea that the Queen could hear them, so they talked and laughed very freely, and the Queen greatly enjoyed their conversation and the music they made.

That day was wonderfully mild and warm, and since it was well on toward the close of the afternoon, those lords and ladies were dressed in very bright and festive clothes. Of all who were there, Sir Gawain was the most splendidly dressed, for he wore sky-blue silk embroidered with silver thread. And Sir Gawain was playing the lute and singing a ballad in a very pleasing voice, so that Queen Guinevere, sitting by the open window, was well content to listen to him.

Sir Gawain strikes the Queen's hound.

Now there was a certain greyhound that Queen Guinevere was wonderfully fond of, so fond that she had put a collar of gold set with carbuncles, those deep red jewels, around its neck. Just then the hound came running into the garden, and his feet were wet and muddy with earth. Hearing Sir Gawain singing and playing on the lute, the hound ran to him and jumped up on him. This made Sir Gawain very angry. He clenched his fist and struck the hound on the head with his knuckles, so that the hound howled loudly.

But when Queen Guinevere saw that blow she was deeply offended, and she called out from her window, "Why do you strike my dog, Sir?" And the lords and ladies below in the garden were very much surprised, and very much embarrassed, to find that the Queen was so close to them that she could overhear everything they had said and see everything they did.

But Sir Gawain spoke up very boldly and said, "Your dog insulted me, Lady, and whoever insults me, I strike."

Then Queen Guinevere grew very angry with Sir Gawain, and she said, "Your words are too bold, Sir." And Sir Gawain said, "Not too bold, Lady; only bold enough to defend my rights."

Of the quarrel of the Queen and Sir Gawain.

At these words the Lady Guinevere's face blazed like fire, and her eyes shone very bright, and she said, "I am sure you forget whom you are speaking to, Sir Knight." At that Sir Gawain smiled very bitterly and said, "And you, Lady, forget that I am the son of a king so powerful that he needs no help from any other king to defend his rights."

At these words everyone there fell as silent as if they had been turned to stone, for that speech was extremely bold and haughty. They all looked at the ground, for they did not dare to look at either Queen Guinevere or Sir Gawain. And the Lady Guinevere was silent too, for a long time, trying to recover herself after that speech; and when she spoke, it was as if she were half choked with anger. She said, "Sir Knight, you are proud and arrogant beyond all measure, for I never heard of anyone who dared to answer his Queen the way you have spoken to me. But this is my Court, and I may give orders in it as I choose. So I now command you to be gone, and to show your face no more, either here or in the Hall or in any of the places where I hold my Court. For you are an offense to me, and in none of these places shall you have leave to show your face until you ask my pardon for the insult you have given me." Then Sir Gawain stood up and bowed very low to Queen Guinevere, and he said, "Lady, I go. And I will not come back here until you are willing to tell me that you are sorry for the discourteous way you have treated me, both now and at other times, in front of my peers."

With these words Sir Gawain left that place, and he did not turn his head to look behind him. And Queen Guinevere went into her room and wept in secret, out of anger and out of shame. For in truth she was deeply grieved at what had happened; and yet she was so proud that she would never have taken back the words she had spoken, even if she could have done so.

Now when the news of that quarrel had gone around the castle, it reached the ears of Sir Uwain. So Sir Uwain went straight to Sir Gawain and asked him what was the matter, and Sir Gawain, who was like a man half out of his mind and in great despair, told him everything. Then Sir Uwain said, "You were certainly wrong to speak to the Queen as you did. All the same, if you are banished from this Court, I will go with you, for you are my first cousin and my companion, and my heart holds fast to you." So Sir Uwain went to King Arthur and said, "Lord, my cousin Sir Gawain has been banished from this Court by the Queen. And though I cannot say that he has not deserved that punishment, I would gladly ask your leave to go with him."

At this King Arthur was very much grieved, but he kept a steady face and said, "Sir, I will not keep you from going wherever you please. As for your kinsman, I dare say he gave the Queen such great offense that she could do nothing other than what she did."

Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain leave the Court.

So Sir Uwain and Sir Gawain both went to their lodgings and ordered their squires to arm them. Then they and their squires rode out of Camelot and took their way toward the forest country.

There those two knights and their squires traveled all that day until the gray of the evening, at the hour when the birds were singing their last songs before closing their eyes for the night. Seeing that the evening was coming on fast, the knights were afraid they would not be able to find a kindly lodging before night came down on them, and they talked together a great deal about it. But as they came to the top of a certain hill, they saw below them a valley, very lovely and well farmed, with many cottages and small farms. And in the middle of that valley was a fine abbey, very beautiful to look at. So Sir Gawain said to Sir Uwain, "If that abbey over there is an abbey of monks, I believe we shall find excellent lodging there tonight."

They come to an abbey of monks.

So they rode down into the valley and to the abbey, and at the small gate they found a porter, from whom they learned that it was indeed an abbey of monks. At this they were very glad, and rejoiced greatly.

When the abbot of that abbey learned who they were, and of what rank and high station, he was extremely pleased to welcome them, and he brought them into the part of the abbey where he himself lived. There he made them welcome and had a good supper set before them, which made them very happy. Now the abbot was a merry soul and took great pleasure in talking with strangers, so he asked those two knights very carefully why they were wandering abroad. But they told him nothing about the quarrel at Court, only that they were in search of adventure. At this the abbot said, "Ha, Sirs! If you are in search of adventures, you may find one not very far from here."

The abbot tells the knights of a good adventure.

So Sir Gawain said, "What adventure is that?" And the abbot answered, "I will tell you. If you travel eastward from this place, you will come after a while to a spot where you will find a very fair castle of gray stone. In front of that castle you will find a good level meadow, and in the middle of the meadow a sycamore tree, and in the sycamore tree a shield, which certain ladies insult in a very curious way. If you forbid those ladies to insult that shield, you will discover a very good adventure."

Then Sir Gawain said, "That is a very strange business. Tomorrow morning we will go to that place and try to find out what sort of adventure it may be." And the abbot said, "Do so," and laughed heartily.

So when the next morning came, Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain said farewell to the abbot and took their leave of that place, riding away eastward as the abbot had advised. After they had ridden in that direction for two or three hours or more, they saw before them the edge of a forest, all green and shady with leaves and very cheerful in the warmth of the early summer day. And there, right at the edge of the woods, stood a fair, strong castle of gray stone, with windows of glass shining very bright against the sky.

Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain see the damsels attacking the shield.

Then Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain saw that everything was just as the abbot had said, for in front of the castle was a smooth, level meadow with a sycamore tree in the middle of it. As they drew near, they could see that a black shield hung in the branches of the tree, and in a little while they could make out that it bore the emblem of three white goshawks with their wings spread. But what was very extraordinary was this: in front of that shield stood seven young damsels, with very lovely faces, and these seven damsels kept heaping insults on the shield. Some of the damsels struck at it every now and then with peeled willow switches, and others threw lumps of clay at it, so that the shield was badly smeared and spoiled. Now near the shield was a very noble-looking knight dressed all in black armor and sitting on a black war-horse, and it was easy to see that the shield belonged to that knight, for he had no other shield. Yet even though it was very likely his shield, the knight made no protest, either by word or by deed, to stop those damsels from insulting it.

Then Sir Uwain said to Sir Gawain, "That is a very strange thing I see over there. Probably one of us is meant to fight that knight." And Sir Gawain said, "Maybe so." Then Sir Uwain said, "If that is so, then I will take on the adventure." "No," said Sir Gawain, "I will take it on myself, since I am the elder of us two, and the more seasoned in knighthood." So Sir Uwain said, "Very well. Let it be so, for you are a much more powerful knight than I am, and it would be a pity for one of us to fail in this undertaking." At that Sir Gawain said, "Leave it to me, then, and I will undertake it."

So he set spurs to his horse and rode swiftly to where the damsels were insulting the black shield. And he set his spear in rest and shouted in a loud voice, "Get away! Get away!" When the damsels saw the armed knight riding at them like that, they ran away from him, shrieking.

Then the Black Knight, who was sitting his horse not far off, rode forward to Sir Gawain in a very stately way and said, "Sir Knight, why do you interfere with those ladies?" And Sir Gawain answered, "Because they were insulting what seemed to me to be a noble and knightly shield." At this the Black Knight spoke very haughtily and said, "Sir Knight, that shield belongs to me, and I assure you that I am very well able to take care of it without the interference of any other defender." To which Sir Gawain said, "It would not seem so, Sir Knight."

Then the Black Knight said, "Sir, if you think you are better able to take care of that shield than I am, I think you would do very well to make your words good with your body." To this Sir Gawain said, "I will do my best to show you that I am better able to guard that shield than you are, who own it."

Sir Gawain and the Black Knight join in battle.

At this the Black Knight, without any more talk, rode to the sycamore tree and took down the shield that hung there. He fastened the shield on his arm, took his spear in hand, and made himself ready. And Sir Gawain likewise made himself ready, and then each knight took up whatever position on the field seemed best to him.

Now when the people of that castle saw that a combat of arms was about to take place, they crowded in great numbers onto the walls, so that as many as forty ladies and squires and people of every rank were looking down from the walls on that field of battle.

So when the knights were all ready, Sir Uwain gave the signal to charge, and each knight shouted aloud and drove his spurs into his horse and rushed forward to the attack with a noise as loud as thunder.

The Black Knight overthrows Sir Gawain.

Now Sir Gawain thought that he would easily beat his opponent in this charge and would be able to throw him out of his saddle without much trouble, for there was hardly a knight in that kingdom equal to Sir Gawain in prowess. Indeed, he had never yet been unhorsed in combat by anyone except King Arthur. So when the two of them rode against each other, Sir Gawain thought it certain that his opponent would fall before him. But it was not so. For in that charge Sir Gawain's spear was broken into many pieces, but the Black Knight's spear held, so that Sir Gawain was hurled violently out of the saddle and struck the dust with a terrible crash. And he was so astonished at that fall that it seemed to him not as though he had fallen from his saddle, but as though the earth had risen up and struck him. So he lay for a while, quite stunned by the blow and by his astonishment.

But when he heard the shouts of the people on the castle wall, he roused himself at once from where he lay in the dust, and he was so filled with rage and shame that he was like a man completely drunk. So he drew his sword and rushed furiously at his enemy, meaning to cut him down by main strength. Then the other knight, seeing him come at him like this, leaped down from his own saddle at once, drew his sword, and set himself ready either to attack or to defend. So they lashed at each other, circling this way and that and striking with such fury that the blows they gave were terrible to see. But when Sir Uwain saw how fierce that fight was, he set spurs to his horse and pushed it in between the two fighters, crying out loud, "Sir Knights! Sir Knights! What is this? There is no reason here for such a desperate battle." But Sir Gawain cried out very furiously, "Leave off! Leave off, and stand aside! This quarrel is none of your business." And the Black Knight said, "On horseback or on foot, I am ready to meet that knight at any time."

But Sir Uwain said, "No. You will fight no more in this quarrel. Shame on you, Gawain! Shame on you, to pick such a desperate quarrel with a knight who only met you in a friendly way in a fair contest!"

Then Sir Gawain saw that Sir Uwain was both just and right, and he put up his sword in silence, although he was ready to weep with vexation at the shame of being overthrown. And the Black Knight put up his sword too, and so peace was made between the two of them.

Then the Black Knight said, "I am glad that this quarrel is over, for I can see, Sirs, that you are surely knights of great nobility and good breeding. So I wish that from now on we might be friends and companions instead of enemies. And so I beg you to come with me a little way from here, to where I have made my lodging, so that we may rest and refresh ourselves in my pavilion."

To this Sir Uwain said, "I thank you for your courtesy, Sir Knight, and we will go with you with all the pleasure in the world." And Sir Gawain said, "I am content." So the three knights left the field of battle at once.

Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain come to the pavilion of the Black Knight.

When they had come to the edge of the forest, Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain saw a very fine pavilion of green silk set up beneath the trees. And around that pavilion were many attendants of various kinds, all dressed in green and white. So Sir Gawain could see that the knight who had overthrown him was certainly someone of very high rank, and this comforted him greatly. Then the squires of the three knights came and took off their helmets, each squire his own knight's, so that the knights might be comfortable. And when this was done, Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain saw that the Black Knight was very handsome, with a ruddy face and hair as red as copper. Then Sir Uwain said to the knight, "Sir Unknown Knight, this knight, my companion, is Sir Gawain, son of King Uriens of Gore, and I am Uwain, the son of King Lot of Orkney. Now I ask you to make yourself known to us in the same way."

"Ha," said the other, "I am glad that you are such very famous and royal knights, for I am of royal blood too. I am Sir Marhaus, the son of the King of Ireland."

Then Sir Gawain was very glad to discover how exalted was the rank of the knight who had overthrown him, and he said to Sir Marhaus, "Sir, I swear that you are one of the most formidable knights in the world. For you have done to me today what only one knight in all the world has ever done before, and that is King Arthur, who is my uncle and my lord. Now you must certainly come to King Arthur's Court, for he will be wonderfully glad to see you, and maybe he will make you a Knight of his Round Table, and there is no honor in all the world as great as that." He said this without thinking; and then he remembered. At that he struck his fist against his forehead and cried out, "Aha! Aha! Who am I to invite you to King Arthur's Court, when only yesterday I was disgraced and banished from it?"

Then Sir Marhaus was very sorry for Sir Gawain, and asked about the trouble that weighed on him, and Sir Uwain told Sir Marhaus all about the quarrel. At that Sir Marhaus was even sorrier for Sir Gawain, and he said, "Sirs, I like you both wonderfully well, and I would gladly become your companion in whatever adventures you are going to undertake. For now I need not stay here any longer. You must know that I was bound to defend those ladies who attacked my shield until I had overthrown seven knights on their behalf. And I must tell you that Sir Gawain was the seventh knight I have overthrown. So, since I have now overthrown him, I am released from my obligation and may go with you."

Sir Marhaus tells his story.

Then Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain were very much astonished that any knight should be under so strange an obligation as that, to defend the very people who were attacking his shield, and they begged Sir Marhaus to tell them why he had been bound to keep such a pledge. So Sir Marhaus said, "I will tell you. It was like this. Some time ago I was traveling in these parts with a hawk on my wrist. I was dressed very lightly at the time, in holiday clothes: I wore a tunic of green silk, with one stocking green and the other white. And I had nothing on me for defense but a light buckler, which is a small round shield, and a short sword. Now I came to a certain stream of water, very deep and swift, and I saw ahead of me a stone bridge crossing it, but so narrow that only one horseman could cross at a time. So I rode onto the bridge, and I was partway across when I saw a knight in armor coming the other way. And behind the knight, riding on a pillion, a cushion fixed behind his saddle, sat a very fair lady with golden hair and a very proud bearing. Now when that knight saw me on the bridge, he shouted, 'Get back! Get back, and let me pass!' But this I would not do. Instead I said, 'No, Sir Knight. Since I have come this far onto the bridge, I surely have the right of way to finish crossing, and it is for you to wait and let me over.' But the knight would not do so. He at once made ready to attack, and came straight at me on the bridge, meaning either to kill me or to drive me back to the other end of it. But this he was not able to do, for I defended myself very well with my light weapons. And I pushed my horse so hard against his horse that I drove him backward off the bridge and into the water, and the horse and the knight and the lady all fell into it with a terrible uproar.

"At this the lady shrieked loudly, and both she and the knight were on the point of drowning in the water, for the knight was clad entirely in armor and could not lift himself above the flood. So, seeing the danger they were in, I leaped off my horse into the water, and with great effort, and at great risk to myself, I was able to bring them both to land.

"But that lady was very much offended with me, because her fine clothes were soaked and spoiled by the water, and she scolded me fiercely. So I knelt down before her and begged her pardon with all humility, but still she went on scolding me. Then I offered to do any penance she might lay on me. At this the lady seemed to be a good deal softened, for she said, 'Very well, I will set you a penance.' And when her knight had recovered, she said, 'Come with us.' So I mounted my horse and followed them. And after we had gone a good distance we came to this place, and here she gave me these commands. 'Sir Knight,' she said, 'this castle belongs to me and to this knight who is my lord. Now this shall be your penance for the insult you have given me: you shall take your shield and hang it up in that sycamore tree, and every day I will send certain damsels of my own out of the castle. They shall abuse that shield, and you shall not only put up with whatever insult they offer it, but you shall defend them against all comers until you have overcome seven knights.'

"And so I have done, until this morning, when you, Sir Gawain, came here. You are the seventh knight I have fought, and since I have overcome you, my penance is over and I am free."

Then Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain wished Sir Marhaus great joy that his penance was finished, and each side was very well pleased with the other. So Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain stayed that night in Sir Marhaus's pavilion, and the next morning they got up, washed themselves in a forest stream, and set out from that place.

So they went into the forest country once more and made their way along certain paths, they did not know where; and they traveled all that morning and on until the afternoon had come.

The three knights enter the Forest of Adventure.

Now as they traveled like this, Sir Marhaus said suddenly, "Sirs, do you know where we have come to?" "No," they said, "we do not." Then Sir Marhaus said, "This part of the forest is called Arroy, and it is also called the Forest of Adventure. For it is very well known that when a knight, or a party of knights, enters this forest, they will surely meet with an adventure of some kind, from which some come out with honor while others fail." And Sir Uwain said, "I am glad we have come here. Now let us go on into this forest."

So the three knights and their squires went on into that woodland, where the silence was so deep that even the tread of their horses on the earth could hardly be heard. There was no note of any bird and no sound of any voice, and hardly any light made its way into the gloom of those woods. So the knights said to one another, "This is truly a very strange place, and maybe a place of enchantment."

They see a white fawn in the forest.

They see a beautiful lady in the forest.

Now when they had come into the very heart of these dark woods, they suddenly saw on the path ahead of them a fawn as white as milk. And around the fawn's neck was a collar of pure gold. The fawn stood still and looked at them, but when they came near it, it turned and ran off along a very narrow path. Then Sir Gawain said, "Let us follow that fawn and see where it goes." And the others said, "We are willing." So they followed that narrow path until suddenly they came out on a little open lawn, very bright with sunlight. In the middle of the lawn was a fountain of water, and there was no fawn to be seen. But there beside the fountain sat a wonderfully beautiful lady, dressed all in green. The lady was combing her hair with a golden comb, and her hair was as black as a raven's wing. On her arms she wore marvelous bracelets of emeralds and opals set in skilfully worked gold. What is more, the lady's face was as white as ivory, and her eyes were bright like jewels set in ivory. Now when this lady saw the knights, she stood up and laid aside her golden comb and tied up her locks with ribbons of scarlet silk; and then she came to the knights and greeted them.

At once the three knights got down from their horses, and Sir Gawain said, "Lady, I believe you are not a mortal woman, but one of the fairy folk." To this the lady said, "Sir Gawain, you are right," and Sir Gawain wondered that she should know his name so well. Then he said to her, "Lady, who are you?" And she answered, "My name is Nimue, and I am the chief of those Ladies of the Lake you may have heard of. For it was I who gave King Arthur his sword Excalibur, since I am a great friend to King Arthur and to all the noble Knights of his Court. That is how I know you all. And I know that you, Sir Marhaus, will become one of the most famous Knights of the Round Table." And all three of them marveled at the lady's words. Then she said, "Please tell me, what are you looking for in these parts?" And they said, "We are looking for adventure." "Well," she said, "I will bring you to an adventure, but it is Sir Gawain who must undertake it." And Sir Gawain said, "That is very welcome news." Then the lady said, "Take me up behind you on your saddle, Sir Gawain, and I will show you that adventure." So Sir Gawain took the lady up behind him on his saddle, and as he did, she brought with her a fragrance such as he had never known before. That fragrance was so delicate that it seemed to Sir Gawain as if the whole forest were breathing out the perfume that the Lady of the Lake carried with her.

So the Lady of the Lake led them by many winding ways out of that part of the forest, and along various roads and paths, until they came out into open country, very fruitful and pleasant to see. And she led them up a very high hill, and from the top of the hill they looked down on a fruitful, level plain spread out below them like a table. And they saw that in the middle of the plain stood a noble castle built all of red stone and red brick, and they saw that there was a small town built of red brick too.

The three knights see a very strange fight.

Now as they sat their horses there on the hilltop, they suddenly saw a knight dressed all in red armor come riding out of a grove of trees. They saw the knight ride up and down the meadow in front of the castle, and they saw that he was challenging those inside the castle. Then they saw the drawbridge of the castle suddenly let down, and ten knights in full armor came riding out. And they saw those ten knights attack the one knight in red armor, and they saw the one knight attack the ten. They saw that for a while the ten held out against the one, but he attacked them so terribly that he struck down four of them very quickly. Then they saw the rest break and flee before that one knight, and the Red Knight chased the others around the meadow with great fury. And they saw him strike one out of his saddle, and then another, and another, until only two of those knights were left.

Then Sir Gawain said, "That is certainly a wonderful thing to see." But the Lady of the Lake only smiled and said, "Wait a little."

So they waited, and they saw that when the Red Knight had struck down all his enemies except those two, and had put those two in great danger of their lives, he suddenly sheathed his sword and surrendered himself to them. And they saw the two knights bring the Red Knight to the castle, and when they had brought him there, a lady on the castle wall spoke to the Red Knight, it seemed, with very violent language. And they saw the two knights take the Red Knight and tie his hands behind his back, and tie his feet together under his horse's belly, and drive him away from that place.

All this they saw from the top of the hill, and the Lady of the Lake said to Sir Gawain, "There you will find your adventure, Sir Gawain." And Sir Gawain said, "I will go." And the Lady of the Lake said, "Do so."

And with that she vanished from their sight, and they were greatly amazed.

Chapter 5: How Sir Gawain Met Sir Pelleas and Promised to Help Him with the Lady Ettard

Now, after that wonderful lady had vanished from their sight in that way, the three knights stood for a little while utterly astonished, for they did not know whether to believe what their eyes had seen. Then, by and by, Sir Gawain spoke. "Truly," he said, "that was a very wonderful thing that happened to us, for never in all my life have I known so strange a miracle to take place. Now, it is very plain that some excellent adventure lies in what we have seen. So let us go down into that valley, for there we shall surely discover the meaning of what we have just now witnessed. For I swear that I have hardly ever seen so terribly powerful a knight as the one who just fought that battle down there, and I cannot in any way understand why, when he had so nearly won the victory over his enemies, he should have surrendered himself to them as he did."

And Sir Uwain and Sir Marhaus agreed that it would be well to go down and find out the meaning of what they had seen.

So the three of them and their attendants rode down into the valley.

And they rode on until they came to a certain grove of trees, and there they saw three handsome pavilions standing: the first pavilion of white cloth, the second of green cloth, and the third of scarlet cloth.

The three knights meet the two.

Now, as the three companions drew near to the pavilions, two knights came out to meet them. And when Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain saw the shields of the two, they knew at once that they were Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte. And in the same way Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte knew Sir Gawain and Sir Uwain, and each party was very much astonished to meet the other in so strange a place. So when they came together they greeted one another very joyfully and clasped hands with strong love and good fellowship.

Then Sir Gawain introduced Sir Marhaus to Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte, and after that the five knights all went together into those three pavilions, talking all the while in great friendship and pleasure. And when they came into the pavilion of Sir Brandiles, they found a good meal laid out there of white bread and wine of excellent flavor.

Then after a while Sir Gawain said to Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte, "Sirs, a little while ago we saw a very strange thing. As we stood together at the top of that hill and looked down into this plain, we saw a single knight dressed all in red armor fighting against ten knights. And that one knight in red armor fought the ten with such fury that he drove them all before him, though they were so many and he was only one. And truly I swear that I have hardly ever seen a knight show such great skill and strength in arms as he did. Yet when he had overcome all but two of those knights, and was well on the way to winning a clear victory, he suddenly gave himself up to the two of them and let them seize him and tie him up and drive him from the field with great indignity. Now, I beg you, tell me the meaning of what we saw, and who that knight was who fought so great a battle and yet surrendered so shamefully."

At this Sir Brandiles and Sir Mador de la Porte made no answer but looked away, for they did not know what to say. And when Sir Gawain saw that they were embarrassed, he began to wonder more than ever what it all meant. So he said, "What is this? Why don't you answer me? I order you to tell me what your looks mean, and who that red knight is!"

Then after a while Sir Mador de la Porte said, "I will not tell you, but you may come and see."

Then Sir Gawain began to think that perhaps there was something here that it would be better not to make public, and that he had better look further into the matter alone. So he said to the other knights, "Wait here a little while, sirs, and I will go with Sir Mador de la Porte."

Sir Mador de la Porte brings Sir Gawain to Sir Pelleas.

So Sir Gawain went with Sir Mador de la Porte, and Sir Mador led him to the white pavilion. And when they came there, Sir Mador drew aside the curtains of the pavilion and said, "Go in!" And Sir Gawain went in.

Now, when he came into the pavilion, he saw that a man sat on a couch of rushes covered with a blue cloth, and after a moment he saw that the man was Sir Pelleas. But Sir Pelleas did not see him at once. He sat with his head bowed, like someone completely overwhelmed by a great despair.

And when Sir Gawain saw who it was that sat on the couch, he was greatly amazed and cried out, "Ha! Is it you, Sir Pelleas? Is it you?"

But when Sir Pelleas heard Sir Gawain's voice, and saw who it was that spoke to him, he let out a terribly bitter cry. And he sprang to his feet and ran as far away as the walls of the pavilion would let him, and turned his face to the wall.

Sir Gawain rebukes Sir Pelleas.

Then, after a while, Sir Gawain spoke very sternly to Sir Pelleas. "Sir," he said, "I am astonished and deeply ashamed that a knight of King Arthur's royal court and of his Round Table should behave in so dishonorable a way as I saw you behave today. For it is hardly to be believed that a knight of your fame and nobility would let himself be captured and tied up by two unknown knights, as you let yourself be today. How could you bring yourself to submit to such indignity and insult? Now I demand that you explain this to me."

But Sir Pelleas was silent and would not answer. Then Sir Gawain cried out very fiercely, "Ha! Will you not answer me?" And Sir Pelleas shook his head.

Then Sir Gawain spoke again, still very fiercely. "Sir!" he said. "You shall answer me one way or another! Either you will tell me the meaning of your shameful conduct, or else you will fight me to the uttermost. For I will not allow you to bring such shame on King Arthur and his Round Table without defending the honor and good name of the King and of the Table myself. Once you and I were dear friends, but unless you clear yourself at once, I shall hold you in contempt and regard you as an enemy."

At this Sir Pelleas spoke like a man nearly out of his mind. "I will tell you everything," he said. Then he confessed it all to Sir Gawain, telling him everything that had happened since the time he left Queen Guinevere's May Court to set out on this adventure, and Sir Gawain listened to him in great amazement. And when Sir Pelleas had finished telling all that had happened to him, Sir Gawain said, "Truly, this is very strange. Indeed, I cannot understand how you came to be so tangled in the charms of this lady, unless she has bewitched you with some powerful enchantment."

To this Sir Pelleas said, "Yes, I believe I have been bewitched, for I am completely beside myself in this matter, and I cannot control my passion at all."

Then Sir Gawain thought for a long while, considering the matter very seriously, and by and by he said, "I have a plan, and it is this. I will go to the Lady Ettard myself and look carefully into this affair. And if I find that anyone has tangled you in enchantments, I will do my very utmost to punish that person with great suffering. For I will not have another enchanter deceive you, as one has already deceived Merlin the Wise."

Then Sir Pelleas said to Sir Gawain, "How will you manage this, so as to get into the presence of the Lady Ettard?"

Sir Gawain makes a plan with Sir Pelleas.

Sir Gawain replied, "That I will tell you. We two shall exchange armor, and I will go to the castle in your armor. When I get there, I shall say that I have beaten you in a fight and taken your armor away from you. Then perhaps they will let me into the castle to hear my story, and I shall have a chance to speak with her."

Then Sir Pelleas said, "Very well. It shall be as you decide."

So Sir Pelleas called for a squire, and Sir Gawain called for his squire, and the two of them took the armor off Sir Pelleas and dressed Sir Gawain in it. When they had done that, Sir Gawain mounted Sir Pelleas's horse and rode openly into the field where Sir Pelleas had paraded before.

Now it happened that the Lady Ettard was walking at that time on a terrace inside the castle walls, from which she could look down into that meadow. So when she saw a red knight parading in the meadow, she thought it was Sir Pelleas come back again, and at that she was annoyed and offended beyond all measure. So she said to those near her, "That knight annoys me so terribly that I am afraid I shall fall ill from vexation if he comes here many more times. I wish I knew how to rid myself of him. For only an hour ago I sent ten good knights against him, and he beat them all with great speed, to their great dishonor and mine."

So she beckoned to the Red Knight, and when he had come close to the castle walls, she said to him, "Sir Knight, why do you come here to torment me and insult me like this? Can't you understand that the more often you come to pester me this way, the more I hate you?"

Then Sir Gawain opened the visor of his helmet and showed his face, and the Lady Ettard saw that the Red Knight was not Sir Pelleas. And Sir Gawain said, "Lady, I am not the man you take me for, but someone else. For look: I have your enemy's armor on my body, and so you can see that I have beaten him. You may well suppose that I could hardly be wearing his armor unless I had taken it from him by force of arms. So you need not trouble yourself about him any longer."

Then the Lady Ettard could not help believing that this knight, whom she did not know, had really overthrown Sir Pelleas in a fight and taken his armor away from him. And indeed she was greatly astonished that such a thing could have happened, for it seemed to her that Sir Pelleas was one of the greatest knights in the world, and she wondered who this knight could be who had overthrown him in battle. So she commanded several of her attendants to go out and bring that red knight into the castle and to pay him great honor, since he must surely be one of the very greatest champions in the world.

Sir Gawain enters Grantmesnle.

So Sir Gawain came into the castle and was brought before the Lady Ettard, where she stood in a wonderfully large and noble hall. That hall was lit by seven tall windows of colored glass, and it was hung all around with tapestries and hangings, very rich and of the finest quality, so that Sir Gawain was greatly astonished at the splendor of everything he saw there.

Now, Sir Gawain had taken the helmet off his head, and he carried it under his arm against his hip. His head was bare, so that everyone there could see his face very plainly. And so they all saw that he was extremely handsome: his eyes were as blue as steel, his nose high and curved, and his hair and beard very dark and rich in color. What is more, he carried himself so steadily and so proudly that those who looked at him were awed by how very knightly he appeared.

Then the Lady Ettard came to Sir Gawain and gave him her hand, and he knelt down and raised it to his lips. And the lady spoke to him very graciously. "Sir Knight," she said, "it would give me a great deal of pleasure if you would tell us your name and let us know your rank and station."

To this Sir Gawain replied, "Lady, I cannot tell you these things at present, for I am bound by a vow to keep them secret just now. So I ask you to be patient with me for a little while."

Then the Lady Ettard said, "Sir Knight, it is a great pity that we may not know your name and rank. All the same, though we are still ignorant of who you are, I hope that you will give us the pleasure of your company for a while, and that you will be gracious enough to stay in this poor place of ours for two or three days, while we offer you whatever refreshment we can."

The magical necklace enchants Sir Gawain.

Now at this point a very unlucky thing happened, and it was this. The Lady Ettard had come to love that necklace of emeralds and opals and gold that she had borrowed from Sir Pelleas, and she loved it so much that she never let it out of her keeping, by day or by night. So at that very moment she was wearing it hung about her neck and throat. And as she talked to Sir Gawain, he looked at that necklace, and its enchantment began to take a very strong hold on him. For soon he began to feel as though his heart were being drawn with a tremendous longing out of his breast and toward the Lady Ettard, so much so that in a little while he could not take his eyes off her at all. And the more he looked at the necklace and at the lady, the more the enchantment of the jewel took hold of his spirits. And so, when the Lady Ettard spoke so graciously to him, he was very glad to accept her kindness. Gazing at her very ardently all the while, he said, "Lady, you are exceedingly kind to show me such great courtesy, and I shall be glad beyond measure to stay with you for a short while."

At these words the Lady Ettard was very greatly pleased, for she said to herself, "Surely this knight, though I do not know who he may be, must be a champion of extraordinary strength and of great deeds. Now if I can persuade him to stay in this castle as my champion, I shall certainly win great credit by it, for I shall have someone to defend my rights who must surely be the greatest knight in all the world." So she put forth every charm and every grace of manner to please Sir Gawain, and Sir Gawain was altogether delighted by the kindness of her ways.

Now Sir Engamore was there at the time, and he was very deeply troubled in spirit. For in the same degree that Sir Gawain received courtesy from the Lady Ettard, in that same degree Sir Engamore was cast down into great sorrow and distress, so much so that it was pitiful to see him. For Sir Engamore said to himself, "Before these foreign knights came here, the Lady Ettard was very kind to me, and was willing to take me for her champion and her lord. But first Sir Pelleas came and overthrew me, and now this strange knight comes and overthrows him. So, beside such a great champion as this one, I have become as nothing in her eyes." So Sir Engamore left that place and went to his own small private room, where he sat down alone in great sorrow.

Now the Lady Ettard had commanded that a very noble and splendid feast be prepared for Sir Gawain and herself, and while it was being made ready, she and Sir Gawain walked together in the pleasure garden of the castle. For there was very pleasant shade in that place, and flowers grew there in great abundance, and many birds sang very sweetly among the blossoms of the trees. And as Sir Gawain and the lady walked together in this way, the attendants stood a little distance off and watched them. And they said to one another, "Surely it would be a very good thing if the Lady Ettard would take this knight for her champion, and if he would stay here in Grantmesnle forever."

Sir Gawain and the Lady Ettard feast together.

So Sir Gawain and the lady walked together, talking very cheerfully, until sunset. By then the supper was ready, and they went in and sat down to it. And as they ate, a number of pages with very fair faces played on harps before them, and several damsels sang very sweetly along with the music, so that Sir Gawain's heart swelled with joy. And he said to himself, "Why should I ever leave this place? After all, I have been banished from King Arthur's court. Why then should I not set up a court of my own here, which might in time come to equal his in glory?" And the Lady Ettard was so beautiful in his eyes that this seemed to him a wonderfully pleasant thought.

Now let us turn to Sir Pelleas.

Sir Pelleas is in doubt.

For after Sir Gawain had left him, the heart of Sir Pelleas began to fear that he had not acted wisely, and at last he said to himself, "Suppose Sir Gawain should forget his duty to me when he meets the Lady Ettard? For it seems that perhaps she has some powerful charm that might easily draw Sir Gawain's heart toward her. And if Sir Gawain should come within the circle of an enchantment like that, he may forget his duty to me and may sin against the honor of his knighthood."

And the more Sir Pelleas thought about this, the more troubled he grew in his mind. So at last, when evening had fallen, he called a squire to him and said, "Go and bring me the robe of a black friar, for I want to go to the castle of Grantmesnle in disguise." So the squire went as he was commanded and brought him such a robe, the black hooded gown of the wandering preachers, and Sir Pelleas dressed himself in it.

By that time darkness had come down entirely over the face of the earth, so that no one could have known Sir Pelleas even if they had seen his face. So he went to the castle, and the people there, thinking that he was a black friar, as he appeared to be, let him into the castle by the postern gate.

Sir Pelleas comes to the castle in disguise.

As soon as Sir Pelleas was inside the castle, he began to ask very carefully where he might find the knight who had come there that afternoon. And the people of the castle, still thinking him a friar of the black order, said to him, "What do you want with that knight?" Sir Pelleas answered, "I have a message for him." The people of the castle said, "You cannot get to that knight just now, for he is at supper with the Lady Ettard, and he is keeping her company in pleasant conversation."

At this Sir Pelleas began to grow very angry, for he greatly disliked the thought that Sir Gawain should at that moment be making merry with the Lady Ettard. So he said, speaking very sternly, "I must speak with that knight at once, and so I order you to take me to him without delay."
