Then the people of the castle said, "Wait, and we will see whether that knight is willing to have you come to him."

So one of the attendants went to the place where Sir Gawain sat at supper with the Lady Ettard, and said, "Sir Knight, a black friar has come here who demands to speak with you at once. He will not be refused, but keeps on making that demand."

At this Sir Gawain was greatly troubled in his conscience, for he knew that he was not dealing honorably with Sir Pelleas, and he wondered whether this black friar might be a messenger from his friend. And yet he could not see how he could refuse to speak with such a messenger. So, after thinking for a while, he said, "Bring the black friar here, and let him deliver his message to me."

So Sir Pelleas, dressed as a black friar, was brought by the attendants into the outer room of the place where Sir Gawain sat at supper with the lady. But for a little while Sir Pelleas did not go into the room. He stood behind the curtain of the anteroom and watched them, for he wanted to make sure whether or not Sir Gawain was true to him.

Now everything in that room where the knight and the lady sat was decked out with extraordinary splendor, and it was lit by several score of wax candles that gave off a most delightful perfume as they burned. And as Sir Pelleas stood behind the curtains, he watched Sir Gawain and the Lady Ettard as they sat together at the table, and he saw that they took great pleasure in each other's company. And he saw that Sir Gawain and the lady drank wine out of the same goblet, and that the cup was of gold. And as he watched the two of them making merry together, he was filled with great anger and indignation, for now he saw that Sir Gawain had betrayed him.

So by and by he could hold himself back no longer, and he took five steps into the room and stood before Sir Gawain and the Lady Ettard. And when they looked up at him in great surprise, he threw back the hood from his face, and they knew him. Then the Lady Ettard shrieked loudly, crying out, "I have been betrayed!" And Sir Gawain sat altogether silent, for he had not a single word to say either to the lady or to Sir Pelleas.

Sir Pelleas insults Sir Gawain.

Then Sir Pelleas came close to the Lady Ettard with such a fierce face that she could not move for fear. And when he had come near her, he seized that necklace of emeralds and opals and gold so violently that he broke its clasp, and so he tore it from her neck. Then he said, "This is mine, and you have no right to it!" And with that he thrust it inside his tunic. Then he turned on Sir Gawain where he sat, and said, "You are false both to your knighthood and to your friendship, for you have betrayed me completely." And with that he raised his arm and struck Sir Gawain across the face with the back of his hand, so hard that the marks of his fingers were left in red all across Sir Gawain's cheek.

Then Sir Gawain turned as pale as ashes, and he cried out, "Sir, I have truly betrayed you, but you have insulted me so deeply that our injuries are equal." To this Sir Pelleas replied, "Not so. For the injury I gave you is only on your cheek, but the injury you gave me is on my heart. All the same, I will answer to you for the insult I have given you. But you also shall answer to me for the wrong you have done me in betraying me."

Then Sir Gawain said, "I am willing to answer to you in full measure." And Sir Pelleas said, "You shall indeed do so." With that he turned and left the place, and he did not so much as look again at either Sir Gawain or the Lady Ettard.

But now that the Lady Ettard no longer had the magic necklace about her neck, Sir Gawain felt nothing of the great enchantment that had drawn him to her so powerfully before. And so he now felt a dislike for her as strong as the liking that had drawn him to her before. And he said to himself, "How could I possibly have betrayed my knighthood like this for this lady, and done so much harm to my friend!" So he pushed back his chair very violently and rose from the table, meaning to leave her.

Sir Gawain and the Lady Ettard speak bitterly together.

But when the Lady Ettard saw what he meant to do, she spoke to him in very great anger, for she was deeply offended that he had deceived her when he said he had beaten Sir Pelleas. So she said hotly, "You may go, and I am very willing to have you do so, for you lied when you told me that you had beaten Sir Pelleas. For now I see that he is both a stronger and a nobler knight than you are. He struck you as though you were his servant, and you still carry the marks of his fingers on your cheek."

At this Sir Gawain was furiously angry, and filled through and through with shame at what had happened to him. So he said, "Lady, I think you have bewitched me, to bring me to such a pitch of dishonor. As for Sir Pelleas, look out into that meadow tomorrow, and see if I do not put a deeper mark on him than he has ever put on me." With that he left the room and went down into the courtyard, and called on the attendants there to bring him his horse. So they did as he commanded, and he rode out at once into the night.

And he was very glad of the darkness of the night, for it seemed to him that it was easier to bear his shame in the dark. So when he came to the grove of trees, he would not go into the pavilion where his friends were. And when Sir Uwain and Sir Marhaus came out to him and asked him to come in, he would not do it but stayed outside in the darkness. For he said to himself, "If I go in where there is a light, they may see the mark of Sir Pelleas's hand upon my face."

So he stayed outside in the darkness and told them to go away and leave him alone.

But when they had gone, he called his squire to him and said, "Take this red armor off me and carry it into the pavilion of Sir Pelleas, for I hate it." So the squire did as Sir Gawain commanded, and Sir Gawain walked up and down the whole night through, greatly troubled in spirit and in heart.

Chapter 6: How the Lady of the Lake Took Back Her Necklace from Sir Pelleas

Sir Gawain challenges Sir Pelleas.

Now when the next morning came, Sir Gawain called his squire to him and said, "Bring my armor here and buckle me into it." And the squire did so. Then Sir Gawain said, "Help me onto my horse," and the squire did so. The morning was still very early. The grass was all shining and sparkling with dew, and the little birds were singing so hard that it might have made anyone glad just to be alive. So, once Sir Gawain was in his armor and seated on his horse, he began to take more courage, and the dark clouds that had hung over him lifted a little. Then he spoke to his squire in a stronger voice and said, "Take this glove of mine and carry it to Sir Pelleas. Tell him that Sir Gawain is riding up and down in the meadow in front of the castle, and that there he challenges Sir Pelleas to meet him on horseback or on foot, whichever that knight may choose."

At these words the squire was greatly astonished. Sir Gawain and Sir Pelleas had always been such close friends that there was hardly another friendship like theirs in all that land, and their love for each other had become a saying on everyone's lips. But he kept his thoughts to himself and only said, "Won't you eat something before you go into battle?" And Sir Gawain said, "No, I will not eat until I have fought. So go and do as I have told you."

So Sir Gawain's squire went to Sir Pelleas in his pavilion, gave that knight Sir Gawain's glove, and delivered Sir Gawain's message. And Sir Pelleas said, "Tell your master that I will come out to meet him as soon as I have had my breakfast."

Now when the news of that challenge reached the ears of Sir Brandiles, Sir Mador de la Porte, Sir Uwain and Sir Marhaus, those knights were very much troubled by it, and Sir Uwain said to the others, "Sirs, let us go and find out what this business is about." So the four knights went to the white pavilion where Sir Pelleas was eating his breakfast.

When they came before Sir Pelleas, Sir Uwain said to him, "What is this quarrel between my kinsman and you?" And Sir Pelleas answered, "I will not tell you. So leave it alone and do not meddle in it."

Then Sir Uwain said, "Would you really fight in earnest with your own friend?" To which Sir Pelleas said, "He is my friend no longer."

Then Sir Brandiles cried out, "It is a great pity that a quarrel should come between such friends as you and Sir Gawain. Won't you let us make peace between you?" But Sir Pelleas replied, "You cannot make peace, for this quarrel cannot be stopped until it is ended."

Then those knights saw that their words were of no use, and they went away and left Sir Pelleas.

So when Sir Pelleas had finished his breakfast, he called for a squire named Montenoir and told him to buckle him into the red armor he had worn all this time, and Montenoir did so. Then, when Sir Pelleas was clad in that armor, he rode out into the meadow in front of the castle where Sir Gawain was waiting. When he got there, those four other knights came to him again and begged him to let peace be made between him and Sir Gawain. But Sir Pelleas would not listen to them, so they went away again and left him, and he rode out into the field in front of the castle of Grantmesnle.

By now a great crowd of people had come out onto the castle walls to watch that fight, for the news of it had spread all around the place. It had also become known that the knight who was to fight Sir Pelleas was that very famous royal knight, Sir Gawain, the son of King Lot of Orkney and a nephew of King Arthur, so all the people were very eager to see so famous a knight do battle.

The Lady Ettard came down to the walls as well, and took her place in a smaller tower that looked out over the field of battle. And when she had taken her place there, she saw that Sir Pelleas was wearing that necklace of emeralds and opals and gold over his armor. Her heart went out to him because of it, and so she hoped that he would be the winner in that encounter.

Sir Pelleas and Sir Gawain do battle.

Sir Pelleas overthrows Sir Gawain.

Then each knight took up whatever position seemed best to him, and each set his spear and his shield and made himself ready for the charge. When they were ready in every way, Sir Marhaus gave the signal. At once each knight left his place and dashed at the other as fast as lightning, and with such fury that the earth thundered and shook under their horses' hoofs. They met squarely in the middle of the course, each knight striking the other in the very center of his shield. In that meeting Sir Gawain's spear burst all the way to the hand guard, but the spear of Sir Pelleas held, so that Sir Gawain was hurled out of his saddle with terrible violence. He struck the ground so hard that he rolled over three times in the dust, and then he lay completely still, as though the life had gone out of him.

At this, all the people on the walls gave a great shout, for it had been a very noble feat of arms.

Then the four knights who had been watching that encounter hurried over to where Sir Gawain lay, and Sir Pelleas also rode back and sat on his horse close by. Sir Uwain and Sir Gawain's squire unlaced Sir Gawain's helmet as fast as they could, and they saw that his face was the color of ashes, and they could not tell that he was breathing at all.

Then Sir Marhaus said, "I believe you have killed this knight, Sir Pelleas." And Sir Pelleas said, "Do you think so?" "Yes," said Sir Marhaus, "and I think it a great pity." To which Sir Pelleas replied, "He has not suffered more than he deserved."

At these words Sir Uwain was filled with anger, and he cried out, "Sir Knight, I think you forget who this knight is. Not only is he a fellow companion of the Round Table, to whom you have vowed complete brotherhood, but he is also the son of a king and the nephew of King Arthur himself."

But Sir Pelleas kept a very steady face at this and answered, "I would not be sorry for this even if that knight were a king in his own right instead of only the son of a king."

Then Sir Uwain raised his voice in great anger and cried out at Sir Pelleas, "Go away! Or something very bad may happen to you." "Very well," said Sir Pelleas, "I will go."

With that he turned his horse and rode away from that place, and went into the woods, and so was gone from their sight.

Sir Pelleas rides away into the forest.

Then the others lifted up Sir Gawain and carried him to the pavilion that had belonged to Sir Pelleas, and there they laid him on Sir Pelleas's couch. But it was more than an hour before he came to himself again, and for a good part of that time the people around him believed that he was dead.

Sir Pelleas is badly wounded.

But not one of those knights knew the truth, which was this: Sir Pelleas had been so badly wounded in the side in that encounter that there was no hope he could live past that day. For though Sir Gawain's spear had burst, and though Sir Pelleas had overthrown him completely, the head of Sir Gawain's spear had pierced Sir Pelleas's armor and gone into his side, and had broken off there. So a piece of the iron spearhead as long as the width of a hand was left in the body of Sir Pelleas, a little above the waist. And all the while Sir Pelleas sat there talking so calmly to those four knights, he was in terrible pain, and the blood was running down inside his armor in streams. What with the loss of blood and the great agony he was suffering, his head swam as light as a feather the whole time he was talking with the others. But he did not say a word to them about the terrible wound he had received. Instead he rode away very proudly into the forest.

But once he was in the forest, he could not hold back any longer. He began to groan very sorely, and he cried out, "Alas! Alas! Surely I have got my death wound in this battle!"

Now it happened that morning that the damsel Parcenet had ridden out to fly a young gerfalcon, which is a great hunting falcon, and a dwarf who belonged to the Lady Ettard had ridden with her to keep her company. As the damsel and the dwarf rode along a certain stretch at the edge of the forest, not very far from Grantmesnle, where the thicker part of the woods began and the thinner part ended, the damsel heard a voice in the woods, lamenting in very great sorrow. So she stopped and listened, and after a while she heard that voice moaning loudly again. Then Parcenet said to the dwarf, "What is that I hear? Surely it is the voice of someone in great trouble. Let us go and see who it is that is moaning so miserably." And the dwarf said, "As you say."

How Parcenet finds Sir Pelleas wounded in the forest.

So the damsel and the dwarf went a little farther, and there they saw a knight sitting on a black horse under an oak tree. That knight was dressed all in red armor, so Parcenet knew it must be Sir Pelleas. She saw that he was leaning on the butt of his spear, which he had planted on the ground, and in that way he was holding himself up on his horse, from which he would otherwise have fallen because he was so weak. And all the while he was making that loud moaning that Parcenet had heard. Seeing him in such a sad state, Parcenet was overcome with great pity, and she hurried to him, crying out, "Oh! Sir Pelleas, what is the matter with you?"

Sir Pelleas looked at her as though she were very far away from him, and because his spirit was so faint, he saw her as if through a film of water. And he said very faintly, "Maiden, I am badly hurt." She said, "How are you hurt, Sir Pelleas?" And he answered, "I have a terrible wound in my side, for a spear point is stuck in it nearly a hand's width deep, so that it reaches almost to my heart. So I think that I will not live very long."

At this the maiden cried out, "Alas! Alas! What is this!" And she wept and lamented and wrung her hands with grief that so noble a knight should have come to such a dreadful state.

Then the dwarf who was with Parcenet, seeing how wild with sorrow she was, said, "Damsel, I know of a certain place in this forest (though it is quite a long way from here) where a very holy hermit lives, who is a wonderfully skillful healer. Now, if we can bring this knight to the chapel where that hermit lives, I believe he may be helped a great deal back to health and ease again."

At this Parcenet said, "Gansaret" (for Gansaret was the dwarf's name), "Gansaret, let us take this knight to that place as quickly as we can. For I am telling you the truth when I say that I love him very dearly." "Well," said the dwarf, "I will show you where that chapel is."

So the dwarf took Sir Pelleas's horse by the bridle and led the way through the forest, and Parcenet rode beside Sir Pelleas and held him up in his saddle. Every so often Sir Pelleas fainted from sickness and pain, so that he would have fallen if she had not held him. So they went on very sorrowfully, and so slowly that it was noon before they came to that very thick and lonely part of the forest where the hermit lived.

When they reached that place, the dwarf said, "There, damsel, is the chapel I told you about."

Then Parcenet raised her eyes and saw a little woodland chapel tucked in among the leafy trees of the forest. Around the chapel was a small open lawn sprinkled with flowers, and near the door of the hermit's dwelling was a spring of water as clear as crystal. It was a very hidden and lonely place, and very quiet and peaceful as well, for in front of the chapel they saw a wild doe and her fawn nibbling the tender grass and plants without any fear of harm. When the dwarf and the maiden and the wounded knight came near, the doe and the fawn looked up with great wide eyes and spread their big ears in wonder. Yet they did not run away, for they feared no harm, and after a while they went back to their grazing. All around the chapel, too, the branches of the trees were full of birds, singing and chirping very cheerfully. Those birds were waiting for their midday meal, which the hermit was in the habit of scattering for them.

(Now this was that same forest sanctuary where King Arthur had come the time he was so badly wounded by Sir Pellinore, as has been told earlier in this history.)

As the maiden and the dwarf and the wounded knight drew near the chapel, a little bell began ringing very sweetly, so that its sound echoed all through those quiet woods, for it was now the hour of noon. Sir Pelleas heard that bell as if from very far away. First he said, "Where have I come to?" and then he managed to cross himself. Parcenet crossed herself too, and the dwarf knelt down and crossed himself. Then, when the bell had stopped ringing, the dwarf called out in a loud voice, "Hello! Hello! Here is someone who needs help!"

Parcenet and the dwarf bring Sir Pelleas to the hermit of the forest.

Then the door of the sanctuary opened, and out came a very old and holy-looking man with a long white beard, as soft as finely combed wool. And as he came out, all the birds that were waiting there flew around him in great flocks, for they thought he had come out to feed them, so the hermit had to brush those little birds away with his hands as he walked over to where the three of them were standing.

When he reached them, he asked them who they were and why they had come there with that wounded knight. So Parcenet told him how things stood with them, and how they had found Sir Pelleas so badly wounded in the forest that morning and had brought him there.

When the hermit had heard her whole story, he said, "Very well, I will take him in." So he took Sir Pelleas into his cell, and when they had helped lay him on the couch, Parcenet and the dwarf went back home again.

After they had gone, the hermit examined Sir Pelleas's wound while Sir Pelleas lay in a deep faint. The faint was so deep that the hermit saw it was the faint that comes before death, and that the knight was near his end. So he said, "This knight is certain to die very soon, for there is nothing I can do to save him." So he left Sir Pelleas's side at once and hurried to prepare the last sacrament, the kind that might be given to a noble knight who was dying.

The Lady of the Lake comes to Sir Pelleas.

Now while the hermit was busy with this, the door suddenly opened, and into the room came a very strange lady. She was dressed all in green, and around her arms she wore bracelets of emeralds and opals set in gold. Her hair, which was very soft, was all black, and it was tied with a crimson ribbon. The hermit saw that her face was as white as ivory, and that her eyes were bright like jewels set in ivory, and so he knew that she was no ordinary mortal.

This lady went straight to Sir Pelleas and bent over him, so close that her breath touched his forehead. And she said, "Alas! Sir Pelleas, that you should lie like this." "Lady," said the hermit, "you may well say 'Alas,' for this knight has only a few minutes to live." To this the lady said, "Not so, holy man, for I tell you that this knight will live for a long time yet." And when she had said this, she bent down and took from around his neck that necklace of emeralds and opals and gold that he wore, and she hung it around her own neck.

When the hermit saw what she was doing, he said, "Lady, what are you doing, and why do you take that ornament from a dying man?"

But the lady answered very calmly, "I gave it to him, so I am only taking back what is mine. But now I beg you to leave me alone with this knight for a little while, for I have great hope that I may bring him back to life again."

Then the hermit was doubtful, and he said, "Do you mean to try to heal him by magic?" And the lady said, "If I do, it will not be by black magic."

So the hermit was satisfied, and he went away and left the lady alone with Sir Pelleas.

The Lady of the Lake heals Sir Pelleas.

Now as soon as the lady was alone with the wounded knight, she began doing a number of very strange things. First she brought out a lodestone of great strength and power, and she held it to the wound. And the iron of the spearhead came out of the wound, and as it came Sir Pelleas groaned with great pain. When the spear point came out, a great rush of blood burst forth like a crimson fountain. But the lady at once pressed a sweet-smelling cloth of fine linen against the wound and stopped the blood, and it bled no more, for she held it inside his veins by very powerful spells. Once the blood was stopped like this, the lady took from the front of her dress a small crystal bottle filled with a liquid of blue color and very rare fragrance. She poured some of this between the cold, gray lips of the knight, and when it touched his lips, life began to come back into his body. For after a little while he opened his eyes and looked around him with a very strange look. The first thing he saw was the lady dressed in green who stood beside him, and she was so beautiful that he thought he must have died and gone to Paradise. So he said, "Am I dead, then?"

"No, you are not dead," said the lady, "but you have been dangerously close to death." "Then where am I?" said Sir Pelleas. And she answered, "You are in a deep part of the forest, and this is the cell of a saintly hermit of the forest." At this Sir Pelleas said, "Who has brought me back to life?" At that the lady smiled and said, "I did."

For a little while Sir Pelleas lay very still. Then after a time he spoke and said, "Lady, I feel very strange." "Yes," said the lady, "that is because you now have a different life." Then Sir Pelleas said, "What has happened to me?" And the lady said, "This: to bring you back to life I gave you a drink of a certain potion, an elixir vitae, the water of life. So now you are only half what you were before. For while one half of you is mortal, the other half of you is fairy."

Sir Pelleas loves the Lady of the Lake.

Then Sir Pelleas looked up and saw that the lady wore around her neck the collar of emeralds and opals and gold that he had worn before. And at once his heart went out to her with a great and burning love, and he said, "Lady, you say that I am half fairy, and I can see that you are altogether fairy. Now I beg you to let me live from now on wherever you are." And the lady said, "It shall be as you ask, for it was for this very reason that I let you come so near to death, and then brought you back to life again."

Then Sir Pelleas said, "When may I go with you?" And she said, "In a little while, when you have had something to drink." "How can that be," said Sir Pelleas, "when I am still as weak as a little child?" To which the lady answered, "When you have drunk some water your strength will come back to you, and you will be completely well and whole again."

So the Lady of the Lake went out, and soon came back carrying a clay jug filled with water from the spring close by. And when Sir Pelleas had drunk that water, he suddenly felt his strength come back to him completely.

Yet he was not at all as he had been before. For now his body felt as light as air, and his soul swelled with a pure joy such as he had never felt before in all his life. So he rose at once from his bed of pain and said, "You have given me life again, and now I give that life to you forever."

Then the lady looked at him and smiled with great tenderness, and she said, "Sir Pelleas, I have cared for you dearly ever since the day I saw you, when you were a young knight, drinking a bowl of milk at a cottage in this forest. The day was warm and you had taken off your helmet, and a young milkmaid with a sunburned face and bare feet came and brought you a bowl of milk, and you drank it down with a hearty appetite. That was the first time I ever saw you, although you did not see me. Since that time I have felt great friendship for all your companions of King Arthur's Court, and for King Arthur himself, all for your sake."

Then Sir Pelleas said, "Lady, will you take me for your knight?" And she said, "Yes." Then Sir Pelleas said, "May I kiss you?" And she said, "Yes, if it pleases you." So Sir Pelleas kissed her on the lips, and so they were promised to each other.

Now let us go back to Parcenet and the dwarf.

Parcenet brings news of Sir Pelleas to Sir Mador de la Porte.

After those two had left the hermitage in the woods, they made their way back toward Grantmesnle. When they had nearly come out of the forest, at a place not far from the grove of trees where the knights-companion had set up their camp, they met one of those knights, dressed in half armor, and that knight was Sir Mador de la Porte. Parcenet called to him by name and said, "Alas! Sir Mador, I have only just left a hermit's cell in the forest, where I left Sir Pelleas wounded to death, and I fear he has only a little while to live."

Then Sir Mador de la Porte cried out, "Ha! Maiden, what is this you are telling me? That is very hard to believe, for when Sir Pelleas left us this morning he showed no sign of any wound or sickness of any kind."

But Parcenet answered, "Even so, I saw him myself lying in great pain and misery, and before he fell into his death faint, he told me himself that he had the iron of a spear in his side."

Then Sir Mador de la Porte said, "Alas! Alas! That is sad news! Now, damsel, with your kind permission, I will leave you and hurry to my companions to tell them this news." And Parcenet said, "Please do."

So Sir Mador de la Porte hurried to the pavilion where his companions were, and he told them the news he had heard.

By this time Sir Gawain had completely recovered from the violent fall he had taken that morning. So when he heard the news that Sir Mador de la Porte brought, he struck his hands together and cried out loud, "Woe is me! What have I done! First I betrayed my friend, and now I have killed him. I will go out at once to find him and to beg his forgiveness before he dies."

But Sir Uwain said, "What is this you mean to do? You are not fit yet to make any journey."

Sir Gawain sets out to find Sir Pelleas.

Sir Gawain said, "I don't care, for I am determined to go and find my friend." And he would not let any of his companions go with him. Instead, when he had told his squire to bring his horse, he mounted it and rode off into the forest alone, heading west, and grieving bitterly as he went.

Now when the afternoon had grown very late, so that the sun was sinking toward its setting and the light fell as red as fire through the forest leaves, Sir Gawain came to that hermit's cell where it stood in the silent and lonely part of the woods. He saw that the hermit was outside his cell, digging in a little garden of lentils. When the hermit saw the armed knight ride onto the lawn, all in the red light of the setting sun, he stopped digging and leaned on his trowel. Then Sir Gawain came near, and, sitting there on his horse, he told the holy man what errand had brought him.

To this the hermit said, "A lady came here several hours ago. She was dressed all in green and was so strange to look at that it was easy to see she was a fairy. By means of certain magic charms that lady cured your friend, and after she had healed him, the two of them rode away into the forest together."

Then Sir Gawain was utterly amazed, and he said, "This is a very strange thing you are telling me: that a dying knight should be brought back to life in so short a time, and should ride out so suddenly from his bed of pain. Now I beg you, tell me which way they went." The hermit said, "They went west." And when Sir Gawain heard this, he said, "I will follow them."

Sir Gawain follows a strange light.

So he rode away and left the hermit gazing after him. As he rode on his way, the twilight began to fall fast, so that after a while the woods grew very dark and strange all around him. But as the darkness came down, a very strange miracle happened. For there appeared in front of Sir Gawain a light of a pale blue color, and it went ahead of him and showed him the way. And he followed it, wondering greatly.

After he had followed the light for a very long time, he came at last, all of a sudden, to the place where the woods ended, and there lay a wide open plain that stretched very far. This plain was all lit up by a strange glow like the light of a clear full moon, although no moon was shining at that time. In that pale, silver light Sir Gawain could see everything wonderfully clearly. So he saw that he was on a plain covered all over with flowers of many kinds, and their perfume so filled the night that it seemed to press upon his chest with a great sweetness. And he saw that in front of him lay a great lake, very wide and still. All these things looked so strange in that light that Sir Gawain knew he had come into Fairyland. So he rode among the tall flowers toward that lake with a kind of fear, for he did not know what was going to happen to him.

As he drew near the lake, he saw a knight and a lady coming toward him. When they came close, he saw that the knight was Sir Pelleas, and that his face was very strange. And he saw that the lady was the one he had seen before, all dressed in green, when he had traveled in the Forest of Adventure with Sir Uwain and Sir Marhaus.

Sir Gawain finds Sir Pelleas.

When Sir Gawain first saw Sir Pelleas he was filled with great fear, for he thought it was a ghost he was looking at. But when he saw that Sir Pelleas was alive, a joy as great as that fear had been rose up in his heart, and he hurried toward Sir Pelleas. When he came near him, he leaped down from his horse, crying out, "Forgive me! Forgive me!" with the greatest passion. Then he would have taken Sir Pelleas in his arms, but Sir Pelleas drew back from Sir Gawain's touch, though not in any anger. And Sir Pelleas spoke in a voice that was very thin and had a silvery clearness, as though it came from far away, and he said, "Do not touch me, for I am not what I was before. I am not all human now, but part fairy. But as for my forgiveness: I forgive you for whatever wrong I may have suffered at your hands. And more than that, I give you my love, and I hope with all my heart for your joy and happiness. But now I am going away to leave you, dear friend, and perhaps I shall never see you again. So I leave this with you as my last wish: that you go back to King Arthur's Court and make your peace with the Queen. In that way you can bring them news of everything that has happened to me."

Then Sir Gawain cried out in great sorrow, "Where are you going?"

And Sir Pelleas said, "I am going to that wonderful city of gold and blue that lies over there in that valley of flowers."

Then Sir Gawain said, "I see no city, only a lake of water."

To this Sir Pelleas replied, "Even so, there is a city over there, and I am going to it. And so I bid you farewell now."

Then Sir Gawain looked into the face of Sir Pelleas and saw again how strange it looked in that light. For it was as white as ivory, and his eyes shone like jewels set in ivory, and a smile lay on his lips that grew neither larger nor smaller but always stayed exactly the same. (For those who were of that kind always had that strange look and smiled in that way: the Lady of the Lake, and Sir Pelleas, and Sir Lancelot of the Lake.)

Sir Pelleas disappears into the lake.

Then Sir Pelleas and the Lady of the Lake turned and left Sir Gawain where he stood, and they went toward the lake, and they rode into the lake. And as soon as the hoofs of Sir Pelleas's horse touched the water of the lake, Sir Pelleas was gone, and Sir Gawain saw him no more, though he stood there for a long time, weeping bitterly.

And so ends the story of Sir Pelleas.

But Sir Gawain went back to King Arthur's Court as he had promised Sir Pelleas he would, and he made his peace with Queen Guinevere. And after that, though the Queen did not love him, there was peace between them. And Sir Gawain told all these things to the Court of King Arthur, and everyone marveled at what he told.

And only twice after that was Sir Pelleas ever seen by any of his old companions.

And Sir Marhaus was made a Companion of the Round Table and became one of its foremost knights.

And the Lady Ettard took Sir Engamore back into her favor, and that summer they were married, and Sir Engamore became lord of Grantmesnle.

And so ends this story.

The Book of Three Worthies, Part Three: The Story of Sir Gawain

Foreword

Here follows the story of Sir Gawain, and of how he showed such wonderful faithfulness to King Arthur, who was his lord, that I do not believe anyone had ever seen such faithfulness before.

For truly, though Sir Gawain was at times very rough and harsh in his manner, and though he was always so plain-spoken that his words hid the gentle nature that lay within him, still, under that pride of manner, there was a great deal of courtesy. And at times he was so gracious in his manner and so soft in his speech that many people called him the Knight of the Silver Tongue.

So here you will read how his faithfulness to King Arthur brought him such a high reward that almost anyone in all the world might envy him his great good fortune.

Chapter 1: How a White Hart Appeared Before King Arthur, How Sir Gawain and His Brother Gaheris Chased It, and What Happened to Them on That Quest

Once upon a time King Arthur, together with Queen Guinevere and all his Court, was making a royal journey through a part of his kingdom that lay a good way from Camelot. At this time the King traveled in very great state, and Queen Guinevere had her own Court around her, so there were many squires and pages. And so, counting the knights, lords, and ladies in attendance, more than a hundred and twenty people were traveling with the King and Queen.

Now it happened that the season was very warm, so that when the middle of the day came the King ordered a number of pavilions to be set up, where they might rest until the heat of the day had passed. So the attendants set up three pavilions in a pleasant glade at the edge of the forest.

When this had been done, the King gave orders that the tables at which they were to eat their midday meal should be set out beneath the shade of that grove of trees. For a gentle wind was blowing and many birds were singing, so that it was very pleasant to sit in the open air.

So the attendants of the Court did as the King commanded, and the tables were set on the grass in the shade, and the King and Queen and all the lords and ladies of their Courts sat down to that cheerful meal.

A white hart and a white hound appear before King Arthur at his feast.

Now while they sat there feasting, well content, with much laughter and pleasant talk among themselves, there suddenly came a great outcry from the woods close by. And with that, out of the cover of that leafy wilderness burst a very beautiful white hart (which is a full-grown stag), chased by a white brachet (which is a kind of hunting hound) just as beautiful. There was not a hair on either of these animals that was not as white as milk, and each wore around its neck a collar of gold that was very beautiful to see.

The hound chased the white hart with a tremendous outcry and baying, and the hart fled in utter terror. In this way they ran three times around the table where King Arthur and his Court sat at their meal. Twice during that chase the hound caught the hart and nipped it on the haunch, and each time the hart leaped away; and everyone sitting there saw that there was blood in two places on its haunch where the hound had nipped it. But each time the hart escaped from the hound, and the hound chased after it, yelling and baying so loudly that King Arthur and Queen Guinevere and all their Court were annoyed at the noise and uproar those two creatures made. Then the hart fled back into the forest by another path, and the hound chased it, and both were gone, and the baying of the hound sounded farther and farther away as it ran off into the woods.

Now, before the King and Queen and their Court had recovered from their astonishment at these things, there suddenly appeared, at the very spot in the forest where the hart and the hound had come out, a knight and a lady. The knight was very lordly in his bearing, and the lady was extremely beautiful. The knight wore half-armor, and the lady was dressed in green as though for the hunt. The knight rode a dapple-gray war horse, and the lady a spotted palfrey. With them were two squires, also dressed for the hunt.

When these people saw the large company gathered there, they stopped as though surprised. And while they stood there, another knight suddenly appeared on a black horse, clad in full armor, and he seemed to be very angry. For he charged at the half-armed knight and struck him such a terrible blow with his sword that the first knight fell from his horse and lay on the ground as though he were dead. At that the lady who was with him shrieked in great grief.

King Arthur and his Court see a knight carry off a lady as his prisoner.

Then the knight in full armor on the black horse rode up to the lady and seized her. He lifted her from her palfrey and laid her across the front of his saddle, and with that he rode back into the forest. The lady screamed so violently that it was a great pity to hear her, but the knight paid no attention to her shrieks and carried her off by main force into the forest.

Then, after he and the lady were gone, the two squires came and lifted the wounded knight onto his horse, and then they too went away into the forest and were gone.

King Arthur and his Court watched all this from a distance, and they were so far away that they could not stop that knight on the black horse from carrying the lady off into the forest. Nor could they bring help to the other knight in half-armor, whom they had seen struck down like that. So they were very much grieved by what they had seen and did not know what to make of it. Then King Arthur said to his Court, "Sirs, is there not one of you who will follow this adventure, and find out what it means that we have just seen, and make that knight tell why he behaved as he did?"

Sir Gawain and Gaheris take on this adventure.

At this Sir Gawain said, "Lord, I will be very glad indeed to take on this adventure, if I have your permission." And King Arthur said, "You have my permission." Then Sir Gawain said, "Lord, I wish you would also let me take my younger brother, Gaheris, with me as my squire on this undertaking. For he is growing up fast, and yet he has never seen any real adventure at arms." So King Arthur said, "You have my permission to take your brother with you."

At this Gaheris was very glad, for he had an adventurous spirit, and so the thought of going with his brother on this quest gave him great pleasure.

So the two of them went to Sir Gawain's pavilion, and there Gaheris, acting as his squire, helped Sir Gawain put on his armor. Then they rode out on the quest that Sir Gawain had undertaken.

They traveled a very long way, following the direction they had seen the hart take when it fled from the hound. And whenever they met any of the forest folk, they would ask them which way the white brachet and the white hart had gone, and which way the knight and the lady had gone. And so they followed that adventure at a good pace.

They come upon two knights fighting.

After a while, when they had gone a long way and the afternoon was far along, they suddenly became aware of a great noise of combat, like the sound of a fierce battle. So they followed this sound, and after a while they came to an open meadow with very fair and level turf. There they saw two knights fighting with furious passion and with deadly purpose. Then Sir Gawain said, "What's this? Let's go and see." So he and Gaheris rode forward to where those two knights were fighting, and as they came near, the two knights stopped their combat and rested on their weapons. Then Sir Gawain said, "Ha! Sirs, what's the matter here, and why are you fighting each other so furiously?" One of the knights said to Sir Gawain, "Sir, this does not concern you." And the other said, "Don't meddle with us, for this battle is of our own choosing."

"Sirs," said Sir Gawain, "I would be very sorry to interfere in your quarrel. But I am in pursuit of a white hart and a white brachet that came this way, and also of a knight who carried off a lady along the same path. Now I would be very much obliged to you if you would tell me whether you have seen anything of either of them."

One of the knights tells their story.

Then the knight who had spoken first said, "Sir, this is a very strange thing, for it was on account of that very white hart and that brachet, and of that knight and lady, that we two were fighting the battle you just saw. For this is how it was. We two are brothers, and we were riding together in perfect friendship when that hart and that hound came this way. Then my brother said he very much hoped the white hart would escape from the hound, and I said I hoped the hound would catch the hart and bring it down. Then that knight came by with the lady, his captive, and I said I would follow that knight and rescue the lady, and my brother said that he would take on that adventure himself.

"Over these points we fell into a dispute. For it seemed to me that I felt great affection for that hound, while my brother felt just as remarkable a fondness for the white hart; and it seemed to me that since I had spoken first, I should have the right to follow that adventure. But my brother was fond of the hart, and he thought that since he was the elder of us two, he had the better right to the adventure. So we quarreled, and before long we fell to that fight in which you saw us."

At this Sir Gawain was very much astonished, and he said, "Sirs, I can't understand how so great a quarrel could come out of so small a dispute. And truly it is a great pity for two brothers to quarrel as you have done, and to give each other such bad cuts and wounds as I can see you have both received."

"Sir," said the second knight, "I think you are right, and I now find that I am very much ashamed of that quarrel." And the other said, "I too am sorry for what I have done."

Then Sir Gawain said, "Sirs, I would be very glad indeed if you would tell me your names." And one knight said, "I am called Sir Sorloise of the Forest." And the other said, "I am called Sir Brian of the Forest."

Then Sir Sorloise said, "Sir Knight, I would think it a very great courtesy if you would tell me who you are."

"I would be very glad to do that," said Sir Gawain, and with that he told them his name and rank. Now when they heard who Sir Gawain was, those two knights were very much astonished and pleased, for no one in all the courts of chivalry was more famous than Sir Gawain, the son of King Lot of Orkney. So those two brothers said, "It is certainly a great joy to us to meet so famous a knight as you, Sir Gawain."

Then Sir Gawain said, "Sir Knights, that hart and that hound came only a short while ago to where King Arthur and Queen Guinevere and their Courts of lords and ladies were feasting. And there, too, we all saw that knight seize the lady and make her his captive. And so my brother and I have come out at King Arthur's command to discover what it was that we saw. Now I will think it a very great courtesy on your part if you will give up this adventure and go in friendship to the King's Court, and tell him what you saw, and how you quarreled, and how we met. For otherwise I will have to fight you both myself, and that would be a great pity, since you are tired from battle and I am fresh."

Then these two knights said, "Sir, we will do as you wish, for we have no desire to fight so powerful a knight as you."

So those two knights went off to King Arthur's Court as Sir Gawain had directed, and Sir Gawain and his brother rode on with their adventure.

Sir Gawain and Gaheris meet a knight beside the river.

After a while they came near a great river, and there they saw ahead of them a single knight in full armor, who carried a spear in his hand and a shield hanging from his saddle. Sir Gawain hurried forward and called out loudly to the knight, and the knight stopped and waited until Sir Gawain had caught up with him. And when Sir Gawain came up to that knight he said, "Sir Knight, have you seen a white hart and a white hound go by this way? And have you seen a knight carrying off a captive lady?"

To this the knight said, "Yes, I saw them both, and I am following them right now, meaning to find out where they are going." Then Sir Gawain said, "Sir Knight, I tell you not to follow this adventure any farther, for I am set on it myself. So I want you to give it up, so that I may take it on in your place." "Sir," said the other knight, speaking very hotly, "I don't know who you are, and I don't much care. But as for following this adventure, I tell you that I mean to follow it to the end, and so I will, no matter who tries to stop me."

At that Sir Gawain said, "Sir, you will not go forward on this adventure unless you first fight me." And the knight said, "Sir, I am very willing to do that."

Sir Gawain overthrows the knight.

So each knight took the position on that field that seemed best to him, and each put himself on guard and set his shield and his spear. Then, when they were ready in every way, they at once charged against each other, rushing together with great speed and with such a thunder that the ground trembled and shook beneath them. So they met in the middle of the course, and the spear of the strange knight burst into small pieces, but Sir Gawain's spear held. And with it he hurled that knight out of his saddle with such violence that he struck the ground with a crash like an earthquake.

Then Sir Gawain rode back to where his enemy lay (for that knight was not able to get up), and he took the helmet off the fallen knight's head and saw that he was very young and handsome.

Now when the fresh air struck the knight's face, he soon woke from his faint and came back to his senses. At that Sir Gawain said, "Do you yield to me?" And the knight said, "I do." Then Sir Gawain said, "Who are you?" And the knight said, "I am called Sir Alardin of the Isles." "Very well," said Sir Gawain. "Then this is my command to you: you will go to King Arthur's Court and give yourself up to him as a captive taken by my prowess. And you are to tell him all that you know of the hart and the hound and the knight and the lady. And you will tell him everything that has happened to you in this encounter."

So the knight said that he would do that, and with that they parted, the one party going one way and the other party going the other.

After that Sir Gawain and his brother Gaheris rode a long way, until at last they came through a forest out onto an open plain, and by now it was about sunset. And there in the middle of the plain they saw a very stately and noble castle with five towers, and of very great strength.

Sir Gawain and Gaheris find the dead brachet.

And right there they saw a sight that filled them with great sorrow. For they saw the dead body of that white brachet lying beside the road like any carrion. And they saw that the hound had been shot through with three arrows, so they knew it had been killed very violently.

Now when Sir Gawain saw that beautiful hound lying dead like that, he was filled with great sorrow. "What a pity it is," he cried, "that this noble hound should be killed like this! For I think it was the most beautiful hound I ever saw in all my life. There has surely been great treachery against it here, for it has been foully dealt with because of that white hart it was chasing. Now I make a vow that if I can find that hart, I will kill it with my own hands, because it was in that chase that this hound met its death."

After that they rode on toward the castle, and as they came near, there, grazing on the meadows in front of the castle, they saw that white hart with the golden collar.

Now as soon as the white hart saw those two strangers, it fled at great speed toward the castle and ran into the castle courtyard. And when Sir Gawain saw the stag, he gave chase at great speed, and Gaheris followed after his brother.

Sir Gawain kills the white hart.

So Sir Gawain chased the white hart into the courtyard of the castle, and from there it could not escape. Then Sir Gawain leaped down from his horse, drew his sword, and killed the hart with a single stroke of his weapon.

He did this in great haste. But when he had done it, and it was too late to undo it, he bitterly regretted what he had done.

Now at all this commotion the lord and the lady of that castle came out. The lord was very haughty and noble in his looks, and the lady was extraordinarily graceful and very beautiful. Sir Gawain looked at the lady and thought he had hardly ever seen so beautiful a lady, and so he was sorrier than ever that he had killed that white hart in his haste.

But when the lady of the castle saw the white hart lying dead on the stone pavement of the courtyard, she struck her hands together and shrieked so shrilly and so loudly that it pierced the ears to hear her. And she cried out, "Oh, my white hart, are you dead, then?" And with that she began to weep passionately. Then Sir Gawain said, "Lady, I am very sorry for what I have done, and I wish I could undo it." Then the lord of that castle said to Sir Gawain, "Sir, did you kill that stag?" "Yes," said Sir Gawain. "Sir," said the lord of the castle, "you have done a very wicked thing, and if you will wait a little while I will take full vengeance on you." To which Sir Gawain said, "I will wait for you as long as you please."

The knight of the castle attacks Sir Gawain.

Then the lord of the castle went into his chamber and put on his armor, and in a little while he came out in a fury. "Sir," said Sir Gawain, "what is your quarrel with me?" And the lord of the castle said, "That you have killed the white hart that was so dear to my lady." To which Sir Gawain said, "I would never have killed the white hart if it weren't that, because of it, the white brachet was so treacherously killed." At this the lord of the castle was angrier than ever, and he rushed at Sir Gawain and struck him before he was ready. The blow cut through the shoulder plate of his armor and through the flesh to the bone of his shoulder, so that Sir Gawain was in great agony from the stroke. Then Sir Gawain was filled with rage at the pain of the wound, and he struck the knight such a terrible blow that it cut through his helmet and into the bone beneath. At that the knight fell to his knees from the fierceness of the blow and could not get up again. Then Sir Gawain seized his helmet and tore it off his head.

At this the knight said in a weak voice, "Sir Knight, I beg you for mercy, and I yield myself to you."

Sir Gawain moves to kill the knight of the castle.

But Sir Gawain was in a furious rage because of the unexpected blow he had received and the great agony of his wound, and so he would show no mercy, but raised his sword, meaning to kill that knight.

Sir Gawain strikes the lady of the castle without meaning to.

Then the lady of the castle saw what Sir Gawain meant to do, and she broke away from her damsels and ran and threw herself on the knight, to shield him with her own body. At that very moment Sir Gawain was striking and could not stop his blow. Even so, he managed to turn his sword in his hand so that its edge did not strike the lady. But the flat of the sword struck her on the neck a very grievous blow, and the blade cut her a little, so that the blood ran down her smooth white neck and over her neckerchief. And with the force of the blow the lady fell down and lay on the ground as though she were dead.

Now when Sir Gawain saw that, he thought he had killed the lady in his haste, and he was filled with dread at what he had done, and he cried, "Woe is me! What have I done?"

"Alas!" said Gaheris, "that was a very shameful blow you struck. And the shame of it is mine too, because you are my brother. Now I wish I had never come with you to this place."

Then Sir Gawain said to the lord of the castle, "Sir, I will spare your life, for I am very sorry for what I have done in my haste."

But the knight of the castle was filled with great bitterness, because he thought his lady was dead, and so he cried out in despair, "I will not take your mercy now, for you are a knight without mercy and without pity. And since you have killed my lady, who is dearer to me than my life, you may kill me too. For that is the only service you can do me now."

But by now the lady's damsels had come to her where she lay, and the chief of them cried out to the lord of the castle, "Ah, sir, your lady is not dead! She has only fainted, and she will soon come to herself again."

Then, when the lord of the castle heard that, he began to weep freely for pure joy; for now that he knew his lady was alive, he could not contain himself for gladness. With that Sir Gawain came to him and lifted him up from the ground where he was, and kissed him on the cheek. Then some others came and carried the lady away to her chamber, and there in a little while she recovered from her faint and was little the worse for the blow she had received.

That night Sir Gawain and his brother Gaheris stayed with the knight and the lady, and when the knight learned who Sir Gawain was, he felt it a great honor to have so famous a knight in his castle. So they feasted together that evening in great friendship.

Now when they had refreshed themselves, Sir Gawain said, "I beg you, sir, to tell me what was the meaning of the white hart and the white brachet that led me into this adventure."

To this the lord of the castle (whose name was Sir Ablamor of the Marise) said, "I will do so." And with that he spoke as follows:

"You must know, sir, that I have a brother who has always been very dear to me, and when I took this lady of mine for my wife, he took her sister for his wife.

The lord of the castle tells the story of the white hart and the white hound.

"Now my brother lived in a castle near this one, and we visited back and forth in great friendship. But one day it happened that my lady and my brother's lady were riding through this forest together, talking very pleasantly. At that moment a lady appeared to them, extremely beautiful and very strange in her looks, for I do not think either my lady or her sister had ever seen anyone like her before.

"This strange lady brought those two ladies a white hart and a white brachet, and she held the hart and the hound each by a silver chain fastened to a golden collar around its neck. The white hart she gave to my lady, and the white brachet she gave to my lady's sister. And then she went away, leaving them very glad.

"But their gladness did not last long. For ever since that time there has been nothing but discord between my brother and me, and between my lady and her sister, because the white hound has always hunted the white hart to destroy it. And so my lady and I have taken great offense at my brother and his lady because they did not keep the white brachet at home. And so it has happened that several times we have tried to destroy the hound, so that my brother and his lady have taken just as much offense at us.

"Now today I happened to be near the edge of the forest to the east of us, when I heard a great outcry in the woods. Soon the white hart that belonged to my lady came fleeing through the woods, and the white brachet that belonged to my brother's lady was chasing it; and my brother and his lady and two squires were riding hard after the hart and the brachet.

"Then I was very angry, for it seemed to me that they were chasing that white hart to spite my lady and me, and so I followed after them as fast as I could.

"So I came upon them at the edge of the woods, near where a number of pavilions were set up in the shade of a grove of trees in the middle of the meadow. And there, in my anger, I struck my brother a great blow, so that I knocked him down from his horse. And I seized his lady and threw her across the front of my saddle and carried her away here to this castle. And here I have kept her, out of revenge, because they chased the white hart that belonged to my lady. For my lady loved that hart as she loved nothing else in the world, except me."

"Sir," said Sir Gawain, "this is a very strange business. Now I beg you to tell me what that lady looked like who gave the white hart and the white hound to those two ladies." "Sir," said the knight, "she was dressed all in crimson, and around her throat and arms she wore a great many ornaments of gold set with stones of many colors. Her hair was red like gold and was caught up in a net of gold, and her eyes were very black and shone with extraordinary brightness, and her lips were like coral, so that she had a very strange appearance."

Sir Gawain hears of Vivien.

"Ha!" said Sir Gawain. "From that description I think that lady could have been none other than the sorceress Vivien. For these days she spends all her time doing mischief like this by her enchantments, out of pure spite. And indeed, I think it would be a very good thing if she were put out of this world, so that she could do no more such mischief. But tell me, sir, where is that lady now, your wife's sister?" "Sir," said the knight, "she is in this castle, and she is a prisoner of honor." "Well," said Sir Gawain, "since both the hart and the hound are now dead, you surely can have no more quarrel with her and your brother. And so I beg you to set her free, and to be friends again with one another, in good will, as you were before." And the lord of the castle said, "Sir, it shall be so."

And so he set the lady free then and there, and after that there was friendship between them, as Sir Gawain had directed.

And the next day Sir Gawain and his brother Gaheris returned to the King's Court, and Sir Gawain told King Arthur and his Court everything that had happened, hiding nothing from them.

Queen Guinevere is displeased with Sir Gawain.

Now Queen Guinevere was very much displeased when she heard how Sir Gawain had refused to show mercy to that knight, and how he had struck the lady with his sword. So she said quietly to one of those who stood near her, "It seems to me a very strange thing for a belted knight to do, to refuse mercy to a fallen enemy and to strike a lady with his sword. For I should think that any sword that had drawn a lady's blood in that way would be dishonored forever. And I cannot think that anyone who would strike a lady like that could hold himself blameless before his vow of knighthood."

Sir Gawain breaks his sword.

Sir Gawain overheard this, and he was extremely angry at it. But he hid his anger at the time. Only after he had gone away did he say to his brother Gaheris, "I believe that lady hates me with all her heart. But someday I will show her that I have more courtesy in me, and am gentler, than she believes. As for my sword, since she thinks it is dishonored by that blow, I will not use it anymore." So he took the sword out of its sheath and broke it across his knee and threw it away.

Now all this has been told to prepare you for what follows. For there you will learn what great and noble things Sir Gawain could do when the need came. For perhaps you who have read this story may feel, as Queen Guinevere did, that Sir Gawain was not truly as courteous as a belted knight should have been in the adventure I have just told.

Chapter 2: How King Arthur Got Lost in the Forest and Met with a Very Strange Adventure in a Castle He Came To

Now it happened, some while after this, that King Arthur was at Tintagalon on certain affairs of state. And Queen Guinevere with her Court and the King's Court made a royal journey from Camelot to Caerleon, and there they stayed, waiting until the King should finish his business at Tintagalon and join them at Caerleon.

It was the springtime of the year, and everything was very bright and merry, and so King Arthur was seized with a great longing for adventure. So he called to him a certain squire of whom he was especially fond, named Boisenard, and he said to him, "Boisenard, this day is so pleasant that I hardly know how to contain myself for the joy I take in it. It seems to me that my heart is nearly ready to burst with a great pleasure of longing. So I have a mind to go roaming, with only you for company."

To this Boisenard said, "Lord, I know of nothing that would give me greater pleasure than that."

So King Arthur said, "Very well. Then let us leave this place in such a way that no one will know we have gone. We will ride to Caerleon and surprise the Queen by arriving there when she does not expect us."

So Boisenard brought armor without any emblem on it, and he dressed the King in that armor. Then the two of them rode out together, and no one knew that they had left the castle.

King Arthur sets out with his squire.

And when they came out into the fields, King Arthur whistled and sang and joked and laughed and made himself merry. For he was like a war-horse turned out to grass, who glories in the sunshine and the warm air and becomes like a young colt again.

So after a while they came into the forest and rode along with great contentment of spirit. They took this path and they took that path for no reason except that the day was so bright and merry. And so, by and by, they lost their way in the mazes of the woodland and did not know where they were.

They are lost in the forest.

Now when they found themselves lost like this, they traveled more carefully, going first this way and then that way, but in no way could they find their way out of their tangle. And so night fell while they still did not know where they were. Everything grew very dark and gloomy, and the woodland all around them was full of strange and unfamiliar sounds.

Then King Arthur said, "Boisenard, this is a very puzzling fix, and I do not know how we shall find lodging for tonight."

To this Boisenard said, "Lord, if I have your permission, I will climb one of these trees and see if I can find any sign of a dwelling in this wilderness." And King Arthur said, "Do so, I ask you."

Boisenard sees a light.

So Boisenard climbed a very tall tree, and from the top of it he saw a light a great distance away. And he said, "Lord, I see a light in that direction." And with that he came down from the tree again.

So King Arthur and Boisenard went in the direction where Boisenard had seen the light. And by and by they came out of the forest into an open place, where they saw a very great castle with several tall towers, very grim and forbidding to look at. And it was from this castle that the light Boisenard had seen was shining. So the two of them rode up to the castle, and Boisenard called out loudly and pounded on the castle gate. At once a porter came and demanded to know what they wanted. Boisenard said to him, "You there, we want to come in and lodge here tonight, for we are very weary." The porter said, "Who are you?", speaking very roughly and rudely to them, for in the darkness he could not see what rank they were. Then Boisenard said, "This is a knight of very high rank, and I am his squire. We have lost our way in the forest, and now we come here looking for shelter."

"Sir," said the porter, "if you know what is good for you, you will sleep in the forest rather than come into this place. For this is no very good refuge for knights-errant to shelter in."

At this King Arthur spoke to the porter, for what the porter had said stirred up a great curiosity in him. So he said, "No, we will not go away from here, and we demand to lodge here tonight."

Then the porter said, "Very well; you may come in." And with that he opened the gate, and they rode into the courtyard of the castle.

King Arthur and his squire enter the castle.

Now at the noise of their coming a great many lights appeared within the castle, and a number of attendants came running out. Some of these helped King Arthur and Boisenard to dismount, and others took the horses, and still others brought basins of water for them to wash in. And after they had washed their faces and hands, other attendants led them into the castle.

As they came into the castle, they heard a great noise of very many people talking and laughing together, with the sound of singing and of harping. And so they came into the hall of the castle, and they saw that it was lit with a great many candles and tapers and torches. Here they found a crowd of people gathered at a table spread for a feast. At the head of the table sat a knight who was well on in years, with hair and beard as white as milk. Yet he was extremely strong and sturdy of build, with wonderfully broad shoulders and a great barrel of a chest. This knight had a very stern and forbidding look. He was dressed all in black, and around his neck he wore a chain of gold with a locket of gold hanging from it.

Now when this knight saw King Arthur and Boisenard come into the hall, he called out to them in a very loud voice, inviting them to come and sit with him at the head of the table. And they did so. Those at the head of the table made room for them, and so they sat there beside the knight.

King Arthur and Boisenard were extremely hungry, and so they ate with a great appetite and enjoyed the hospitality they were given. Meanwhile the knight kept them in very pleasant conversation, talking to them about whatever would entertain them most. So after a while the feast was over and they stopped eating.

The knight of the castle challenges King Arthur to an adventure.

Then, all of a sudden, the knight said to King Arthur, "Sir, you are young and bold of spirit, and I do not doubt that you have a great heart inside you. What do you say now to a little sport between the two of us?" At this King Arthur looked at that knight very steadily, and he believed that the knight's face was not as old as it seemed. For his eyes were extremely bright and shone like sparks of fire, and so Arthur was doubtful, and he said, "Sir, what sport do you have in mind?" At this the knight burst out laughing very heartily, and he said, "The sport I have in mind is a very strange one, for it is this: that you and I shall prove to each other how much courage each of us has." And King Arthur said, "How shall we prove that?" The knight answered, "This is what we shall do. You and I will stand out in the middle of this hall, and you shall have leave to try to strike off my head. And if I can take that blow without dying of it, then I shall have leave to strike off your head in the same way."

At this speech King Arthur was filled with great dread, and he said, "That is a very strange sport for two men to take up."

Now when King Arthur said this, everyone in the hall burst out laughing beyond all measure, as though they would never stop their merriment. Then, when they had grown somewhat quiet again, the knight of the castle said, "Sir, are you afraid of that sport?" At this King Arthur grew very angry, and he said, "No, I am not afraid. No man has ever yet had reason to say that I showed myself afraid of anyone." "Very well," said the knight of the castle, "then let us try that sport I spoke of." And King Arthur said, "I am willing."

Then Boisenard came to King Arthur where he stood, and he said, "Lord, do not enter into this thing. Let me take on this venture in your place instead, for I am certain that some great treachery is being plotted against you." But King Arthur said, "No. No man shall take my danger upon himself. I will bear my own danger without calling on any man to bear it for me." So he said to the knight of the castle, "Sir, I am ready for that sport you spoke of. But who is to strike the first blow, and how shall we draw lots for it?" "Sir," said the knight of the castle, "there will be no lots drawn. For since you are the guest in this place, you shall have the first try at that sport."

With that the knight stood up and laid aside his black robe. Underneath he was dressed in a shirt of fine linen, very skilfully worked, and he wore crimson hose. Then he opened that linen shirt at the throat and turned down its collar so as to lay his neck bare to the blow. And he said, "Now, Sir Knight, you will have to strike well if you want to win at this sport."

King Arthur cuts off the head of the knight of the castle.

But King Arthur showed no dread of that task. He rose and drew Excalibur, so that the blade of the sword flashed with an extraordinary brightness. Then he measured his distance, and lifted the sword, and struck the knight of the castle on the neck with all his might. And the blade cut through the knight's neck with wonderful ease, so that the head flew off the body and landed a long way away.

But the trunk of that knight's body did not fall. Instead it stood there, and it walked over to where the head lay, and the hands of the trunk picked up the head and set it back on the body. And there was that knight, as sound and whole as he had ever been in all his life.

At this all the people of the castle shouted and laughed with great merriment, and they called out to King Arthur that now it was his turn to try that sport. So the King made himself ready, laying aside his surcoat and opening his shirt at the throat, as the knight of the castle had done. And at that Boisenard broke into loud weeping. Then the knight of the castle said, "Sir, are you afraid?" And King Arthur said, "No, I am not afraid. Every man must come to his death some time, and it seems that my time has now come, and that I am to lay down my life in this foolish way through no fault of my own."

Then the knight of the castle said, "Well, stand a little way off, so that I do not strike you too close and so lose the force of my blow."

The knight torments King Arthur.

So King Arthur stood out in the middle of the hall, and the knight of the castle swung his sword several times but did not strike. And several times, too, he laid the blade of the sword on King Arthur's neck, and it was very cold. Then King Arthur cried out in great passion, "Sir, it is your right to strike, but I beg you not to torment me like this." "No," said the knight of the castle, "it is my right to strike when it pleases me, and I will not strike a moment before. If it pleases me, I will torment you for a long while before I kill you." So he laid his sword on King Arthur's neck several more times. And King Arthur said nothing more, but bore that torment with a very steady spirit.

Then the knight of the castle said, "You seem to be a very brave and honorable knight, and I have a mind to make a bargain with you." And King Arthur said, "What is that bargain?" "It is this," said the knight of the castle. "I will spare your life for a year and a day, if you will pledge me your knightly word to come back here at the end of that time."

Then King Arthur said, "Very well; so be it." And with that he pledged his knightly word to return at the end of that time, swearing to the pledge on the cross of Excalibur's hilt.

The knight of the castle sets King Arthur a riddle.

Then the knight of the castle said, "I will make another bargain with you." "What is it?" said King Arthur. "My second bargain is this," said the knight of the castle. "I will give you a riddle. If you answer that riddle when you come back here, and make no mistake in your answer, then I will spare your life and set you free." And King Arthur said, "What is the riddle?" And the knight answered, "The riddle is this: What is it that a woman wants most of all in the world?"

"Sir," said King Arthur, "I will try to find the answer to that riddle. And I thank you for sparing my life for as long as you have, and for giving me the chance to escape my death." At this the knight of the castle smiled very sourly, and he said, "I do not offer you this out of mercy, but because I take pleasure in tormenting you. For what delight can you have in living your life when you know that you must surely die at the end of one short year? And what pleasure can you have in living even that year, when you will be tormented with worry to find the answer to my riddle?"

Then King Arthur said, "I think you are very cruel." And the knight said, "I do not deny it."

So that night King Arthur and Boisenard slept at the castle, and the next day they went on their way from there. And King Arthur was very heavy and troubled in spirit. Even so, he ordered Boisenard to say nothing about what had happened, but to keep it secret. And Boisenard did as the King commanded and said nothing about that adventure.

Now in the year that followed, King Arthur set his affairs in order. He also searched everywhere to find the answer to that riddle. Many people gave him answers in plenty. One said that a woman most wanted wealth, and another said she most wanted beauty; one said she wanted the power to please, and another said that she most wanted fine clothes. And one said this, and another said that, but no answer seemed to King Arthur good and fitting for his purpose.

So the year went by until only two weeks were left. Then King Arthur could not bear to stay where he was any longer, for it seemed to him that his time was very close at hand. He was filled with a very bitter anxiety of soul, and so he was very restless to be gone.

So he called Boisenard to him, and he said, "Boisenard, help me arm, for I am going away."

Then Boisenard burst into a flood of tears, and he said, "Lord, do not go."

At this King Arthur looked very sternly at his squire and said, "Boisenard, what is this? Would you tempt me to break my honor? It is not very hard to die, but it would be very bitter to live my life in dishonor. So tempt me no more, but do as I bid you and hold your peace. And if I do not come back a month from now, then you may tell everything that has happened. And you may tell Sir Constantine of Cornwall that he is to search the papers in my cabinet, and there he will find everything that is to be done if death should overtake me."

King Arthur sets out to return to the castle of the evil knight.

So Boisenard put a plain suit of armor on King Arthur, though he could hardly see what he was doing for the tears that streamed from his eyes. And over the King's armor he laced a surcoat without any emblem, and he gave the King a shield without any emblem. Then King Arthur rode away without caring where his road took him. And of everyone he met he asked what it was that a woman most wanted, and no one could give him an answer that seemed to him to be the right one. And so he was in great doubt and torment of spirit.

King Arthur comes to the hut of an old woman.

Now on the day before King Arthur was to keep his bargain at that castle, he was wandering through the nearby forest in great anguish of soul, for he did not know what to do to save his life. As he wandered, he suddenly came upon a small hut built under an overhanging oak tree, so that it was very hard to tell where the oak ended and the hut began. And all around lay a great many large rocks covered with moss, so that the King might very easily have passed the hut by, except that he saw smoke rising from it, as from a fire burning inside. So he went to the hut and opened the door and went in. At first he thought there was no one there, but when he looked again he saw an old woman sitting bent over a small fire that burned on the hearth. And King Arthur had never seen such an ugly old hag as the one who sat there bending over that fire. Her ears were very huge and flapping, and her hair hung down over her head like snakes. Her face was covered all over with wrinkles, so that there was no place at all on it without a wrinkle. Her eyes were bleary and covered over with a film, and the eyelids were red as if from constant weeping. She had only one tooth in her mouth, and her hands, which she spread out to the fire, were like claws of bone.

Then King Arthur greeted her, and she greeted the King, and she said to him, "My lord King, where do you come from? And why have you come to this place?"

King Arthur was greatly astonished that this old woman should know who he was, and he said, "Who are you, who seem to know me?" "Never mind that," she said. "I am one who wishes you well. So tell me what trouble it is that brings you here at this time." So the King told that old woman all his trouble, and he asked her whether she knew the answer to the riddle, "What is it that a woman most wants?" "Yes," said the old woman, "I know the answer to that riddle very well. But I will not tell it to you unless you promise me something in return."

At this King Arthur was filled with very great joy that the old woman knew the answer to the riddle; but he was also filled with doubt about what she would ask of him. And so he said, "What is it you must have in return for that answer?"

Then the old woman said, "If I help you to answer your riddle rightly, you must promise that I shall become the wife of one of the knights of your Court, whichever one I may choose, when you go home again."

"Ha!" said King Arthur. "How can I promise that on anyone else's behalf?" At this the old woman said, "Are not the knights of your Court so noble that they will do that to save you from death?" "I believe they are," said King Arthur. And with that he thought for a long while, saying to himself, "What will my kingdom do if I die now? I have no right to die." So he said to the old woman, "Very well, I will make that promise."

The old woman tells King Arthur how to answer the riddle.
